Error estimates for DeepONets: A deep learning framework in infinite dimensions Thanks: E-Mail : samuel.lanthaler@math.ethz.ch
Samuel Lanthaler, Siddhartha Mishra, George Em Karniadakis

Abstract
. DeepONets have recently been proposed as a framework for learning nonlinear operators mapping between infinite dimensional Banach spaces. We analyze DeepONets and prove estimates on the resulting approximation and generalization errors. In particular, we extend the universal approximation property of DeepONets to include measurable mappings in non-compact spaces. By a decomposition of the error into encoding, approximation and reconstruction errors, we prove both lower and upper bounds on the total error, relating it to the spectral decay properties of the covariance operators, associated with the underlying measures. We derive almost optimal error bounds with very general affine reconstructors and with random sensor locations as well as bounds on the generalization error, using covering number arguments. We illustrate our general framework with four prototypical examples of nonlinear operators, namely those arising in a nonlinear forced ODE, an elliptic PDE with variable coefficients and nonlinear parabolic and hyperbolic PDEs. While the approximation of arbitrary Lipschitz operators by DeepONets to accuracy ϵ \epsilon is argued to suffer from a “curse of dimensionality” (requiring a neural networks of exponential size in 1 / ϵ 1/\epsilon ), in contrast, for all the above concrete examples of interest, we rigorously prove that DeepONets can break this curse of dimensionality (achieving accuracy ϵ \epsilon with neural networks of size that can grow algebraically in 1 / ϵ 1/\epsilon ) . Thus, we demonstrate the efficient approximation of a potentially large class of operators with this machine learning framework.



1.
Introduction

Deep neural networks

Goodfellow et al. 2016
have been very successfully used for a diverse range of regression and classification learning tasks in science and engineering in recent years
LeCun et al. 2015
. These include image and text classification, computer vision, text and speech recognition, natural language processing, autonomous systems and robotics, game intelligence and protein folding
Evans et al. 2018
.

As deep neural networks are
universal approximators
, i.e., they can approximate any continuous (even measurable) finite-dimensional function to arbitrary accuracy
Barron 1993
;
Hornik et al. 1989
;
Cybenko 1989
;
Tianping Chen & Liu 1990
, it is natural to use them as ansatz spaces for the solutions of partial differential equations (PDEs). They have been used for solving high-dimensional parabolic PDEs by emulating explicit representations such as the Feynman-Kac formula as in
E et al. 2017
;
Han et al. 2018
;
Beck et al. 2021
and references therein, and as
physics informed neural networks
(PINNs) for solving both forward problems
Raissi & Karniadakis 2018
;
Raissi et al. 2019
;
Mao et al. 2020
;
Mishra & Molinaro 2020
;
Mishra & Molinaro 2021b
, as well as inverse problems
Raissi et al. 2019
;
Raissi et al. 2018
;
Mishra & Molinaro 2021a
;
Lu et al. 2021
for a variety of linear and non-linear PDEs.

Deep neural networks are also being widely used in the context of
many query
problems for PDEs, such as uncertainty quantification (UQ)
(
O’Leary-Roseberry et al. 2022
;
Zhu & Zabaras 2018
;
Lye et al. 2020
, see e.g.)
, optimal control (design), deterministic and Bayesian inverse problems
Adler & Öktem 2017
;
Khoo & Ying 2019
and PDE constrained optimization
Guo et al. 2016
;
Lye et al. 2021
. In such
many query
problems, the inputs are functions such as the initial and boundary data, source terms and/or coefficients in the underlying differential operators. The outputs are either the solution field (in space-time or at fixed time instances) or possibly
observables
(functionals of the solution field). Thus, the input to output map is, in general, a (possibly) non-linear
operator
, mapping one function space to another.

Currently, it is standard to approximate the underlying input function with a finite, but possibly very high-dimensional,
parametric
representation. Similarly, the resulting output function is approximated by a finite dimensional representation, for instance, values on a grid or coefficients of a suitable basis. Thus, the underlying
operator, mapping infinite dimensional spaces,
is approximated by a function that maps a finite but high dimensional input spaces into another finite-dimensional output space. Consequently, this finite-dimensional map for the resulting
parametric PDE
can be
learned
with
standard
deep neural networks, as for elliptic and parabolic PDEs in
Schwab & Zech 2019
;
Opschoor et al. 2019
;
Opschoor et al. 2020
;
Kutyniok et al. 2021
, for transport PDEs
Laakmann & Petersen 2021
and for hyperbolic and related PDEs
(
DeRyck & Mishra 2021
;
Lye et al. 2020
;
Lye et al. 2021
, and references therein)
.

However, this finite dimensional parametrization of the underlying infinite dimensional problem is subject to the inherent and non-vanishing error both at the input end, due to the finite dimensional representation as well as at the output end, on account of numerical errors at finite resolution. More fundamentally, a parametric representation requires explicit knowledge of the underlying measure on input space such that a finite dimensional approximation of inputs can be performed. Such explicit knowledge may not always be available. Finally, the parametric approach does not cover a large number of situations where the underlying physics, in the form of governing PDEs, may not even be known explicitly, yet large amounts of (possibly noisy) data for the input-output mapping is available. It is not obvious how such a learning task can be performed with standard neural networks.

Hence,
operator learning
, i.e. learning nonlinear operators mapping one infinite-dimensional Banach space to another, from data, is increasingly being investigated in the contexts of PDEs and possibly other fields.
One research direction has focused largely on operators which can be expressed as solution operators to a suitable PDE/ODE; examples of this approach include the identification of individual terms of the underlying differential equation from data, expressed in terms of non-local integral operators
(
Patel et al. 2021
;
You et al. 2021
, and references therein)
, the identification of suitable closure models for turbulent flows
(
Duraisamy et al. 2019
;
Ahmed et al. 2021
, and references therein)
or the discovery of the governing equations of an underlying dynamical system, expressed in terms of an ODE (or PDE),
(
Brunton et al. 2016
, and references therein)
. In a different research direction, the aim is to use deep neural networks to directly learn the
underlying (solution–)operator
, itself. Several frameworks have been proposed for this task; we refer to
Li, Kovachki, Azizzadenesheli, Liu, Stuart, Bhattacharya
& Anandkumar 2020
and
Li, Kovachki, Azizzadenesheli, Liu, Bhattacharya, Stuart
& Anandkumar 2020
for graph kernel operators,
Bhattacharya et al. 2021
for a recent approach based on principal component analysis, and
Li et al. 2021
and references therein on
Fourier neural operators
.
A different approach was proposed by
Chen & Chen 1995
, where they presented a neural network architecture, termed as
operator nets
, to approximate a non-linear operator
\mathcal{G}:K\to K^{\prime}
, where
K,K^{\prime}
are compact subsets of infinite dimensional Banach spaces,
K\subset C(D)
,
K^{\prime}\subset C(U)
with
D,\,U
compact domains in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, respectively. Then, an operator net can be formulated in terms of two shallow, i.e., one hidden layer, neural networks. The first is the so-called
branch net

\bm{\beta}(u)=(\beta_{1}(u),\dots,\beta_{p}(u))
, defined for
1\leq k\leq p
as,

(1.1)

\beta_{k}(u)=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}c_{k}^{i}\sigma\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\xi_{ki}^{j}u(x_{j})+\theta_{k}^{i}\right).

Here,
\{x_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq m}\subset D
, are the so-called
sensors
and
c^{i}_{k},\xi^{j}_{ki}
are weights and
\theta_{k}^{i}
are biases of the neural network.

The second neural network is the so-called
trunk net

\bm{\tau}(y)=(\tau_{1}(y),\dots,\tau_{p}(y))
, defined as,

(1.2)

\tau_{k}(y)=\sigma(w_{k}\cdot y+\zeta_{k}),\quad 1\leq k\leq p,

for any
y\in U
and with weights
w_{k}
and biases
\zeta_{k}
. Here,
\sigma
is a non-linear activation function in the branch net (
1.1
) and (a possibly different one) in the trunk net (
1.2
). The branch and trunk nets are then combined to approximate the underlying non-linear operator in the
operator net

(1.3)

\mathcal{G}(u)(y)\approx\sum_{k=1}^{p}\beta_{k}(u)\tau_{k}(y),\quad u\in K,\;y\in U.

More recently,
Lu et al. 2019
replace the shallow branch and trunk nets in the operator net (
1.3
) with deep neural networks to propose
deep operator nets
(
DeepONets
in short), which are expected to be more expressive than shallow operator nets and have already been successfully applied to a variety of problems with differential equations. These include learning linear and non-linear dynamical systems and reaction-diffusion PDEs with source terms
Lu et al. 2019
, learning the PDEs governing electro-convection
Mao et al. 2021
, Navier-Stokes equations in hypersonics with chemistry
Cai et al. 2021
and the dynamics of bubble growth
Lin et al. 2021
, among others. A simple example that illustrates DeepONets (
1.3
) and their ability to learn an operator efficiently is included in Appendix
D
(cf. Figure
2
).

Why are DeepONets able to approximate operators mapping infinite dimensional spaces, efficiently? A first answer to this question lies in a remarkable
universal approximation theorem
for the operator network (
1.3
) first proved by
Chen & Chen 1995
, and extended to DeepONets by
Lu et al. 2019
, where it is shown that as long as the underlying operator
\mathcal{G}
is
continuous
and maps a
compact subset
of the infinite-dimensional space into another Banach space, there always exists an operator network of the form (
1.3
), that approximates
\mathcal{G}
to arbitrary precision, i.e. to any given error tolerance. However, the assumptions on continuity and in particular, compactness of the input space, in the universal approximation theorem do not cover most examples of practical interest, such as many of the operators considered by
Lu et al. 2019
. Moreover, this universal approximation property does not provide any explicit information on the computational complexity of the operator network, i.e. no explicit knowledge of the number of sensors
m
, number of branch and trunk nets
p
and the sizes (number of weights and biases) as well as depths (for DeepONets) of these neural networks can be inferred from the universal approximation property.

Given the infinite dimensional setting, it could easily happen that the computational complexity of the DeepONet for attaining a given tolerance
\epsilon
scales
exponentially
in
1/\epsilon
. In fact, we will provide a heuristic argument strongly suggesting that such exponential scaling can not be overcome in the approximation of
general
Lipschitz continuous operators (cp. Remark
3.4
; and Thm. 2.2 of
Mhaskar & Hahm 1997
for related work on rigorous lower bounds). This (worst-case) scaling will be referred to as the
curse of dimensionality
and can severely inhibit the efficiency of DeepONets at realistic learning tasks. Although numerical experiments presented in
Lu et al. 2019
;
Cai et al. 2021
;
Mao et al. 2021
;
Lin et al. 2021
strongly indicate that DeepONets may not suffer from this curse of dimensionality for many cases of interest, no rigorous results to this end are available currently. Moreover, no rigorous results on the DeepONet
generalization error
, i.e., the error due to finite sampling of the input space, are available currently.

The above considerations motivate our current paper where we seek to provide rigorous and explicit bounds on the error incurred by DeepONets in approximating nonlinear operators on Banach spaces. As a first step, we extend the universal approximation theorem from continuous to
measurable
operators, while removing the compactness requirements of
Chen & Chen 1995
. Next, using a very natural decomposition of DeepONets (cf Figure
1
) into an
encoder
that maps the infinite-dimensional input space into a finite-dimensional space, an
approximator
neural network that maps one finite-dimensional space into another and a trunk net induced affine
reconstructor
, that maps a finite dimensional space into the infinite dimensional output space, we decompose the total DeepONet approximation error in terms of the resulting
encoding, approximation and reconstruction errors
and estimate each part separately. This allows us to derive rigorous
upper
as well as
lower
bounds on the DeepONet error, under very general hypotheses on the underlying nonlinear operator and underlying measures on input Hilbert spaces. In particular, optimal bounds on the encoding and reconstruction errors stem from a careful analysis of the eigensystem for the covariance operators, associated with the underlying input measure.

A similar error decomposition has been employed in
Bhattacharya et al. 2021
, to analyze an operator learning architecture combining principal components analysis (PCA) autoencoders for the encoding and reconstruction with a neural network for the non-linear approximation step. In particular, the authors derive a quantitative error estimate for the
empirical
PCA autoencoder, which is based on a
finite number
of input/output samples
(u_{j},\mathcal{G}(u_{j}))
,
j=1,\dots,N
. However, we need significant additional efforts to translate ”PCA” based ideas into quantitative error and complexity estimates for the point-evaluation encoder and the neural network reconstruction of DeepONets (even in the limit of infinite data). Another key distinction of the present work with
Bhattacharya et al. 2021
is a detailed discussion of the
efficiency
of the DeepONet approximation, providing quantitative error and complexity bounds not only for the encoding and reconstruction steps, but also for the approximator network.

In addition to our analysis of the encoding, approximation and reconstruction errors,
we illustrate these abstract error estimates with four prototypical differential equations, namely a nonlinear ODE with a forcing term, a linear elliptic PDE with variable diffusion coefficients, a semi-linear parabolic PDE (Allen-Cahn equation) and a quasi-linear hyperbolic PDE (scalar conservation law), thus covering a wide spectrum of differential equations with different types of inputs and different levels of Sobolev regularity of the resulting solutions. For each of these four problems, we rigorously prove that the underlying operators possess additional structure, through which DeepONets can achieve an approximation accuracy
\epsilon
with a size that scales only algebraically in
1/\epsilon
, i.e.
DeepONets can break the curse of dimensionality
, associated with the approximation of the infinite-dimensional input-to-output map. Thus providing the first rigorous proofs of their possible efficiency at operator approximation. An appropriate notion of the curse of dimensionality in the DeepONet context will be given in Definition
3.5
(cp. also Remark
3.4
). Finally, we also provide a rigorous bound for the
generalization error
of DeepONets and show that, despite the underlying infinite dimensional setting, the estimate on generalization error scales (asymptotically) as
1/\sqrt{N}
with
N
being the number of training samples (up to log terms), which is consistent with the standard finite dimensional bound with statistical learning theory techniques.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows: In section
2
, we formulate the underlying operator learning problem and introduce DeepONets. The abstract error estimates are presented in section
3
and are illustrated on four concrete model problems in section
4
. Sections
3
and
4
focus on quantitative bounds for the
best-approximation error
that is achievable, in principle, by the given DeepONet architecture; additional (generalization) errors due to the availability of only a finite number of training samples, are discussed in section
5
, where estimates on the DeepONet generalization error are derived. The proofs of our theoretical results are presented in the appendix. Other error sources, e.g. due to (imperfect) training algorithms such as stochastic gradient descent, errors due to uncertain and noisy data, or errors due to a mismatch between the training and evaluation data, will not be discussed in the present work. The analysis of such errors represent avenues for extensive future work. Moreover, for simplicity of the exposition, the results of the present work are formulated for neural networks with
ReLU activation function
; this particular choice of activation function is, however, not essential to reach the main conclusions.

2.
Deep Operator Networks

Our main aim in this section is to follow
Lu et al. 2019
and introduce DeepONets, i.e., deep version of the shallow operator network (
1.3
) for approximating operators. To this end, we start with a brief recapitulation of what a neural network is.

2.1.
Neural Networks.

Let
\mathbb{R}^{d_{in}}
and
\mathbb{R}^{d_{out}}
denote the input and output spaces, respectively. Given any input vector
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{in}}
, a feedforward neural network (also termed as a multi-layer perceptron), transforms it to an output through layers of units (neurons) consisting of either affine-linear maps between units (in successive layers) or scalar non-linear activation functions within units
Goodfellow et al. 2016
, resulting in the representation,

(2.1)

\mathcal{L}_{\theta}(y)=C_{K}\circ\sigma\circ C_{K-1}\ldots\ldots\ldots\circ\sigma\circ C_{2}\circ\sigma\circ C_{1}(y).

Here,
\circ
refers to the composition of functions and
\sigma
is a scalar (non-linear) activation function. A large variety of activation functions have been considered in the machine learning literature
Goodfellow et al. 2016
, including adaptive activation functions in
Jagtap et al. 2020
. Popular choices for the activation function
\sigma
in (
2.1
) include the sigmoid function, the
\tanh
function and the
ReLU
function defined by,

(2.2)

\sigma(z)=\max(z,0).

In the present work, we will only consider neural networks with ReLU activation function, i.e., the term “neural network” should be understood synonymous with “ReLU neural network”.

For any
1\leq k\leq K
, we define

(2.3)

C_{k}z_{k}=W_{k}z_{k}+b_{k},\quad\text{for}~W_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{k+1}\times d_{k}},z_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}},b_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{k+1}}.

For consistency of notation, we set
d_{1}=d_{in}
and
d_{K+1}=d_{out}
.

Thus in the terminology of machine learning, the neural network (
2.1
) consists of an input layer, an output layer and
(K-1)
hidden layers for some
1<K\in\mathbb{N}
. The
k
-th hidden layer (with
d_{k+1}
neurons) is given an input vector
z_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}}
and transforms it first by an affine linear map
C_{k}
(
2.3
) and then by a nonlinear (component wise) activation
\sigma
. A straightforward addition shows that our network contains
\left(d_{in}+d_{out}+\sum\limits_{k=2}^{K}d_{k}\right)
neurons.
We also denote,

(2.4)

\theta=\{W_{k},b_{k}\},

to be the concatenated set of (tunable) weights and biases for our network. It is straightforward to check that
\theta\in\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{M}
with

(2.5)

M=\sum_{k=1}^{K}(d_{k}+1)d_{k+1}.

We also introduce the following nomenclature for a deep neural network
\mathcal{L}
,

(2.6)

\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{L}_{\theta}):=\|\theta\|_{\ell^{0}},\quad 0pt(\mathcal{L}_{\theta})=K-1,

with
\|\theta\|_{\ell^{0}}=\#\{\theta_{k}\neq 0\}
denoting the total number of
non-zero
tuning parameters (weights and biases) of the neural network and
K-1
being the number of hidden layers of the network. Henceforth, the explicit
\theta
-dependence is suppressed for notational convenience and we denote the neural network (
2.1
) as
\mathcal{L}
.

2.2.
DeepONets

A DeepONet, as proposed in
Lu et al. 2019
is a deep neural network extension of the operator network (
1.3
). Roughly speaking, the shallow branch and trunk nets in (
1.1
) and (
1.2
) are replaced by deep neural networks of the form (
2.1
). However, we present a slightly more general form of DeepONets in this paper, as compared to the DeepONets of
Lu et al. 2019
. To this end, we recall that
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
are compact domains (e.g. with Lipschitz boundary) and introduce the following
operators
(cp. Figure
1
):

•

Encoder.
Given a set of
sensor
points
x_{j}\in D
, for
1\leq j\leq m
, we define the linear mapping,

(2.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}:C(D)\to\mathbb{R}^{m},\quad\mathcal{E}(u)=(u(x_{1}),\dots,u(x_{m})),

as the
encoder
mapping. Note that the encoder
\mathcal{E}
is well-defined as one can evaluate continuous functions pointwise.

•

Approximator.
Given the above sensor points
\{x_{j}\}
for
1\leq j\leq m
, the
approximator
is a deep neural network of the form (
2.1
) and defined as,

(2.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p},\;\{u_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}\mapsto\{\mathcal{A}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{p},

Note that
d_{in}=m
and
d_{out}=p
in the approximator neural network
\mathcal{A}
of form (
2.1
). Given the encoder and approximator, we define the
branch net

\bm{\beta}:C(D)\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
as the composition
\bm{\beta}(u)=\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{E}(u)
.

•

Reconstructor.
First, we denote a
trunk net

\bm{\tau}
as a neural network

\bm{\tau}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{p+1},\;y=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})\mapsto\{\tau_{k}(y)\}_{k=0}^{p},

with each
\tau_{k}
of the form (
2.1
), with
d_{in}=n
and
d_{out}=1
and for any
y\in U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Then, we define a
\bm{\tau}
-induced
reconstructor
as

(2.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{\bm{\tau}}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to C(U),\quad\mathcal{R}_{\bm{\tau}}(\alpha_{k}):=\tau_{0}(y)+\sum_{k=1}^{p}\alpha_{k}\tau_{k}(y).

Henceforth for notational convenience, we will suppress the
\bm{\tau}
-dependence of the
\bm{\tau}
-induced reconstructor and simply label it as
\mathcal{R}
. Note that the reconstructor is well-defined as the activation function
\sigma
in (
2.1
) is at least continuous.

Figure 1
.
Schematic illustration of the decomposition of a DeepONet into the encoder
\mathcal{E}
, approximator
\mathcal{A}
and reconstructor
\mathcal{R}
.

Given the above ingredients, we combine them into a DeepONet as,

(2.10)

\mathcal{N}:C(D)\to C(U),\quad\mathcal{N}(u)=(\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{E})(u).

I.e. a DeepONet is composed of three components:

(1)

Encoding: The encoder mapping
\mathcal{E}:C(D)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
u\mapsto\{u(x_{j})\}_{j=1}^{m}
,

(2)

Approximation: The encoded (finite-dimensional) data is approximated by a neural network mapping
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
,

(3)

Reconstruction: The result is decoded by
\mathcal{R}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to C(U)
,
\{\mathcal{A}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{p}\mapsto\tau_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{p}\mathcal{A}_{k}\tau_{k}
, with
\bm{\tau}
being the trunk net.

A graphical depiction of the constituent parts of a DeepONet is shown in figure
1
.
The only difference between our version of the DeepONet (
2.10
) and the version presented in the recent paper
Lu et al. 2019
, lies in the fact that we use a more general affine reconstruction step. In other words, setting
\tau_{0}(y)\equiv\tau_{0}
, for some
\tau_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
in (
2.9
) recovers the DeepONet of
Lu et al. 2019
. We remark in passing that although the above formulation assumes a mapping between (scalar) functions,
\mathcal{N}:C(D)\to C(U)
, all results in this work extend trivially to the more general case of DeepONet approximations for
systems

\mathcal{N}:C(D;\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}})\to C(U;\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}})
. For clarity of the exposition and simplicity of notation, we will focus on the case
d_{u}=d_{v}=1
, in the following.

We recall that the DeepONet (
2.10
) contains parameters corresponding to the weights and biases of the approximator neural network
\mathcal{A}
and the trunk net
\bm{\tau}
, that need to be tuned (
trained
) such that the DeepONet (
2.10
) approximates the underlying operator
\mathcal{G}:X\to Y
. To this end, we need to define a distance between
\mathcal{G}
and the DeepONet
\mathcal{N}
. A natural way to do this, is to fix a probability measure
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(X)
, and to consider the following error, measured in the
L^{2}(\mu)
-
norm
:

(2.11)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}=\left(\int\limits_{X}\int\limits_{U}\left|\mathcal{G}(u)(y)-\mathcal{N}(u)(y)\right|^{2}\,dy\,d\mu(u)\right)^{1/2},

with
\mathcal{N}
being the DeepONet (
2.10
). Note that we have replaced the function spaces
C(D)
and
C(U)
by more general function spaces
X
and
Y
, for which we will assume that there exists an embedding
X{\hookrightarrow}L^{2}(D)
,
Y{\hookrightarrow}L^{2}(U)
. In particular, for the error (
2.11
) to be well-defined, it suffices that

•

there exists a Borel set
A\subset X
, such that
\mu(A)=1
, and
A\subset C(D)
so that
\mathcal{E}(u)=(u(x_{1}),\dots,u(x_{m}))
is well-defined on
A
,

•

The mapping

\mathcal{G}:X\to Y,

maps given data
u\in X
to a
L^{2}(U)
function
v(y)=\mathcal{G}(u)(y)
defined on
U
, and
\mathcal{G}\in L^{2}(\mu):=L^{2}(\mu;\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{L^{2}(U)})
, in the sense that

\int_{X}\|\mathcal{G}(u)\|_{L^{2}(U)}^{2}\,d\mu(u)<\infty.

We formalize these concepts with the following definition:

Definition 2.1

(Data for DeepONet approximation)
.

Let
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be bounded domains. Let
X
,
Y
be separable Banach spaces with a continuous embedding
\iota:\,X{\hookrightarrow}L^{2}(D)
and
\overline{\iota}:\,Y{\hookrightarrow}L^{2}(U)
. We call
\mu
,
\mathcal{G}

data for the DeepONet approximation problem
, provided
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(X)
is a Borel probability measure on
X
, there exists a Borel set
A\subset X
, such that
\mu(A)=1
and
A
consists of continuous functions, and
\mathcal{G}:X\to Y
is a Borel measurable mapping, such that
\mathcal{G}\in L^{2}(\mu)
, i.e.
\int_{X}\|\mathcal{G}(u)\|_{L^{2}(U)}^{2}\,d\mu(u)<\infty
. Here,
\mathcal{P}_{2}(X)
is the set of probability measures with finite second moments
\int_{X}\|u\|_{X}^{2}\,d\mu(u)<\infty
.

Remark 2.2
.

The setting considered in Definition
2.1
corresponds to a “perfect data setting”, in which the input/output pairs
(u,\mathcal{G}(u))
,
u\sim\mu
are provided exactly, i.e. in the absence of measurement noise and uncertainty. Furthermore, the main focus of this work will be on the problem of finding complexity bounds on the best-approximation provided by the DeepONet architecture (
2.7
)–(
2.9
). Concerning the
finite data setting
, i.e. when only a finite number of input/output pairs
(u_{1},\mathcal{G}(u_{1})),\dots,(u_{N},\mathcal{G}(u_{N}))
,
u_{j}\sim\mu
are available, first results on the generalization error will be presented in Section
5
.

In the framework of nonlinear operators that arise in differential equations, the Banach spaces
X
and
Y
will be function spaces on
D
and
U
, respectively; a typical example is
X
,
Y=H^{s}(D)
for some
s\geq 0
, where
H^{s}(D)
denotes the
L^{2}
-based Sobolev space on
D
. The embeddings
\iota:\,X{\hookrightarrow}L^{2}(D)
,
\overline{\iota}:\,Y{\hookrightarrow}L^{2}(U)
will thus be canonical, and henceforth, we will identify
X\simeq\iota(X)
,
Y\simeq\overline{\iota}(Y)
as subsets of
L^{2}(D)
and
L^{2}(U)
, respectively.

Remark 2.3
.

A technical difficulty associated with DeepONets arises due to the specific form of the encoder (
2.7
), which is defined via point-wise evaluations. In principle, the components of this encoder could easily be replaced by more general functionals of
u
, and in fact, this might be more natural in certain settings (e.g. to model physical measurements; or for mathematical reasons, see Section
4.4
). For our general discussion, we will focus instead on encoders of the particular form (
2.7
). The main reason for this choice is the possibility for direct comparison with the numerical experiments of
Lu et al. 2019
, which are based on the DeepONet architecture (
2.7
)–(
2.9
). Furthermore, fixing a particular choice will allow us to analyse the encoding error associated with
\mathcal{E}
in great detail in Section
3.5
(see Section
3.2.3
for an overview).

Since the point-wise encoder
\mathcal{E}(u)=(u(x_{1}),\dots,u(x_{m}))
is not well-defined on spaces such as
L^{2}(D)
, we first show that (
2.11
) is nevertheless well-defined. From Lemma
B.1
, we infer that if
A\subset X
is a Borel measurable set such that
A\subset C(D)
, then
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{E}:A\to L^{2}(U)
is measurable, and possesses a measurable extension
\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{A}\circ\overline{\mathcal{E}}:L^{2}(D)\to L^{2}(U)
. Clearly, since
\mu(A)=1
, we then have

\displaystyle\int_{X}\int_{U}\big|\mathcal{G}(u)(y)-

\displaystyle(\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{A}\circ\overline{\mathcal{E}})(u)(y)\big|^{2}\,dy\,d\mu(u)

\displaystyle=\int_{A}\int_{U}\left|\mathcal{G}(u)(y)-(\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{E})(u)(y)\right|^{2}\,dy\,d\mu(u),

for any extension
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
. This allows us to define the error (
2.11
) uniquely and allows to formulate the following precise definition of DeepONets,

Definition 2.4

(DeepONet)
.

Let
\mu
,
\mathcal{G}
be given data for the DeepONet approximation problem (see Definition
2.1
). A
DeepONet

\mathcal{N}
, approximating the nonlinear operator
\mathcal{G}
, is a mapping
\mathcal{N}:C(D)\to L^{2}(U)
of the form
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{E}
, where
\mathcal{E}:(X,\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{X})\to(\mathbb{R}^{m},\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{\ell^{2}})
denotes the encoder given by (
2.7
),
\mathcal{A}:(\mathbb{R}^{m},\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{\ell^{2}})\to(\mathbb{R}^{p},\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{\ell^{2}})
denotes the approximator network (
2.8
), and
\mathcal{R}:(\mathbb{R}^{p},\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{\ell^{2}})\to(L^{2}(U),\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{L^{2}(U)})
denotes the reconstruction of the form (
2.9
), induced by the trunk net
\bm{\tau}
.

3.
Error bounds for DeepONets

Our aim in this section is to derive bounds on the error (
2.11
) incurred by the DeepONet (
2.10
) in approximating the underlying nonlinear operator
\mathcal{G}
.

3.1.
A universal approximation theorem

As a first step in showing that the DeepONet error (
2.11
) can be small, we have the following
universal approximation theorem
, that generalizes the universal approximation property of
Chen & Chen 1995
to significantly more general nonlinear operators,

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(C(D))
be a probability measure on
C(D)
. Let
\mathcal{G}:C(D)\to L^{2}(D)
be a Borel measurable mapping, with
\mathcal{G}\in L^{2}(\mu)
, then for every
\epsilon>0
, there exists an operator network
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{E}
, such that

\|\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{N}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=\left(\int_{X}\|\mathcal{G}(u)-\mathcal{N}(u)\|_{L^{2}(U)}^{2}\,d\mu(u)\right)^{1/2}<\epsilon.

The proof of this theorem is based on an application of Lusin’s Theorem to approximate measurable maps by continuous maps on compact subsets and then using the universal approximation theorem of
Chen & Chen 1995
. It is presented in detail in Appendix
C.1
.

Remark 3.2
.

The universal approximation theorem of
Chen & Chen 1995
states that DeepONets can approximate continuous operators
\mathcal{G}
uniformly over compact subsets
K\subset X
. In contrast, the above theorem removes both the compactness constraint, as well as the continuity assumption on
\mathcal{G}
and paves the way for the theorem to be applied in realistic settings, for instance in the approximation of nonlinear operators that arise when considering differential equations such as those of
Lu et al. 2019
and later in this paper. However, this extension comes at the expense of considering a weaker distance (
L^{2}(\mu\otimes dy)
vs.
L^{\infty}(\mu\otimes dy)
) than in
Chen & Chen 1995
. In practice, it is indeed the
L^{2}
-distance that is minimized during the training process. Moreover, the above theorem also allows us to consider cases of practical interest where
\mu
is supported on an
unbounded
subset, as is e.g. the case when
\mu
is a non-degenerate Gaussian measure. Indeed, in most of the numerical examples in
Lu et al. 2019
, the underlying measure
\mu
is a Gaussian measure given by the law of a Gaussian random field.

The universal approximation theorem
3.1
shows that for any given tolerance
\epsilon
, there exists a DeepONet of the form (
2.10
) such that the resulting approximation error (
2.11
) is smaller than this tolerance. However, this theorem does not provide any explicit information about the number of sensors
m
, the number of branch and trunk net outputs
p
or the hyperparameters of the approximator neural network
\mathcal{A}
and the trunk net
\bm{\tau}
. As discussed in the introduction, these numbers specify the complexity of a DeepONet and we would like to obtain explicit bounds (information) on the computational complexity of a DeepONet for achieving a given error tolerance and ascertain whether DeepONets are efficient at approximating a given nonlinear operator
\mathcal{G}
. In practice, we are thus interested in deriving
quantitative
error and complexity bounds for the DeepONet approximation of operators. This will be the focus of the remainder of the present section.

3.2.
Overview of quantitative error bounds

We will first provide an overview of the main results on quantitative error bounds derived in the present work. An extended discussion of these results can be found in the following subsections, which include detailed derivations and proofs.

3.2.1.
Error decomposition and the curse of dimensionality

Given the decomposition of the DeepONet (
2.10
) into an encoder
\mathcal{E}
, approximator
\mathcal{A}
and reconstructor
\mathcal{R}
, it is natural to expect that the total error (
2.11
) also decomposes into errors associated with them. For a given encoder
\mathcal{E}
and reconstructor
\mathcal{R}
, we can define (approximate) inverses
\mathcal{D}
(the
decoder
) and
\mathcal{P}
(the
projector
), which are required to satisfy the following relations exactly

\mathcal{E}\circ\mathcal{D}=\mathrm{Id}:\,{\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}},\quad\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{R}=\mathrm{Id}:\,{\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}},

and should satisfy

\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}\approx\mathrm{Id}:\,{X\to X},\quad\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}\approx\mathrm{Id}:\,{Y\to Y}.

We note that
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{P}
are not necessarily unique, and need to be chosen. All mappings are illustrated in the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{2}(D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{2}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}}

Given choices for the decoder
\mathcal{D}
and the projector
\mathcal{P}
, we can now define the
encoding error

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}
, the
approximation error

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{A}}
, and the
reconstruction error

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}
, respectively, as follows:

(3.1)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}

\displaystyle:=\left(\int_{X}\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}(u)-u\|_{X}^{2}\,d\mu(u)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

(3.2)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle:=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\|\mathcal{A}(\bm{u})-\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}(\bm{u})\|_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{p})}^{2}\,d(\mathcal{E}_{\#}\mu)(\bm{u})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(3.3)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}

\displaystyle:=\left(\int_{L^{2}(U)}\|\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}(u)-u\|_{L^{2}(U)}^{2}\,d(\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu)(u)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

We could also have written these errors as
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}=\left\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}-\mathrm{Id}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
,
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{A}}=\|\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{E}_{\#}\mu)}
, and
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}=\left\|\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}-\mathrm{Id}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu)}
.
Intuitively, the encoding and reconstruction errors,
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}
and
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}
, measure the loss of information by the DeepONet’s finite-dimensional encoding of the underlying infinite-dimensional spaces; these error sources are weighted by the input measure
\mu
on the input side, and the push-forward measure
\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu
on the output side, respectively. The approximation error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{A}}
measures the error due to the approximation by the approximator network
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
of the “encoded/projected” operator,
G=\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
.

To state our main result on the error decomposition of
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}
in terms of
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}
,
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}
, we first recall the following notation for a mapping
\mathcal{F}:X\to Y
between arbitrary Banach spaces
X,Y
:

\mathrm{Lip}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{F}:X\to Y):=\sup_{u,u^{\prime}\in X}\frac{\|\mathcal{F}(u)-\mathcal{F}(u^{\prime})\|_{Y}}{\|u-u^{\prime}\|_{X}^{\alpha}},\quad\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{F}):=\mathrm{Lip}_{1}(\mathcal{F}).

We then have the following error estimate, whose proof can be found in Appendix
C.2
:

Theorem 3.3
.

Consider the setting of Definition
2.1
. Let the nonlinear operator
\mathcal{G}:A\subset X\to Y
be
\alpha
-Hölder continuous (or Lipschitz continuous if
\alpha=1
), where
X{\hookrightarrow}L^{2}(D)
,
Y{\hookrightarrow}L^{2}(U)
. Choose an arbitrary encoder
\mathcal{E}:C(D)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
, approximator
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
and an arbitrary reconstruction
\mathcal{R}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to L^{2}(U)
, of the form (
2.9
). Then the error (
2.11
) associated with the DeepONet
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{E}
satisfies the following upper bound,

(3.4)

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}\leq\mathrm{Lip}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{G})\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P})(\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}})^{\alpha}+\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{R})\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{A}}+\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}.

The last theorem shows that the total error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}
is indeed controlled by
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}
,
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}
.
Furthermore, this theorem provides us with a clear strategy for estimating the DeepONet error (
2.11
) for a concrete operator
\mathcal{G}
of interest:

•

First, we will bound the encoding/reconstruction errors, providing suitable estimates for

\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}\approx\mathrm{Id},\quad\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}\approx\mathrm{Id}.

In this step, we need to choose
\mathcal{D}
,
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{R}
,
\mathcal{P}
in order to minimize the resulting encoding and reconstruction errors.

•

In a second step, we estimate the approximation error

\|\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{E}_{\#}\mu)},

for fixed projector
\mathcal{P}
and decoder
\mathcal{D}
. The second step boils down to the conventional approximation of a function
G:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
by neural networks.

Our goal will be to analyze the total error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}
in terms of this decomposition, with the aim of showing the
efficiency
of the DeepONet approximation for a wide range of operators of interest. To this end, we will first need to discuss a suitable notion of “efficiency”, which is motivated by the following remark.

Remark 3.4
.

As shown above, the error introduced by the approximation step in the DeepONet decomposition is naturally related to the error in the approximation of a high-dimensional mapping
G=\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
by the neural network
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
. The relevant function
G
can be thought of as a finite-dimensional projection of the operator
\mathcal{G}
. In particular,
G
inherits regularity properties of
\mathcal{G}
such as Lipschitz continuity. As shown in
(
Yarotsky 2018
, Theorem 1)
, the approximation of a
general
Lipschitz continuous function to accuracy
\sim\epsilon
, requires a ReLU network of size
\gtrsim\epsilon^{-m/2}
, and hence suffers from the
curse of dimensionality
in high dimensions,
m\gg 1
. In the context of DeepONets, we recall that
m
is the number of sensors used in the encoding step
u\mapsto\mathcal{E}(u)=(u(x_{1}),\dots,u(x_{m}))
of the DeepONet architecture. Achieving a small error of order
\sim\epsilon
in this encoding step requires
m=m(\epsilon)
to depend on
\epsilon
, with
m(\epsilon)\to\infty
as
\epsilon\to 0
. Therefore, general neural network approximation results indicate that the required DeepONet complexity for the approximation
\mathcal{N}\approx\mathcal{G}
of an
arbitrary
Lipschitz continuous operator
\mathcal{G}
to a given accuracy
\epsilon
requires at least
\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{N})\gtrsim\epsilon^{-m(\epsilon)/2}
. In particular, this scaling is faster than
any algebraic rate
in
\epsilon^{-1}
. This connection between the curse of dimensionality, as commonly understood in the literature
Chkifa et al. 2015
;
Cohen et al. 2011
;
Cohen et al. 2010
, and this worse-than-algebraic asymptotic growth of DeepONet size in
\epsilon^{-1}
provides an appropriate notion of the curse of dimensionality in the infinite-dimensional DeepONet context.

Given Remark
3.4
above, we can say that “DeepONets break the curse of dimensionality” in the approximation of a given operator
\mathcal{G}
, if, for any accuracy
\epsilon>0
, there exists a DeepONet which achieves an approximation error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}\leq\epsilon
(cp. (
2.11
)), with a complexity which scales
at most algebraically
in
\epsilon^{-1}
.
This key concept with respect to computational complexity and efficiency of DeepONet approximation is made precise below:

Definition 3.5

(Curse of Dimensionality for DeepONets)
.

Given a DeepONet
\mathcal{N}
(
2.10
), we define the
size
of the DeepONet as the sum of the sizes of the approximator neural network
\mathcal{A}
and the trunk net
\bm{\tau}
, i.e.
\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{N})=\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{A})+\mathrm{size}(\bm{\tau})
(cp. (
2.6
)). For a given tolerance
\epsilon>0
, let
\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon}
be a DeepONet such that the error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}
(
2.11
) is less than
\epsilon
, and

(3.5)

\mathrm{size}\left(\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon}\right)\sim{\mathcal{O}}\left(\epsilon^{-\vartheta_{\epsilon}}\right),

for some
\vartheta_{\epsilon}\geq 0
. Note that the universal approximation theorem
3.1
guarantees the existence of such a
\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon}
and
\vartheta_{\epsilon}
for every
\epsilon>0
.

The DeepONet approximation of a nonlinear operator
\mathcal{G}
, with underlying measure
\mu
(check from Definition
2.1
) is said to incur a
curse of dimensionality
, if

(3.6)

\lim\limits_{\epsilon\to 0}\vartheta_{\epsilon}=+\infty.

On the other hand, the DeepONet approximation is said to
break the curse of dimensionality
if there exist DeepONets
\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon}
such,

(3.7)

\lim\limits_{\epsilon\to 0}\vartheta_{\epsilon}=\overline{\vartheta}<+\infty.

This definition emphasizes the fundamental role played by bounds on the size of the DeepONet for obtaining a certain level of error tolerance. We provide such explicit bounds later in this paper. In the following subsections, we survey our main results on the reconstruction error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}
, the encoding error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}
and the approximation error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{A}}
.

3.2.2.
Reconstruction error

The reconstruction error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}
(
2.9
) is intimately related to the eigenfunctions and eigenvalues of the covariance operator
\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}
of the push-forward measure
\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu
,

(3.8)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}=\int_{Y}(v-\mathbb{E}[v])\otimes(v-\mathbb{E}[v])\,d(\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu)(v),

where
\mathbb{E}[v]=\int_{Y}v\,d(\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu)(v)
denotes the mean of
\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu
. General results on the relation between the eigenstructure of covariance operators and optimal projections onto finite-dimensional linear and affine subspace are presented in Section
3.3
, below. Based on these results, it will be shown that

(1)

for any affine reconstruction
\mathcal{R}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to L^{2}(U)
, there exists a (unique) optimal projection
\mathcal{P}:L^{2}(U)\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
, and that this optimal
\mathcal{P}
is itself
affine
(cp. Lemma
3.13
),

(2)

among all affine reconstructions
\mathcal{R}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to L^{2}(U)
, there exists an optimal choice
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{opt}}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to L^{2}(U)
, which achieves the minimum reconstruction error:
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{opt}}}=\min_{\mathcal{R}}\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}
(cp. Theorem
3.14
).

As a consequence of the analysis of the optimal reconstruction, we can then derive the following
lower bound
on the reconstruction and total approximation errors:

Theorem 3.6
.

Consider the setting of Definition
2.1
, let
\mathcal{G}:X\to L^{2}(U)
be an operator. Let
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{E}:X\to L^{2}(U)
be an arbitrary DeepONet approximation of
\mathcal{G}
, with encoder
\mathcal{E}:C(D)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
, approximator
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
and reconstruction
\mathcal{R}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to L^{2}(U)
. Let
\mathcal{P}:L^{2}(U)\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
be the optimal affine projection associated with
\mathcal{R}
. Then the total error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}
and the reconstruction error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}
can be estimated
from below
by

(3.9)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{k}}\leq\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}\leq\widehat{\mathscr{E}},

in terms of the eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots
of the covariance operator
\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}
associated with the push-forward measure
\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu
.

Theorem
3.6
provides a definite, a priori limitation on the best error which can be achieved by a DeepONet approximation. The proof of this theorem can be found on page
3.4
.

The next goal is to provide
upper
bounds on
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}
for a suitably chosen trunk net reconstruction
\mathcal{R}
(
2.9
). As mentioned in point (2) above, in principle, there exists a provably
optimal
choice
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{opt}}
among all affine reconstructions. However, given that the trunk net basis functions
\tau_{0},\dots,\tau_{p}
are represented by
neural networks
, this optimal choice
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{opt}}
cannot in general be represented exactly by the trunk net reconstruction
\mathcal{R}
, leading to an additional contribution to the reconstruction error, which depends on how well the eigenfunctions of the covariance operator can be approximated by neural networks (cp. Proposition
3.15
).

As will be discussed in Section
3.4.1
, due to the distortion by
\mathcal{G}
, the eigenstructure of the push-forward measure
\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu
can be very different from that of
\mu
. In fact, even if
\mu
has exponentially decaying spectrum of the covariance operator
\Gamma_{\mu}
, the push-foward under
\mathcal{G}
can destroy such high rates of decay of the eigenvalues of
\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}
(cp. Proposition
3.19
). Thus, with the exception of
linear
operators (cp. Proposition
3.20
), the eigenstructure of
\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu
may to depend in a very complicated way on both
\mathcal{G}
and
\mu
, for
non-linear

\mathcal{G}
, making it difficult to analyze
\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}
.

As it can be very difficult to obtain the necessary information on the eigenfunctions needed to
quantify
their approximability by the trunk net
\bm{\tau}
, we will discuss an alternative way to obtain estimates on
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}
, in Section
3.4
. This alternative relies on a comparison principle with a given (non-optimal) reconstructor
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to L^{2}(U)
(cp. Lemma
3.16
). As one concrete application, this general comparison principle is applied to the reconstructor
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}=\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Fourier}}
, obtained by expansion in the standard Fourier basis. This allows us to derive the following quantitative upper reconstruction error and complexity estimate, which depends only on the
average smoothness
of the output functions
\mathcal{G}(u)\in L^{2}(U)
(stated in the periodic setting, for simplicity):

Theorem 3.7
.

If
\mathcal{G}
defines a Lipschitz mapping
\mathcal{G}:X\to H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{n})
, for some
s>0
, with

\int_{X}\|\mathcal{G}(u)\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\,d\mu(u)\leq M<\infty,

then there exists a constant
C=C(n,s,M)>0
, such that for any
p\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a trunk net
\bm{\tau}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
(with bias term
\tau_{0}\equiv 0
), with

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}\mathrm{size}(\bm{\tau})\leq Cp(1+\log(p)^{2}),\\
0pt(\bm{\tau})\leq C(1+\log(p)^{2}),\end{gathered}

and such that the associated reconstruction
\mathcal{R}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n})
,

\mathcal{R}(\alpha)=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\alpha_{k}\tau_{k}
satisfies

(3.12)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}\leq Cp^{-s/n}.

Furthermore, the reconstruction
\mathcal{R}
and the associated optimal projection
\mathcal{P}
satisfy
\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{R}),\,\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{P})\leq 2
.

Theorem
3.7
follows from the discussion in Section
3.4.3
. Thus, (
3.12
) provides us with a quantitative algebraic rate of decay for the reconstruction error (
3.3
) as long as the size of the trunk net scales as in (
3.7
) and the nonlinear operator
\mathcal{G}
maps onto the Sobolev space
H^{s}
. Such nonlinear operators arise frequently in PDEs as we will see in Section
4
. For further details and extended analysis of the reconstruction error, we refer to Section
3.2.2
.

3.2.3.
Encoding error

Next, we aim to bound the encoding error (
3.1
), associated with the DeepONet (
2.10
). Full details and an extended discussion will be given in Section
3.5
.

We observe from (
3.1
) that the encoding error
does not depend
on the nonlinear operator
\mathcal{G}
, but only depends on the underlying probability measure
\mu
. Following the architecture used by
Lu et al. 2019
, we have fixed the form of the encoder
\mathcal{E}
(
2.7
) to be the point-wise evaluation of the input functions at
sensors
, i.e. points
\{x_{j}\}\subset D
with
1\leq j\leq m
. Thus, key objectives of our analysis are to determine suitable choices of sensors for a fixed
m
, as well as to find the appropriate form of a decoder
\mathcal{D}
in order to minimize the encoding error (
3.1
). To this end, we start with a result that provides a lower bound on the encoding error:

Theorem 3.8
.

Let
\mu
be a probability measure on
X=L^{2}(D)
with
\int_{X}\|u\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,d\mu(u)<\infty
and
\int_{X}u\,d\mu(u)=0
. If
\mathcal{E}:X\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\mathcal{D}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to X
are any encoder/decoder pair with a
linear
decoder
\mathcal{D}
, then

\int_{X}\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}-\mathrm{Id}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,d\mu\geq\sum_{k>m}\lambda_{k}.

Here,
\lambda_{k}
refers the
k
-th eigenvalue of the covariance operator
\Gamma_{\mu}
associated with the measure
\mu
.
In particular, we then have the lower bound

(3.13)

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}\geq\sqrt{\sum_{k>m}\lambda_{k}}.

The proof is presented in Appendix
C.10
. The bound (
3.13
) provides a lower bound on the encoding error and connects this error to the spectral decay of the underlying covariance operator, at least for linear decoders.

Our next aim is to derive upper bounds on the encoding error. To illustrate our main ideas, we will restrict our discussion to the case
X=L^{2}(D)
. The results are readily extended to more general spaces, such as Sobolev spaces
X=H^{s}(D)
for
s>0
. We fix a probability measure
\mu
on
L^{2}(D)
, and write the covariance operator
\Gamma_{\mu}
as an eigenfunction decomposition,

\Gamma_{\mu}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{\ell}(\phi_{\ell}\otimes\phi_{\ell}),

where
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots
are the decreasing eigenvalues, and such that the
\phi_{\ell}
are an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(D)
. We will assume that all
\phi_{\ell}
are continuous functions, so that point-wise evaluation of
\phi_{\ell}
makes sense. In this case, it can be shown (cp. Lemma
3.22
) that the encoding error is composed of the optimal lower bound (
3.13
), and an additional
aliasing
contribution, due to the encoding in terms of point-evaluations:

(\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}})^{2}=\sum_{\ell>m}\lambda_{\ell}+(\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{aliasing}})^{2}.

An explicit expression for
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{aliasing}}
can be given (cp. (
3.41
)), but finding sensor locations
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
which minimize this aliasing error contribution appears to be very difficult, in general. We therefore propose to replace the sensors
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
by
random
sensor locations
X_{1},\dots,X_{M}
(iid, uniformly distributed over the domain
D
), and study the corresponding
random encoder
,

\mathcal{E}(u)=(u(X_{1}),\dots,u(X_{M})).

Surprisingly, it can be shown that this random encoder can be close to optimal, as is made precise by the following theorem:

Theorem 3.9
.

If the eigenbasis of the uncentered covariance operator
\overline{\Gamma}
(
3.15
), associated with the underlying measure
\mu
, is bounded in
L^{\infty}
, then there exists a constant
C\geq 1
, depending only on
\sup_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\|\phi_{\ell}\|_{L^{\infty}}
and
|D|
, such that the encoding error (
3.1
) with
M
random sensors satisfies (for almost all
M\in\mathbb{N}
):

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{M})\leq C\sqrt{\sum_{\ell>M/C\log(M)}\lambda_{\ell}},\quad(\text{as }M\to\infty),

with probability
1
in the iid random sensors
X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},\dots\sim\mathrm{Unif}(D)
.

Thus, for an underlying measure
\mu
whose covariance operator has a bounded eigenbasis, then as the number of randomly chosen sensors increases, the resulting encoding error goes to zero almost surely. The rate of decay only depends on the spectral decay of the covariance operator. Moreover, given the lower bound (
3.13
) on the encoding error, we have the surprising result that
randomly chosen sensor points
lead to an optimal (up to a
\log
) decay of the encoding error (
3.1
), corresponding to the DeepONet (
2.10
). We refer the interested reader to the discussion leading to Lemma
3.24
, page
3.24
, for precise details of the derivation of Theorem
3.9
.

In addition to the above general results, we also consider the specific case of an input measure
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
, which is given as the
law
of a random field of the form

u(x;Y)=\overline{u}(x)+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}Y_{k}\alpha_{k}\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{k}(x),

where
\{\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{k}(x)\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
denotes the trigonometric basis (cp. Appendix
A
),
Y_{k}\in[-1,1]
are centered random variables, and the coefficients
\alpha_{k}\geq 0
satisfy a decay of the form
\alpha_{k}\lesssim\exp(-\ell|k|_{\infty})
, for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, for a fixed “length-scale”
\ell>0
. In this case, we study the encoder
\mathcal{E}
obtained by evaluation at sensor locations on an
equidistant grid
on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
. Given this setting, we show that there exists a decoder
\mathcal{D}
, such that the corresponding encoding error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}
can be estimated by an exponential upper bound,
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}\lesssim\exp(-c\ell m^{1/d})
(cp. Theorem
3.28
). We refer to Section
3.5.3
for the precise details.

3.2.4.
Approximation error

Given a particular choice of encoder/decoder and reconstruction/projection pairs
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D})
and
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{P})
, the approximation error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{A}}
(
3.2
) for the approximator
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
in the DeepONet (
2.10
) is a measure for the non-commutativity of the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{2}(D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{2}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}}

I.e., it measures the error in the approximation
\mathcal{A}\approx G=\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}
. Thus, bounding the approximation error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{A}}
can be viewed as a special instance of the general problem of the neural network approximation of a high-dimensional mapping
G:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
. As already pointed out in Remark
3.4
, relying on “mild” regularity properties of
G
(or
\mathcal{G}
), such as Lipschitz continuity, leads to complexity bounds which suffer from the curse of dimensionality (cp. Definition
3.5
, and Section
3.6.1
, below).

Given this possible curse of dimensionality in bounding the approximation error (
3.2
) for Lipschitz continuous maps, we seek to find a class of nonlinear operators
\mathcal{G}
for which this curse of dimensionality can be avoided:

•

One possible class is the class of
holomorphic mappings

[-1,1]^{\mathbb{N}}\to V
,
\bm{y}\mapsto\mathcal{F}(\bm{y})
, with
V
an arbitrary Banach space: Such operators have been shown in recent papers to be efficiently approximated by ReLU neural networks,
breaking the curse of dimensionality
.

•

Another strategy relies on the use of additional structure of the underlying operator
\mathcal{G}
, which is
not captured
by its smoothness properties. In this direction, we show that many PDE operators may possess such internal structure, making them not only amenable to approximation by classical numerical methods, but also enabling DeepONets to
break the curse of dimensionality
.

The first approach relying on holomorphy is discussed at an abstract level in Section
3.6.2
, where we apply the main results of
Schwab & Zech 2019
;
Opschoor et al. 2019
;
Opschoor et al. 2020
to DeepONets. The discussion of this specific parametrized setting ultimately leads to Theorem
3.35
, which provides quantitative estimates on the approximation error for the DeepONet approximation of holomorphic operators. This abstract result is applied to two concrete examples of holomorphic operators in Sections
4.1
and
4.2
.

In contrast to the holomorphic case, generally applicable results which rely on internal structure of
\mathcal{G}

other than smoothness
, appear to be much more difficult to state at an abstract level; therefore, in this case, we instead focus on a case-by-case discussion of the approximation error for concrete operators of interest, which we defer to Section
4.3
, for a parabolic PDE, and in Section
4.4
, for a hyperbolic PDE.

The remaining subsections of the present Section
3
provide the details as well as an extended discussion of the error associated with projections onto linear and affine subspaces (Section
3.3
), bounds on the reconstruction error (Section
3.4
), bounds on the encoding error (Section
3.5
), and bounds on the approximation error (Section
3.6
).

3.3.
On the error due to projections of Hilbert spaces onto linear and affine subspaces.

We start with the observation that the encoder
\mathcal{E}
is a linear mapping from
X
to
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. As long as we choose the decoder
\mathcal{D}
to also be a linear mapping from
\mathbb{R}^{m}
to
X
, we see that the encoding error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}
(
3.1
) can be bounded from below by the following
projection error
:

(3.14)

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{Proj}}(\widehat{V};\mu)=\int_{X}\inf_{\widehat{v}\in\widehat{V}}\|v-\widehat{v}\|^{2}_{X}\,d\mu(v),

where
\widehat{V}=\mathrm{Im}(\mathcal{D})
.

Hence, we need to study general properties of the projection error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{Proj}}(\widehat{V};\nu)
onto a finite-dimensional linear subspace
\widehat{V}\subset H
, for an arbitrary Hilbert space
H
, and given a probability measure
\nu\in\mathcal{P}(H)
with finite second moment
\int_{H}\|v\|^{2}_{H}\,d\nu(v)
. This study results in the following theorem that characterizes
optimal finite-dimensional subspaces
of the Hilbert space
H
,

Theorem 3.10
.

Let
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(H)
be a probability measure on a separable Hilbert space
H
. For any
p\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists an optimal
p
-dimensional subspace
V_{p}\subset H
, such that

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{Proj}}(V_{p};\nu)=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widehat{V}\subset V,\\
\dim(\widehat{V})=p\end{subarray}}\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{Proj}}(\widehat{V};\nu).

Furthermore, we can characterize the set of optimal subspaces
\widehat{V}\subset H
as follows:
Let
\overline{\lambda_{1}}>\overline{\lambda_{2}}>\dots
denote the distinct eigenvalues of the operator

(3.15)

\displaystyle\overline{\Gamma}=\int_{H}(v\otimes v)\,d\nu(v).

Let
E_{k}={\left\{v\in H\,\middle|\,\overline{\Gamma}v=\overline{\lambda}_{k}v\right\}}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
denote the corresponding eigenspaces. Choose
n\in\mathbb{N}
, such that

\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\dim(E_{k})<p\leq\sum_{k=1}^{n}\dim(E_{k}).

A
p
-dimensional subspace
\widehat{V}\subset H
is optimal for
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{Proj}}(\widehat{V};\nu)
, if and only if,

\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n-1}E_{k}\subset\widehat{V}\subset\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}E_{k}.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a
unique
optimal subspace
V_{p_{n}}\subset H
, of dimension
p_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\dim(E_{k})
. For any optimal subspace
\widehat{V}\subset H
, the resulting projection error is given by

(3.16)

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{Proj}}(\widehat{V})=\sum_{j>p}\lambda_{j},

where

\underbrace{\lambda_{1}=\dots=\lambda_{n_{1}}}_{=\overline{\lambda}_{1}}>\underbrace{\lambda_{n_{1}+1}=\dots=\lambda_{n_{1}+n_{2}}}_{=\overline{\lambda}_{2}}>\dots,

are the eigenvalues of
\overline{\Gamma}
repeated according to multiplicity, with
n_{j}=\dim(E_{j})
.

The proof of this theorem is based on a series of highly technical lemmas and is presented in detail in Appendix
C.3
. The essence of the above theorem is the connection between optimal linear subspaces (that minimize projection errors) of a Hilbert space and the eigensystem of its (uncentered) covariance operator (
3.15
). Thus given (
3.16
), the study of projection errors with respect to finite-dimensional linear subspaces requires a careful investigation into the decay of the eigenvalues of the operator (
3.15
) and will be instrumental in providing bounds on the encoding error (
3.1
) and enable us to identify suitable sensors
\{x_{j}\}
for defining the encoder
\mathcal{E}
.

Remark 3.11
.

We would like to point out that the main observations of Theorem
3.10
, and in particular, the important identity (
3.16
) for the minimal projection error have previously been observed in
Bhattacharya et al. 2021
. In the finite-dimensional case, the underlying ideas are well-known in principal component analysis. While the basic ideas are not new, we nevertheless include Theorem
3.10
in the present work for completeness, due to its central importance to our discussion.

Similarly, we observe that the trunk-net induced reconstructor
\mathcal{R}
(
2.9
) is an affine mapping between
\mathbb{R}^{p}
and the output Banach space
Y
. As will be shown in Lemma
3.13
, the reconstruction error (
3.3
) for any
\mathcal{R}
can be bounded from below by the error with respect to projection onto affine subspaces of the output Hilbert space. We formalize this notion below.

Given a separable Hilbert space
H
, let
\widehat{V}_{0}
now denote an affine subspace of the form

\widehat{V}_{0}={\left\{\widehat{v}=\widehat{v}_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{p}\alpha_{j}\widehat{v}_{j}\,\middle|\,\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{p}\in\mathbb{R}\right\}}.

for
\widehat{v}_{0},\dots,\widehat{v}_{p}\in H
. Note that for
any

\widehat{v}^{\prime}\in\widehat{V}_{0}
, the set

\widehat{V}_{0}-\widehat{v}^{\prime}={\left\{\widehat{v}-\widehat{v}^{\prime}\,\middle|\,\widehat{v}\in\widehat{V}\right\}},

is a vector space
\widehat{V}
, spanned by
\widehat{v}_{1},\dots,\widehat{v}_{p}
. It is easy to see that the vector space
\widehat{V}
, associated with the affine space
\widehat{V}_{0}
is unique and only depends on
\widehat{V}_{0}
, and not on a particular choice of the
\widehat{v}_{0},\dots,\widehat{v}_{p}
.

The following theorem provides a complete characterization of finite-dimensional optimal affine subspaces of
H
and the resulting projection error.

Theorem 3.12
.

Let
H
be a separable Hilbert space and
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(H)
be a probability measure with finite second moment. Let
p\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\widehat{V}_{0}
be an affine subspace with associated vector space
\widehat{V}
such that
\dim(\widehat{V})=p
. Then there exists a unique element
\widehat{v}_{0}\in\widehat{V}_{0}
such that

\|\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v}_{0}\|=\inf_{\widehat{v}\in\widehat{V}_{0}}\|\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v}\|,

and the projection error given by,

(3.17)

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{Proj}}(\widehat{V}_{0})=\int_{H}\inf_{\widehat{v}\in\widehat{V}_{0}}\|{v}-\widehat{v}\|^{2}\,d\nu(v)

can be written as,

(3.18)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{Proj}}(\widehat{V})=\|\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v}_{0}\|^{2}+\int_{H}\inf_{\widehat{v}\in\widehat{V}}\|v-\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v}\|^{2}\,d\nu(v),

Furthermore, the affine space
\widehat{V}_{0}
is a minimizer of
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{Proj}}(\widehat{V}_{0})
(in the class of affine subspaces of dimension
p
), if and only if,

\widehat{v}_{0}=\mathbb{E}[v],\quad\text{and}\quad\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n-1}E_{j}\subset\widehat{V}\subset\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}E_{j}.

Here
E_{j}
denote the eigenspaces of the covariance operator

(3.19)

\Gamma=\int_{H}(v-\mathbb{E}[v])\otimes(v-\mathbb{E}[v])\,d\nu(v),

associated with the distinct eigenvalues
\overline{\lambda}_{1}>\overline{\lambda}_{2}>\dots
, and
n\in N
is chosen such that

\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\dim(E_{j})<p\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}\dim(E_{j}).

In this case, the projection error is given by

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{Proj}}(\widehat{V}_{0})=\sum_{j>p}\lambda_{j},

where

\underbrace{\lambda_{1}=\dots=\lambda_{n_{1}}}_{=\overline{\lambda}_{1}}>\underbrace{\lambda_{n_{1}+1}=\dots=\lambda_{n_{1}+n_{2}}}_{=\overline{\lambda}_{2}}>\dots,

are the eigenvalues of
\Gamma
repeated according to multiplicity, with
n_{j}=\dim(E_{j})
.

The above theorem is proved in Appendix
C.3.2
. It is the extension of Theorem
3.10
to affine subspaces of a Hilbert space. It serves to relate the projection error to the decay of eigenvalues of the associated covariance operator (
3.19
) and will be the key to proving bounds on the reconstruction error (
3.3
) and in the identification of the optimal trunk network for the DeepONet (
2.10
).

3.4.
Bounds on the reconstruction error (
3.3
)

In this section, we will apply results from the previous sub-section to bound the reconstruction error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}
(
3.3
). We start by recalling that the reconstructor
\mathcal{R}
(
2.9
) in the DeepONet (
2.10
) is affine. The following lemma, whose proof is provided in Appendix
C.4
, identifies the optimal projector
\mathcal{P}
for a given reconstructor
\mathcal{R}
.

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{\bm{\tau}}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to L^{2}(U)
be an affine reconstructor of the form (
2.9
), for
\tau_{0}\in L^{2}(U)
and linearly independent
\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{p}\in L^{2}(U)
. Let
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(L^{2}(U))
be a probability measure with finite second moments. Then, the reconstruction error (
3.3
) is minimized in the class of Borel measurable projectors
\mathcal{P}:L^{2}(U)\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
, for

(3.20)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(u):=\left(\langle u-\tau_{0},\tau^{\ast}_{1}\rangle,\dots,\langle u-\tau_{0},\tau^{\ast}_{p}\rangle\right),

where
\tau^{\ast}_{1},\dots,\tau^{\ast}_{p}\in\mathrm{span}(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{p})
denotes the dual basis of
\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{p}
, i.e. such that

\langle\tau_{\ell},\tau^{\ast}_{k}\rangle=\delta_{\ell k},\quad\forall\,k,\ell\in\{1,\dots,p\}.

In this case, we have

(3.21)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}(u)=\tau_{0}^{\perp}+\sum_{k=1}^{p}\langle u,\tau^{\ast}_{k}\rangle\tau_{k}

where

\tau_{0}^{\perp}=\tau_{0}-\sum_{k=1}^{p}\langle\tau_{0},\tau^{\ast}_{k}\rangle\tau_{k},

is the projection of
\tau_{0}
onto the orthogonal complement of
\mathrm{span}(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{p})\subset L^{2}(U)
.

Next, we can directly apply Theorem
3.12
to identify an
optimal
reconstructor
\mathcal{R}
, and apply Lemma
3.13
to identify the associated
optimal
projector
\mathcal{P}
, in the following theorem,

Theorem 3.14
.

Denote
\nu:=\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu
. If the reconstruction
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{\bm{\tau}}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to L^{2}(U)
is fixed to be of the form (
2.9
) with
\tau_{j}\in L^{2}(U)
for
j=0,\dots,p
,
then the reconstruction error (
3.3
), in the class of affine reconstructions
\mathcal{R}
and
arbitrary measurable
projections
\mathcal{P}:L^{2}(U)\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
, is minimized by the choice

(3.22)

\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{opt}}=\mathcal{R}_{\widehat{\bm{\tau}}}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{p})=\widehat{\tau}_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{p}\alpha_{j}\widehat{\tau}_{j},

where

\widehat{\tau}_{0}=\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[v]=\int_{L^{2}(U)}v\,d\nu,

is the mean and
\widehat{\tau}_{j}
,
j=1,\dots,p
are
p
eigenvectors of the covariance operator

\Gamma=\int_{Y}(v-\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[v])\otimes(v-\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[v])\,d\nu(v),

corresponding to the
p
largest eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{p}\geq\dots
. Furthermore, the optimal reconstruction error satisfies the lower bound

(3.23)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{opt}}}\geq\sqrt{\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{k}},

in terms of the spectrum
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots
of
\Gamma
(eigenvalues repeated according to their multiplicity). Given
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{opt}}
, the corresponding optimal measurable projection
\mathcal{P}:L^{2}(U)\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
is affine and is given by the orthogonal projection

(3.24)

\mathcal{P}(v)=\left(\langle(v-\widehat{\tau}_{0}),\widehat{\tau}_{1}\rangle,\dots,\langle(v-\widehat{\tau}_{0}),\widehat{\tau}_{p}\rangle\right).

Given the existence of an
optimal
affine reconstructor (
3.22
) and the associated optimal projector (
3.24
), the proof of Theorem
3.6
is now straightforward.

Proof of Theorem
3.6
.

Let
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{E}
be a DeepONet approximation of
\mathcal{G}
. We aim to show that

\sqrt{\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{k}}\leq\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}\leq\widehat{\mathscr{E}},

where
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots
denote the eigenvalues of
\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}
.

By Lemma
3.13
, the optimal projector
\mathcal{P}:L^{2}(U)\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
for a the given reconstructor
\mathcal{R}
is such that
\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}=\Pi_{V_{0}}:L^{2}(U)\to L^{2}(U)
is the orthogonal projection onto the affine subspace
V_{0}=\mathrm{Im}(\mathcal{R})\subset L^{2}(U)
. To prove the lower bound
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}\leq\widehat{\mathscr{E}}
, we observe that for any
u\in X
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{G}(u)-\mathcal{N}(u)\|_{L^{2}(U)}

\displaystyle=\|\mathcal{G}(u)-\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{E}(u)\|_{L^{2}(U)}

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{v\in V_{0}}\|\mathcal{G}(u)-v\|_{L^{2}(U)}

\displaystyle=\|\mathcal{G}(u)-\Pi_{V_{0}}\mathcal{G}(u)\|_{L^{2}(U)}

\displaystyle=\|\mathcal{G}(u)-\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}(u)\|_{L^{2}(U)},

and hence,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}=\|\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{E}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\geq\|\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=\|\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu)}=\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}.

Furthermore, by Theorem
3.14
, we thus have

(3.25)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{k}}=\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{opt}}}\leq\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}\leq\widehat{\mathscr{E}},

where
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots
denote the eigenvalues of the covariance operator
\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}
of the push-forward measure
\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu
. This proves the claim.
∎

The lower bound in (
3.25
) is fundamental, as it reveals that the spectral decay rate for the operator
\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}
of the push-forward measure essentially determines how low the approximation error of DeepONets can be for a given output dimension
p
of the trunk nets.

After establishing the lower bound on the reconstruction error, we seek to derive upper bounds on this error. We observe that the optimal reconstructor is given by eigenfunctions of the covariance operator
\Gamma
(
3.19
). In general, these eigenfunctions are not neural networks of the form (
2.1
). However, given the fact that neural networks are universal approximators of functions on finite dimensional spaces, one can expect that the trunk nets in (
2.9
) will approximate these underlying eigenfunctions to high accuracy. This is indeed established in the following,

Proposition 3.15
.

Let
\nu=\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(Y)
be a probability measure with finite second moments. Write the covariance operator in the form

\Gamma_{\nu}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}(\phi_{k}\otimes\phi_{k}),

with
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots
and orthonormal eigenbasis
\phi_{k}
. Let
\widehat{\tau}_{k}
,
k=0,1,\dots,p
, denote the optimal choice for an affine reconstruction
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{opt}}
, as in Theorem
3.14
, more precisely, let
\widehat{\tau}_{0}=\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[v]
,
\widehat{\tau}_{k}=\phi_{k}
for
k=1,\dots,p
. Let
\tau_{0},\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{p}
be the trunk net functions of an arbitrary DeepONet (
2.10
). The reconstruction error for the reconstruction
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{\bm{\tau}}
:

\mathcal{R}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{p})=\tau_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{p}\alpha_{k}\tau_{k},

with corresponding (unique optimal) projection
\mathcal{P}
(
3.20
) satisfies

(3.26)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}\leq\sqrt{1+{\mathrm{Tr}}(\Gamma_{\nu})}\max_{k=0,\dots,p}\|\widehat{\tau}_{k}-\tau_{k}\|_{L^{2}_{y}}+\sqrt{\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{k}}.

This proposition is proved in Appendix
C.5
. The estimate (
3.26
) shows that an upper bound on the reconstruction error has two contributions, one of them arises from the decay rate of the eigenvalues of the covariance operator associated with the push-forward measure
\nu=\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu
and does not depend on the underlying neural networks. On the other hand, the second contribution to (
3.26
) depends on the choice and approximation properties of the trunk net in the DeepONet (
2.10
).

Thus, to bound the reconstruction error, we need some explicit information about the optimal reconstruction in terms of the eigensystem of the covariance operator. However in practice, one does not have access to the form of the nonlinear operator
\mathcal{G}
, but only to measurements of
\mathcal{G}
on a finite number of training samples. Hence, it may not be possible to determine what the optimal reconstructor for a particular nonlinear operator
\mathcal{G}
is. Therefore, we have the following lemma that compares the reconstruction error of the DeepONet (
2.10
) with the reconstruction error that arises from another, possibly non-optimal, choice of affine reconstructor.

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(Y)
be a probability measure with finite second moment. Let
\bm{\tau}=(0,\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{p})
denote the trunk net functions of a DeepONet
without bias
(
\tau_{0}\equiv 0
), and with associated reconstruction
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{\bm{\tau}}
:

\mathcal{R}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{p})=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\alpha_{k}\tau_{k}.

Let
\widetilde{\bm{\tau}}=(0,\widetilde{\tau}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\tau}_{p})
denote the basis functions for a reconstruction
{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\widetilde{\bm{\tau}}}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to Y
without bias (
\widetilde{\tau}_{0}\equiv 0
), of the form

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{p})=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\alpha_{k}\widetilde{\tau}_{k}.

Assume that the functions
\widetilde{\tau}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\tau}_{p}\in Y
are
orthonormal
. Let
\mathcal{P},\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}:Y\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
denote the corresponding projection mappings (
3.20
), and let
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}
,
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}
denote the reconstruction errors of
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{P})
and
(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}},\widetilde{\mathcal{P}})
, respectively. Let
\epsilon\in(0,1/2)
,
p\geq 1
be given. If

(3.27)

\displaystyle\max_{k=1,\dots,p}\|\tau_{k}-\widetilde{\tau}_{k}\|_{L^{2}}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{p^{3/2}},

then we have

(3.28)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}\leq\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}+C\epsilon.

where
C\geq 1
depends only on
\int_{L^{2}}\|u\|^{2}\,d\nu(u)
. Furthermore, we have the estimate

(3.29)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{P}),\;\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{R})\leq 2,

for the Lipschitz constant of the projection
\mathcal{P}
(cp. (
3.20
)) and
\mathcal{R}
, respectively, where

\displaystyle\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{P})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Lip}\left(\mathcal{P}:(L^{2}(U),\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{L^{2}})\to(\mathbb{R}^{p},\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{\ell^{2}})\right),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{R})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Lip}\left(\mathcal{R}:(\mathbb{R}^{p},\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{\ell^{2}})\to(L^{2}(U),\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{L^{2}})\right).

The proof of this lemma is provided in Appendix
C.6
and we would like to point out that for simplicity of exposition, we have set the
biases

\tau_{0}=\widetilde{\tau}_{0}\equiv 0
in the above. One can readily incorporate these bias terms to derive an analogous version of the bound (
3.28
).

We illustrate the comparison principle elucidated in Lemma
3.16
with an example. To this end, we set the target space as the
n
-dimensional torus, i.e.
U=[0,2\pi]^{n}
(or
U=\mathbb{T}^{n}
). With respect to this
U
, a possible
canonical
reconstructor is given by the
n
-dimensional Fourier reconstruction;

(3.30)

\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Fourier}}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{p})=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\alpha_{j}\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{j}(x),

where we use the notation introduced in Appendix
A
. With respect to this Fourier reconstructor, we prove in Appendix
C.7
the following estimate on approximation by trunk neural networks,

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
n,p\in\mathbb{N}
, and consider the Fourier reconstruction
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Fourier}}
on
[0,2\pi]^{n}\simeq\mathbb{T}^{n}
. There exists a constant
C>0
, independent of
p
, such that for any
\epsilon\in(0,1/2)
, there exists a trunk net
\bm{\tau}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
, with

\begin{gathered}\mathrm{size}(\bm{\tau})\leq Cp(1+\log(\epsilon^{-1}p)^{2}),\\
0pt(\bm{\tau})\leq C(1+\log(\epsilon^{-1}p)^{2}),\end{gathered}

and such that

(3.31)

\displaystyle p^{3/2}\max_{j=1,\dots,p}\|\tau_{j}-\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{j}\|_{L^{2}([0,2\pi]^{n})}\leq\epsilon,

where
\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{1},\dots,\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{p}
denote the first
p
elements of the Fourier basis.

Furthermore, the Lipschitz norm of
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{\bm{\tau}}
and of the linear projection
\mathcal{P}:L^{2}(U)\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
