associated with the
\bm{\tau}
-induced reconstruction
\mathcal{R}_{\bm{\tau}}
via (
3.20
) can be estimated by

\mathrm{Lip}\left(\mathcal{R}:(\mathbb{R}^{p},\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{\ell^{2}})\to(L^{2}(U),\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{L^{2}})\right)\leq 2,

and

\mathrm{Lip}\left(\mathcal{P}:(L^{2}(U),\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{L^{2}})\to(\mathbb{R}^{p},\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{\ell^{2}})\right)\leq 2.

Now combining the results of the above Lemma with the estimate (
3.28
) yields the following result,

Lemma 3.18
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and fix
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n}))
. There exists a constant
C>0
, depending only on
n
and
\int_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n})}\|u\|^{2}\,d\nu(u)
, such that for any
\epsilon\in(0,1/2)
, there exists a trunk net
\bm{\tau}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
, with

\begin{gathered}\mathrm{size}(\bm{\tau})\leq C(1+p\log(\epsilon^{-1}p)^{2}),\\
0pt(\bm{\tau})\leq C(1+\log(\epsilon^{-1}p)^{2}),\end{gathered}

and such that the reconstruction

\mathcal{R}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n}),\quad\mathcal{R}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{p})=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\alpha_{k}\tau_{k},

satisfies

(3.32)

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}\leq\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Fourier}}}+C\epsilon.

Furthermore,
\mathcal{R}
and the associated projection
\mathcal{P}:(L^{2}(U),\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{L^{2}}\to(\mathbb{R}^{p},\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{\ell^{2}})
(cp. (
3.20
)) satisfy
\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{R}),\,\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{P})\leq 2
.

The significance of bound (
3.32
) lies in the fact that it reduces the problem of estimating the reconstruction error (
3.3
) for a DeepONet to estimating the reconstruction error for a Fourier reconstructor (
3.30
), which might be much easier to derive in concrete examples. Our next goal will be to obtain further insight into the decay of the eigenvalues of
\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}
(in terms of
\mathcal{G}
and
\mu
), and – in view of the explicit complexity estimate provided by Lemma
3.18
– to estimate
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Fourier}}}
.

3.4.1.
On the decay of spectrum for the push-forward measure

From the bound (
3.4
), it is clear that the decay of eigenvalues of the covariance operator, associated with the push forward measure
\mathcal{G}_{\#}{\mu}
plays a crucial role in estimating the total error (
2.11
), and in particular, the reconstruction error (
3.3
).

If
\mathcal{G}
is at least Lipschitz continuous, then we can write

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Tr}}(\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu})

\displaystyle=\int_{Y}\left\|v-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}[v]\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(U)}\,d(\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu)(v)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\widehat{v}\in{L^{2}(U)}}\int_{L^{2}(U)}\|v-\widehat{v}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(U)}\,d(\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu)(v)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\widehat{v}\in{L^{2}(U)}}\int_{X}\|\mathcal{G}(u)-\widehat{v}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(U)}\,d\mu(u).

Making the particular (sub-optimal) choice
\widehat{v}:=\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[u])\in L^{2}(U)
and utilizing the Lipschitz continuity of
\mathcal{G}
, we can estimate the last expression as

\displaystyle\leq\int_{X}\left\|\mathcal{G}(u)-\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[u])\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(U)}\,d\mu(u)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{G})^{2}\int_{X}\left\|u-\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[u]\right\|^{2}_{X}\,d\mu(u)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{G})^{2}{\mathrm{Tr}}\left({\Gamma}_{\mu}\right).

Thus,
\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}
is a trace-class operator for Lipschitz continuous
\mathcal{G}
, implying that

\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{k}\to 0,\quad(\text{as }p\to\infty),

However, the efficiency of DeepONets in approximating the nonlinear operator
\mathcal{G}
relies on the
precise rate
of this spectral decay. In particular, an exponential decay of the eigenvalues would facilitate efficient approximation by DeepONets.

Clearly, the spectral decay of
\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}
depends on both
\mathcal{G}
and
\mu
(possibly in a complicated manner). If the eigenvalues of
\Gamma_{\mu}
decay rapidly, e.g. exponentially, one might hope that the same is true for the eigenvalues of
\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}
, under relatively mild conditions on
\mathcal{G}
. The following lemma, proved in Appendix
C.8
shows that this is unfortunately not the case under just the assumption that the operator
\mathcal{G}
is only Lipschitz continuous,

Proposition 3.19
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(X)
be any non-degenerate Gaussian measure; in particular, the spectrum of
\Gamma_{\mu}
may have arbitrarily fast spectral decay. Given any sequence
(\gamma_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

\gamma_{k}\geq 0,\quad\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N},\qquad\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k^{2}\gamma_{k}<\infty,

there exists a Lipschitz continuous map
\mathcal{G}:X\to L^{2}(U)
, such that the spectrum of covariance operator
\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}
of the push-forward measure
\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu
is given by
(\gamma_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Thus, the above proposition clearly shows that, in general, a nonlinear operator
\mathcal{G}
can possibly destroy high rates of spectral decay for the covariance operator associated with a push-forward measure, even if the eigenvalues of the covariance operator associated with the underlying measure
\mu
, decay exponentially rapidly.

However, there are special cases where one can indeed obtain fast rates of spectral decay for the covariance operator associated with a push-forward measure. We identify two of these special cases, with wide ranging applicability, below.

3.4.2.
Reconstruction error for linear operators
\mathcal{G}:X\to Y

If the operator
\mathcal{G}:X\to Y
is a bounded
linear
operator, then the spectrum of the push-forward measure
\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu
can be bounded above by the spectrum of
\mu
. More precisely, we have

Proposition 3.20
.

Let
X
be a separable Hilbert space. Let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(X)
be a probability measure with finite second moment
\int_{X}\|u\|_{X}^{2}\,d\mu(u)<\infty
. Let
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots
denote the eigenvalues of the covariance operator
\Gamma_{\mu}
of
\mu
, repeated according to multiplicity. If
\mathcal{G}:X\to L^{2}(U)
is a bounded linear operator, then for any
p\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a
p
-dimensional affine subspace
W_{0}\subset L^{2}(U)
, such that

(3.33)

\displaystyle\int_{L^{2}(U)}\inf_{w\in W_{0}}\|w-u\|_{L^{2}(U)}^{2}\,d(\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu)(u)\leq\|\mathcal{G}\|^{2}\,\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{k}.

Here
\|\mathcal{G}\|
(
=\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{G})
) denotes the operator norm of
\mathcal{G}:X\to L^{2}(U)
. If the affine reconstruction/projection pair is chosen such that
\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}:L^{2}(U)\to L^{2}(U)
is the orthogonal projection onto
W_{0}
, then the reconstruction error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}
is bounded by

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}\leq\|\mathcal{G}\|\sqrt{\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{k}}.

The proof of this proposition is presented in Appendix
C.9
.

3.4.3.
Reconstruction error for operators with
smooth
image

Another class of operators for which we can readily estimate the spectral decay of the covariance operator associated with the push-forward measure are those operators that map into smoother (more regular) subspaces of the target space.

As a concrete example, we set
U=\mathbb{T}^{n}
as the periodic torus. Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
. We denote by
P_{N}:L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n})
the orthogonal Fourier projection onto the Fourier basis

P_{N}u=\sum_{|{k}|_{\infty}\leq N}\widehat{u}_{{k}}\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{{k}}({x}),

where the sum is over
{k}=(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, such that

|{k}|_{\infty}:=\max_{j=1,\dots,n}|k_{j}|\leq N.

Note that the size of this set is
|{\left\{{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\,\middle|\,|{k}|_{\infty}\leq N\right\}}|=(2N+1)^{n}
. Hence, with
p
degrees of freedom, we can represent
P_{N}
for

(3.34)

\displaystyle p\geq(2N+1)^{n}\quad\Rightarrow\quad N\leq\left\lfloor\frac{(p^{1/n}-1)}{2}\right\rfloor\leq p^{1/n}.

Given
p\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathcal{P}:L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n})\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
be a mapping encoding all Fourier coefficients with
|{k}|_{\infty}\leq N
, where
N\leq p^{1/n}
is the largest integer satisfying (
3.34
). Let
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Fourier}}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n})
denote the corresponding Fourier reconstruction (
3.30
), so that
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Fourier}}\circ\mathcal{P}:L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
satisfies
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Fourier}}\circ\mathcal{P}=P_{N}
. It is well-known that for
u\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{n})
, we have

\|P_{N}u-u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n})}\leq\frac{1}{N^{s}}\|u\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{n})}.

Hence the resulting reconstruction error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}_{Fourier}}
is given by,

\displaystyle\int_{Y}\|\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Fourier}}\circ\mathcal{P}-\mathrm{Id}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\,d(\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu(u))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N^{2s}}\int_{Y}\|u\|^{2}_{H^{s}(\mathbb{T})}\,d(\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu(u))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2s}}\int_{X}\|\mathcal{G}(u)\|^{2}_{H^{s}(\mathbb{T})}\,d\mu(u).

This elementary calculation leads to the following result,

Proposition 3.21
.

If
\mathcal{G}
defines a Lipschitz mapping
\mathcal{G}:X\to H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{n})
, for some
s>0
, with

\int_{X}\|\mathcal{G}(u)\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\,d\mu(u)\leq M<\infty,

then we have the following estimate on the reconstruction error:

(3.35)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Fourier}}}\leq CMp^{-s/n},

where
C=C(n,s)>0
depends on
n
,
s
, but is independent of
p
.

Combining (
3.35
) and (
3.32
), and setting
\epsilon=p^{-s/n}
in (
3.32
) immediately implies Theorem
3.7
, which has already been stated in the overview Section
3.2.2
. Theorem
3.7
provides a complexity and error estimate for the trunk net approximation under the assumptions of the previous proposition.

3.5.
Bounds on the encoding error (
3.1
)

Our aim in this section is to bound the encoding error (
3.1
), associated with the DeepONet (
2.10
). This error
does not depend
on the nonlinear operator
\mathcal{G}
, but only on the underlying probability measure
\mu
. Key objectives of our analysis are to determine suitable choices of sensors for a fixed
m
as well as to find the appropriate form of a decoder
\mathcal{D}
in order to minimize the encoding error (
3.1
). We first recall the lower bound of Theorem
3.8
, on the encoding error:

(3.36)

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}})^{2}=\int_{X}\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}(u)-u\|_{L^{2}(D)}^{2}\,d\mu(u)\geq\sum_{k>m}\lambda_{k},

if
\mathcal{D}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to X
is a
linear
decoder,
\mu
is mean-zero and
\lambda_{k}
refers to the
k
-th eigenvalue of the covariance operator
\Gamma_{\mu}=\int_{X}u\otimes u\,d\mu(u)
. The proof presented in Appendix
C.10
relies on Theorem
3.10
and from this proof, we can readily see that the restriction on the zero mean of the measure
\mu
can be relaxed by using an affine decoder and Theorem
3.12
. We note in passing that the above bound (
3.36
) in fact holds for any encoder (not necessarily linear) as long as the decoder is linear.

Our next aim is to derive
upper
bounds on the encoding error. To illustrate our main ideas, we will restrict our discussion to the case
X=L^{2}(D)
. The results are readily extended to more general spaces, such as Sobolev spaces
X=H^{s}(D)
for
s>0
. We fix a probability measure
\mu
on
L^{2}(D)
, and write the covariance operator
\Gamma
as an eigenfunction decomposition

\Gamma=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{\ell}(\phi_{\ell}\otimes\phi_{\ell}),

where
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots
are the decreasing eigenvalues, and such that the
\phi_{\ell}
are an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(D)
. We will assume that all
\phi_{\ell}
are continuous functions, so that point-wise evaluation of
\phi_{\ell}
makes sense. Note that since the
\phi_{\ell}
are orthonormal in
L^{2}(D)
, they are also linearly independent as elements in
C(D)
.

Assume now that for some
M\in\mathbb{N}
, there exist sensors
X_{1},\dots,X_{M}\in D
, such that the matrix
\Phi_{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times M}
with entries

(3.37)

\displaystyle[\Phi_{M}]_{ij}=[\phi_{i}(X_{j})],\quad\text{for $i=1,\dots,m$, $j=1,\dots,M$,}

has full rank, i.e.

(3.38)

\displaystyle\det(\Phi_{M}\Phi_{M}^{T})\neq 0.

Then we can define a “
projection
” onto
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{m}
by

(3.39)

\displaystyle u(x)\overset{\mathcal{E}}{\to}(u(X_{1}),\dots,u(X_{m}))\overset{\mathcal{D}}{\to}\sum_{j,k=1}^{m}[\Phi_{M}^{\dagger}]_{kj}u(X_{j})\phi_{k}(x),

where

(3.40)

\displaystyle\Phi_{M}^{\dagger}:=\left(\Phi_{M}\Phi_{M}^{T}\right)^{-1}\Phi_{M},

denotes the pseudo-inverse of
\Phi_{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times M}
. Written in somewhat simpler matrix/vector-multiplication notation, we might write this projection as

u(x)\mapsto\left\langle\Phi_{M}^{\dagger}u(\bm{X}),\bm{\varphi}(x)\right\rangle_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{m})},

where
\bm{X}:=(X_{1},\dots,X_{M})
,
u(\bm{X})=(u(X_{1}),\dots,u(X_{M}))
and
\bm{\varphi}(x)=(\phi_{1}(x),\dots,\phi_{m}(x))
.

Note that for
u(x)=\phi_{i}(x)
,
1\leq i\leq m
, we have

\sum_{j=1}^{M}[\Phi_{M}]_{\ell,j}\phi_{i}(X_{j})=\sum_{j=1}^{M}[\Phi_{M}]_{\ell,j}[\Phi_{M}]_{i,j}=[\Phi_{M}\Phi_{M}^{T}]_{\ell,i}.

Hence

\displaystyle\phi_{i}(x)

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{M}[\Phi_{M}]_{\ell,j}\phi_{i}(X_{j})\left[(\Phi_{M}\Phi_{M}^{T})^{-1}\right]_{k,\ell}\phi_{k}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{m}[\Phi_{M}\Phi_{M}^{T}]_{\ell,i}\left[(\Phi_{M}\Phi_{M}^{T})^{-1}\right]_{k,\ell}\phi_{k}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\delta_{ik}\phi_{k}(x)=\phi_{i}(x).

So the map (
3.39
) clearly provides a projection onto
\mathrm{span}(\phi_{j};\;j=1,\dots,m)
.

Next, we have the following Lemma, provided in Appendix
C.11
, which characterizes the encoding error (
3.1
), associated with the encoder/decoder pair given by (
3.39
).

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(L^{2}(D))
be a measure with finite second moments, i.e. such that
\int_{L^{2}(D)}\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,d\mu(u)<\infty
.
Let
\Phi_{M}
be given by (
3.37
), and assume that the non-singularity condition (
3.38
) holds. Then the encoding error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}
for the pair
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{D}
, defined by (
3.39
) can be written as

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}})^{2}

\displaystyle=(\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{aliasing}})^{2}+(\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\perp})^{2},

where

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\perp})^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\|P^{\perp}_{m}u\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\,d\mu(u)=\sum_{\ell>m}\lambda_{\ell},

with
P^{\perp}_{m}:L^{2}\to L^{2}
the orthogonal projection onto the orthogonal complement of
\mathrm{span}(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{m})
, where
\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\dots
denote a basis of orthonormal eigenfunctions of the covariance operator
\Gamma_{\mu}
of
\mu
, with corresponding eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots
, and

(3.41)

(\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{aliasing}})^{2}=\sum_{\ell>m}\lambda_{\ell}\|\Phi_{M}^{\dagger}\phi_{\ell}(\bm{X})\|_{\ell^{2}}^{2}.

Thus, the above Lemma
3.22
provides us a strategy to bound the encoding error as long as the non-singularity condition (
3.38
) on the matrix (
3.37
) is satisfied. Moreover, one component of the encoding error is completely specified in terms of the spectral decay of the associated covariance operator. However, the other component measures the error due to
aliasing
, with the notation being motivated by an example from Fourier analysis, that is considered in the following subsection. Hence, bounding the aliasing error and checking the validity of the non-singularity condition (
3.38
) require us to specify the location of sensors.

Given the form of the matrix (
3.37
), it would make sense to relate the sensor locations with the eigenfunctions of the covariance operator
\Gamma
. However in general, we may not have any information on these eigenfunctions
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{m}
, and hence, finding suitable sensors
X_{1},\dots,X_{M}
satisfying the non-singularity condition (
3.38
) might be a very difficult task. Instead, we propose to choose them iid randomly in
D
, allowing for
M\geq m
, and study the corresponding random encoder

\mathcal{E}(u)=(u(X_{1}),\dots,u(X_{M})),

with associated decoder
\mathcal{D}
given by (
3.39
). Note that the decoder
\mathcal{D}
is merely used in the analysis of the encoding error, but its explicit form is not needed, when DeepONets are used in practice. In fact,
the use of random sensors and the simplicity of the resulting encoder could constitute one of the key benefits of the DeepONets
.

More precisely, in the following we fix a probability space
(\Omega,\mathrm{Prob})
, and a sequence of iid random variables
\{X_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
:

\omega\mapsto(X_{1}(\omega),X_{2}(\omega),X_{3}(\omega),\dots),\quad(\omega\in\Omega),

such that
X_{k}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(D)
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. For any
M\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\omega\in\Omega
, we can then study the corresponding encoder
\mathcal{E}:C(D)\to\mathbb{R}^{M}
,

\mathcal{E}(u;X_{1}(\omega),\dots,X_{M}(\omega))=(u(X_{1}(\omega)),\dots,u(X_{M}(\omega))).

As is common in probability theory, we will usually suppress the argument
\omega
, and write, e.g.,
X_{1}
instead of
X_{1}(\omega)
.

Hence, the matrix
\Phi_{M}=[\phi_{i}(X_{j})]_{i,j}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times M}
is a random matrix, depending on
X_{1},\dots,X_{M}
. In order for the resulting decoder in (
3.39
) to be well-defined, we need to show that there is a non-zero probability that
\Phi_{M}\Phi_{M}^{T}
is non-singular, for sufficiently large
M
.

Moreover, by Lemma
3.22
, the aliasing error (
3.41
) depends on

\|\Phi_{M}^{\dagger}\phi_{\ell}(\bm{X})\|_{\ell^{2}}^{2}\leq\|\Phi_{M}^{\dagger}\|_{\ell^{2}\to\ell^{2}}^{2}\|\phi_{\ell}(\bm{X})\|_{\ell^{2}}^{2}.

We note that

\|\Phi_{M}^{\dagger}\|_{\ell^{2}\to\ell^{2}}^{2}=\frac{|D|}{M\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}\left(\frac{|D|}{M}\Phi_{M}\Phi_{M}^{T}\right)},

can be written in terms of the smallest singular value, i.e.
\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}\left(\frac{|D|}{M}\Phi_{M}\Phi_{M}^{T}\right)
, of the rescaled matrix
\frac{|D|}{M}\Phi_{M}\Phi_{M}^{T}
(the reason for introducing the rescaling
|D|/M
will be explained below). Hence, to bound the aliasing error and the overall encoding error, we will need to provide a lower bound (with high probability) on this smallest singular value. We investigate these two issues in the following.

First, we note that for iid random sensors
X_{1},\dots,X_{M}
, by our definition of
\Phi_{M}
, we have

\left[\frac{|D|}{M}\Phi_{M}\Phi_{M}^{T}\right]_{k,\ell}=\frac{|D|}{M}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\phi_{k}(X_{j})\phi_{\ell}(X_{j}),

and the last sum can be interpreted as a Monte-Carlo estimate of

|D|\,\mathbb{E}[\phi_{k}(X)\phi_{\ell}(X)]=\int_{D}\phi_{k}(x)\phi_{\ell}(x)\,dx=\delta_{k\ell}.

For such Monte-Carlo estimates, we can rely on well-known bounds from probability theory and have the following lemma on the bounds for the smallest singular value:

Lemma 3.23
.

For any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, denote

\omega_{m}:=\max_{k\leq m}\|\phi_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}}.

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{M}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(D)
be iid random variables. Define
\Phi_{M}
by (
3.37
). Then we have

(3.42)

\mathrm{Prob}\left[\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}\left(\frac{|D|}{M}\Phi_{M}\Phi_{M}^{T}\right)<1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right]\leq 2m^{2}\exp\left(-\left(\frac{M}{|D|\omega_{m}^{2}m}\right)^{2}\right).

This lemma is proved using the well-know Hoeffding’s inequality and the proof is presented in Appendix
C.12
. A direct application of the bound (
3.42
) allows us to bound the aliasing error (
3.41
) in the following,

Lemma 3.24
.

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{M}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(D)
be iid random variables, uniform on
D
. Let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}(D))
be a probability measure concentrated on continuous functions. Let
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dots,
denote the eigenvalues of the covariance operator
\overline{\Gamma}_{\mu}
(
3.15
) of
\mu
, with associated orthonormal eigenbasis
\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\dots
. The aliasing error (
3.41
), resulting from this random choice of sensors, is bounded by

\int\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}(P_{m}^{\perp}u)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\,d\mu(u)\leq\frac{1}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}\left(\frac{|D|}{M}\Phi_{M}\Phi_{M}^{T}\right)}\frac{|D|}{M}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\left(\sum_{\ell>m}\lambda_{\ell}|\phi_{\ell}(X_{j})|^{2}\right).

Denote
\omega_{m}:=\max_{k\leq m}\|\phi_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}}
. Then, with probability

\mathrm{Prob}\geq 1-2m^{2}\exp\left(-\left(\frac{M}{|D|\omega_{m}^{2}m}\right)^{2}\right),

we have

\int\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}(P_{m}^{\perp}u)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\,d\mu(u)\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}|D|}{\sqrt{2}-1}\sum_{\ell>m}\lambda_{\ell}\|\phi_{\ell}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}.

Moreover, let
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
, and define
M=M(m)=\lceil\kappa|D|m\omega_{m}^{2}\log(m)\rceil
. Then, with probability

\mathrm{Prob}\geq 1-\frac{2}{m^{\kappa-2}},

it holds that for
M
iid uniformly chosen random sensors
X_{1},\dots,X_{M}\in D
, the encoder

\mathcal{E}:C(D)\to\mathbb{R}^{M},\quad u(x)\mapsto(u(X_{1}),\dots,u(X_{M})),

possesses a decoder
\mathcal{D}
given by (
3.39
), such that

(3.43)

(\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}})^{2}\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}|D|}{\sqrt{2}-1}\sum_{\ell>m}\lambda_{\ell}(1+\|\phi_{\ell}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}).

A direct consequence of this lemma is Theorem
3.9
, stated in the overview Section
3.2.3
, and whose proof is provided in appendix
C.13
. Theorem
3.9
shows the remarkable result that
randomly chosen sensor points
can lead to an optimal (up to a
\log
) decay of the encoding error (
3.1
), corresponding to the DeepONet (
2.10
).

3.5.1.
Examples

In this section, we seek to illustrate the estimates on the encoding error for concrete prototypical examples. We recall that the encoding error (
3.1
) is independent of the operator
\mathcal{G}
, depending only on the probability measure
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(X)
. We assume that
X=L^{2}(D)
in the following. Assuming furthermore that
\mu
has finite second moments
\int_{L^{2}(D)}\|u\|^{2}\,d\mu(u)<\infty
, then it is well-known
Stuart 2010
that by the Karhunen-Loève expansion, we can write
\mu
as the law of a random variable
u=u({\,\cdot\,};\bm{Z})
, of the form

(3.44)

\displaystyle u({\,\cdot\,};\bm{Z})=\overline{u}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\sqrt{\lambda_{\ell}}Z_{\ell}\phi_{\ell},\quad\bm{Z}=(Z_{1},Z_{2},\dots),

where
\overline{u}\in L^{2}(D)
is the mean,
\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\dots\in L^{2}(D)
are an orthonormal basis consisting of eigenfunctions of the covariance operator
\Gamma_{\mu}
of
\mu
,
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots
denote the corresponding eigenvalues, and
Z_{1},Z_{2},\dots
are real-valued random variables satisfying

\mathbb{E}[Z_{\ell}]=0,\quad\mathbb{E}[Z_{k}Z_{\ell}]=\delta_{k\ell},\quad\forall\,k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}.

In practice, the probability measure
\mu
is often specified as the law of an expansion of the form (
3.44
)
Stuart 2010
(see e.g. examples 5 and 6 of
Lu et al. 2019
). This provides a very convenient method to sample from the measure
\mu
, which is defined on an infinite dimensional space
X
.

A particularly important class of measures on infinite-dimensional spaces are the so-called
Gaussian measures

Stuart 2010
. A Gaussian measure
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}(D))
is uniquely characterized by its mean
\overline{u}=\int_{L^{2}(D)}u\,d\mu(u)\in L^{2}(D)
, and its covariance operator
\Gamma=\int_{L^{2}(D)}(u\otimes u)\,d\mu(u)
, which may, e.g., be expressed in terms of a covariance integral kernel
k(x,x^{\prime})\in L^{2}(D\times D)
, and through which the covariance operator
\Gamma
is defined by integration against
k(x,x^{\prime})
:

(3.45)

\Gamma:L^{2}(D)\to L^{2}(D),\quad u(x)\mapsto\int_{D}k(x,x^{\prime})u(x^{\prime})\,dx^{\prime}.

3.5.2.
Encoding error for a particular Gaussian measure.

For this concrete example, we consider a Gaussian measure that was used in the context of DeepONets in
Lu et al. 2019
. For definiteness and simplicity of exposition, we consider the one-dimensional periodic case by setting
D=\mathbb{T}=[0,2\pi]
and consider a Gaussian measure
\mu
, defined on it with the periodization,

(3.46)

\displaystyle k_{p}(x,x^{\prime}):=\sum_{h\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}}\exp\left(\frac{-|x-x^{\prime}-h|^{2}}{2\ell^{2}}\right).

of the frequently used covariance kernel

k(x,x^{\prime})=\exp\left(\frac{-|x-x^{\prime}|^{2}}{2\ell^{2}}\right).

We have the following result, proved in Appendix
C.14
on the encoder and the encoding error for this Gaussian measure,

Lemma 3.25
.

Let
\mu
be given by the law of the Gaussian process with covariance kernel
k_{p}(x,x^{\prime})
(
3.46
). Let

x_{j}=\frac{2\pi(j-1)}{m},

denote equidistant points on
[0,2\pi]
for
m=2K+1
,
K\in\mathbb{N}
. Define the
pseudo-spectral
encoder
\mathcal{E}:L^{2}(\mathbb{T})\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
by
\mathcal{E}(u)=(u(x_{1}),\dots,u(x_{m}))
, then a decoder
\mathcal{D}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
is given via the discrete Fourier transform:

(3.47)

\mathcal{D}(u_{1},\dots,u_{m})(x)=\sum_{k=-K}^{K}\widehat{u}_{k}e^{ikx},

where

\widehat{u}_{k}:=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}u_{j}e^{-2\pi ijk}.

The encoding error (
3.1
) for the resulting DeepONet (
2.10
) satisfies,

(3.48)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2\sum_{|k|>\lfloor m/2\rfloor}\lambda_{k}}\leq\sqrt{4\pi\,\mathrm{erfc}\left(\frac{\lfloor m/2\rfloor\ell}{\sqrt{2}}\right)}.

Here
\lambda_{k}=\sqrt{2\pi}\,\ell\exp(-(\ell k)^{2}/2)
are the eigenvalues of the covariance operator (
3.45
) for the Gaussian measure and
\mathrm{erfc}
denotes the complementary error function, i.e.

\mathrm{erfc}(x):=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{x}^{\infty}e^{-t^{2}}\,dt

By an asymptotic expansion of
\mathrm{erfc}(x)
, the above lemma implies that there exists a constant
C>0
, such that

(3.49)

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}\leq C\exp\left(-(\lfloor m/2\rfloor\ell)^{2}/4\right)\lesssim C\exp\left(-\gamma m^{2}\ell^{2}\right),\quad\forall\,\gamma<\frac{1}{16},

i.e., the encoding error decays
super-exponentially
in this case.

Thus, we have a very fast decay of the encoding error with a
pseudo-spectral encoder
for this Gaussian measure. In view of the earlier discussion in this subsection, it is natural to examine what happens if the encoder was a
random encoder
, i.e. based on pointwise evaluation at uniformly distributed random points in
[0,2\pi]
. Given Lemma
3.24
, one would expect a similar super-exponential decay (modulo a logarithmic correction). This is indeed the case as shown in the following Lemma (proved in appendix
C.15
),

Lemma 3.26
.

Let
\mu
be a Gaussian measure on the one-dimensional periodic torus
D=\mathbb{T}=[0,2\pi]
, characterized by the covariance kernel (
3.46
). Let
X_{1},\cdots,X_{M}
be uniformly distributed random sensors on
D
. There exists a constant
\gamma>0
, such that with probability
1
, the encoding error (
3.1
) corresponding to the random encoder (pointwise evaluations at random sensors) is bounded by,

(3.50)

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}\lesssim\exp\left(-\frac{\gamma M^{2}\ell^{2}}{\log(M)^{2}}\right).

Comparing the bounds (
3.49
) and (
3.50
), we see that the random encoder also decays super-exponentially (up to a log). However, as remarked before, no information about the underlying measure is used in defining the random encoder.

3.5.3.
Encoding error for a parametrized measure.

As a second example, we define the underlying measure
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}(D))
, as the
law
of its Karhunen-Loeve expansion (
3.44
) with the following ansatz on the resulting random field,

(3.51)

\displaystyle u(x;Y)=\overline{u}(x)+\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}Y_{\ell}\alpha_{\ell}\psi_{\ell}(x),

with
\alpha_{\ell}>0
,
\overline{u},\,\psi_{\ell}\in L^{2}(D)
are such that
\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{\ell}\|\psi_{\ell}\|_{L^{2}}<\infty
is bounded, and where
Y_{\ell}\in[-1,1]
are mean-zero random variables, distributed according to some measure
d\rho(\bm{y})
on
\bm{y}\in[-1,1]^{\mathbb{N}}
. In this case, the series in (
3.51
) converges uniformly in
L^{2}(D)
for any
\bm{y}=(y_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\in[-1,1]^{\mathbb{N}}
, and

\|u({\,\cdot\,};\bm{y})\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|\overline{u}\|_{L^{2}}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{\ell}\|\psi_{\ell}\|_{L^{2}}.

For the sake of definiteness, we shall only discuss a prototypical case, where the (spatial) domain
D
is either periodic, i.e.
D=\mathbb{T}^{d}
, or rectangular
1
1

1

Generalization to a more general domain of the form
D=\prod_{i=1}^{d}[\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}]
, for
\alpha_{i}<\beta_{i}
is straight-forward.

D=[0,2\pi]^{d}
, and the expansion functions are given by the trigonometric basis
\{\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
, indexed by
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
(check notation from Appendix
A
). The underlying ideas readily extend to more general choices of basis functions
\psi_{\ell}
. To be definite, we will assume that the random field
u({x};\bm{y})
is expanded as

(3.52)

\displaystyle u({x};\bm{y})=\overline{u}({x})+\sum_{{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}y_{{k}}\alpha_{{k}}\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{{k}}({x}),

where
\overline{u}\in C^{\infty}(D)
, and such that there exist constants
C_{\alpha}>0
,
\ell>0
, such that

(3.53)

\displaystyle|\alpha_{k}|\leq C_{\alpha}\exp(-\ell|k|_{\infty}),\quad\forall\,k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}.

We furthermore assume that the
Y_{k}\in[-1,1]
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, are centered random variables, implying that
\mathbb{E}[u]=\overline{u}
. We let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}(D))
denote the law of the random variable
u({\,\cdot\,};(Y_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}})
. By the assumed decay (
3.53
), we have
\mathrm{supp}(\mu)\subset C^{\infty}(D)
. We remark that one can also readily consider the setup where the coefficients
\alpha_{k}
decay at an algebraic rate. Note that the expansion (
3.52
) appears to be similar to that of the Karhunen-Loeve expansion of the Gaussian measure, considered in the last section. The main difference lies in the fact that
Y_{k}
are no longer assumed to be normally distributed, nor necessarily iid.

Given
\mu
as the law of random field (
3.52
) as the underlying measure, we need to construct a suitable encoder and decoder and then estimate the resulting encoding error (
3.1
). To this end, we adapt the pseudo-spectral encoder and the resulting discrete Fourier transform based decoder (
3.47
) to the current setup. For multi-indices
i=(i_{1},\dots,i_{d})\in\{0,\dots,2N\}^{d}
, let

x_{i}:=\frac{2\pi\bm{i}}{2N+1}=\left(\frac{2\pi i_{1}}{2N+1},\frac{2\pi i_{2}}{2N+1},\dots,\frac{2\pi i_{d}}{2N+1}\right),

denote the
(2N+1)^{d}
points on
[0,2\pi]^{d}
on an equidistant cartesian grid with grid size
2\pi/(2N+1)
. In the following, we will denote by
\mathcal{I}_{N}
the index set

\mathcal{I}_{N}:={\left\{i=(i_{1},\dots,i_{d})\,\middle|\,i_{r}\in\{0,\dots,2N\},\;\forall r=1,\dots,d\right\}}.

Define the encoder
\mathcal{E}:C(D)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
, for
m=(2N+1)^{d}=|\mathcal{I}_{N}|
, by

(3.54)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u)=(u(x_{i}))_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{N}}.

To construct a suitable decoder
\mathcal{D}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to L^{2}(D)
corresponding to the encoder
\mathcal{E}
above, we will first recover (an approximation of) the coefficients
y_{k}
from the encoded values
\mathcal{E}(u({\,\cdot\,};\bm{y}))
. This can be achieved by the following sequence of mappings:

(1)

Subtract the mean
\overline{u}
: Define

(3.55)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{m},\quad(u_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{N}}\mapsto(u_{i}-\overline{u}(x_{i}))_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{N}}.

(2)

Discrete Fourier transform: Define

(3.56)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\mathcal{T}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{m},\quad(\widetilde{u}_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{N}}\mapsto\left(\frac{(2\pi)^{d}}{|\mathcal{I}_{N}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{N}}\widetilde{u}_{i}\,\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{k}(x_{i})\right)_{k\in\mathcal{K}_{N}},

where the set of Fourier wavenumbers
k\in\mathcal{K}_{N}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{N}={\left\{k=(k_{1},\dots,k_{d})\,\middle|\,k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},\;-N\leq k_{j}\leq N\;\forall j\right\}}.

Note that we have
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{T}(\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{k})=\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{k}
for all
k\in\mathcal{K}_{N}
.

(3)

Approximation of
(y_{1},\dots,y_{m})
: Given the discrete Fourier coefficients
\widehat{u}_{k}=\mathcal{F}\mathcal{T}(\widetilde{u})_{k}
,
k\in\mathcal{K}_{N}
, we define (with
\mathcal{J}=\mathbb{Z}^{d}
)

(3.57)

\displaystyle\textbf{}\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{Y}:\;\mathbb{R}^{m}\to[-1,1]^{\mathcal{J}},\\
&\left(\widehat{u}_{k}\right)_{k\in\mathcal{K}_{N}}\mapsto(\widehat{Y}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}:=\left(\mathrm{shrink}\left(\widetilde{Y}_{j}\right)\right)_{j\in\mathcal{J}},\end{aligned}\right.

where

(3.58)

\displaystyle\widetilde{Y}_{j}:=\begin{cases}\widehat{u}_{k}/\alpha_{{k}},&(j=k\in\mathcal{K}_{N}),\\
0,&(\text{otherwise}),\end{cases}

and the shrink-operator
\mathrm{shrink}:\mathbb{R}\to[-1,1]
ensures that
\widehat{Y}_{k}\in[-1,1]
for all
k
:

\mathrm{shrink}(Y)=\begin{cases}Y,&|Y|\leq 1,\\
Y/|Y|,&|Y|>1.\end{cases}

Finally, the decoder
\mathcal{D}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is defined by the composition

(3.59)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(u_{i}):=u({\,\cdot\,},\widehat{Y}(u_{i})),

where
u({\,\cdot\,};Y)
is given by (
3.52
) and

(3.60)

\displaystyle\widehat{Y}(u_{i}):=(\mathcal{Y}\circ\mathcal{F}\mathcal{T}\circ\mathcal{M})(u_{i}).

It is easy to see that the main difference between the above encoder/decoder pair and the pseudo-spectral encoder/decoder of (
3.47
) is the non-linear shrink operation in (
3.57
), which we introduce to ensure that
\widehat{Y}\in[-1,1]^{\mathbb{N}}
. As a consequence of this observation, we find that the usual error estimates for pseudo-spectral methods imply similar error estimates for the encoding error
\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}(u)-u\|_{L^{2}}
. For instance, we have the following proposition (proved in Appendix
C.16
),

Proposition 3.27
.

Let
s>d/2
, and assume that
u\in H^{s}(D)
. There exists a constant
C=C(s,d)>0
, such that the encoder/decoder pair
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D})
defined by (
3.54
) and (
3.59
) satisfy the estimate

(3.61)

\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}(u)-u\|_{L^{2}}\leq CN^{-s}\|u\|_{H^{s}}.

On the other hand, if the coefficients
\alpha_{k}
decay exponentially as in the random field (
3.51
), one can expect exponential decay rates for the encoding error as in the following Theorem,

Theorem 3.28
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}(D))
, with
D=\mathbb{T}^{d}
or
D=[0,2\pi]^{d}
, denote the law of the random field
u({\,\cdot\,};Y)
defined by (
3.52
), with random variables
Y=(Y_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\in[-1,1]^{\mathcal{J}}
,
\mathcal{J}=\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, and with
\alpha_{k}
satisfying the decay assumption (
3.53
). Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
, consider the encoder/decoder pair
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D})
based on the discrete Fourier transformation on a regular grid with grid size
m=(2N+1)^{d}
on
D
. Then there exists constants
C,c>0
, independent of
m
, such that the encoding error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}
for the encoder/decoder pair
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{D}
defined by (
3.54
) and (
3.59
), can be bounded by

(3.62)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}\leq C\exp(-c\ell m^{1/d}).

Furthermore, if
\mathcal{G}:L^{2}(D)\to L^{2}(U)
is an operator, and
\mathcal{F}:[-1,1]^{\mathcal{J}}\to L^{2}(U)
,
\bm{y}\mapsto\mathcal{F}(\bm{y})
is defined by

\mathcal{F}(\bm{y}):=\mathcal{G}(u({\,\cdot\,};\bm{y})),

then we have the identity

(3.63)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}\left((u_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{N}}\right)=\mathcal{F}(\widehat{Y}\left((u_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{N}}\right),\quad\forall\,(u_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{I}_{N}}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{m}.

This theorem, proved in Appendix
C.17
, shows that the encoding error for a very general form of the underlying measure
\mu
, decays exponentially in the number of sensors and suggests that DeepONets will have a small encoding error with a few sensors. The map
\bm{u}\mapsto\widehat{Y}(\bm{u})
defined by (
3.60
), plays a key role in defining the decoder (
3.59
) as well as the action of operator
\mathcal{G}
on the decoder. It turns out that this map can be efficiently approximated by a neural network of moderate size as follows:

Lemma 3.29
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and denote
m:=(2N+1)^{d}=|\mathcal{K}_{N}|
. Let
\kappa:\{1,\dots,m\}\to\mathcal{K}_{N}
be a bijection. There exists a constant
C>0
, independent of
N
,
m
, such that for every
N
there exists a ReLU neural network
\mathcal{N}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
, with

\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{N})\leq C(1+m\log(m)),\quad 0pt(\mathcal{N})\leq C(1+\log(m)),

and such that
\mathcal{N}(\bm{u})=(\widehat{Y}_{\kappa(1)}(\bm{u}),\dots,\widehat{Y}_{\kappa(m)}(\bm{u}))
, for all
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

The proof is based on a simple observation that,

\widehat{Y}=\mathcal{Y}\circ\mathcal{F}\mathcal{T}\circ\mathcal{M}
, with

(1)

\mathcal{M}
an affine mapping, introducing a bias,

(2)

\mathcal{F}\mathcal{T}
a linear mapping, implementing the discrete Fourier transform

(3)

\mathcal{Y}
a linear scaling followed by a shrink operation.

The map
\mathcal{M}
can evidently be represented by a neural network of
0pt=\mathcal{O}(1)
, and
\mathrm{size}=\mathcal{O}(m)
. The discrete Fourier transform can be efficiently computed using the fast Fourier transform (FFT) algorithm in
\mathcal{O}(N^{d}\log(N))=\mathcal{O}(m\log(m))
operations. We note that each step of this recursive algorithm is linear, and requires
\mathcal{O}(m)
multiplications and
\mathcal{O}(\log(m))
recursive steps to compute the FFT. Each step in the recursion can be represented exactly by a finite number
\mathcal{O}(1)
of ReLU neural network layers of size
\mathcal{O}(m)
. The
\mathcal{O}(\log(m))
steps in the recursion can thus be represented by a composition of
\mathcal{O}(\log(m))
neural network layers. Thus, the whole algorithm can be represented by a neural network of
\mathrm{size}=\mathcal{O}(m\log(m))
and depth
\mathcal{O}(\log(m))
. Finally, the linear scaling step can clearly be represented by a neural network of
0pt=\mathcal{O}(1)
and
\mathrm{size}=\mathcal{O}(m)
, and the shrink operation can be written in the form

\mathrm{shrink}(Y)=1-\max(0,2-\max(0,1+Y))=1-\sigma(2-\sigma(1+Y)),

where
\sigma(x)=\max(0,x)
denotes the ReLU activation function. Hence,
\mathcal{Y}
can be represented by a neural network of
\mathrm{size}=\mathcal{O}(m)
and
0pt=\mathcal{O}(1)
. Combining these three steps, we conclude that also the composition
\widehat{Y}
can be represented by a neural network of
\mathrm{size}=\mathcal{O}(m\log(m))
and
0pt=\mathcal{O}(\log(m))
. Similarly, any smooth activation function such as sigmoid or
\tanh
can be used to define a neural network of the same size to approximate
\widehat{Y}
.

3.6.
Bounds on the approximation error (
3.2
)

Given a particular choice of encoder/decoder and reconstruction/projection pairs
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D})
and
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{P})
, the approximation error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{A}}
(
3.2
) for the approximator
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
in the DeepONet (
2.10
) is a measure for the non-commutativity of the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{2}(D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{2}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}}

i.e., it measures the error in the approximation
\mathcal{A}\approx\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}
. Thus, bounding the approximation error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{A}}
can be viewed as a special instance of the general problem of the neural network approximation of high-dimensional mappings
\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
. Our aim in this section is to review some of the available results on neural network approximation in a finite-dimensional setting and relate it to the problem of deriving bounds on the approximation error (
3.2
) for the DeepONet (
2.10
). We start by considering the neural network approximation for regular high-dimensional mappings.

3.6.1.
Regular high-dimensional mappings

Evidently, the neural network approximation of a mapping
G:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
,
x\mapsto G(x)=(G_{1}(x),\dots,G_{p}(x))
can be carried out by independently approximating each of the components
G_{j}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
by a neural network
\mathcal{A}_{j}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
, and then combining these individual approximations to a single neural network
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
,
x\mapsto\mathcal{A}(x)=(\mathcal{A}_{1}(x),\dots,\mathcal{A}_{p}(x))
, with

\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{A})=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{A}_{j})\leq p\max_{j=1,\dots,p}\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{A}_{j}).

The approximation of
G_{j}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
(over a bounded domain
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
) by neural networks is a fundamental approximation theoretic problem. One approach for deriving general approximation results relies on the Sobolev regularity of
G_{j}
. As a prototype of the available results in this direction, we cite the following result due to Yarotsky
Yarotsky 2017
, for
G_{j}\in W^{k,\infty}([0,1]^{m})
,

Theorem 3.30

(
(
Yarotsky 2017
, Theorem 1)
)
.

Let
m
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
be given. There exists a constant
C=C(m,k)>0
, such that for any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and
G_{j}:[0,1]^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\|G_{j}\|_{W^{k,\infty}}\leq 1
, there exists a ReLU neural network
\mathcal{A}_{j}:[0,1]^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
, with

0pt(\mathcal{A}_{j})\leq C(1+\log(\epsilon^{-1})),\quad\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{A}_{j})\leq C\epsilon^{-m/k}(1+\log(\epsilon^{-1})),

such that

\|G_{j}(x)-\mathcal{A}_{j}(x)\|_{L^{\infty}([0,1]^{m})}\leq\epsilon.

In the particular case of a Lipschitz mapping
G:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
, the upper limit on the required size of the approximating neural network
\mathcal{A}
is thus
\mathcal{O}(p\epsilon^{-m})
. In particular, this size scales exponentially in the input dimension (=number of DeepONet sensors)
m
. By Definition
3.5
, this constitutes a
curse of dimensionality
as the number of sensors
m
need to grow as
\epsilon\to 0
from bounds such as (
3.61
) on the encoding error (
3.1
). Thus, the above Theorem of Yarotsky may not suffice to find optimal sizes of the approximator network
\mathcal{A}
in the DeepONet (
2.10
).

3.6.2.
Holomorphic infinite-dimensional mappings

Given this possible curse of dimensionality in bounding the approximation error (
3.2
) for Lipschitz continuous maps, we seek to find a class of nonlinear operators
\mathcal{G}
, mapping infinite-dimensional Banach spaces, for which this curse of dimensionality can be avoided. One possible class is the class of
holomorphic mappings

\bm{y}\mapsto\mathcal{F}(\bm{y})
, which has been shown in recent papers
Schwab & Zech 2019
;
Opschoor et al. 2019
;
Opschoor et al. 2020
to be efficiently approximated by ReLU neural networks,
breaking the curse of dimensionality
. For instance, the class of mappings considered in
Opschoor et al. 2020
are infinite dimensional mappings

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}:[-1,1]^{\mathbb{N}}\to V,\quad\bm{y}=(y_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\to\mathcal{F}(\bm{y}),

where
V
is a Banach space. To simplify notation, we shall replace
\mathbb{N}
by an arbitrary countable index set
\mathcal{J}
and consider mappings

(3.64)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}:[-1,1]^{\mathcal{J}}\to V,\quad\bm{y}=(y_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\to\mathcal{F}(\bm{y}),

in the following. In this context, we require the following definition
Opschoor et al. 2020
:

Definition 3.31

(
(b,\epsilon)
-admissibility)
.

Let
V
be a Banach space. Let
\bm{b}=(b_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
be a given sequence of monotonically decreasing positive reals
b_{j}>0
such that
\bm{b}\in\ell^{p}(\mathbb{N})
for some
p\in(0,1]
. Let
\kappa:\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{J}
be an enumeration of the index set
\mathcal{J}
. A poly-radius
\bm{\rho}=(\rho_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\in(1,\infty)^{\mathcal{J}}
is called
(\bm{b},\epsilon;\kappa)
-admissible for some
\epsilon>0
, if

\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}b_{j}(\rho_{\kappa(j)}-1)\leq\epsilon.

We further recall that for a radius
\rho>1
, the Bernstein ellipse
\mathcal{E}_{\rho}\subset\mathbb{C}
is defined by

(3.65)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\rho}:={\left\{\frac{z+z^{-1}}{2}\,\middle|\,0\leq|z|<\rho\right\}}.

We define holomorphy, following
(
Opschoor et al. 2020
, Definition 3.3)
as:

Definition 3.32

(
(b,\epsilon)
-holomorphy)
.

Let
V
be a Banach space. Let
\kappa:\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{J}
be an enumeration of the index set
\mathcal{J}
. A continuous mapping
\mathcal{F}:[-1,1]^{\mathcal{J}}\to V
is called
(\bm{b},\epsilon,\kappa)
-holomorphic
, if there exists a constant
C=C(\mathcal{F})
, such that the following holds: For every
(\bm{b},\epsilon,\kappa)
-admissible
\bm{\rho}
with
\mathcal{E}_{\bm{\rho}}:=\prod_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mathcal{E}_{\rho_{j}}\subset\mathbb{C}^{\mathcal{J}}
, there exists an extension
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}:\mathcal{E}_{\bm{\rho}}\to V_{\mathbb{C}}
, such that

(3.66)

\displaystyle\bm{z}\mapsto\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(\bm{z})\text{ is holomorphic}

as a function of each
z_{j}\in E_{\rho_{j}}
,
j\in\mathcal{J}
, and such that

(3.67)

\displaystyle\sup_{\bm{z}\in E_{\bm{\rho}}}\left\|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(\bm{z})\right\|_{V_{\mathbb{C}}}\leq C(\mathcal{F}).

Here
V_{\mathbb{C}}
denotes the complexification of the (real) Banach space
V
.

We remark that such
holomorphic operators
arise naturally in the context of elliptic and parabolic PDEs, for instance as the data to solution map of diffusion equations with random coefficients
Schwab & Zech 2019
and references therein. We see further examples of these operators in the next section.

We can now state the following result which follows from
(
Opschoor et al. 2020
, Theorem 4.11)
(our statement here is closer to the formulation of
(
Schwab & Zech 2019
, Theorem 3.9)
):

Proposition 3.33
.

Let
V
be a Banach space. Let
\mathcal{F}:[-1,1]^{\mathcal{J}}\to V
be a
(\bm{b},\epsilon,\kappa)
-holomorphic map for some
\bm{b}\in\ell^{q}(\mathbb{N})
and
q\in(0,1)
, and an enumeration
\kappa:\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{J}
. Then there exists a constant
C>0
, such that for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists an index set

\Lambda_{N}\subset{\left\{\bm{\nu}=(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\dots)\in\textstyle{\prod}_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mathbb{N}_{0}\,\middle|\,\nu_{j}\neq 0\;\text{for finitely many $j\in\mathcal{J}$}\right\}},

with
|\Lambda_{N}|=N
, a finite set of coefficients
\{c_{\bm{\nu}}\}_{\bm{\nu}\in\Lambda_{N}}\subset V
, and a ReLU network
\mathcal{N}:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda_{N}}
,
y\mapsto\{\mathcal{N}_{\bm{\nu}}(y)\}_{\bm{\nu}\in\Lambda_{N}}
with

\displaystyle\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{N})\leq C(1+N\log(N)\log\log(N)),

\displaystyle 0pt(\mathcal{N})\leq C(1+\log(N)\log\log(N)),

and such that

(3.68)

\displaystyle\sup_{\bm{y}\in[-1,1]^{\mathcal{J}}}\left\|\mathcal{F}(\bm{y})-\sum_{\bm{\nu}\in\Lambda_{N}}c_{\bm{\nu}}\mathcal{N}_{\bm{\nu}}(y_{\kappa(1)},\dots,y_{\kappa(N)})\right\|_{V}\leq CN^{1-1/q}.

The following corollary of the above proposition, proved in Appendix
C.18
, enables us to apply the result of the theorem to the specific structure of the approximator neural network
\mathcal{A}
and the resulting approximation error (
3.2
) for our DeepONet (
2.10
).

Corollary 3.34
.

Let
V
be a Banach space. Let
\mathcal{F}:[-1,1]^{\mathcal{J}}\to V
be a
(\bm{b},\epsilon,\kappa)
-holomorphic map for some
\bm{b}\in\ell^{q}(\mathbb{N})
and
q\in(0,1)
, where
\kappa:\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{J}
is an enumeration of
\mathcal{J}
. In particular, it is assumed that
\{b_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is a monotonically decreasing sequence. If
\mathcal{P}:V\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
is a continuous linear mapping, then there exists a constant
C>0
, such that for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a ReLU network
\mathcal{N}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
, with

\displaystyle\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{N})\leq C(1+pm\log(m)\log\log(m)),

\displaystyle 0pt(\mathcal{N})\leq C(1+\log(m)\log\log(m)),

and such that

\sup_{\bm{y}\in[-1,1]^{\mathcal{J}}}\|\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{F}(\bm{y})-\mathcal{N}(y_{\kappa(1)},\dots,y_{\kappa(m)})\|_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{p})}\leq C\|\mathcal{P}\|\,m^{-s},

where
s:=q^{-1}-1>0
and
\|\mathcal{P}\|=\|\mathcal{P}\|_{V\to\ell^{2}}
denotes the operator norm.

With the above setup, we recall that maps of the form (
3.64
) arise naturally from general nonlinear operators
\mathcal{G}:X\to L^{2}(U)
,
u\mapsto\mathcal{G}(u)
, when the probability measure
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(X)
is given in the parametrized form (
3.51
). As in the subsection
3.5.3
, we will focus on the case where either
D=\mathbb{T}^{d}
is periodic, or
D=[0,2\pi]^{d}
is a rectangular domain and an expansion (
3.51
) in terms of the standard Fourier basis
\{\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
. If the underlying measure
\mu
can be written as the law of a random field in the parametrized form (
3.51
) with exponentially decaying coefficients (
3.53
), then we can define a “parametrized version” of the operator
\mathcal{G}
by the following mapping
\mathcal{F}:[-1,1]^{\mathcal{J}}\to L^{2}(U)
,
\mathcal{J}=\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, defined by

(3.69)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\bm{y}):=\mathcal{G}\left(u({\,\cdot\,};\bm{y})\right),

for
\bm{y}=(y_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\in[-1,1]^{\mathcal{J}}
.

To show how the neural network approximation of such
\mathcal{F}:[-1,1]^{\mathcal{J}}\to L^{2}(U)
relates to the approximator network
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
, we recall that the encoder/decoder pair
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D})
of section
3.5.3
was constructed in terms of an approximate sensor data-to-parameter mapping
\bm{u}:=(u_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{N}}\mapsto\widehat{Y}(\bm{u})
such that (cf. Theorem
3.28
)

(3.70)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D})(\bm{u})=\mathcal{F}(\widehat{Y}(\bm{u})),\quad\forall\,\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.

Let
\mathcal{P}:L^{2}(U)\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
be the projection mapping corresponding to a reconstruction
\mathcal{R}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to L^{2}(U)
, and let the neural network
\mathcal{N}
be constructed as in Corollary
3.34
, corresponding to an enumeration
\kappa:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, which enumerates
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with increasing
|k|_{\infty}
, i.e., such that
j\mapsto|\kappa(j)|_{\infty}
is monotonically increasing. Then, it is straightforward to see that for
m=(2N+1)^{d}
, the Fourier wavenumbers
\kappa(1),\dots,\kappa(m)\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
correspond precisely to the Fourier wavenumbers in

\mathcal{K}_{N}={\left\{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\,\middle|\,|k|_{\infty}\leq N\right\}}.

In this case, we define

(3.71)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\bm{u}):=\mathcal{N}(\widehat{Y}_{\kappa(1)}(\bm{u}),\dots,\widehat{Y}_{\kappa(m)}(\bm{u})),

which we shall also denote more compactly as
\mathcal{A}(\bm{u})=\mathcal{N}(\widehat{Y}_{\kappa}(\bm{u}))
, where
\widehat{Y}_{\kappa}=(\widehat{Y}_{\kappa(1)},\dots,\widehat{Y}_{\kappa(m)})
. We now note the following theorem, proved in Appendix
C.19
, on the approximation of
\mathcal{A}
:

Theorem 3.35
.

Let
\mathcal{G}:X\to L^{2}(U)
be a non-linear operator. Assume that the parametrized mapping
\mathcal{F}
given by (
3.69
), defines a
(\bm{b},\epsilon,\kappa)
-holomorphic mapping
\mathcal{F}:[-1,1]^{\mathcal{J}}\to L^{2}(U)
, with
\bm{b}\in\ell^{q}(\mathbb{N})
and
\kappa:\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{J}
an enumeration. Assume that
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(X)
is given as the law of the random field (
3.51
). Let the encoder/decoder pair be constructed as in section
3.5.3
, so that (
3.70
) holds. Given an affine reconstruction
\mathcal{R}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to L^{2}(U)
, let
\mathcal{P}:L^{2}(U)\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
denote the corresponding optimal linear projection (
3.20
). Then given
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a constant
C_{k}>0
, independent of
m
,
p
, such that the approximator
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
defined by (
3.71
) can be represented by a neural network with

\begin{gathered}\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{A})\leq C_{k}(1+mp\log(m)\log\log(m)),\\
0pt(\mathcal{A})\leq C_{k}(1+m\log(m)\log\log(m)).\end{gathered}

and such that the approximation error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{A}}
can be estimated by

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{A}}\leq C_{k}\|\mathcal{P}\|\,m^{-k},

where
\|\mathcal{P}\|=\|\mathcal{P}\|_{L^{2}(U)\to\mathbb{R}^{p}}
is the operator norm of
\mathcal{P}
.

Thus, the above theorem shows that an approximator neural network
\mathcal{A}
of log-linear size in the product of the number of sensors
m
and number of trunk nets
p
can lead to very small approximation error if the underlying operator
\mathcal{G}
yields a holomorphic reduction (
3.69
). This will allow us to overcome the curse of dimensionality for the approximation error (
3.2
) for the DeepONet (
2.10
) in many cases.

4.
Error bounds on DeepONets in concrete examples.

In the last section, we decomposed the error (
2.11
) that a DeepONet (
2.10
) incurs in approximating a nonlinear operator
\mathcal{G}:X\to Y
, with an underlying measure
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(X)
, into the encoding error (
3.1
), the reconstruction error (
3.3
) and approximation error (
3.2
). We provided explicit bounds on each of these three errors. In particular, the encoding error was estimated in terms of the spectral decay of the underlying covariance operator of the measure
\mu
and it was shown that under a boundedness assumption on the eigenfunctions, even a random choice of sensor points provided an optimal encoding error (modulo a log term). More judicious choices of sensor points, for instance with the pseudo-spectral encoder (
3.47
), allowed us to recover optimal (up to constants) encoding error. Similarly, we showed that the reconstruction error (
3.3
) relies on the spectral decay properties of the covariance operator with respect to the push-forward measure
\mathcal{G}_{\#}{\mu}
and can be bounded by using the smoothness of the operator
\mathcal{G}
as in (
3.12
). Finally, estimating the approximation error (
3.2
) boils down to a neural network approximation of finite, but high, dimensional mappings and one can use either Sobolev regularity or if available, holomorphy, of the map
\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}
to bound this component. The above discussion provides the following workflow to bound the DeepONet error (
2.11
) in concrete cases, i.e., for concrete instances of the operator
\mathcal{G}
and the underlying measure
\mu
:

•

For a given measure
\mu
, estimate the spectral decay rate for the associated covariance operator. Use random sensors in the case of no further information about the measure to obtain almost optimal bounds on the encoding error. If more information is available, one can use bespoke sensor points to obtain optimal bounds on the encoding error.

•

For a given operator
\mathcal{G}
, use smoothness of the operator to estimate the reconstruction error (
3.3
) as in (
3.12
).

•

For the approximation error (
3.2
), use the regularity, in particular possible holomorphy, of the operator
\mathcal{G}
to estimate this error.

The simplest examples for
\mathcal{G}
correspond to those cases where it is a
bounded linear operator
. In this case, the above workflow is carried out in Appendix
D
, where we show that DeepONet approximation of these general linear operators depends on the spectral decay of the underlying measure
\mu
and on the approximation property of the trunk net
\tau
. However for nonlinear operators
\mathcal{G}
, one has to carry out the above workflow in each specific case. To this end, we will illustrate this workflow for four concrete examples of
nonlinear operators
, that are chosen to represent different types of differential equations, namely, a nonlinear ODE, an elliptic PDE, a nonlinear parabolic and a nonlinear hyperbolic PDE. Within each class, we choose a concrete example that is widely agreed as a prototype for this class of problems.

4.1.
A nonlinear ODE: Gravity pendulum with external force.

4.1.1.
Problem formulation.

We consider the following nonlinear ODE system, already considered in the context of approximation by DeepONets in
Lu et al. 2019
:

(4.1)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{dv_{1}}{dt}&=v_{2},\\
\frac{dv_{2}}{dt}&=-\gamma\sin(v_{1})+u(t).\end{aligned}\right.

with initial condition
v(0)=0
and where
\gamma>0
is a parameter. Let us denote
v=(v_{1},v_{2})
,

g(v):=\begin{pmatrix}v_{2}\\
-\gamma\sin(v_{1})\end{pmatrix},\quad U(t):=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
u(t)\end{pmatrix},

so that equation (
4.1
) can be written in the form

(4.2)

\displaystyle\frac{dv}{dt}=g(v)+U,\quad v(0)=0.

In (
4.2
),
v_{1},v_{2}
are the angle and angular velocity of the pendulum and the constant
\gamma

denotes a frequency parameter. The dynamics of the pendulum is driven by an external force
u=u(t)
. It is straightforward to see that for each
r>0
, there exists a constant
C_{r}>0
, such that

(4.3)

\displaystyle\|g^{(r)}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\leq C_{r},

where
g^{r}
denotes the
r
-th derivative.

With the external force
u
as the input, the output of the system is the solution vector
v(t)
and the underlying nonlinear operator is given by
\mathcal{G}:L^{2}([0,T])\to L^{2}([0,T])
,
u\mapsto\mathcal{G}(u)=v
. The following Lemma, proved in Appendix
E.1
, provides a precise characterization of this operator.

Lemma 4.1
.

There exists a constant
C=C(\|g^{(1)}\|_{L^{\infty}},T)>0
, such that for any two
u,u^{\prime}\in L^{2}([0,T])
, we have

\|\mathcal{G}(u)-\mathcal{G}(u^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}([0,T])}\leq C\|u-u^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}([0,T])}.

In particular,
\mathcal{G}:L^{2}([0,T])\to L^{2}([0,T])
, mapping
u(t)\to v(t)
, with
v
being the solution of the ODE (
4.2
), is Lipschitz continuous.

Next, in order to define the data for the DeepONet approximation (see Definition
2.1
), we need to specify an underlying measure
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}([0,T]))
. Following the discussion in the previous section and for the sake of definiteness, we choose a parametrized measure
\mu
, as considered in section
3.5.3
, as a law of a random field
u
, that can be expanded in the form

(4.4)

\displaystyle u(t;Y)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}Y_{k}\alpha_{k}\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{k}\left(\frac{2\pi t}{T}\right),\quad t\in[0,T],

where
\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{k}(x)
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, denotes the one-dimensional standard Fourier basis on
[0,2\pi]
(with notation of Appendix
A
) and the coefficients
\alpha_{k}\geq 0
decay to zero. We will assume that there exist constants
C_{\alpha},\ell>0
, such that

\alpha_{k}\leq C_{\alpha}\exp(-|k|\ell).

Furthermore, we assume that the
\{Y_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
are iid random variables on
[-1,1]
.

With the above data for the DeepONet approximation problem, we will provide explicit bounds on the total error (
2.11
) for a DeepONet (
2.10
) approximating the operator
\mathcal{G}
. Following the workflow outlined above, we proceed to bound the following sources of error.

4.1.2.
Bounds on the encoding error (
3.1
).

Given the underlying measure
\mu
defined as the law of the random field (
4.4
), we choose the encoder-decoder pair as used in section
3.5.3
, i.e., the encoder is the pointwise evaluation (
3.54
) on equidistant points
t_{1},\dots,t_{m}
on
[0,T]
and the corresponding decoder is given by (
3.59
). A direct application of Theorem
3.28
yields the following bound on the encoding error,

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}([0,T]))
denote the law of the random field
u({\,\cdot\,};Y)
defined by (
4.4
). Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
consider the encoder/decoder pair
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D})
with
m=2N+1
grid points and given by (
3.54
) and (
3.59
), respectively. Then, there exists a constant
C>0
, independent of
m
, such that the encoding error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}
(
3.1
) can be bounded by

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}\leq C\exp(-\ell\lfloor m/2\rfloor).

Furthermore, denoting by
\mathcal{F}:[-1,1]^{\mathbb{Z}}\to L^{2}([0,T])
,
\bm{y}\mapsto\mathcal{F}(\bm{y})
, the mapping

(4.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\bm{y}):=\mathcal{G}(u({\,\cdot\,};\bm{y})),

we have the identity
\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}(\bm{u})=\mathcal{F}(\widehat{Y}(\bm{u}))
, for all
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

Moreover, as in Lemma
3.29
,
\widehat{Y}
in (
4.5
) can be represented by a neural network, in the sense that
\widehat{Y}_{k}\equiv 0
, for
|k|>N
, and there exists a neural network
\mathcal{N}
with

\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{N})=\mathcal{O}(m\log(m)),\quad 0pt(\mathcal{N})=\mathcal{O}(\log(m)),

and
\mathcal{N}(\bm{u})=(\widehat{Y}_{-N}(\bm{u}),\dots,\widehat{Y}_{0}(\bm{u}),\dots,\widehat{Y}_{N}(\bm{u}))
, for all
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

4.1.3.
Bounds on the reconstruction error (
3.3
)

Following our program outlined above, we will bound the reconstruction error (
3.3
) for a DeepONet approximation the operator
\mathcal{G}
for the forced pendulum by appealing to the smoothness of the image
\mathrm{Im}(\mathcal{G})
of
\mathcal{G}
. To this end, we have the following lemma, proved in Appendix
E.2
,

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
T>0
, and consider the solution
v(t)
of (
4.2
) for
t\in[0,T]
, where
g(v)
satisfies
L^{\infty}
-bound (
4.3
) for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
k\geq 1
. Then for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a constant
A_{k}>0
(possibly depending on
g
and
T
, in addition to
k
, but independent of
u
), such that

\|v^{(k)}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq A_{k}\left(1+\|u\|_{H^{k}}^{k}\right).

Here

\|u\|_{H^{k}}:=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\|u^{(\ell)}\|_{L^{2}([0,T])}.

Given the desired smoothness of the image of the operator
\mathcal{G}
, we need to find a suitable reconstructor (
2.9
). To this end, we will use Legendre polynomials to build our reconstructor and have the following result, proved in Appendix
E.3
,

Lemma 4.4
.

If
\widetilde{\tau}_{k}
,
k=1,\dots,p
are the first
p
Legendre polynomials, then the reconstruction error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}
for the reconstruction mapping

\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{p})\mapsto\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}(\alpha)=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\alpha_{k}\widetilde{\tau}_{k},

induced by the trunk net
\widetilde{\bm{\tau}}=(0,\widetilde{\tau}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\tau}_{p})
, satisfies

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}\leq\frac{C}{p^{k}}\left(\int_{X}(1+\|u\|_{H^{k}([0,T])})^{2k}\,d\mu(u)\right)^{1/2}.

For some constant
C=C(k,T)>0
. In particular, if
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}(D))
is concentrated on
H^{k}(D)
and
\int_{L^{2}(D)}\|u\|_{H^{k}}^{2k}\,d\mu(u)<\infty
, then there exists a constant
C=C(T,k,\mu)>0
independent of
p
, such that

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}\leq Cp^{-k}.

From
(
Opschoor et al. 2019
, Proposition 2.10)
, we have the following result; for any
p\in\mathbb{N}
,
\delta\in(0,1)
, there exists a ReLU neural network
\bm{L}_{\delta}:[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
,
t\mapsto\bm{L}_{\delta}(t)=(L_{1,\delta}(t),\dots,L_{p,\delta}(t))
, which approximates the first
p
Legendre polynomials
L_{1}(t),\dots,L_{p}(t)
with

\max_{j=1,\dots,p}\|L_{j}-L_{j,\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\delta,

and for a constant
C>0
, independent of
\delta
and
p
, it holds

\mathrm{size}(\bm{L}_{\delta})\leq Cp^{3}+p^{2}\log(\delta^{-1}).\quad 0pt(\bm{L}_{\delta})\leq C(1+\log(p))(p+\log(\delta^{-1})),

We will leverage the above neural network to find a suitable trunk net for the DeepONet approximation of the operator
\mathcal{G}
for the forced pendulum. To this end, let
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}
denote the reconstruction

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}(\alpha)=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\alpha_{k}\widetilde{\tau}_{k},

where
\widetilde{\tau}_{k}=L_{k}(t)
denotes the
k
-th Legendre polynomial. By Lemma
4.4
, for any
r\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
C>0
, depending on
r
but independent of
p
, such that we have
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}\leq Cp^{-r}
, for all
p\in\mathbb{N}
.
Choosing
\bm{\tau}=\bm{L}_{\delta}
as above with
\delta=p^{-r-3/2}
, it follows that for any
p\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a trunk net
\bm{\tau}
with

p^{3/2}\max_{k=1,\dots,p}\|\tau_{k}-\widetilde{\tau}_{k}\|_{L^{2}([0,T])}\leq p^{-r},

and

\mathrm{size}(\bm{\tau})=\mathcal{O}(p^{3}).\quad 0pt(\bm{\tau})=\mathcal{O}(p\log(p)).

From Lemma
3.16
, it follows that for the reconstruction
\mathcal{R}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to L^{2}([0,T])
,

\mathcal{R}(\alpha)=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\alpha_{k}\tau_{k},

induced by the trunk net
\bm{\tau}=(0,\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{p})
, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}

\displaystyle\leq\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}+p^{3/2}\max_{k=1,\dots,p}\|\tau_{k}-\widetilde{\tau}_{k}\|_{L^{2}([0,T])}\leq Cp^{-r}.

We summarize these observations in the following proposition,

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
\mu
denote the law of the random field
u({\,\cdot\,};Y)
given by (
4.4
). For any
r\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a constant
C>0
, depending on
T
,
\mu
and
r
, but independent of
p
, such that for any
p\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a trunk neural network
\bm{\tau}:[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{p+1}
,
s\mapsto(\tau_{0}(s),\dots,\tau_{p}(s))
, with

\mathrm{size}(\bm{\tau})\leq C(1+p^{3}),\quad 0pt(\bm{\tau})\leq C(1+p\log(p))),

such that the reconstruction error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}
with corresponding projection
\mathcal{P}
, satisfies the bound

(4.6)

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}\leq Cp^{-r},\quad(p\in\mathbb{N}).

Furthermore, for any
p\in\mathbb{N}
, the reconstruction
\mathcal{R}
and the projection satisfy the uniform bound
\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{R}),\,\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{P})\leq 2
, where

\displaystyle\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{R})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Lip}\left(\mathcal{R}:(\mathbb{R}^{p},\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{\ell^{2}})\to(L^{2}([0,T]),\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{L^{2}})\right)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{P})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Lip}\left(\mathcal{P}:(L^{2}([0,T]),\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{L^{2}})\to(\mathbb{R}^{p},\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{\ell^{2}})\right).

Thus, we observe from (
4.6
) that the reconstruction error for a DeepONet can be made very small for a moderate number of trunk nets. Moreover, the corresponding size of the trunk net is moderate in the number of trunk nets.

4.1.4.
Bounds on the approximation error (
3.2
)

Following our workflow, we will derive bounds on the approximation error (
3.2
) for a DeepONet (
2.10
) approximating the operator
\mathcal{G}
for the forced gravity pendulum by showing that the corresponding operator is holomorphic in the sense of Definition
3.32
.

To this end, we assume that

(4.7)

\displaystyle u(t)=u(t;\bm{y})=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}y_{k}\alpha_{k}\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{k}(t),\quad(\bm{y}=(y_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}),

can be expanded by the Fourier basis functions
\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{k}\in L^{2}([0,T])
, such that

(4.8)

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}\|\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C_{\alpha}e^{-|k|\ell}\leq 1,\quad\forall\,k\in\mathbb{Z}.

Letting
\kappa:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{Z}
denote the enumeration of appendix
A
, with
j\mapsto|\kappa(j)|
increasing, we define a monotonically decreasing sequence
(b_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
by

(4.9)

\displaystyle b_{j}:=C_{\alpha}e^{-|\kappa(j)|\ell}.

We will show that that the mapping

\mathcal{F}:[-1,1]^{\mathbb{N}}\to L^{2}([0,T]),\quad\bm{y}\mapsto v(\bm{y}),

where
v({\bm{y}})
solves the gravity pendulum equation (
4.2
) with forcing
U(t;\bm{y})=(0,u(t;\bm{y}))
can be extended to a holomorphic mapping on suitable (admissible) poly-ellipses
E_{\bm{\rho}}=\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}E_{\rho_{j}}\subset\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{N}}
,

\mathcal{F}:\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}E_{\rho_{j}}\to L^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}([0,T]),\quad\bm{z}\mapsto v(\bm{z}),

where
\bm{\rho}=(\rho_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
,
\rho_{j}>1
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
. For
\rho>1
,
E_{\rho}\subset\mathbb{C}
denotes the (interior of the) Bernstein ellipse (cp. (
3.65
)). It is straightforward to see that,

(4.10)

\displaystyle E_{\rho}\subset{\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}\,\middle|\,|\mathrm{Re}(z)|<\rho,\,|\mathrm{Im}(z)|<\rho-1\right\}}.

To prove the existence of an analytic continuation to suitable poly-ellipses
E_{\bm{\rho}}
, our first goal is to show that there exists a
\delta>0
, such that the ODE-system (
4.2
) has a complex-valued solution
v:[0,T]\to\mathbb{C}^{2}
for any forcing
U:[0,T]\to\mathbb{C}^{2}
with
|\mathrm{Im}(U(t))|\leq\delta
. To this end, we first note that the complex-valued ODE (
4.2
) is equivalent to the following system for
v(t)=v_{r}(t)+iv_{i}(t)
, with
v_{r}=(v_{r,1},v_{r,2}),v_{i}=(v_{i,1},v_{i,2}):[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
:

(4.11)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{dv_{r}}{dt}&=g_{r}(v_{r},v_{i})+\mathrm{Re}(U),\quad v_{r}(0)=0,\\
\frac{dv_{i}}{dt}&=g_{i}(v_{r},v_{i})+\mathrm{Im}(U),\quad v_{i}(0)=0,\end{aligned}\right.

where
U(t)=(0,u(t))
,
u:[0,T]\to\mathbb{C}
and

g_{r}(v_{r},v_{i})=\begin{pmatrix}v_{r,2}\\
-\gamma\sin(v_{r,1})\cosh(v_{i,1})\end{pmatrix},\quad g_{i}(v_{r},v_{i})=\begin{pmatrix}v_{i,2}\\
-\gamma\cos(v_{r,1})\sinh(v_{i,1})\end{pmatrix}.

We note that the second equation of (
4.11
) implies that

\displaystyle|v_{i}(t)|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}|g_{i}(v_{r}(s),v_{i}(s))|\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}|\mathrm{Im}(U(s))|\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\left(|v_{i}(s)|+\gamma|\sinh(v_{i,1}(s))|\right)\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}|\mathrm{Im}(u(s))|\,ds.

Furthermore, we have

|\sinh(x)|=\left|\int_{0}^{x}\cosh(\xi)\,d\xi\right|\leq|x|\sup_{|\xi|\leq|x|}\cosh(\xi)\leq|x|e^{|x|},

for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
. We thus conclude that

(4.12)

\displaystyle|v_{i}(t)|\leq\int_{0}^{t}|v_{i}(s)|\left(1+\gamma e^{|v_{i}(s)|}\right)\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}|\mathrm{Im}(u(s))|\,ds,

for all
t\in[0,T]
.

The above estimate paves the way for the following lemma, proved in Appendix
E.4
on the boundedness of the imaginary part of the solution of the complex extension of the forced gravity pendulum (
4.11
):

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
u(t)\in L^{\infty}([0,T])
. Assume that the solution of (
4.11
) exists on
[0,T_{0}]
for some
0<T_{0}\leq T
. There exists a constant
\delta>0
, such that if
\sup_{s\in[0,T]}|\mathrm{Im}(u(s))|\leq\delta
, then
\sup_{s\in[0,T_{0}]}|v_{i}(s)|\leq 1
.

This lemma allows us to prove the following lemma (see the detailed proof in Appendix
E.5
), which establishes global solutions for the complex extension of the forced pendulum (
4.11
),

Lemma 4.7
.

If
\delta
is chosen as in Lemma
4.6
, and if
\|\mathrm{Im}(u)\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])}\leq\delta
, then the maximal existence interval of the solution of the ODE system (
4.11
) contains
[0,T]
, and
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|v_{i}(t)|\leq 1
.

Now assume that
u(t)=u(t;\bm{z})
is parametrized as in (
4.7
). If
\bm{z}\in E_{\bm{\rho}}
belongs to a poly-ellipse in
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{N}}
, then clearly

(4.13)

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{Im}(u)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}|\mathrm{Im}(z_{k})|\underbrace{\alpha_{k}\|\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}}}_{\leq b_{j}\,(k=\kappa(j))}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}(\rho_{\kappa(j)}-1)b_{j}.

We recall (cp. Definition
3.31
), that a sequence
\bm{\rho}=(\rho_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
, with
\rho_{j}>1
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
is called
(\bm{b},\delta,\kappa)
-admissible, if

(4.14)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}b_{j}(\rho_{\kappa(j)}-1)<\delta,

where
b_{j}
is defined by (
4.9
), and where
\delta>0
is chosen as in Lemma
4.7
. It is now clear from Lemma
4.7
, that for any
\bm{z}\in E_{\bm{\rho}}
with
(\bm{b},\delta,\kappa)
-
admissible

\bm{\rho}
, the solution
v(\bm{z})\in C([0,T];\mathbb{C}^{2})
of (
4.11
) is well-defined. Hence, we can define a mapping

(4.15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}:E_{\bm{\rho}}=\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}E_{\rho_{j}}\to L^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}([0,T]),\quad\mathcal{F}(\bm{z}):=v(\bm{z}),

where
v(\bm{z})
is the solution of (
4.11
) with forcing
U(t)=(u(t),0)
, where
u(t)=u(t;\bm{z})
is given by (
4.7
). This allows us to prove in Appendix
E.6
the following lemma on the holomorphy of the map
\mathcal{F}
(
4.15
),

Lemma 4.8
.

The mapping
\mathcal{F}
defined by (
4.15
) is
(\bm{b},\delta,\kappa)
-holomorphic, according to Definition
3.32
; i.e., for each index
j\in\mathbb{N}
, the componentwise mapping

E_{\rho_{j}}\mapsto L^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}([0,T]),\quad z_{j}\mapsto\mathcal{F}(\bm{z}),

where the
z_{k}\in E_{\rho_{k}}
for
k\neq j
are held fixed, is (complex-)differentiable. Moreover, there exists a constant
C>0
, such that for any admissible
\bm{\rho}
, we have

\sup_{\bm{z}\in E_{\bm{\rho}}}\|\mathcal{F}(\bm{z})\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}([0,T])}\leq C.

As a consequence of Lemma
4.8
, we can now state the following approximation result, which follows from the general approximation result for
(\bm{b},\epsilon,\kappa)
-holomorphic mappings, Theorem
3.35
:

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D})
denote the encoder/decoder pair (
3.54
), (
3.59
) with
m
sensors, let
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{P})
denote the reconstruction/projection pair, constructed in Proposition
4.5
, for a given
p\in\mathbb{N}
. For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a constant
C>0
, depending on
k
, the final time
T
and the probability measure
\mu
, but independent of
m
and
p
, such that there exists a neural approximator network
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
with

\displaystyle\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{A})\leq C(1+pm\log(m)\log\log(m)),

\displaystyle 0pt(\mathcal{A})\leq C(1+\log(m)\log\log(m)),

and such that the approximation error

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{A}}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\|\mathcal{A}(\bm{u})-\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}(\bm{u})\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}}\,d(\mathcal{E}_{\#}\mu)(\bm{u})\right)^{1/2},

can be estimated by

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{A}}\leq Cm^{-k}.

The proof follows from a direct application of Theorem
3.35
, with the observation that
\|\mathcal{P}\|=\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{P})\leq 2
in Proposition
4.5
is bounded independently of
m
and
p
.

4.1.5.
Bounds on the DeepONet approximation error (
2.11
)

We combine propositions
4.2
,
4.5
,
4.9
to state the following theorem on the DeepONet error (
2.11
) for the forced gravity pendulum;

Theorem 4.10
.

Consider the DeepONet approximation problem for the gravity pendulum (
4.1
), where the forcing
u(t)
is distributed according to a probability measure
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}([0,T]))
given as the law of the random field (
4.4
). For any
k,r\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a constant
C=C(k,r)>0
, and a constant
c>0
, independent of
m
,
p
, such that for any
m,\,p\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a DeepONet (
2.10
) with trunk net
\bm{\tau}
and branch net
\bm{\beta}
, such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{size}(\bm{\tau})\leq C(1+p^{3}),\quad 0pt(\bm{\tau})\leq C(1+p\log(p)),

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{size}(\bm{\beta})\leq C(1+pm\log(m)\log\log(m)),

\displaystyle 0pt(\bm{\beta})\leq C(1+\log(m)\log\log(m)),

and such that the DeepONet approximation error (
2.11
) is bounded by

(4.16)

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}\leq Ce^{-c\ell m}+Cm^{-k}+Cp^{-r}.

Remark 4.11
.

Theorem
4.10
guarantees that for
\epsilon>0
, a DeepONet approximation error of
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}\sim\epsilon
can be achieved provided that
m\gtrsim\max(\ell^{-1}\log(\epsilon^{-1}),\epsilon^{-1/k})
and
p\gtrsim\epsilon^{-1/r}
. As long as the intuitively obvious restriction that
m\ell\gg 1
is satisfied, i.e., that the sensors can resolve the typical length scale
\ell
, these requirements can be achieved provided that
m\gtrsim\epsilon^{-1/k}
and
p\gtrsim\epsilon^{-1/r}
.
In this case, an error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}\lesssim\epsilon
can be achieved by a DeepONet with size of the order of

(4.17)

\mathrm{size}(\bm{\tau},\bm{\beta})\sim\epsilon^{-1/r}\left(\epsilon^{-2/r}+\epsilon^{-1/k}\log(\epsilon^{-1})\log\log(\epsilon^{-1})\right).

As the
k,r\in\mathbb{N}
were arbitrary, this shows that the required size only grows
sub-algebraically
with
\epsilon^{-1}\to\infty
, i.e. the required size scales asymptotically
\ll\epsilon^{-1/s}
, as
\epsilon\to 0
, for any
s>0
. Given Definition
3.5
, this clearly implies that the DeepONet approximation for this problem does not suffer from the curse of dimensionality.

We however point out that the implied constants in these asymptotic estimates depend on
k,r
. In particular, these constants might deteriorate as
k,r\to\infty
. On the other hand, we can fix
k,r
to be large and conclude from the complexity estimate (
4.17
) that the complexity of the DeepONet in this case can only grow algebraically with the error tolerance
\epsilon
. Given Definition
3.5
(cp. equation (
3.7
)), this suffices to prove that a DeepONet can approximate the operator
\mathcal{G}
in the forced gravity pendulum problem with the measure
\mu
(
4.4
) by
breaking the curse of dimensionality
.

Remark 4.12
.

In
Lu et al. 2019
, the authors used a Gaussian measure, with a covariance kernel (
3.46
), as the underlying measure
\mu
for the forced gravity pendulum. An estimate, analogous to (
4.16
) can be proved in this case, but with a super-exponential decay of the encoding error with respect to the number of sensors
m
, given the bound (
3.49
). However, the overall complexity still has sub-algebraic asymptotic growth in
\epsilon^{-1}
, as the other error terms scale exactly as in (
4.17
).

A different underlying measure
\mu
results from choosing an algebraic decay of the coefficients
\alpha_{k}
in (
4.4
). Clearly, the bound (
4.16
) will also hold in this case but with an algebraic decay in the encoding error. Nevertheless, the total complexity of the problem still scales algebraically (polynomially) with the error tolerance
\epsilon
and shows that the resulting DeepONet breaks the curse of dimensionality also in this case.

Remark 4.13
.

The estimates on the sizes of the trunk and branch nets rely on expressivity results for ReLU deep neural networks in approximating holomorphic functions
Opschoor et al. 2019
. These in turn, depend on the results of
Yarotsky 2017
for approximation of functions using ReLU networks. These approximation results may not necessarily be optimal and might suggest networks of larger size as well as depth, than what is needed in practice
Lu et al. 2019
. Moreover, the constants in complexity estimate (
4.17
) depend on the final time
T
and can grow exponentially in
T
(with a familiar argument based on the Grönwall’s inquality).

4.2.
An elliptic PDE: Multi-d diffusion with variable coefficients.

4.2.1.
Problem formulation.

We will consider the following very popular model problem for elliptic PDEs with unknown diffusion coefficients
Cohen et al. 2011
and references therein. For the sake of definiteness and simplicity, we shall assume a periodic domain
D=\mathbb{T}^{d}
in the following. We consider an elliptic PDE with variable coefficients
a
:

(4.18)

\displaystyle-\nabla\cdot(a(x)\nabla u(x))=f(x),

for
u\in H^{1}(D)
with suitable boundary conditions, and for fixed
f\in H^{-1}(D)
.

We also fix a probability measure
\mu
on the coefficients
a
on
L^{2}(D)
, such that
\mathrm{supp}(\mu)\subset L^{\infty}(D)
. To ensure coercivity of the problem (
4.18
), we will assume that

(4.19)

\displaystyle\mu({\left\{a\in L^{2}(D)\,\middle|\,0<\lambda(a)\leq\Lambda(a)<\infty\right\}})=1,

where

(4.20)

\displaystyle\lambda(a)

\displaystyle:=\essinf_{x\in D}a(x),

(4.21)

\displaystyle\Lambda(a)

\displaystyle:=\esssup_{x\in D}a(x)

denote the essential infimum and supremum of
a
, respectively. To ensure uniqueness of solutions to (
4.18
), we require that
u\in H^{1}_{0}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
have zero mean, i.e., that
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u(x)\,dx=0
. We note that the condition (
4.19
) on
\mu
is for example satisfied by the log-Gaussian measures commonly employed in hydrology
Charrier 2012
.

We note that the variable coefficient
a
can model the rock permeability in a Darcy flow with
u
modeling the pressure and
f
a source/injection term. Similarly,
a
may model variable conductivity in a medium with
u
modeling the temperature. In many applications, one is interested in inferring the solution
u
for a given coefficient
a
as the input. Thus, the
nonlinear
operator
\mathcal{G}
maps the input coefficient
a
into the solution field
u
of the PDE (
4.18
). The well-definedness of this operator is given in the following Lemma (proved in appendix
E.7
),

Lemma 4.14
.

Assume that the coefficients
a,a^{\prime}\in C(\mathbb{T}^{d})
satisfy the uniform coercivity assumption

0<\lambda\leq\inf_{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}a(x),\;\inf_{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}a^{\prime}(x).

Let
u,u^{\prime}\in H^{1}_{0}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
denote the solution to (
4.18
