) with coefficients
a,a^{\prime}
, respectively, and with the same right-hand side
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. There exists a constant
C=C(\lambda,\mathbb{T}^{d},\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})})
, such that

\|u-u^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leq C\|a-a^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}.

In particular the operator
\mathcal{G}:X\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,
a\mapsto u
, is Lipschitz continuous for any set
X\subset C(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, and satisfying the coercivity assumption.

By Sobolev embedding, Lemma
4.14
ensures the well-definedness, and Lipschitz continuity, of the operator
\mathcal{G}:H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
for any
s>d/2
. We complete the data for a DeepONet approximation problem (Definition
2.1
) by specifying an underlying measure
\mu
. Following standard practice
Cohen et al. 2011
and references therein, and aided by the fact that we enforce periodic boundary conditions, the underlying measure
\mu
is the law of a random field
a
, that is expanded in terms of the Fourier basis. More precisely, we assume that we can write
a
in the following form

(4.22)

\displaystyle a(x,Y)=\overline{a}(x)+\sum_{{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\alpha_{k}Y_{k}\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{k}(x),

with notation from Appendix
A
, and where for simplicity
\overline{a}(x)\equiv 1
is assumed to be constant. Furthermore, we will consider the case of smooth coefficients
x\mapsto a(x;Y)
, which is ensured by requiring that there exist constants
C_{\alpha}>0
and
\ell>1
, such that

(4.23)

\displaystyle|\alpha_{k}|\leq C_{\alpha}\exp(-\ell|k|_{\infty}),\quad\forall\;k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}.

Let us define
\bm{b}=(b_{1},b_{2},\dots)\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{N})
by

(4.24)

\displaystyle b_{j}:=C_{\alpha}\exp(-\ell|\kappa(j)|_{\infty}),

where
\kappa:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is the enumeration for the standard Fourier basis, defined in appendix
A
. Note that by assumption on the enumeration
\kappa
, we have that
b_{1}\geq b_{2}\geq\dots
is a monotonically decreasing sequence. In the following, we will assume throughout that
\|\bm{b}\|_{\ell^{1}}<1
, ensuring the uniform coercivity condition
\lambda(a)\geq\lambda
for some
\lambda>0
and all random coefficients
a=a({\,\cdot\,};Y)
in (
4.18
). In (
4.23
), the parameter
\ell>0
can be interpreted as the correlation length scale of the random coefficients. We furthermore assume that the
Y_{j}\in[-1,1]
are centered random variables, implying that
\mathbb{E}[a]=\overline{a}
. We let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
denote the law of the random coefficient (
4.22
). By the assumed decay (
4.23
), we have
\mathrm{supp}(\mu)\subset C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

Given the above setup, our aim is to find a DeepONet (
2.10
) which approximates the underlying
\mathcal{G}
, corresponding to the elliptic PDE (
4.18
) efficiently. To this end, we will follow the program outlined at the beginning of this section, and bound the encoding, reconstruction and approximation errors, separately in the following sections.

4.2.2.
Bounds on the encoding error (
3.1
)

We are almost in the setup that was already considered in section
3.5.3
, with the only difference that we now consider
X=H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
for a fixed
s>d/2
, instead of
X=L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. We can again consider the Fourier based encoder/decoder pair
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D})
given by (
3.54
) and (
3.59
), respectively. Applying a straightforward extension of Theorem
3.28
to
H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, we observe that due to the exponential decay of the (Fourier) coefficients
\alpha_{k}
, the exponential decay of the pseudo-spectral projection continues to hold also in the
H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
-norm, yielding the following error estimate for the encoding error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}
:

Proposition 4.15
.

Given
m\in\mathbb{N}
, let
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D})
denote the Fourier based encoder/decoder pair
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D})
given by (
3.54
) and (
3.59
), respectively.
There exists a constant
C>0
, depending on
C_{\alpha},\,\ell>0
and on
s>d/2
, but independent of
m
, and a universal constant
c>0
, independent of
m
such that

(4.25)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}=\left(\int_{X}\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}(u)-u\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}\,d\mu(u)\right)^{1/2}\leq C\exp(-c\ell m^{1/d}).

4.2.3.
Bounds on the reconstruction error (
3.3
)

We follow the program outlined at the beginning of this section and bound the reconstruction error via smoothness of the image of the operator
\mathcal{G}
for the elliptic PDE (
4.18
). To this end, we have the following Lemma (proved in Appendix
E.8
),

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
u
be a solution of (
4.18
), with coefficient
a\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, right-hand side
f\in H^{k}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and
\lambda=\min_{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}a(x)>0
. Then for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a constant
C>0
, depending only on
k
and
\lambda
, such that

\|u\|^{2}_{H^{k+1}}\leq C\|f\|_{H^{k}}^{2}\left(1+\|a\|^{2k}_{C^{k}}\right).

Given the above smoothness estimate, one can directly apply Proposition
3.21
and Theorem
3.7
to obtain the following bound on the reconstruction error,

Proposition 4.17
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
be a probability measure with
\mathrm{supp}(\mu)\subset C^{k}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. Assume that there exists
\lambda_{0}>0
, such that
\mu(\lambda(a)\geq\lambda_{0})=1
, and that
\int_{L^{2}}\|a\|_{C^{k}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2k}\,d\mu(a)<\infty
. Define an operator
\mathcal{G}:C(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
by
a\mapsto u=\mathcal{G}(a)
, where
u
is the solution of (
4.18
) with a smooth right-hand side
f\in C^{k}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. Then there exists a constant
C(k,\|f\|_{C^{k}},\mu)>0
, depending only on
\|f\|_{C^{k}}
,
k
, and
\mu
, such that for any
p\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a trunk net
\bm{\tau}:U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
,
y\mapsto\bm{\tau}(y)=(0,\tau_{1}(y),\dots,\tau_{p}(y))
with

\mathrm{size}(\bm{\tau})\leq Cp(1+\log(p)^{2}),\quad 0pt(\bm{\tau})\leq C(1+\log(p)^{2}),

such that the corresponding reconstruction

\mathcal{R}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\simeq L^{2}([0,2\pi]^{d}),\quad\mathcal{R}(\alpha):=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\alpha_{j}\tau_{j},

satisfies the following reconstruction error bound:

(4.26)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}\leq C(k,\|f\|_{C^{k}},\mu)\,p^{-k/d}.

Furthermore, the reconstruction
\mathcal{R}
and the associated projection
\mathcal{P}:L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
given by (
3.20
) satisfy
\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{R}),\,\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{P})\leq 2
.

4.2.4.
Bounds on the approximation error (
3.2
)

For choices of the encoder
\mathcal{E}
in Proposition
4.15
, and the reconstructor
\mathcal{R}
in Proposition
4.17
, the approximation error with approximator network
\mathcal{A}
is given by

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\|\mathcal{A}(a_{i})-\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}(a_{i})\|^{2}\,d(\mathcal{E}_{\#}\mu)(a_{i}),

where the decoder
\mathcal{D}
is given by (
3.59
), and the projector
\mathcal{P}
has a Lipschitz constant bounded by
\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{P})\leq 2
.

From
(
Cohen et al. 2011
, Theorem 1.3)
(see also
(
Schwab & Zech 2019
, Example 2.2)
for a more detailed discussion relevant to the present setting), it follows that the mapping

\mathcal{F}:[-1,1]^{\mathbb{N}}\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),\quad Y\mapsto\mathcal{G}(a({\,\cdot\,};Y)),

is
(\bm{b},\epsilon,\kappa)
-holomorphic according to our Definition
3.32
, with
\bm{b}
defined by (
4.24
), provided that
\epsilon<1-\|\bm{b}\|_{\ell^{1}}
, and where
\kappa:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{Z}^{d}
denotes the enumeration of the standard Fourier basis (cp. appendix
A
). Following the discussion in section
3.6.2
, such
\mathcal{F}
can be efficiently approximated by neural networks. In particular, we can directly apply Theorem
3.71
, together with the observation that
\|\mathcal{P}\|=\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{P})\leq 2
is bounded independently of
m
and
p
, to conclude the following bound on the approximation error (
3.2
),

Proposition 4.18
.

Let the operator
\mathcal{G}
be defined as mapping the coefficient
a
to the solution
u
of the elliptic PDE (
4.18
) and the measure
\mu
be the law of the random field (
4.22
). Let the encoder
\mathcal{E}:C(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
be given by (
3.54
) and the decoder
\mathcal{D}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
be given by (
3.59
). Let the reconstruction/projection pair
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{P})
be given as in Proposition
4.17
for given
p\in\mathbb{N}
. Then for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a constant
C>0
, depending on
k
, but independent of the trunk net size
p
and number of sensors
m
, such that for any
m,\,p\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists an approximator network
\mathcal{A}
, with

(4.27)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathrm{size}(\mathcal{A})&\leq C\left(1+pm\log(m)\log\log(m)\right),\\
\mathrm{depth}(\mathcal{A})&\leq C\left(1+\log(m)\log\log(m)\right)\end{aligned}

and approximation error

(4.28)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{A}}\leq Cm^{-k}.

4.2.5.
Bounds on the DeepONet approximation error (
2.11
)

Finally, combining the results of Propositions
4.15
,
4.17
,
4.18
, we conclude that

Theorem 4.19
.

For any
k,r\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a constant
C>0
, such that for any
m,p\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a DeepONet
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{E}
with
m
sensors, a trunk net
\bm{\tau}=(0,\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{p})
with
p
outputs and branch net
\bm{\beta}=(0,\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{p})
, such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{size}(\bm{\beta})\leq C(1+pm\log(m)\log\log(m)),

\displaystyle 0pt(\bm{\beta})\leq C(1+\log(m)\log\log(m)),

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{size}(\bm{\tau})\leq C(1+p\log(p)^{2})

\displaystyle 0pt(\bm{\tau})\leq C(1+\log(p)^{2})

such that the DeepONet approximation error (
2.11
) satisfies

(4.29)

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}\leq Ce^{-c\ell m^{\frac{1}{d}}}+Cm^{-k}+Cp^{-r}.

As the bound (
4.29
) is very similar to the bound (
4.16
), we can directly apply the discussion in Remark
4.11
to derive the following complexity estimate

(4.30)

\mathrm{size}(\bm{\tau},\bm{\beta})\sim\epsilon^{-1/r}\left(\epsilon^{-2/r}+\epsilon^{-1/k}\log(\epsilon^{-1})\log\log(\epsilon^{-1})\right).

To derive (
4.30
), we choose
m\sim\epsilon^{-1/k}
sensors, and we assume that we are in the regime, where
e^{-c\ell m^{1/d}}\ll m^{-k}\sim\epsilon
. This clearly requires that
m\gg\ell^{-d}|\log(\epsilon)|^{d}
, i.e., that the
m
sensors resolve scales of length
\ell>0
in the
d
-dimensional domain
D=[0,2\pi]^{d}
. This is a reasonable assumption in low dimensions
d\in\{1,2,3\}
of the domain
D
, where this is practically feasible.

As the
k,r\in\mathbb{N}
were arbitrary, this shows that the required size only grows
sub-algebraically
with
\epsilon^{-1}\to\infty
. Thus from Definition
3.5
, we can conclude that there exists a DeepONet (
2.10
), which breaks the curse of dimensionality in approximating the nonlinear operator
\mathcal{G}
mapping the coefficient
a
to the solution
u
of the elliptic PDE (
4.18
).

Remark 4.20
.

We emphasize that the above operator provides a mapping
\mathcal{G}:C(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
in
low spatial dimensions
d\in\{1,2,3\}
, in which case we show that
\mathcal{G}
can be efficiently approximated by a DeepONet
\mathcal{N}
. Thus, the “curse of dimensionality” here refers to the approximation problem for the high-dimensional (in fact,
\infty
-dimensional) input-to-output mapping
\mathcal{G}
, in accordance with our Definition
3.5
(cp. also Remark
3.4
). Whether DeepONets also provide an efficient approximation for similar operators with large
spatial dimension
d\gg 1
will be the subject of future research.

4.3.
Nonlinear parabolic PDE: A reaction-diffusion equation.

As a prototype for nonlinear reaction-diffusion parabolic type PDEs, we consider the following version of the well-known Allen-Cahn equation which arises in the study of phase transitions in materials such as alloys,

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{gathered}\frac{\partial v}{\partial t}=\Delta v+f(v),\\
v(t=0)=u,\end{gathered}\right.

where the non-linearity is given by
f(v)=v-v^{3}
. For the sake of simplicity, the Allen-Cahn PDE (
4.3
) is supplemented with periodic boundary conditions on the space-time domain
[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
. For initial data
u=u(x)
drawn from a probability measure
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
, our aim in this section will be to consider the DeepONet approximation of the data-to-solution mapping
\mathcal{G}:L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,
u\mapsto\mathcal{G}(u):=v(t=T)
, where
v
solves (
4.3
).

As a first step, we need to show that the operator
\mathcal{G}
is well-defined. To this end, we recall some well-known existence and boundedness results for the Allen-Cahn equation (
4.3
),

Theorem 4.21

(see e.g.
(
Yang et al. 2018
, Cor. 1)
)
.

Let
v(x,t)
solve (
4.3
), with initial data
u
. If the initial data satisfies
\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq 1
, then the solution of (
4.3
) satisfies
\|v(t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq 1
for all
t\in[0,T]
.

Note that the Allen-Cahn equation (
4.3
) is the
L^{2}
-gradient flow of a Ginzburg-Landau energy functional with a double-well potential. Normalizing the wells, it makes sense to consider the initial data such that
-1\leq u(x)\leq 1
and the above theorem guarantees that the maximum principle holds. Using standard parabolic regularity theory, in appendix
E.11
, we prove the following regularity result for the Allen-Cahn equation,

Theorem 4.22
.

There exists an increasing function
\eta:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
,
s\mapsto\eta(s)
, with the following property: If
u\in C^{4,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,
\alpha\in(0,1)
, is initial data for the Allen-Cahn equation (
4.3
) with
\alpha
-Hölder continuous 4th derivatives, and such that
\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq 1
, then the solution
v
of (
4.3
) has Hölder continuous partial derivatives

\frac{\partial^{k+\ell}v}{\partial t^{k}\partial x_{i_{1}}\dots\partial x_{i_{\ell}}},\quad\forall\,i_{1},\dots,i_{\ell}\in\{1,\dots,d\},\;2k+\ell\leq 4,

and
\|v\|_{C^{(2,4)}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}
defined by

(4.33)

\displaystyle\|v\|_{C^{(2,4)}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}:=\max_{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}\|v({\,\cdot\,},x)\|_{C^{2}([0,T])}+\max_{t\in[0,T]}\|v(t,{\,\cdot\,})\|_{C^{4}(\mathbb{T}^{d})},

can be bounded from above

(4.34)

\displaystyle\|v\|_{C^{(2,4)}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leq\eta\left(\|u\|_{C^{4,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\right).

As a corollary of the above theorems, we can show in Appendix
E.12
, the following Lipschitz continuity of the solution mapping at time
T
:
u\mapsto v(T)
.

Corollary 4.23
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Let
u,u^{\prime}\in C^{4,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
be such that
\|u\|_{L^{\infty}},\|u^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 1
. Let
v,v^{\prime}\in C^{(2,4)}(\mathbb{T})
denote the solution of (
4.3
) with initial data
u,u^{\prime}
, respectively. There exists a constant
C=C(T)>0
, such that

\|v(T)-v^{\prime}(T)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leq C\|u-u^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}.

It follows from Corollary
4.23
that the mapping
C^{4,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\cap B^{\infty}_{1}\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,
u\mapsto v(T)
, where
B^{\infty}_{1}={\left\{u\in C^{4,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\,\middle|\,\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 1\right\}}
admits a unique Lipschitz continuous extension

\mathcal{G}:L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\cap B^{\infty}_{1}\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),\quad u\mapsto\mathcal{G}(u)=v(T),

with
\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{G})\leq C=e^{4T}
. In the following, we will discuss the approximation of this mapping
\mathcal{G}
by DeepONets (
2.10
). To this end, we assume that the underlying measure
\mu
satisfies

(4.35)

\displaystyle\mathrm{supp}(\mu)\subset{\left\{u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\,\middle|\,\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 1\right\}}\subset L^{2}_{x}\cap L^{\infty}_{x}.

Motivated by the a priori estimates of Theorems
4.21
and
4.22
, we shall assume that the initial measure
\mu
is the law of a random field
u
of the form

(4.36)

\displaystyle u(x)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\alpha_{k}Y_{k}\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{k}(x),

in terms of the Fourier basis
\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{k}
(cf. Appendix
A
) and with
Y_{k}\in[-1,1]
are iid random variables. To ensure sufficient smoothness of the solutions, we further assume that

(4.37)

\displaystyle|\alpha_{k}|\leq C\exp\left(-\ell|k|_{\infty}\right),\quad\forall\,k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},

for some
C>0
, and length scale
\ell>0
; Note that (
4.37
) in particular implies that
u\in C^{4,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
for some
\alpha>0
. Furthermore, we shall assume that

(4.38)

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|\alpha_{k}|\leq 1,

so that
|u(x)|\leq 1
for all
x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
.

4.3.1.
Bounds on the encoding error (
3.1
)

With measure
\mu
defined by (
4.36
), we can readily apply Theorem
3.28
to obtain the following bounds on the encoding error:

Proposition 4.24
.

Let
d\in\{2,3\}
. Let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
denote the law of the random field (
4.36
) with coefficients
\alpha_{k}
satisfying the decay and boundedness assumptions (
4.37
), (
4.38
). For
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
x_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,m=(2N+1)^{d}
be an enumeration of the grid points of a regular cartesian grid on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
. Define the encoder
\mathcal{E}:C(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
by

\mathcal{E}(u)=(u(x_{1}),\dots,u(x_{m})),

and define the corresponding decoder
\mathcal{D}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
by Fourier interpolation onto Fourier modes
|k|_{\infty}\leq N
. Then the encoding error for the encoder/decoder pair
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D})
can be bounded by

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}\leq C\exp(-c\ell m^{1/d}),

for some constants
C,c>0
, independent of
N
.

4.3.2.
Bounds on the reconstruction error (
3.3
)

To bound the reconstruction error, we recall that for
u\in C^{4,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, we have
\mathcal{G}(u)=v(T)\in C^{4}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, by Theorem
4.22
, with

\|\mathcal{G}(u)\|_{C^{4}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leq\eta\left(\|u\|_{C^{4,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\right)\leq\eta\left(\sup_{u\in\mathrm{supp}(\mu)}\|u\|_{C^{4,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\right)<\infty,

uniformly bounded from above. It follows that
\|\mathcal{G}(u)\|_{H^{4}}\leq M
is uniformly bounded, and by (
3.35
), the reconstruction error for the Fourier reconstructor/projector pair onto the standard Fourier basis
\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{1},\dots,\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{p}
, satisfies

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{Fourier}}}\leq Cp^{-4/d}.

From Lemma
3.17
, we obtain:

Proposition 4.25
.

There exists a constant
C>0
, independent of
p
, such that for any
p\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a trunk net
\bm{\tau}:U=\mathbb{T}^{d}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
, with

\begin{gathered}\mathrm{size}(\bm{\tau})\leq Cp(1+\log(p)^{2}),\\
0pt(\bm{\tau})\leq C(1+\log(p)^{2}),\end{gathered}

and such that the reconstruction error for
\mathcal{R}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,
\mathcal{R}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{p})=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\alpha_{k}\tau_{k}
, can be bounded by

(4.39)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}\leq Cp^{-4/d}.

Furthermore, we have
\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{R}),\,\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{P})\leq 2
, where
\mathcal{P}:L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{n})\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
denotes the optimal projection (
3.20
) associated with
\mathcal{R}
.

4.3.3.
Bounds on the approximation error (
3.2
)

For the last two concrete examples, bounds on the approximation error (
3.2
) leveraged the fact that the underlying operator
\mathcal{G}
was
holomorphic
in an appropriate sense. However, it is unclear if the operator
\mathcal{G}
for the Allen-Cahn equation (
4.3
) is holomorphic. In fact, we have only provided that it is Lipschitz continuous. Hence, the approximator network
\mathcal{A}\approx\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
approximates a
m
-dimensional Lipschitz function. General neural network approximation results for such mappings
Yarotsky 2017
imply that for a fixed
M>0
, there exists a neural network
\mathcal{A}
with
\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{O}(p\epsilon^{-1/m})
, such that
\|\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}\|_{L^{\infty}([-M,M]^{m})}\leq\epsilon
.

Recall that the total error (
2.11
) for the DeepONet is given by (
3.4
) with
\alpha=1
as
\mathcal{G}
is Lipschitz. In this case from Proposition
4.24
, we have that

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}\lesssim\exp(-cm),

we require at least
m\sim\log(\epsilon^{-1})
sensors to achieve an encoding error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}\leq\epsilon
. Therefore, the general results of
Yarotsky 2017
would suggest that the required size of the approximator network
\mathcal{A}
scales at best like
\epsilon^{-|\log(\epsilon)|}
, where we note that the exponent
|\log(\epsilon)|\to\infty
as
\epsilon\to 0
. Hence, by our Definition
3.5
, such a DeepONet would incur the curse of dimensionality.

Is it possible for us to break this curse of dimensionality for the Allen-Cahn equation? It turns out that the approach of a recent paper
DeRyck & Mishra 2021
might suggest a way around the obstacle discussed above. Following
DeRyck & Mishra 2021
, we will leverage the fact that neural networks can
emulate
conventional numerical methods for approximating a PDE. To this end, we will consider the following finite difference scheme:

4.3.4.
A convergence finite difference scheme for the Allen-Cahn equation.

In
Tang & Yang 2016
, it has been shown that an implicit-explicit finite difference scheme of the following form

\frac{U^{n+1}-U^{n}}{\Delta t}=D_{\Delta x}U^{n}+f(U^{n}),

converges to the exact solution as
\Delta t,\Delta h\to 0
. Here
U^{n}=(U^{n}_{1},\dots,U^{n}_{m})
,
m\sim(\Delta x)^{-d}
are approximate values of the solution at time
t=t_{n}
, i.e.
U^{n}_{i}\approx u(x_{i},t_{n})
, with
x_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,m=(2N+1)^{d}
an enumeration of a cartesian grid with grid size
\Delta x
on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
. The values of
U^{0}
at
t=0
are initialized as

U^{0}_{i}:=u(x_{i}).

The evaluation of the nonlinearity is carried out pointwise,
f(U^{n})=(f(U^{n}_{1}),\dots,f(U^{n}_{m}))
.
D_{\Delta x}
denotes the discrete matrix of the Laplace operator, whose one-dimensional analogue in the presence of periodic boundary conditions is given by

\Lambda_{\Delta x}=\frac{1}{\Delta x^{2}}\begin{pmatrix}-2&1&&&1\\
1&-2&1&&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&1&-2&1\\
1&&&1&-2\end{pmatrix}_{N\times N}

For
d=2
dimensions, we can write

D_{\Delta x}=\Lambda_{\Delta x}\otimes I+I\otimes\Lambda_{\Delta x},

where
I
is the
m\times m
unit-matrix
\otimes
denotes the Kronecker product. For
d=3
, we have

D_{\Delta x}=\Lambda_{\Delta x}\otimes I\otimes I+I\otimes\Lambda_{\Delta x}\otimes I+I\otimes I\otimes\Lambda_{\Delta x}.

For our purposes, we simply note that the update rule of the numerical scheme of
Tang & Yang 2016
can be written in the form

(4.40)

\displaystyle U^{n+1}=R_{\Delta t,\Delta x}\left(U^{n}+\Delta tf(U^{n})\right),\quad U^{0}_{i}=u(x_{i}),\;i=1,\dots,m.

where
R_{\Delta t,\Delta x}=(I-\Delta tD_{\Delta x})^{-1}
is a
m\times m
matrix. We furthermore note the following result of
Tang & Yang 2016
:

Theorem 4.26

(
(
Tang & Yang 2016
, Thm. 2.1)
)
.

Consider the Allen-Cahn problem (
4.3
) with periodic boundary conditions. If the initial value is bounded by
1
, i.e.
\max_{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}|u(x)|\leq 1
, then the numerical solution of the fully discrete scheme (
4.40
) is also bounded by
1
in the sense that
\|U^{n}\|_{\ell^{\infty}}\leq 1
for all
n>0
, provided that the stepsize satisfies
0<\Delta t\leq\frac{1}{2}
.

We also prove in Appendix
E.11
, the following convergence result for the scheme (
4.40
),

Theorem 4.27
.

Consider the Allen-Cahn problem (
4.3
) with periodic boundary conditions. Assume that the solution
v\in C^{(2,4)}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
, there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
v
,
\Delta t
and
\Delta x
, such that the error
E^{n}_{i}:=|U^{n}_{i}-v(x_{i},t_{n})|
,
i=1,\dots,m
is bounded by

\|E^{n}\|_{\ell^{\infty}}\leq(\Delta t+\Delta x^{2})\exp\left({Ct_{n}\|v\|_{C^{(2,4)}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}}\right).

Next, we proceed to bound the approximation error (
3.2
). To provide an upper bound on the size of the approximator network
\mathcal{A}
, we next show that the numerical scheme (
4.40
) can be efficiently approximated by a suitable neural network. By “efficient”, we imply that the required size of the neural network
\mathcal{A}
increases at most polynomially with the number of sensor points
m
, rather than exponentially. We begin with the following observation, proved in Appendix
E.12
,

Lemma 4.28
.

Let
f(v)=v-v^{3}
be the nonlinearity in the Allen-Cahn equation (
4.3
). There exist constants
C,M>0
, such that for any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, there exists a ReLU neural network
g_{\epsilon}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\xi\mapsto g_{\epsilon}(\xi)
with
\mathrm{size}(g_{\epsilon})\leq C(1+|\log(\epsilon)|)
,
0pt(g_{\epsilon})\leq C(1+|\log(\epsilon)|)
, and such that

\sup_{\eta\in[-1,1]}|f(\eta)-g_{\epsilon}(\eta)|<\epsilon,

and
\mathrm{Lip}(g_{\epsilon})\leq M
.

Thus, the above lemma shows that there exists a small neural network with ReLU activation function that approximates the nonlinearity in the Allen-Cahn equations to high accuracy. In fact, if we use a smooth, i.e.
C^{3}
activation function, one can see from Remark
E.4
in Appendix
E.12
that an even smaller network (with size that does not need to increase with increasing accuracy) suffices to represent this nonlinearity accurately. However, we stick to ReLU activation functions here for definiteness.
Given
\epsilon>0
, let
g_{\epsilon}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a ReLU neural network as in Lemma
4.28
. It is now clear that if
g_{\epsilon}(\eta)
is represented by a (small) neural network, then there exists a larger neural network
\mathcal{N}
, such that

\mathcal{N}(\widetilde{U}^{0})=\widetilde{U}^{n},

where
\widetilde{U}^{k}
,
k=1,\dots,n
is determined by the recursion relation

(4.41)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \widetilde{U}^{k+1}&=R_{\Delta x,\Delta t}\left(\widetilde{U}^{k}+\Delta tg_{\epsilon}(\widetilde{U}^{k})\right)\\
\widetilde{U}^{0}&\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.\end{aligned}\right.

More precisely, we have the following lemma, proved in Appendix
E.13
, for the
emulation
of the finite difference scheme (
4.41
) with a suitable ReLU neural network,

Lemma 4.29
.

There exists a constant
C>0
, such that for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\epsilon>0
, there exists a neural network
\mathcal{N}
with
\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{N})\leq Cn(m^{2}+m|\log(\epsilon)|)
, and
0pt(\mathcal{N})\leq C(1+n|\log\epsilon|)
, such that

\mathcal{N}(\widetilde{U}^{0})=\widetilde{U}^{n}
, maps any initial data
\widetilde{U}^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
to the solution
\widetilde{U}^{n}
of the recursion (
4.41
).

Finally, we estimate the
error
i.e. difference between the result
U^{n}
of the exact update rule for the numerical scheme (
4.40
), and the approximate neural network version (
4.41
)
\widetilde{U}^{n}
in the following Lemma (proved in Appendix
E.14
).

Lemma 4.30
.

Let
U^{n}
,
\widetilde{U}^{n}
be obtained by (
4.40
) and (
4.41
), respectively, with initial data
U^{0}=\widetilde{U}^{0}
such that
\|U^{0}\|_{\ell^{\infty}}\leq 1
, and
\mathrm{Lip}(g_{\epsilon})\leq M
. Then

\|U^{n}-\widetilde{U}^{n}\|_{\ell^{\infty}}\leq Te^{MT}\epsilon,

where
T=n\,\Delta t
.

After having emulated the finite difference scheme (
4.40
) with a ReLU neural network and estimated the error in doing so, we are in a position to state the bounds on the approximation error in the following proposition, proved in Appendix
E.15
,

Proposition 4.31
.

Let
\mu
be given as the law of (
4.36
).
Let the encoder/decoder pair
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{D}
be given as in Proposition
4.24
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\mathcal{R}
be given as in Proposition
4.25
, for
p\in\mathbb{N}
. There exists a constant
C>0
, independent of
m
and
p
, such that there exists a neural network
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
, such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{A})\leq C(1+m^{2+2/d}+mp),\quad 0pt(\mathcal{A})\leq C(1+m^{2/d}\log(m)),

and

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{A}}\leq Cm^{-1/d}.

Remark 4.32
.

Choosing the number of trunk nets
p\lesssim m^{1+2/d}
, it follows from the previous proposition that an approximation error
\mathcal{A}
of order
\epsilon
can be achieved, with
m\sim\epsilon^{-d}
, i.e. a neural network of size

\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{-2(d+1)}),\quad 0pt(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{-2}|\log(\epsilon)|).

This is polynomial in
\epsilon^{-1}
(recall that
d=2,3
is a fixed constant independent of the accuracy
\epsilon
), and hence does not suffer from the curse of dimensionality for the infinite-dimensional approximation problem (cp. Definition
3.5
). We would nevertheless expect that sharper error estimates could be obtained by basing the neural network emulation result on higher-order finite difference methods. In the present work, our main objective is to show that neural networks can break the curse of dimensionality, rather than attempting to establish optimal complexity bounds.

4.3.5.
Bounds on the DeepONet approximation error (
2.11
)

Combining Propositions
4.24
,
4.25
,
4.31
, we can now state the following theorem

Theorem 4.33
.

Consider the DeepONet approximation problem for the Allen-Cahn equation (
4.3
), where the initial data
u
is distributed according to a probability measure
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
is the law of the random field (
4.36
). There exist constants
C,\,c>0
, such that for any
m,\,p\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a DeepONet (
2.10
) with trunk net
\bm{\tau}
and branch net
\bm{\beta}
, such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{size}(\bm{\tau})\leq C(1+p\log(p)^{2}),\quad 0pt(\bm{\tau})\leq C(1+\log(p)^{2}),

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{size}(\bm{\beta})\leq C(1+m^{2+2/d}+pm),

\displaystyle 0pt(\bm{\beta})\leq C(1+\log(m)\log\log(m)),

and such that the DeepONet approximation (
2.11
) is bounded by

(4.42)

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}\leq C\exp(-c\ell m^{1/d})+Cp^{-4/d}+Cm^{-1/d}.

Remark 4.34
.

The bound (
4.42
) guarantees that for
\epsilon>0
, a DeepONet approximation error of
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}\sim\epsilon
can be achieved provided that
m\gtrsim\max(\ell^{-d}\log(\epsilon^{-1})^{d},\epsilon^{-d})
and
p\gtrsim\epsilon^{-d/4}
. Assuming that
m^{1/d}\ell\gg 1
is satisfied, i.e., that the sensors can resolve the typical correlation length scale
\ell
in the
d
-dimensional domain
D=\mathbb{T}^{d}
, this can be ensured provided that
m\gtrsim\epsilon^{-d}
and
p\gtrsim\epsilon^{-d/4}
.
In this case, an error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}\lesssim\epsilon
can thus be achieved with an overall DeepONet size of order

(4.43)

\mathrm{size}(\bm{\tau},\bm{\beta})\lesssim\epsilon^{-d/4}\log(\epsilon^{-1})^{2}+\epsilon^{-2(d+1)}\lesssim\epsilon^{-2(d+1)}.

For the cases of interest,
d=2,3
, this upper bound on the required size of the DeepONet thus scales as
\mathrm{size}\lesssim\epsilon^{-6}
, and
\mathrm{size}\lesssim\epsilon^{-8}
, respectively. This shows that the required size scales at worst algebraically in
\epsilon^{-1}
, thus
breaking the curse of dimensionality
, as per Definition
3.5
. As already pointed out in Remark
4.32
, the explicit exponents
-6
and
-8
may be considerably improved if the emulation result were based on a
higher-order order
numerical scheme in place of the low-order finite difference scheme (
4.40
).

4.4.
Nonlinear hyperbolic PDE: Scalar conservation laws.

As a final concrete example in this paper, we will consider this prototypical example of nonlinear hyperbolic PDEs. We remark that the image of the underlying operator
\mathcal{G}
in the previous three examples consisted of smooth functions. On the other hand, it is well known that solutions of conservation laws can be discontinuous, on account of the formation of shock waves. Thus, one of our objectives in this section is to show that DeepONets can even approximate nonlinear operators, that map into discontinuous functions, efficiently.

4.4.1.
Problem formulation.

We consider a scalar conservation law on a one-dimensional domain
D\subset\mathbb{R}
:

(4.44)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}v+\partial_{x}(f(v))=0,\\
v(t=0)=u,\end{aligned}\right.

with initial data
u
drawn from some underlying measure
\mu
, and with a given flux function
f\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. For simplicity, we will assume that
D=\mathbb{T}=[0,2\pi]
, and periodic boundary conditions for (
4.44
). Our results are however readily generalized to other boundary conditions and to several space dimensions. Solutions of (
4.44
) are interpreted in the weak sense, imposing the entropy conditions

\partial_{t}\eta(v)+\partial_{x}q(v)\leq 0,\quad\text{(in the distributional sense)}

for all entropy/entropy flux pairs
(\eta,q)
, consisting of a convex
C^{1}
-function
\eta:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and the corresponding flux
q:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
with derivative satisfying
q^{\prime}(u)=f^{\prime}(u)\eta^{\prime}(u)
for all
u\in\mathbb{R}
.

Under these conditions, it is well known
Godlewski & Raviart 1991
that the solution
v
of (
4.44
) is unique for any initial data
u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
, and the solution operator
\mathcal{S}_{t}
is contractive as a function
L^{1}(\mathbb{T})\to L^{1}(\mathbb{T})
for any
t\in[0,\infty)
:

(4.45)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{S}_{t}(u)-\mathcal{S}_{t}(u^{\prime})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T})}\leq\|u-u^{\prime}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T})},\quad\forall\,u,u^{\prime}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}).

We note that
\mathcal{S}_{t}:\mathrm{BV}(\mathbb{T})\to\mathrm{BV}(\mathbb{T})
, maps functions of bounded variation to functions of bounded variation, in fact we have

(4.46)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{S}_{t}(u)\|_{\mathrm{BV}}\leq\|u\|_{\mathrm{BV}}.

Here, we denote
\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{\mathrm{BV}}=\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{L^{1}}+\mathrm{TV}({\,\cdot\,})
, as the BV-norm with
\mathrm{TV}(w)
representing the total variation of a function
w

Godlewski & Raviart 1991
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{S}_{t}(u)
satisfies the maximum principle, so that

\|\mathcal{S}_{t}(u)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\leq\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}.

Next, in order to specify the DeepONet approximation problem (cf. Definition
2.1
), we take the nonlinear operator
\mathcal{G}:L^{1}(D)\to L^{1}(D)
as
\mathcal{G}(u):=\mathcal{S}_{T}(u)
, mapping the initial data
u
of the scalar conservation law (
4.44
) to the solution
\mathcal{S}_{T}(u)=v({\,\cdot\,},t=T)
at the final time
T>0
. Clearly, given the
L^{1}
-contractivity (
4.45
), the operator
\mathcal{G}
is well-defined and Lipschitz continuous.

Defining the set

(4.47)

\displaystyle\mathrm{BV}_{M}:={\left\{u\in\mathrm{BV}(\mathbb{T})\,\middle|\,\|u\|_{\mathrm{BV}}\leq M\right\}},

we will consider any measure
\mu
that satisfies
\mu(\mathrm{BV}_{M})=1
, as the underlying measure for the DeepONet approximation problem.

4.4.2.
DeepONet approximation in the Banach space
L^{1}(D)

So far, we have only considered the DeepONet approximation of operators, defined on the Hilbert spaces. However, given the fact that the solution operator
\mathcal{S}_{t}
and the resulting operator
\mathcal{G}
are contractive on the Banach space
L^{1}(D)
, it is very natural to consider the DeepONet approximation problem in this function space. Thus, we need to modify the definition of the DeepONet error (
2.11
) and define its
L^{1}
-version by,

(4.48)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{L^{1}}=\int_{L^{1}(D)}\|\mathcal{G}(u)-\mathcal{N}(u)\|_{L^{1}}\,d\mu(u),

with the DeepONet
\mathcal{N}
(
2.10
) approximating the operator
\mathcal{G}
. It is of the form,

(4.49)

\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{A}\circ\overline{\mathcal{E}},

with
\mathcal{R}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to C(D)
is the usual affine reconstruction defined in (
2.9
) based on the trunk network
\bm{\tau}
,
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
is the approximator neural network used to define the branch network
\bm{\beta}
, and we have introduced a generalized encoder
\overline{\mathcal{E}}:L^{1}(D)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
, which is defined by taking local averages in cells
C_{1},\dots,C_{m}\subset D
:

(4.50)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{E}}(u)=\left(\fint_{C_{1}}u(x)\,dx,\dots,\fint_{C_{m}}u(x)\,dx\right),

with cells
C_{1},\dots,C_{m}
given by

(4.51)

\displaystyle C_{j}:=[x_{j}-\Delta x/2,x_{j}+\Delta x/2],\quad(\text{for }j=1,\dots,m),

where
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
denote
m
equidistant sensors on the periodic domain
D=\mathbb{T}
, with
\Delta x=2\pi/m
. Note that the encoder
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
is well-defined (and in fact continuous) for any
u\in L^{1}(D)
. In particular, this encoding allows us to consider discontinuous initial data
u
, and thus we do not need to assume that the underlying measure
\mu
is concentrated on
C(D)
. This choice of encoder constitutes a key difference with pointwise encoder
\mathcal{E}
, considered in section
2
and all the previous examples.

Given this architecture for the DeepONet (
4.49
), we aim to bound the resulting approximation error (
4.48
). As we are no longer in the Hilbert space setting, we cannot directly appeal to the abstract error estimates of section
3
, nor follow the program outlined at the beginning of this section. Nevertheless, we will still follow a key idea from the last subsection, namely emulating numerical schemes approximating the underlying PDEs with neural networks. To this end, we recall a straightforward adaptation of the results of a recent paper
DeRyck & Mishra 2021
;

Theorem 4.35
.

Let
M>0
be given. Consider initial data
u\in\mathrm{BV}_{M}
for the scalar conservation law (
4.44
) with flux function
f\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Let the operator
\mathcal{G}(u)=\mathcal{S}_{T}(u)
be given by mapping
u\mapsto v(T,{\,\cdot\,})
, where
v
solves (
4.44
). There exists a constant
C=C(T,\|f\|_{C^{2}})>0
, such that for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a neural network
\mathcal{N}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
with

\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{N})\leq Cm^{5/2},\quad 0pt(\mathcal{N})\leq Cm,

such that

\left\|\mathcal{G}(u)-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{N}_{j}(\overline{\mathcal{E}}(u))\,1_{C_{i}}({\,\cdot\,})\right\|_{L^{1}(D)}\leq\frac{C}{m^{\alpha}}.

Here
\alpha>0
is the convergence rate of the well-known Lax-Friedrichs scheme
Godlewski & Raviart 1991
and
1_{C_{j}}({\,\cdot\,})
denotes the indicator function of the cell
C_{i}
(cp. (
4.51
)). For the neural network
\mathcal{N}
we furthermore have

\|\mathcal{N}(\overline{\mathcal{E}}(u))\|_{\ell^{\infty}}\leq M,\quad\text{ for all $u\in\mathrm{BV}_{M}$.}

Remark 4.36
.

The worst-case estimate for the convergence rate
\alpha
of the Lax-Friedrichs scheme guarantees that
\alpha\geq\frac{1}{2}

Godlewski & Raviart 1991
. However, in practice, the observed convergence rate is often higher,
\alpha\approx 1
.

The proof of Theorem
4.35
is based on the fact that neural networks can emulate the Lax-Friedrichs difference scheme for (
4.44
). In fact, the neural network
\mathcal{N}
constructed in
DeRyck & Mishra 2021
is an exact form of the Lax-Friedrichs scheme applied to a scalar conservation law (
4.44
), but with a neural network approximated flux
\widehat{f}\approx f
, and satisfying a suitable CFL condition. The bound on
\|\mathcal{N}(\overline{\mathcal{E}}(u))\|_{\ell^{\infty}}\leq M
therefore follows from the fact that
\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\|u\|_{\mathrm{BV}}\leq M
and the fact that the Lax-Friedrichs scheme satisfies the maximum principle.

To obtain a DeepONet approximation result from
4.35
, we have the following simple Lemma (proved in Appendix
E.16
), on the approximation of characteristic functions by ReLU neural networks,

Lemma 4.37
.

There exists a constant
C>0
, such that for any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
,
a<b
, and
\epsilon>0
, there exists a ReLU neural network
\chi_{[a,b]}^{\epsilon}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, with

\mathrm{size}(\chi^{\epsilon}_{[a,b]})\leq C,\quad 0pt(\chi^{\epsilon}_{[a,b]})=1,

and

\|\chi^{\epsilon}_{[a,b]}-1_{[a,b]}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\epsilon.

This allows us to prove in Appendix
E.17
, our main approximation result for the DeepONet approximation problem (
4.48
) for scalar conservation laws,

Theorem 4.38
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(L^{1}(D))
be a probability measure such that there exists
M>0
such that
\mu(\mathrm{BV}_{M})=1
. Let the underlying operator
\mathcal{G}
map the initial data
u
to the solution (at final time)
v(.,T)
of the scalar conservation law (
4.44
). Let the encoder
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
of the DeepONet (
4.49
) be given by (
4.50
) with
m
equidistant cells
C_{j}
(
4.51
). There exists a constant
C>0
, independent of
m
and
p
, such that for any
m,\,p\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a DeepONet
(\bm{\tau},\bm{\beta})
with trunk net
\bm{\tau}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to C(D)
, and branch net
\bm{\beta}:L^{1}(D)\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
, of the form
\bm{\beta}(u)=\mathcal{A}(\overline{\mathcal{E}}(u))
for a neural network
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
, with

\mathrm{size}(\bm{\beta})\leq Cm^{5/2},\quad 0pt(\bm{\beta})\leq Cm,

and

\mathrm{size}(\bm{\tau})\leq Cp,\quad 0pt(\bm{\tau})=C,

such that

(4.52)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{L^{1}}\leq C\max\left(1-\frac{p}{m},0\right)+Cm^{-\alpha},

with the DeepONet approximation error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{L^{1}}
defined in (
4.48
).

Remark 4.39
.

To achieve an error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{L^{1}}\sim\epsilon
, the estimate (
4.52
) provided by Theorem
4.38
shows that it is sufficient that
p\geq(1-\epsilon)m
, i.e.
p\sim m
, and
m\gtrsim\epsilon^{-1/\alpha}
. Thus, a DeepONet approximation error of order
\epsilon
can be achieved with a DeepONet
(\bm{\beta},\bm{\tau})
of size

(4.53)

\mathrm{size}(\bm{\beta})\sim\epsilon^{-5/2\alpha},\quad\mathrm{size}(\bm{\tau})\sim\epsilon^{-1/\alpha}.

For the worst-case rate of
\alpha=1/2
, this yields a total DeepONet size of order
\sim\epsilon^{-5}
. For more realistic values of
\alpha\approx 1
, we require a size of order
\sim\epsilon^{-2.5}
. Clearly, this scales polynomially in
\epsilon^{-1}
, and thus DeepONets can efficiently approximate the solution operator for scalar conservation laws
by breaking the curse of dimensionality
, see Definition
3.5
.

4.4.3.
A lower bound on the DeepONet approximation error

Theorem
4.38
shows that with the natural scaling ,
p=m
, we have
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{L^{1}}\leq Cp^{-\alpha}
, where
\alpha>0
is the convergence rate of the Lax-Friedrichs scheme, and
p\in\mathbb{N}
is the output dimension of the branch/trunk net
\bm{\beta},\,\bm{\tau}
. The goal of this section is to present an example of a measure
\mu\in\mathrm{BV}_{M}
, for which we also have a lower bound of the form
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{L^{1}}\gtrsim p^{-1}
and demonstrate that the DeepONet approximation, as described above,
is almost optimal
.

To this end, we will rely on the lower bound (
3.9
) for the (
L^{2}
-based) error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}\geq\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}\geq\sqrt{\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{k}}
, of Theorem
3.6
. Recall
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}
denotes the DeepONet error (
2.11
) in the
L^{2}
norm,
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}
denotes the reconstruction error (
3.3
) and
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots
denote the eigenvalues of the covariance operator
{\Gamma}_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}=\int(v-\mathbb{E}[v])\otimes(v-\mathbb{E}[v])\,d(\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu)(v)
, associated with the push-forward measure
\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu
.

To provide a concrete example which exhibits the decay
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{L^{1}}\lesssim Cp^{-1}
in terms of the output dimension
p
of the trunk net, we now consider the measure
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))
given as the law

(4.54)

\displaystyle\mu=\mathrm{law}{\left\{-\sin({\,\cdot\,}-\widehat{x})\,\middle|\,\widehat{x}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb{T})\right\}},

for the Burgers’ equation, i.e., scalar conservation law (
4.44
),

(4.55)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}v+\partial_{x}\left(v^{2}/2\right)&=0,\\
v(t=0)=u.\end{aligned}\right.

Let
v_{0}(x,t)
denote the solution of (
4.55
) with initial data

v_{0}(x,t=0)=u_{0}(x):=-\sin(x).

The characteristic starting at
x_{0}
for this data is given by

x(t;x_{0})=x_{0}-\sin(x_{0})t.

By the method of characteristics, the solution
v(x,t)
can be expressed in the form

v(x(t),t)=u_{0}(x_{0}),

for any
t\geq 0
which is sufficiently small such that the mapping
x_{0}\mapsto x(t;x_{0})
is one-to-one. Since

\frac{\partial x}{\partial x_{0}}=1-\cos(x_{0})t,

it follows that a classical solution exists for any
t<1
, but the characteristics cross at time
t=1
, corresponding to the formation of a stationary shock wave at
x_{0}=0
. The size of the jump of
v(x,t)
at
x=0
at the particular
t=\pi/2
is given by

v(0+,\pi/2)-v(0-,\pi/2)=2,

corresponding to the time
t
, at which the characteristics emanating from
x_{0}=\pm\pi/2
reach the stationary shock at the origin. Furthermore, the function
v(x,t)
is smooth on
\mathbb{T}\setminus\{0\}
. From basic properties of the Fourier coefficients of functions with jump-discontinuities, we can conclude

Lemma 4.40
.

The solution
v_{t}(x):=v(x,t)
at time
t=\pi/2
of the Burgers equation (
4.55
) with initial data
u_{0}(x)=-\sin(x)
, has Fourier coefficients with asymptotic decay

\widehat{v}_{t}(k)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}v_{t}(x)e^{-ikx}\,dx=\frac{-i}{\pi k}+o\left(\frac{1}{k}\right),\quad(k\to\infty).

Based on this lemma, we can now estimate the spectral decay of the covariance operator
\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}
of the push-forward measure
\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu
:

Lemma 4.41
.

Let
\mathcal{G}(u):=v_{t}
, where
v(x,t)=v_{t}(x)
is the solution of the inviscid Burgers equation with initial data
u
, evaluated at time
t=\pi/2
. Let
\mu
be given as the law (
4.54
). Then the eigenfunctions of
\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}
is given by the standard Fourier basis
\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, with eigenvalues

\lambda_{k}=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}k^{2}}+o\left(\frac{1}{k^{2}}\right),\quad(k\to\infty).

The proof of this lemma relies on the observation that
\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu
is a translation-invariant measure on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
, and hence, the integral kernel representing its covariance operator
\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}
is
stationary
. In particular, this implies that the eigenfunctions of
\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}
are given by the standard Fourier basis. Furthermore, the asymptotics of the eigenvalues
\lambda_{k}\propto k^{-2}
, as
k\to\infty
can in this case be determined explicitly, based on Lemma
4.40
. The details of the argument are provided in appendix
E.18
.

As a consequence of Lemma
4.41
, we can now state the following result:

Theorem 4.42
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))
be given by the law (
4.54
). Let
u\mapsto\mathcal{G}(u)
denote the operator, mapping initial data
u(x)
to the solution
v_{t}(x)=v(x,t)
at time
t=\pi/2
, where
v
solves the inviscid Burgers equation (
4.55
). Then there exists a universal constant
C>0
(depending only on
\mu
, but independent of the neural network architecture), such that the DeepONet approximation error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}
for any trunk net of size
p
is bounded from below by

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}\geq\frac{C}{\sqrt{p}}.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.6
, the DeepONet error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}
satisfies the lower bound

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}\geq\sqrt{\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{k}},

where
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots
denote the ordered (repeated) eigenvalues of the covariance operator
\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}
, corresponding to a complete orthonormal eigenbasis
\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\dots
of
\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}
. By Lemma
4.41
, the asymptotic decay of the eigenvalues can be estimated from below by
\lambda_{k}\geq\left(\frac{c}{k}\right)^{2}
, for a suitable constant
c>0
. It follows that

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}\geq\sqrt{\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{k}}\geq c\sqrt{\sum_{k>p}\frac{1}{k^{2}}}\geq\frac{C}{\sqrt{p}},

for some
C>0
, as claimed.
∎

Remark 4.43
.

Note that since
-\sin(x-\widehat{x})=\sin(\widehat{x})\cos(x)-\cos(\widehat{x})\sin(x)
, the probability measure
\mu
of Theorem
4.42
is supported on a (compact subset of a)
two-dimensional
subspace
\mathrm{span}(\cos(x),\sin(x))\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
. In particular, the spectrum of
\Gamma_{\mu}
decays
at an arbitrarily fast rate
, asymptotically (since almost all eigenvalues are zero), and the encoding error can be made to vanish,
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}=0
, with a suitable choice of only two sensors
x_{1},x_{2}
. Nevertheless, Theorem
4.42
shows that the push-forward measure
\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu
under the inviscid Burgers equation is sufficiently complex that the reconstruction error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}
cannot decay faster than
p^{-1/2}
in the dimension of the reconstruction space
p
. In particular, the fast spectral decay of
\mu
does not imply a similarly fast spectral decay of
\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu
under the inviscid Burgers dynamics.

We use the above result to claim that the
L^{1}
-error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{L^{1}}
satisfies the following lower bound:

Theorem 4.44
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{BV}_{M})
denote the probability measure (
4.54
). Let
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{L^{1}}
be the DeepONet approximation error given by (
4.48
). Let
\overline{M}>0
. Then there exists a constant
C=C(\overline{M})>0
, independent of
p
, such that

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{L^{1}}=\int_{\mathrm{BV}_{M}}\|\mathcal{G}(u)-\mathcal{N}(u)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T})}\,d\mu(u)\geq\frac{C}{p},

for any DeepONet
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{A}\circ\overline{\mathcal{E}}
, such that
\sup_{u\in\mathrm{supp}(\mu)}\|\mathcal{N}(u)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\overline{M}
.

Remark 4.45
.

Although we do not have a proof, it appears reasonable to conjecture that the
L^{1}
-optimal DeepONet approximations of
\mathcal{G}
satisfy a uniform bound of the form
\sup_{u\in\mathrm{supp}(\mu)}\|\mathcal{N}(u)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\leq\overline{M}
for some
\overline{M}>0
, independent of
p
. If this is indeed the case, then Theorem
4.44
is valid without the additional assumption on
\sup_{u\in\mathrm{supp}(\mu)}\|\mathcal{N}(u)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\overline{M}
.

Clearly, the DeepONet constructed in Theorem
4.38
belongs to the class of DeepONets, satsifying a bound
\sup_{u\in\mu}\|\mathcal{N}(u)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\leq\overline{M}
(in fact, with
\overline{M}=M
). Thus, we cannot expect to improve the upper bound (
4.52
) to a convergence rate
\alpha>1
.

Proof.

Let
X
denote the support of
\mu
, let
\mathcal{R}
be any reconstruction. Following the argument of Theorem
3.6
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{k}\leq(\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}})^{2}\leq\int_{X}\|\mathcal{G}(u)-\mathcal{N}(u)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\,d\mu(u).

By Theorem
4.42
, we have
\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{k}\geq Cp^{-1}
, for an absolute constant
C>0
. We now note the following interpolation inequality

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{G}(u)-\mathcal{N}(u)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{G}(u)-\mathcal{N}(u)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T})}\,\|\mathcal{G}(u)-\mathcal{N}(u)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}.

If
u\in\mathrm{BV}_{M}
, then
\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq M
and hence also
\|\mathcal{G}(u)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\leq M
. Furthermore, by assumption, we have
\|\mathcal{N}(u)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\leq\overline{M}
. Thus, we conclude that

\frac{C}{p}\leq(M+\overline{M})\int_{X}\|\mathcal{G}(u)-\mathcal{N}(u)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T})}\,d\mu(u),

for any such DeepONet
\mathcal{N}(u)
. This clearly implies that claimed lower bound.
∎

5.
On the generalization error for DeepONets

In section
3
and with the concrete examples in section
4
, we have shown that there exists a DeepONet
\mathcal{N}
of the form (
2.10
) which approximates an underlying operator
\mathcal{G}
efficiently, i.e., the DeepONet approximation error (
2.11
) can be made small without incurring the curse of dimensionality in terms of the complexity of the DeepONet (
2.10
). However, in practice, one needs to
train
the DeepONet (
2.10
) by using a gradient descent algorithm to find neural network parameters (weights and biases for the trunk and branch nets) that minimize the
loss function

(5.1)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{N}):=\int_{L^{2}(D)}\int_{U}\left|\mathcal{G}(u)(y)-\mathcal{N}(u)(y)\right|^{2}\,dy\,d\mu(u),

which is related to the approximation error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}
by
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}=\sqrt{\widehat{\mathcal{L}}}
(cf. (
2.11
)).

However the loss function
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}
cannot be computed exactly, and is usually approximated by sampling in both the target space
y\in U
and the input function space
u\in L^{2}
. As is standard in deep learning
Goodfellow et al. 2016
, one can follow
Lu et al. 2019
and take
N_{u}
iid samples
U_{1},\dots,U_{N_{u}}\sim\mu
(with underlying measure
\mu
for the DeepONet approximation problem), and for each sample
U_{j}
to evaluate
\mathcal{G}(U_{j}):U\to\mathbb{R}
at
N_{y}
points
Y_{j}^{1},\dots,Y_{j}^{N_{y}}
with corresponding weights
w_{j}^{k}>0
, leading to the following
empirical loss

\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{N_{u},N_{y}}\approx\widehat{\mathcal{L}}
, for the DeepONet
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{E}
approximation of
\mathcal{G}
:

(5.2)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{N_{u},N_{y}}(\mathcal{N}):=\frac{1}{N_{u}}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{u}}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{y}}w_{k}^{j}\left|\mathcal{G}(U_{j})(Y^{j}_{k})-\mathcal{N}(U_{j})(Y^{j}_{k})\right|^{2}.

If we denote
\Delta(u,y):=|\mathcal{G}(u)(y)-\mathcal{N}(u)(y)|^{2}
, then (
5.2
) can be written in the form

(5.3)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{N_{u},N_{y}}(\mathcal{N})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{u}}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{u}}I^{N_{y}}_{j}(\Delta(U_{j},{\,\cdot\,})),

(5.4)

\displaystyle I^{N_{y}}_{j}(\Delta(U_{j},{\,\cdot\,})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{N_{y}}w_{k}^{j}\Delta(U_{j},Y^{k}_{j}).

So far, we have not specified how the
Y_{j}^{k}
,
k=1,\dots,N_{y}
,
j=1,\dots,N_{u}
are to be chosen. As we want the innermost sum to be an approximation

I_{j}^{N_{y}}(\Delta(U_{j},{\,\cdot\,}))\approx\int_{U}\Delta(U_{j},y)\,dy,

we propose two intuitive options:

(1)

Choose
Y_{j}^{k}
to be random variables, independent of all
U_{\widetilde{j}}
, and drawn iid uniform on
U
, i.e.

(5.5)

\displaystyle Y_{j}^{k}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(U)\text{ iid},\quad w_{j}^{k}=\frac{|U|}{N_{y}},

for
j=1,\dots,N_{u}
,
k=1,\dots,N_{y}
.

(2)

Let
y_{k}\in U
,
w_{k}>0
, for
k=1,\dots,N_{y}
, be the evaluation points and weights of a suitable quadrature rule on
U
. Then choose

(5.6)

\displaystyle Y_{j}^{k}=y_{k},\quad w_{j}^{k}=w_{k}\quad\text{for }k=1,\dots,N_{y}.

5.1.
Deterministic choice of
Y^{j}_{k}
.

If the
\mathcal{G}(u)
and
\mathcal{N}(u)
have bounded
k
-th derivatives in
y
, uniformly for all
u\in\mathrm{supp}(\mu)
, then a suitable choice of the quadrature points and weights
y_{k}
,
w_{k}
in (
5.6
) can lead to a quadrature error

(5.7)

\displaystyle\left|I^{N_{y}}(\Delta(U_{j},{\,\cdot\,}))-\int_{U}\Delta(U_{j},y)\,dy\right|\leq C\|\Delta(U_{j},{\,\cdot\,})\|_{C^{k}(U)}\,N_{y}^{-k/n}.

Thus, for small dimensions (e.g.
n=1,2,3
), and sufficiently regular
\mathcal{G}(u)
, one might expect a deterministic quadrature rule to significantly outperform a random sampling in
y
. Note that if
y\mapsto\Delta(U,y)
possesses a complex analytic extension to a neighbourhood of
U
for suitable
U
and quadrature (e.g.
U
is a torus, and the quadrature is given by the trapezoidal rule), then the error in (
5.7
) in fact decays
exponentially
in
N_{y}
. This might be of relevance for problems such as the forced gravity pendulum in section
4.1
and the elliptic PDE considered in section
4.2
.

We will be interested in the difference
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{N_{u},N_{y}}(\mathcal{N})-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{N})
in the following. We denote

\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{N_{u},\infty}(\mathcal{N}):=\frac{1}{N_{u}}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{u}}\int_{U}|\mathcal{G}(U_{j})(y)-\mathcal{N}(U_{j})(y)|^{2}\,dy.

We can decompose the difference
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{N_{u},N_{y}}(\mathcal{N})-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{N})
as follows:

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{N_{u},N_{y}}(\mathcal{N})-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{N})

\displaystyle=\left[\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{N_{u},N_{y}}(\mathcal{N})-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{N_{u},\infty}(\mathcal{N})\right]+\left[\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{N_{u},\infty}(\mathcal{N})-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{N})\right].

Then, for any parametrized network
\mathcal{N}_{\theta}=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\beta_{k}(u;\theta)\tau_{k}(y;\theta)
with parameter
\theta
, we have (for a suitable choice of quadrature points)

\displaystyle\left|\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{N_{u},N_{y}}(\mathcal{N}_{\theta})-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{N_{u},\infty}(\mathcal{N}_{\theta})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N_{u}}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{u}}\left|I^{N_{y}}(\Delta_{\theta}(U_{j},{\,\cdot\,}))-\int_{U}\Delta_{\theta}(U_{j},y)\,dy\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N_{u}}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{u}}C\|\Delta_{\theta}(U_{j},{\,\cdot\,})\|_{C^{k}(U)}\,N_{y}^{-k/n}

\displaystyle\leq C\left[\|\mathcal{G}(u)\|_{C^{k}(U)}^{2}+\sum_{k=1}^{p}|\beta_{k}(u;\theta)|^{2}\|\tau_{k}({\,\cdot\,};\theta)\|_{C^{k}(U)}^{2}\right]\,N_{y}^{-k/n}.

Thus, if

\left\{\begin{aligned} \sup_{\theta}\|\tau_{k}({\,\cdot\,};\theta)\|_{C^{k}(U)}\leq C_{0},\\
\sup_{\theta}|\beta_{k}({\,\cdot\,};\theta)|^{2}\leq\Psi(u),\\
\|\mathcal{G}(u)\|_{C^{k}(U)}^{2}\leq\Psi(u),\end{aligned}\right.

for some integrable
\Psi(u)\geq 0
, such that
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\Psi(u)]<\infty
, then we can estimate

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\theta}\left|\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{N_{u},N_{y}}(\mathcal{N}_{\theta})-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{N_{u},\infty}(\mathcal{N}_{\theta})\right|\right]\leq CN_{y}^{-k/n},

for some
C>0
depending on
k
, the quadrature points,
\Psi
, the upper bound
C_{0}
and
\mu
, but independent of
N_{y}
.

5.2.
Random choice of
U_{j}
,
Y^{j}_{k}

However, in addition to the sampling in the target space
Y^{j}_{k}\in U
, one also needs to sample from the underlying input function space
U_{j}\in X\subset L^{2}(D)
. Random sampling is the only viable option for this infinite dimensional space. For the sake of simplicity of notation and exposition, we will choose
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and
N_{u}=N
,
N_{y}=1
in (
5.5
): i.e., choose mutually independent random variables
U_{j}
,
Y_{j}
,
j=1,\dots,N
, such that

(5.8)

\displaystyle U_{1},\dots,U_{N}\sim\mu\text{ are iid},\quad Y_{1},\dots,Y_{N}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(U)\text{ are iid}.

Let
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{N}:=\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{N_{u},1}
, i.e.

(5.9)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{N}(\mathcal{N}):=\frac{|U|}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}|\mathcal{G}(U_{j})(Y_{j})-\mathcal{N}(U_{j})(Y_{j})|^{2},

be the corresponding empirical loss. We note that the random variables
(U_{j},Y_{j})
,
j=1,\dots,N
are iid random variables with joint distribution
(U_{j},Y_{j})\sim\mu\otimes\mathrm{Unif}(U)
.

Fix a DeepONet neural network architecture of the form (
2.10
) with parameters (weights and biases in the corresponding trunk and branch nets)
\theta\mapsto\mathcal{N}_{\theta}
. We assume that the weights and biases are bounded,
\theta\in[-B,B]^{d_{\theta}}
for
B>0
, and some large
d_{\theta}\in\mathbb{N}
, representing the number of tuning parameters in the DeepONet
\mathcal{N}_{\theta}
. Let
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{N}=\mathcal{N}_{\widehat{\theta}}
denote an optimizer of the empirical loss
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{N}
among all choices of the weights and biases
\theta
, let
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
be an optimizer of the loss function
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}:=(\widehat{\mathscr{E}})^{2}
(
5.1
). The quantity

(5.10)

(\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{gen}})^{2}=\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{N}\right)-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{N}}\right),

with
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{N}\right)
denoting the empirical loss (
5.2
), is referred to as the
generalization error
. It provides a measure of how far the empirical optimizer
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{N}
of
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{N}
is from being an optimizer of
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}
. The generalization error has been studied in detail for conventional neural networks defined on
finite-dimensional
spaces. In the present section, we consider the extension of these results to the setting of DeepONets, which are defined on
infinite-dimensional
spaces.

Let us make the following assumptions:

Assumption 5.1

(Boundedness)
.

We assume that there exists a function
\Psi:L^{2}(D)\to[0,\infty)
,
(u)\mapsto\Psi(u)
, such that

|\mathcal{G}(u)(y)|\leq\Psi(u),\quad\sup_{\theta\in[-B,B]^{d_{\theta}}}|\mathcal{N}_{\theta}(u)(y)|\leq\Psi(u),

for all
u\in L^{2}(D)
,
y\in U
, and there exist constants
C,\kappa>0
, such that

(5.11)

\displaystyle\Psi(u)\leq C(1+\|u\|_{L^{2}})^{\kappa}.

Assumption 5.2

(Lipschitz continuity)
.

There exists a function
\Phi:L^{2}(D)\to[0,\infty)
,
u\mapsto\Phi(u)
, such that

|\mathcal{N}_{\theta}(u)(y)-\mathcal{N}_{\theta^{\prime}}(u)(y)|\leq\Phi(u)\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{\ell^{\infty}},

for all
u\in L^{2}(D)
,
y\in U
, and

\Phi(u)\leq C(1+|u\|_{L^{2}})^{\kappa},

for the same constants
C,\kappa>0
as in (
5.11
).

Note that both the boundedness and Lipschitz continuity assumptions are satisfied for the concrete examples of operators
\mathcal{G}
considered in section
4
.

To simplify the notation for the following estimates, we denote by
Z_{j}=(U_{j},Y_{j})
a (joint) random variable on
L^{2}(D)\times U
, and by a slight abuse of notation we write
\mathcal{G}(Z_{j})=\mathcal{G}(U_{j})(Y_{j})
,
\mathcal{N}_{\theta}(Z_{j})=\mathcal{N}_{\theta}(U_{j})(Y_{j})
. Recall that with the random choice (
5.8
), the
Z_{j}
are iid random variables.
We denote

(5.12)

\displaystyle S_{\theta}^{N}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}|\mathcal{G}(Z_{j})-\mathcal{N}_{\theta}(Z_{j})|^{2}.

We have the following bound on the generalization error (
5.10
),

Theorem 5.3
.

Let
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{N}
denote the minimizer of the loss (
5.1
) and empirical loss (
5.9
), respectively. If the assumptions
5.1
and
5.2
hold, then the generalization error (
5.10
) is bounded by

(5.13)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{N})-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(\widehat{\mathcal{N}})\right|\right]\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{N}}\left(1+Cd_{\theta}\log(CB\sqrt{N})^{2\kappa+1/2}\right),

where
C=C(\mu,\Psi,\Phi)
is a constant independent of
B
,
d_{\theta}
and
N
and
\kappa
is specified in (
5.11
).

The proof of Theorem
5.3
relies on a series of technical lemmas and is detailed in Appendix
F
.

Remark 5.4
.

The generalization error bound (
5.13
) shows that even if the underlying approximation problem is in infinite dimensions, the DeepONet generalization error (
5.10
), at worst, scales (up to a log) with the standard Monte Carlo scaling of
1/\sqrt{N}
in the number of samples
N
from the infinite dimensional input space. Thus, one can reduce the generalization error by increasing the number of samples, even in this infinite dimensional setting. In particular, the curse of dimensionality is also overcome for the generalization error.

Remark 5.5
.

A careful observation of the bound (
5.13
) on the generalization error (
5.10
) reveals that the bound depends explicitly on the number of parameters
d_{\theta}
of the DeepONet (
2.10
). As we have seen in the previous sections, one might need a large number of parameters in order to reduce the approximation error (
2.11
) to the desired tolerance. Thus, this estimate, like all estimates based on covering number and other statistical learning theory techniques
Cucker & Smale 2002
, applies in the underparametrized regime i.e
N\gg d_{\theta}
.

Remark 5.6
.

The bound (
5.13
) can also blow up if the bound on weights
B\to\infty
. However, we note that this blowup is modulated by a log and the weights can indeed be unbounded asymptotically i.e
B\sim e^{N^{r}}
for some
r<1/2
will still result in a decay of the error with increasing
N
. Given that such an exponential blowup may not occur in practice, it is reasonable to assume that the explicit dependence on the weight bounds may not affect the decay rate of the generalization error. This also holds if the bound on weights
B
, blows up as
B\sim d_{\theta}^{r_{\theta}}
, for some
r_{\theta}>0
. Given the
\log
-term in (
5.13
), this blow up of weights will only translate into a
d_{\theta}\log(d_{\theta})
-dependence of the generalization error. As long as one is in the under-parametrized regime, this dependence does not affect the overall decay of the generalization error as
N\to\infty
.

6.
Discussion

Operators, mapping infinite-dimensional Banach spaces, arise naturally in the study of differential equations.
Learning
such operators from data using neural networks can be very challenging on account of the underlying infinite-dimensional setup. In this paper, we analyze a neural network architecture termed
DeepONets
for approximating such operators. DeepONets are a recent extension
Lu et al. 2019
of operator networks, first considered in
Chen & Chen 1995
and have been recently successfully applied in many different contexts
Lu et al. 2019
;
Mao et al. 2021
;
Cai et al. 2021
;
Lin et al. 2021
and references therein. However, apart from the universal approximation result of
Chen & Chen 1995
and its extension to DeepONets in
Lu et al. 2019
, very few rigorous results for DeepONets are available currently. In particular, given the underlying infinite-dimensional setup, it is essential to demonstrate that DeepONets can overcome the
curse of dimensionality
, associated with the
\infty
-dimensional input-to-output mapping (see Definition
3.5
).

Our main aim in this article has been to analyze a form of DeepONets (
2.10
) and prove rigorous bounds on the error (
2.11
), incurred by a DeepONet in approximating a nonlinear operator
\mathcal{G}
, with an underlying measure
\mu
(see Definition
2.1
). To this end, we have presented the following results in the paper:

•

We extend the universal approximation theorem of
Chen & Chen 1995
to Theorem
3.1
, where we show that given any
measurable
operator
\mathcal{G}:X\to Y
, for
X=C(D)
,
Y=L^{2}(U)
, with respect to an underlying measure
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(X)
, there exists a DeepONet of the form (
2.10
), which can approximate it to arbitrary accuracy. In particular, we remove the continuity (of
\mathcal{G}
) and compactness (of subsets of
X
) assumptions of
Chen & Chen 1995
and pave the way for the application of DeepONets to approximate operators that arise in applications of PDEs to fields such as hypersonics
Mao et al. 2021
.

•

We provide an upper bound (Theorem
3.3
) on the DeepONet error (
2.11
) by decomposing it into three parts, i.e., an encoding error (
3.1
) stemming from the encoder
\mathcal{E}
, an approximation error that arises from the approximator neural network
\mathcal{A}
that maps between finite-dimensional spaces and a reconstruction error (
3.3
), corresponding to the trunk net induced affine reconstructor
\mathcal{R}
(
2.9
).

•

In Theorem
3.14
, we prove
lower bounds
on the reconstruction error (
3.3
) by utilizing
optimal errors
for projections on finite dimensional affine subspaces of separable Hilbert spaces (Theorem
3.12
). This allows us in Theorems
3.3
and
3.6
to prove
two-sided bounds
on the DeepONet error (
2.11
). In particular, the lower bound is explicitly given in terms of the decay of the eigenvalues of the covariance operator (
3.19
), associated with the push-forward measure
\mathcal{G}_{\#}{\mu}
(
3.9
). Moreover, this construction also allows us to infer the number of trunk nets
p
and that these trunk nets should approximate the eigenfunctions of the covariance operator in-order to obtain optimal reconstruction errors. Furthermore, we also provide bounds (
3.12
) on the reconstruction error that leverage the Sobolev regularity of the image of the nonlinear operator
\mathcal{G}
.

•

To control the encoding error (
3.1
) corresponding to the encoder
\mathcal{E}
, which is a pointwise evaluation of the input at
m

sensor locations
, we construct a
decoder

\mathcal{D}
(approximate inverse of the encoder) (
3.39
). We show in Theorem
3.9
that sensors chosen
at random
on the underlying domain
D
suffice to provide an
almost optimal
(optimal modulo a
\log
) bound on the encoding error. This further highlights the fact that DeepONets allow for a general approximation framework, i.e., no explicit information is needed about the location of sensor points and they can be chosen randomly.

•

Finally, estimating the approximation error (
3.2
) reduces to deriving bounds on a neural network
\mathcal{A}
that maps one finite (but possibly very-high) dimensional space to another. Hence, standard neural network approximation results such as from
Yarotsky 2017
can be applied. In particular, approximation results for holomorphic maps, such as those derived in
Schwab & Zech 2019
;
Opschoor et al. 2019
;
Opschoor et al. 2020
are important in this context.

The above results provide a workflow for deriving bounds on DeepONet approximation for general nonlinear operators
\mathcal{G}
with underlying measures
\mu
, which is outlined at the beginning of Section
4
. We illustrate this program with very general bounded linear operators and with the following four concrete examples of nonlinear operators, each corresponding to a differential equation that serves as a model for a large class of related problems:

•

First, we consider the forced gravity pendulum (
4.1
), with the operator
\mathcal{G}
mapping the forcing term into the solution of the ODE and a parametrized random field defining the underlying measure. We bound the reconstruction error by smoothness of the image of
\mathcal{G}
and the encoding error decays exponentially in the number of sensors on account of the decay of eigenvalues of the covariance operator associated with the underlying measure. The approximation error is bounded by showing that the operator allows for a complex analytic extension. Combining these ingredients in Theorem
4.10
, we prove an error bound (
4.16
) on the DeepONet approximation error. In particular, it is shown in (
4.17
) that the size of the DeepONet (total number of parameters in the trunk and branch nets) only grow sub-algebraically with respect to the error tolerance.

•

As a second example, we consider the standard elliptic PDE (
4.18
) with a variable coefficient
a
, which, for instance, arises in the modeling of groundwater Darcy flow. The nonlinear operator
\mathcal{G}
in this case maps the coefficient to the solution of the elliptic PDE and the underlying measure is the law of the random field (
4.22
). Again, we utilize the spectral properties of the underlying covariance operator, smoothness of the image of
\mathcal{G}
and holomorphy of an associated map to prove the bound (
4.29
) on the DeepONet approximation error. As in the case of the forced gravity pendulum, we show that the size of the DeepONet only grows
sub-algebraically
with decreasing error tolerance.

•

As a third example, we consider the Allen-Cahn equation (
4.3
) that models phase transitions, as a model for nonlinear parabolic PDEs of the reaction-diffusion type. The operator
\mathcal{G}
maps the initial data into the solution (at a given time) for the Allen-Cahn equation. In this case, no holomorphic extension of the underlying mapping is available. Nevertheless, we use a novel strategy to
emulate
a convergent finite difference scheme (
4.40
) by neural networks and derive an upper bound (
4.42
) on the DeepONet approximation error. In particular, the size of the DeepONet only grows polynomially (
4.43
) with respect to decreasing error tolerance.

•

In the final example, we consider a scalar conservation law (
4.44
) as a prototype for nonlinear hyperbolic PDEs. In this case, the operator
\mathcal{G}
is defined as the mapping between the initial data and the entropy solution of the conservation law at a given time. This example differs from the other three in two crucial respects. First, the underlying solutions are discontinuous and thus a pointwise evaluation based encoder cannot be used and is replaced by local averages (
4.50
). Second, the operator
\mathcal{G}
is contractive (hence Lipschitz continuous) in
L^{1}
. Thus, the usual Hilbert space setup, will a priori, will lead to sub-optimal error bounds. Hence, we adapt our theory to an
L^{1}
-Banach space version and are able to prove the upper bound (
4.52
) on the resulting DeepONet approximation error (
4.48
). This bound also allows us to conclude in (
4.53
) that the size of the DeepONet only grows polynomially with respect to the error tolerance. Moreover, we also construct an explicit example to show a lower bound in Theorem
4.44
on the DeepONet error in this case. This shows that the derived upper bounds are almost optimal for scalar conservation laws.

Hence, in all the four concrete examples which cover a large spectrum of nonlinear operators arising in the study of differential equations,
we prove that there exist DeepONets, which break the curse of dimensionality
in approximating the underlying operators. These examples and the underlying abstract theory provide a comprehensive study of the approximation error (
2.11
) for DeepONets.

Finally, we also study the generalization error (
5.10
) that arises from replacing the loss function (population risk) (
5.1
) with its sampled version, the so-called empirical loss (empirical risk) (
5.2
) that is used during training. Under very general assumptions on the underlying operator
\mathcal{G}
and the approximating branch and trunk nets, we apply covering number estimates to prove the bound (
5.13
) on the generalization error. In spite of the overall infinite-dimensional setup, this bound shows that the generalization errors decays (up to a log) with the reciprocal of the square-root of the total number of samples in the input function space, thus also overcoming the curse of dimensionality in this respect.

Thus, the analysis and results of this paper clearly prove that DeepONets can efficiently approximate operators in very general settings that include many examples of PDEs. The analysis also reveals some reasons for why DeepONets can work so well in practical applications and as building blocks for complex multi-physics systems, such as in the DeepM&Mnet architectures introduced in
Mao et al. 2021
;
Cai et al. 2021
. The main reason is the generality and flexibility of DeepONets. In particular
no a priori information about the underlying measure and operator are necessary at an algorithmic level
, apart from being able to sample from the underlying measure. Given our analysis, one can even use a small number of
randomly distributed sensors
to achieve almost optimal encoding error. Similarly, a small number of trunk-nets, with a very general neural network architecture, will be able to learn the eigenfunctions of the underlying covariance operator such that an optimal reconstruction error is attained with the resulting affine reconstructor (
2.9
). Finally, the branch nets can be trained simultaneously to minimize the approximation error.

At this point, we contrast DeepONets with other recently proposed frameworks for operator learning. In particular, we focus on a recent paper
Bhattacharya et al. 2021
, where the authors present an operator learning framework based on a principal component analysis (PCA) autoencoder for both the encoding and reconstruction steps. Thus, in that approach, one has to explicitly construct an approximate eigenbasis of the empirical covariance operator for the input measure and its push-forward with respect to the underlying operator. Neural networks are only used to approximate the operator on PCA projected finite-dimensional spaces. In contrast, DeepONets do not require any explicit knowledge of the covariance operator. In fact, our analysis shows that DeepONets
implicitly
and
concurrently
learn a suitable basis in output space along with an approximation of the projected operator. Although, many elements of our analysis overlap with that of
Bhattacharya et al. 2021
, we provide significantly more general results, including the alleviation of the curse of dimensionality for DeepONets. Moreover, our analysis can be readily extended to the framework of
Bhattacharya et al. 2021
to prove the mitigation of the curse of dimensionality in that context.

It is also instructive to compare our error bounds with the numerical results of
Lu et al. 2019
;
Mao et al. 2021
;
Cai et al. 2021
;
Lin et al. 2021
. In particular for the forced gravity pendulum, the authors of
Lu et al. 2019
considered a Gaussian random field with covariance kernel, similar to (
3.46
), as the underlying measure and observed an exponential decay of the test error with respect to the number of sensors (see Figure
2
(B) for a simpler example). Indeed, this observation is consistent with both the exponential decay of the encoding error (
3.49
) and the spectral decay of the overall error (
4.16
), as long as the correlation scale is resolved, i.e.
m\sim 1/\ell
, which is also observed in the numerical experiments of
Lu et al. 2019
. On the other hand, the decay of the generalization error with respect to the number of training samples, both in examples considered in
Lu et al. 2019
as well as in figure
2
(C,D) shows a very interesting behavior. For a small number of samples, the training error decays exponentially enabling fast training for DeepONets. Only for a relatively large number of training samples, the generalization error decays algebraically with respect to the number of training samples, at a rate consistent with the error bound (
5.13
). This bi-phasic behavior of the generalization error is certainly not explained by the bound (
5.13
) and will be a topic of future work.

The methods and results of this paper can be extended in different directions. We can apply the abstract framework presented in section
3
to other examples of differential equations, for instance the Navier-Stokes equations of fluid dynamics. Although we showed that the curse of dimensionality is broken by DeepONets for all the examples that we consider, it is unclear if our bounds on computational complexity of DeepONets are sharp. We show almost sharpness for scalar conservation laws and given the sub-algebraic decay of DeepONet size, we believe that the results for the pendulum and elliptic PDE are also close to optimal. However, there is certainly room for a sharp estimate for the Allen-Cahn equation. Finally, one can readily extend DeepONets, for instance by endowing them with a recurrent structure, to approximate the whole time-series for a time-parametrized operator, such as the solution operators of time-dependent PDEs. Extending the rigorous results of this paper to cover this recurrent case will also be considered in the future. Another possible avenue of future work is the extension of the approximation results in this paper to the case of multiple nonlinear operators (MNOs), already considered in
Back & Chen 2002
, where the authors prove an universal approximation property, similar to
Chen & Chen 1995
for these operators.

Appendix A
Notation for Standard Fourier basis

In several instances in this paper, we employ the following “standard” real Fourier basis
\{\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{{k}}\}_{{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
in
d
dimensions: For
{k}=(k_{1},\dots,k_{d})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we define

(A.1)

\displaystyle\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{{k}}:=C_{{k}}\begin{cases}\cos({k}\cdot{x}),&(k_{1}\geq 0),\\
\sin({k}\cdot{x}),&(k_{1}<0),\end{cases}

where the factor
C_{k}>0
ensures that
\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{{k}}
is properly normalized, i.e. that
\|\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{{k}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}=1
, or explicitly,

C_{{k}}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\begin{cases}2,&({k}\neq 0),\\
1,&({k}=0).\end{cases}

We note that the basis
\{\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{{k}}\}_{{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
simply consists of the real and imaginary parts of the complex Fourier basis
\{e^{i{k}\cdot{x}}\}_{{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
.

On occasion, it will also be convenient to write the standard Fourier basis in the form
\{\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
