(indexed by integer
j\in\mathbb{N}
, rather than
{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
). In this case, we identify

\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{j}({x}):=\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{{\upkappa}(j)}({x}),\quad(j\in\mathbb{N}),

where
{\upkappa}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is a fixed enumeration of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, with the property that
j\mapsto|{\upkappa}(j)|_{\infty}
is monotonically increasing, i.e. such that
j\leq j^{\prime}
implies that
|{\upkappa}(j)|_{\infty}\leq|{\upkappa}(j^{\prime})|_{\infty}
, where

(A.2)

\displaystyle|{k}|_{\infty}:=\max_{\ell=1,\dots,d}|k_{\ell}|,\quad{k}=(k_{1},\dots,k_{d})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}.

Appendix B
On the definition of Error (
2.11
)

We need the following lemma in order to conclude that the error (
2.11
) is well-defined on
L^{2}(D)
, even if the encoder
\mathcal{E}
is only well-defined on continuous functions.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
\mathcal{E}:C(D)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
denote the point-wise encoder
u\mapsto\mathcal{E}(u)=(u(x_{1}),\dots,u(x_{m}))
, for some
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\in D
. There exists a Borel measurable extension
\overline{\mathcal{E}}:L^{2}(D)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
, such that
\overline{\mathcal{E}}(u)=\mathcal{E}(u)
for any
u\in C(D)\cap L^{2}(D)
.

Proof.

It suffices to consider the case
m=1
. In this case, we note that
\mathcal{E}(u)=\limsup_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{E}_{k}(u)
for any
u\in C(D)
, where

\mathcal{E}_{k}:L^{2}(D)\to\mathbb{R},\quad\mathcal{E}_{k}(u)=\fint_{B_{1/k}(x_{1})}u(y)\,dy,

is continuous for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
. In particular, it follows that for
u\in L^{2}(D)
, the functional

\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}:L^{2}(D)\to[-\infty,\infty],\quad\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(u)=\limsup_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{E}_{k}(u),

is Borel measurable. We can now define a measurable extension
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
of
\mathcal{E}
by e.g. setting

\overline{\mathcal{E}}(u)=\begin{cases}\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(u),&(\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(u)\in\mathbb{R}),\\
0,&(\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(u)=\pm\infty).\end{cases}

∎

Appendix C
Proofs of Results in Section
3

C.1.
Proof of Theorem
3.1

The proof of the universal approximation theorem will be based on an application of the following well-known version of Lusin’s theorem (see e.g.
Bogachev 2007
, Thm. 7.1.13), which we will state for the special case of probability measures on Polish spaces, below:

Theorem C.1

(Lusin’s theorem)
.

Let
X,Y
be separable and complete metric spaces. Let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(X)
be a probability measure, and let
\mathcal{G}:X\to Y
be a Borel measurable mapping. Then for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a compact set
K\subset X
, such that
\mu(X\setminus K)<\epsilon
, and such that the restriction
\mathcal{G}|_{K}:K\to Y
is continuous.

In addition to Lusin’s theorem, the following clipping lemma will be used in the proof of Theorem
3.1
:

Lemma C.2

(Clipping lemma)
.

Let
\epsilon>0
, and fix
0<R_{1}<R_{2}
. There exists a ReLU neural network
\gamma:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
, such that

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\|\gamma(x)-x\|_{\ell^{2}}<\epsilon,&\text{if }\|x\|_{\ell^{2}}\leq R_{1},\\
\|\gamma(x)\|_{\ell^{2}}\leq R_{2},&\forall\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\epsilon\leq R_{2}-R_{1}
. We first note that
x\mapsto\sigma_{R_{1}}(x):=\min(\max(x,-R_{1}),R_{1})
maps
\mathbb{R}^{p}\to[-R_{1},R_{1}]^{p}
, and
\sigma_{R_{1}}
can be represented exactly by a (two-layer) ReLU neural network. Furthermore, for any
x\in[-R_{1},R_{1}]^{p}
, we have
\sigma_{R_{1}}(x)=x
. Define a continuous function
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
by

\phi(x):=\begin{cases}x,&(\|x\|_{\ell^{2}}\leq R_{1}),\\
R_{1}\dfrac{x}{\|x\|_{\ell^{2}}},&(\|x\|_{\ell^{2}}>R_{1}).\end{cases}

By the universal approximation theorem, there exists a ReLU network
\widetilde{\gamma}:[-R_{1},R_{1}]^{p}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
, such that

\|\widetilde{\gamma}(x)-\phi(x)\|_{\ell^{2}}<\epsilon,\quad\forall\,x\in[-R_{1},R_{1}]^{p}.

Define now
\gamma:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
, by
\gamma(x):=\widetilde{\gamma}\circ\sigma_{R_{1}}(x)
. Then, for
\|x\|_{\ell^{2}}\leq R_{1}
, we have

\|{\gamma}(x)-x\|_{\ell^{2}}=\|\widetilde{\gamma}(x)-\phi(x)\|_{\ell^{2}}<\epsilon,

and

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\|{\gamma}(x)\|_{\ell^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\xi\in[-R_{1},R_{1}]^{p}}\|\widetilde{\gamma}(\xi)\|_{\ell^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\xi\in[-R_{1},R_{1}]^{p}}\left\{\|\widetilde{\gamma}(\xi)-\phi(\xi)\|_{\ell^{2}}+\|\phi(\xi)\|_{\ell^{2}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon+R_{1}\leq R_{2}.

∎

Using the above clipping lemma, we can now prove the universal approximation theorem.

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Let
\epsilon>0
be given. By assumption
\mathcal{G}:C(D)\to L^{2}(U)
is a measurable mapping,
\mathcal{G}\in L^{2}(\mu)
, where
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(C(D))
. We have to show that there exists a DeepONet
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{E}
, such that

\|\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{N}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}<\epsilon.

Given
M>0
, define
\mathcal{G}_{M}
by clipping
\mathcal{G}
at size
M>0
, i.e. define

\mathcal{G}_{M}(u):=\begin{cases}\mathcal{G}(u),&(\|\mathcal{G}(u)\|_{L^{2}(U)}\leq M),\\
M\dfrac{\,G(u)}{\|\mathcal{G}(u)\|_{L^{2}(U)}},&(\|\mathcal{G}(u)\|_{L^{2}(U)}>M).\end{cases}

so that
\|\mathcal{G}_{M}(u)\|_{L^{2}(U)}\leq M
for all
u\in C(D)
. Then

(C.1)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{N}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq\|\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{M}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}+\|\mathcal{G}_{M}-\mathcal{N}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)},

and the first term goes to
0
by the dominated convergence theorem and the fact that
\mathcal{G}_{M}(u)\to\mathcal{G}(u)
pointwise, as
M\to\infty
. We can thus choose
M>\epsilon
, such that

(C.2)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{M}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}<\epsilon/3.

It now remains to approximate
\mathcal{G}_{M}
by a suitable DeepONet
\mathcal{N}
, where
\mathcal{G}_{M}
is bounded.

Next, we note that
C(D)
and
L^{2}(U)
are Polish spaces (separable, complete metric spaces). This allows us to invoke Lusin’s theorem, Theorem
C.1
, which shows that there exists a compact set
K\subset C(D)
, such that the restriction
\mathcal{G}_{M}|_{K}
of
\mathcal{G}_{M}
to
K
,

\mathcal{G}_{M}|_{K}:K\to L^{2}(U),

is continuous, and such that
\mu(C(D)\setminus K)<(\epsilon/9M)^{2}
.

Next, fix an orthonormal basis
\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\phi_{3},\dots\subset L^{2}(U)
consisting of continuous functions. For
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
, let
P_{\kappa}:L^{2}(U)\to C(U)
denote the projection onto
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{\kappa}
, i.e.

P_{\kappa}(v)=\sum_{k=1}^{\kappa}\langle v,\phi_{k}\rangle\phi_{k}.

We note that
P_{\kappa}:L^{2}(U)\to C(U)
is continuous for any fixed
\kappa
. Let now
K^{\prime}:=\mathcal{G}_{M}(K)
denote the image of the compact set
K
under
\mathcal{G}_{M}
. Since
\mathcal{G}_{M}|_{K}:K\to L^{2}(U)
is continuous,
K^{\prime}
is compact as a subset of
L^{2}(U)
. By the compactness of
K^{\prime}
, there exists
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
, such that
\max_{v\in K^{\prime}}\|v-P_{\kappa}(v)\|<\epsilon/6
. We conclude that the composition
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}:=P_{\kappa}\circ\mathcal{G}_{M}:K\to C(U)
, with
K\subset C(D)
a compact subset, is continuous, and

(C.3)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \max_{u\in K}\left\|\mathcal{G}_{M}(u)-\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(u)\right\|_{L^{2}(U)}&=\max_{u\in K}\left\|\mathcal{G}_{M}(u)-P_{\kappa}\circ\mathcal{G}_{M}(u)\right\|_{L^{2}(U)}^{2}\\
&=\max_{v\in K^{\prime}}\left\|v-P_{\kappa}(v)\right\|_{L^{2}(U)}<\epsilon/6.\end{aligned}

We can apply the universal approximation theorem of continuous operators on compact subsets of
Chen & Chen 1995
to the continuous mapping
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}:K\subset C(D)\to C(U)
to conclude that there exists an operator network
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
with a single hidden layer in the approximator network and a single hidden layer in the trunk network (and with
\tau_{0}\equiv 0
), such that

\displaystyle\sup_{u\in K}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(u)-\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(u)\|_{L^{2}(U)}<\epsilon/12.

Note that this implies in particular that

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(u)\|_{L^{2}(U)}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{G}_{M}(u)\|_{L^{2}(U)}+\|\mathcal{G}_{M}(u)-\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(u)\|_{L^{2}(U)}+\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(u)-\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(u)\|_{L^{2}(U)}

\displaystyle\leq M+\epsilon/6+\epsilon/6<2M,

for all
u\in K
. We note that after suitably modifying
2
2

2

If
A\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
is an invertible matrix, then the transformed trunk and branch nets
\widehat{\tau}:=A\cdot\tau
and
\widehat{\beta}:=A^{-T}\cdot\beta
represent the same DeepONet, i.e. we have
\sum_{k=1}^{p}\widehat{\beta}_{k}(u)\widehat{\tau}_{k}(y)=\sum_{k=1}^{p}{\beta}_{k}(u){\tau}_{k}(y)
for all
u,y
.
the (linear) output layers of the branch and trunk nets of
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
, we can write
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
in the form

\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(u)(y)=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\beta_{k}(u)\tau_{k}(y),

with
orthonormal
trunk net functions
\{\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{p}\}\subset L^{2}(U)
. In particular, we then have

\|\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(u)\|_{L^{2}}=\|\beta(u)\|_{\ell^{2}},\quad\forall\,u\in C(D),

and

\|\beta(u)\|_{\ell^{2}}\leq M+\epsilon/3<2M,\quad\forall\,u\in K.

Applying Lemma
C.2
with
R_{1}=M+\epsilon/3
,
R_{2}=2M
, we conclude that there exists a ReLU neural network
\gamma:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
, such that

\begin{cases}\|\gamma(x)-x\|_{\ell^{2}}<\epsilon/12,&\text{if }\|x\|_{\ell^{2}}\leq R_{1},\\
\|\gamma(x)\|_{\ell^{2}}\leq 2M,&\forall\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}.\end{cases}

We now define a “clipped” DeepONet
\mathcal{N}:C(D)\to L^{2}(U)
, by

\mathcal{N}(u):=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\gamma_{k}(\beta(u))\tau_{k}(y).

Then
\mathcal{N}(u)
satisfies

(C.4)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \max_{u\in K}\|\mathcal{N}(u)-\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(u)\|_{L^{2}}&\leq\max_{u\in K}\|\mathcal{N}(u)-\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(u)\|_{L^{2}}+\max_{u\in K}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(u)-\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(u)\|_{L^{2}}\\
&=\max_{u\in K}\|\gamma(\beta(u))-\beta(u)\|_{\ell^{2}}+\max_{u\in K}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(u)-\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(u)\|_{L^{2}}\\
&\leq\max_{\|x\|_{\ell^{2}}\leq R_{1}}\|\gamma(x)-x\|_{\ell^{2}}+\max_{u\in K}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(u)-\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(u)\|_{L^{2}}\\
&\leq\epsilon/12+\epsilon/12=\epsilon/6,\end{aligned}

and
\mathcal{N}(u)
is bounded from above by

\|\mathcal{N}(u)\|_{L^{2}}=\|\gamma(\beta(u))\|_{\ell^{2}}\leq 2M,\quad\forall\,u\in C(D).

It follows that for this clipped DeepONet
\mathcal{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{G}_{M}-\mathcal{N}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{G}_{M}-\mathcal{N}\|_{L^{2}(\mu;K)}+\|\mathcal{G}_{M}\|_{L^{2}(\mu;X\setminus K)}+\|\mathcal{N}\|_{L^{2}(\mu;X\setminus K)}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{G}_{M}-\mathcal{N}\|_{L^{2}(\mu;K)}+3M\mu(X\setminus K)^{1/2}

(C.5)

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{G}_{M}-\mathcal{N}\|_{L^{2}(\mu;K)}+\epsilon/3,

and, using (
C.3
) and (
C.4
), we find

(C.6)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|\mathcal{G}_{M}-\mathcal{N}\|_{L^{2}(\mu;K)}&\leq\max_{u\in K}\left\{\|\mathcal{G}_{M}(u)-\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(u)\|_{L^{2}(U)}+\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(u)-\mathcal{N}(u)\|_{L^{2}(U)}\right\}\\
&<\epsilon/3.\end{aligned}

Hence, by (
C.5
) and (
C.6
), we have

(C.7)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{G}_{M}-\mathcal{N}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}<2\epsilon/3.

Combining (
C.7
) and (
C.2
), we conclude that the DeepONet
\mathcal{N}
satisfies

\|\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{N}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}<2\epsilon/3+\epsilon/3=\epsilon.

∎

C.2.
Proof of Theorem
3.3

Proof.

The upper estimate follows by a suitable decomposition of the difference. We write

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}-\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{E}-\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle=\left[\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{E}-\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\right]+\left[\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{E}-\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\left[\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}-\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\left[\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}\right]

\displaystyle=:T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}.

We can now estimate the norm of each of the three terms, as follows:

\displaystyle\|T_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{X}\|\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{E}-\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(U)}\,d\mu\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{R})\left(\int_{X}\|\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{E}-\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{p})}\,d\mu\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{R})\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\|\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}\|^{2}_{\ell(\mathbb{R}^{p})}\,d(\mathcal{E}_{\#}\mu)\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{R})\|\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{E}_{\#}\mu)}.

In the second line above, we denote

\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{R})=\mathrm{Lip}\left(\mathcal{R}:(\mathbb{R}^{p},\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{p})})\to(L^{2}(U),\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{L^{2}(U)})\right).

For the second term, we obtain

\displaystyle\|T_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{X}\|\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}-\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\|_{L^{2}(U)}^{2}\,d\mu\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P})\left(\int_{X}\|\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}-\mathcal{G}\|_{L^{2}(U)}^{2}\,d\mu\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P})\mathrm{Lip}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{G})\left(\int_{X}\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}-\mathrm{Id}\|_{X}^{2\alpha}\,d\mu\right)^{1/2},

where

\displaystyle\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}:(L^{2}(U),\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{L^{2}(U)})\to(L^{2}(U),\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{L^{2}(U)})),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Lip}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{G})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Lip}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{G}:(A\subset X,\|{\,\cdot\,}\|)\to(L^{2}(U),\|{\,\cdot\,}\|_{L^{2}(U)})),

and
\alpha\in(0,1]
. Since
\alpha\in(0,1]
, we can estimate the last term by Jensen’s inequality to obtain:

\displaystyle\|T_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P})\mathrm{Lip}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{G})\left(\int_{X}\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}-\mathrm{Id}\|_{X}^{2\alpha}\,d\mu\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P})\mathrm{Lip}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{G})\left(\int_{X}\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}-\mathrm{Id}\|_{X}^{2}\,d\mu\right)^{\alpha/2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P})\mathrm{Lip}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{G})\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}-\mathrm{Id}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{\alpha}.

Finally for the third term, we have (by the definition of the push-forward)

\displaystyle\|T_{3}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{X}\|\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(U)}\,d\mu\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{L^{2}(U)}\|\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}-\mathrm{Id}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(U)}\,d(\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu)\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\|\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}-\mathrm{Id}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu)}.

∎

C.3.
Proof of Theorem
3.10

The proof of Theorem
3.10
is a consequence of the following series of lemmas.

Lemma C.3
.

Given a separable Hilbert space
H
and a
p
-dimensional subspace
\widehat{V}\subset H
, we have

(C.8)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{Proj}}(\widehat{V})=\int_{H}\|v\|^{2}\,d\mu(v)-\sum_{i=1}^{p}\int_{H}\left|\langle v,\widehat{v}_{i}\rangle\right|^{2}\,d\mu(v),

where
\widehat{v}_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,p
is any orthonormal basis of
\widehat{V}
. In particular, minimizing
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{Proj}}
is equivalent to maximizing

(C.9)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{p}\int_{X}\left|\langle v,\widehat{v}_{i}\rangle\right|^{2}\,d\mu(v)=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\left[\left|\langle\mathbb{E}[v],\widehat{v}_{i}\rangle\right|^{2}+\langle\widehat{v}_{i},\Gamma\widehat{v}_{i}\rangle\right],

where

\Gamma=\int_{X}(v-\mathbb{E}[v])\otimes(v-\mathbb{E}[v])\,d\mu(v).

Proof.

Let
\widehat{v}_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,p
be any orthonormal basis of
\widehat{V}
. Then

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{v}\in\widehat{V}}\|v-\widehat{v}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|v-\sum_{k=1}^{p}\langle v,\widehat{v}_{i}\rangle\,\widehat{v}_{i}\right\|^{2},

and since
v-\sum_{k}\langle v,\widehat{v}_{i}\rangle\,\widehat{v}_{i}
is perpendicular to all
\widehat{v}_{i}
, we have

\displaystyle\|v\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|v-\sum_{k=1}^{p}\langle v,\widehat{v}_{i}\rangle\,\widehat{v}_{i}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{p}\langle v,\widehat{v}_{i}\rangle\,\widehat{v}_{i}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\inf_{\widehat{v}\in\widehat{V}}\|v-\widehat{v}\|^{2}+\sum_{k=1}^{p}\left|\langle v,\widehat{v}_{i}\rangle\right|^{2}.

This is equivalent to (
C.8
). Furthermore, for any
i=1,\dots,p
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{H}|\langle v,\widehat{v}_{i}\rangle|^{2}\,d\mu(v)

\displaystyle=\int_{H}|\langle v-\mathbb{E}[v],\widehat{v}_{i}\rangle+\langle\mathbb{E}[v],\widehat{v}_{i}\rangle|^{2}\,d\mu(v)

\displaystyle=\int_{H}\left\{|\langle v-\mathbb{E}[v],\widehat{v}_{i}\rangle|^{2}+2\langle v-\mathbb{E}[v],\widehat{v}_{i}\rangle\langle\mathbb{E}[v],\widehat{v}_{i}\rangle+|\langle\mathbb{E}[v],\widehat{v}_{i}\rangle|^{2}\right\}\,d\mu(v)

\displaystyle=\int_{H}\left\{|\langle v-\mathbb{E}[v],\widehat{v}_{i}\rangle|^{2}+|\langle\mathbb{E}[v],\widehat{v}_{i}\rangle|^{2}\right\}\,d\mu(v)

\displaystyle=\langle\widehat{v}_{i},\Gamma\widehat{v}_{i}\rangle+|\langle\mathbb{E}[v],\widehat{v}_{i}\rangle|^{2}.

∎

Lemma C.4
.

The covariance operator
\Gamma
of a probability measure
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(H)
is a compact, self-adjoint operator. In particular, there exists a discrete set of (unique) eigenvalues
\overline{\lambda}_{1}>\overline{\lambda}_{2}>\dots
, and orthogonal subspaces
E_{1},E_{2},\dots
, such that

H=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{\infty}E_{j},\quad\text{and}\quad\Gamma=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\overline{\lambda}_{j}P_{E_{j}}.

Here
P_{E}:H\to E
denotes the orthogonal projection onto the subspace
E\subset H
.

Proof.

The proof can e.g. be found in
(
Pinelis & Molzon 2016
, Appendix E,F)
.
∎

As an immediate corollary, we also obtain

Lemma C.5
.

For any
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(H)
, the operator

\overline{\Gamma}=\int_{H}(v\otimes v)\,d\nu(v),

is a self-adjoint, compact operator, and hence it also possesses an eigendecomposition as in Lemma
C.4
.

Proof.

We can write
\overline{\Gamma}=\Gamma+\mathbb{E}[v]\otimes\mathbb{E}[v]
, where
\mathbb{E}[v]:=\int_{H}v\,d\nu(v)
is the expected value under
\nu
. It is now immediate that
\overline{\Gamma}
is self-adjoint, since both
\Gamma
and
\mathbb{E}[v]\otimes\mathbb{E}[v]
are self-adjoint. Furthermore,
\overline{\Gamma}
is also compact, since
\overline{\Gamma}=\Gamma+\mathbb{E}[v]\otimes\mathbb{E}[v]
is the sum of a compact operator
\Gamma
and the finite-rank operator
\mathbb{E}[v]\otimes\mathbb{E}[v]
.
∎

The next lemma shows that the quantity (
C.9
) to be maximized can be written in an equivalent form, which only involves the orthogonal projection onto
V_{p}
.

Lemma C.6
.

Let
\overline{\Gamma}=\Gamma+\mathbb{E}[v]\otimes\mathbb{E}[v]
. Let
\phi_{k}
,
(k\in\mathbb{N})
, be an orthonormal basis of eigenvectors of
\overline{\Gamma}
with corresponding eigenvalues
\lambda_{k}
. Let
\widehat{v}_{1},\dots,\widehat{v}_{p}
be any orthonormal basis of
V_{p}
. Then

(C.10)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{p}\langle\widehat{v}_{i},\overline{\Gamma}\widehat{v}_{i}\rangle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}\|P_{V_{p}}\phi_{k}\|^{2}.

Here
P_{V_{p}}:H\to V_{p}
denotes the orthogonal projection onto
V_{p}
.

Proof.

Let
u_{1},\dots,u_{p}\in V_{p}
be an orthonormal basis
V_{p}
. Let
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots
denote the eigenvalues of
\overline{\Gamma}
with corresponding orthonormal eigenbasis
\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\dots
. Then

u_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\langle u_{j},\phi_{k}\rangle\phi_{k},

and hence

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{p}\left\langle u_{j},\overline{\Gamma}u_{j}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\left\langle u_{j},\overline{\Gamma}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\langle u_{j},\phi_{k}\rangle\phi_{k}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\langle u_{j},\phi_{k}\rangle\left\langle u_{j},\overline{\Gamma}\phi_{k}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}\left|\langle u_{j},\phi_{k}\rangle\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{p}\left|\langle u_{j},\phi_{k}\rangle\right|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}\left\|P_{V_{p}}\phi_{k}\right\|^{2}.

∎

Lemma C.7
.

If
V_{p}
is a minimizing subspace, i.e. a subspace which minimizes
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{Proj}}
, then

(C.11)

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n-1}E_{j}\subset V_{p}\subset\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}E_{j},

where
n
is chosen such that

\dim\Big(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n-1}E_{j}\Big)=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\dim(E_{j})<p\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}\dim(E_{j})=\dim\Big(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}E_{j}\Big).

Here, the
E_{j}
denote the eigenspaces of

\overline{\Gamma}=\Gamma+\mathbb{E}[v]\otimes\mathbb{E}[v]=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\overline{\lambda}_{j}P_{E_{j}},

corresponding to the (distinct) eigenvalues
\overline{\lambda}_{1}>\overline{\lambda}_{2}>\overline{\lambda}_{3}>\dots
of
\overline{\Gamma}
.

Proof.

Let
V_{p}\subset H
be a minimizer of
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{Proj}}
with respect to
\nu
of dimension
\dim(V_{p})=p
. For each eigenspace
E_{j}
of
\overline{\Gamma}
, denote

p_{j}=\dim(E_{j}),

and define
p_{0}:=0
. Now, we choose an orthonormal eigenbasis
\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\dots
of
\overline{\Gamma}
, with corresponding eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots
, such that

E_{j}=\mathrm{span}(\phi_{p_{j-1}+1},\dots,\phi_{p_{j}}),\quad(j\in\mathbb{N}).

Note that the eigenvalues thus satisfy

\underbrace{\lambda_{1}=\dots=\lambda_{p_{1}}}_{=\overline{\lambda}_{1}}>\underbrace{\lambda_{p_{1}+1}=\dots=\lambda_{p_{2}}}_{=\overline{\lambda}_{2}}>\dots

By Lemma
C.3
and
C.6
,
V_{p}
is a maximizer of the mapping

\displaystyle\widehat{V}\mapsto\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}\left\|P_{\widehat{V}}\phi_{k}\right\|^{2},

among all
p
-dimensional subspaces
\widehat{V}\subset X
. Given a
p
-dimensional subspace
\widehat{V}\subset X
, let
u_{1},\dots,u_{p}
be an orthonormal basis of
\widehat{V}
. We note that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left\|P_{\widehat{V}}\phi_{k}\right\|^{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\left|\langle u_{j},\phi_{k}\rangle\right|^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\|u_{j}\|^{2}=p,

And for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

0\leq\left\|P_{\widehat{V}}\phi_{k}\right\|^{2}\leq 1.

Thus, for any
p
-dimensional subspace, the coefficients

\alpha_{k}=\alpha_{k}(\widehat{V}):=\|P_{\widehat{V}}\phi_{k}\|^{2},\quad(k\in\mathbb{N}),

belong to the set

\mathcal{A}_{p}:={\left\{\alpha=(\alpha_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\,\middle|\,\alpha_{k}\in[0,1]\,\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N},\;\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}=p\right\}}.

And we are interested in the maximizer
\widehat{V}=V_{p}
of

(\alpha_{k}(\widehat{V}))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mapsto\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}\alpha_{k}(\widehat{V}).

We now make the following claim:

Claim
.

For any
(\alpha_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
, we have

(C.12)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}\alpha_{k}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k},

with equality, if and only if

\alpha_{k}=\begin{cases}1,&\left(\text{if }k\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}p_{j}\right),\\
0,&\left(\text{if }k>\sum_{j=1}^{n}p_{j}\right),\end{cases}

where
n
is chosen such that

\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}p_{j}\leq p<\sum_{j=1}^{n}p_{j}.

Before proving the above claim, we show that it implies (
C.11
). Indeed, for the subspace
\widehat{V}=\mathrm{span}(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{p})
, we clearly have

\alpha_{k}(\widehat{V})=\|P_{\widehat{V}}\phi_{k}\|^{2}=\begin{cases}1,&(k\leq p),\\
0,&(k>p),\end{cases}

and hence

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}\alpha_{k}(\widehat{V})=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k},

achieves the upper bound in (
C.12
). If
V_{p}
is another optimizer, then we must thus also have

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}\alpha_{k}(V_{p})=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}.

The claim then implies that

\|P_{V_{p}}\phi_{k}\|^{2}=\begin{cases}1,&\left(\text{if }k\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}p_{j}\right),\\
0,&\left(\text{if }k>\sum_{j=1}^{n}p_{j}\right).\end{cases}

The latter is equivalent to the statement that

\phi_{k}\in V_{p},\;\text{for }k\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}p_{j},\quad\text{and}\quad\phi_{k}\perp V_{p},\;\text{for }k>\sum_{j=1}^{n}p_{j},

i.e. that

\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n-1}E_{j}\subset V_{p},\quad\text{and}\quad\bigoplus_{j=n+1}^{\infty}E_{j}\perp V_{p}.

Thus, assuming the claim (
C.12
), it follows that for any optimal subspace
V_{p}
, we must have

\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n-1}E_{j}\subset V_{p}\subset\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}E_{j}.

We finally need to
prove the claim
: To prove the inequality (
C.12
), we simply note that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}\alpha_{k}-\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}\alpha_{k}-\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}\alpha_{k}-\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}(1-\alpha_{k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{k}\alpha_{k}-\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}(1-\alpha_{k}).

Since the sequence of eigenvalues
\lambda_{k}
is monotonically decreasing and
\alpha_{k}\geq 0
,
1-\alpha_{k}\geq 0
, we have

\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{k}\alpha_{k}\leq\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{p}\alpha_{k},\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}(1-\alpha_{k})\geq\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{p}(1-\alpha_{k}),

and hence

\displaystyle\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{k}\alpha_{k}-\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}(1-\alpha_{k})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{p}\alpha_{k}-\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{p}(1-\alpha_{k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{p}\alpha_{k}-p\lambda_{p}+\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{p}\alpha_{k}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{p}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}-p\right)

\displaystyle=0.

The last line follows from the fact that
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}=p
, for any
(\alpha_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
. We thus conclude that

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}\alpha_{k}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k},

for all
(\alpha_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
. Furthermore, if
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}\alpha_{k}=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}
, then in all the above estimates, we must have equality. In particular, we must have

\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{k}\alpha_{k}=\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{p}\alpha_{k},\quad\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}(1-\alpha_{k})=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{p}(1-\alpha_{k}).

The first equality is only possible, if
\alpha_{k}=0
, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, such that
\lambda_{k}<\lambda_{p}
, i.e. we must have

\alpha_{k}=0,\quad\text{if }k>\sum_{j=1}^{n}p_{j}.

The second equality is only possible, if
\alpha_{k}=1
, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, such that
\lambda_{k}>\lambda_{p}
, i.e. we must have

\alpha_{k}=1,\quad\text{if }k\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}p_{j}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark C.8
.

It is also straight-forward to check that if
\widehat{V}\subset H
is a
p
-dimensional subspace, such that

\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n-1}E_{k}\subset\widehat{V}\subset\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}E_{k},

where

\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\dim(E_{k})<p\leq\sum_{k=1}^{n}\dim(E_{k}),

then

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}\|P_{\widehat{V}}\phi_{k}\|^{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k},

i.e.
\widehat{V}
is optimal for
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{Proj}}(\widehat{V};\nu)
, in this case.

From the last lemma, we immediately have the following corollary.

Corollary C.9
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the minimizing subspace
V_{p}
of
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{Proj}}(V_{p};\nu)
of dimension

p=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\dim(E_{k}),

is unique.

Proof.

By Lemma
C.7
, if
V_{p}
is any minimizer of
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{Proj}}
, then

V_{p}\subset\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}E_{k}.

Since
\dim(V_{p})=p=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\dim(E_{k})
, by assumption, it follows that
V_{p}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}E_{k}
is uniquely determined by the eigenspaces
E_{1},\dots,E_{n}
.
∎

C.3.1.
Proof of Theorem
3.10

Proof.

The existence and characterization of optimal subspaces
\widehat{V}
is a consequence of Lemma
C.7
and Remark
C.8
. Uniqueness of the optimal subspace
V_{p}\subset H
for
p_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\dim(E_{k})
is proved in Corollary
C.9
. Finally, the identity for the projection error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{Proj}}(\widehat{V})=\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{k}
for the optimal subspace
V_{p}=\mathrm{span}(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{p})
, with
\phi_{k}
denote the eigenfunctions of the uncentered covariance operator
\overline{\Gamma}
corresponding to decreasing eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots
, follows from

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{Proj}}(\widehat{V})

\displaystyle\overset{\mathclap{\underset{\downarrow}{\eqref{eq:linear}}}}{=}\int_{H}\|v\|^{2}\,d\mu(u)-\sum_{i=1}^{p}\int_{H}|\langle v,\phi_{i}\rangle|^{2}\,d\mu(v)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{Tr}}(\overline{\Gamma})-\sum_{i=1}^{p}\langle\phi_{i},\overline{\Gamma}\phi_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle\overset{\mathclap{\underset{\downarrow}{\eqref{eq:Eproj-equiv2}}}}{=}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}\|P_{V_{p}}\phi_{k}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}-\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}=\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{k}.

∎

C.3.2.
Proof of Theorem
3.12

Proof.

Since
V_{0}
is a finite-dimensional affine subspace, the existence and uniqueness of

\widehat{v}_{0}\in\argmin_{\widehat{v}\in\widehat{V}_{0}}\|\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v}\|^{2},

is straight-forward. We also note that
\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v}_{0}\perp\widehat{V}
. We can now write

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{Proj}}(\widehat{V}_{0})

\displaystyle=\int_{H}\inf_{\widehat{v}\in\widehat{V}_{0}}\|{v}-\widehat{v}\|^{2}\,d\nu(v)

\displaystyle=\int_{H}\inf_{\widehat{v}\in\widehat{V}}\|{v}-\widehat{v}_{0}-\widehat{v}\|^{2}\,d\nu(v)

\displaystyle=\int_{H}\inf_{\widehat{v}\in\widehat{V}}\|[{v}-\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v}]-[\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v}_{0}]\|^{2}\,d\nu(v)

\displaystyle=\int_{H}\inf_{\widehat{v}\in\widehat{V}}\left\{\|\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v}_{0}\|^{2}-2\langle{v}-\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v},\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v}_{0}\rangle\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\left.+\|{v}-\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v}\|^{2}\right\}\,d\nu(v)

Since
\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v}_{0}\perp\widehat{v}
for all
\widehat{v}\in\widehat{V}
, we have

2\langle{v}-\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v},\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v}_{0}\rangle=2\langle{v}-\mathbb{E}[v],\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v}_{0}\rangle,

and thus

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{Proj}}(\widehat{V}_{0})

\displaystyle=\int_{H}\inf_{\widehat{v}\in\widehat{V}}\left\{\|\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v}_{0}\|^{2}-2\langle{v}-\mathbb{E}[v],\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v}_{0}\rangle\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\left.+\|{v}-\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v}\|^{2}\right\}\,d\nu(v)

\displaystyle=\int_{H}\|\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v}_{0}\|^{2}\,d\nu(v)

\displaystyle\quad-2\int_{H}\langle{v}-\mathbb{E}[v],\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v}_{0}\rangle\,d\nu(v)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{H}\inf_{\widehat{v}\in\widehat{V}}\|{v}-\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v}\|^{2}\,d\nu(v).

The first term is independent of
v
, the second term averages to zero. Hence, we finally obtain

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{Proj}}(\widehat{V}_{0})=\|\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v}_{0}\|^{2}+\int_{H}\inf_{\widehat{v}\in\widehat{V}}\|{v}-\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v}\|^{2}\,d\nu(v).

Note now if
\widehat{V}_{0}
is an affine subspace with associated vector space
\widehat{V}
for which the first term is not equal to
0
, then the affine subspace
\widehat{V}_{0}^{\prime}:=\mathbb{E}[v]+\widehat{V}
satisfies

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{Proj}}(\widehat{V}_{0}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{H}\inf_{\widehat{v}\in\widehat{V}}\|v-\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v}\|^{2}\,d\nu(v)

\displaystyle<\|\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v}_{0}\|^{2}+\int_{H}\inf_{\widehat{v}\in\widehat{V}}\|v-\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v}\|^{2}\,d\nu(v)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{Proj}}(\widehat{V}_{0}).

Thus, if
\widehat{V}_{0}
is a minimizer among affine subspaces, then we must have
\widehat{v}_{0}=\mathbb{E}[v]
. Next, define a measure
\overline{\nu}\in\mathcal{P}(H)
by

\int_{H}\Phi(v)\,d\overline{\nu}(v):=\int_{H}\Phi(v-\mathbb{E}[v])\,d\nu(v),\quad\forall\,\Phi\in L^{\infty}(H).

Then
\int_{H}v\,d\overline{\nu}(v)=0
, and

\int_{H}\inf_{\widehat{v}\in\widehat{V}}\|v-\mathbb{E}[v]-\widehat{v}\|^{2}\,d\nu(v)=\int_{H}\inf_{\widehat{v}\in\widehat{V}}\|v-\widehat{v}\|^{2}\,d\overline{\nu}(v),

is the projection error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{Proj}}(\widehat{V};\overline{\nu})
of the
p
-dimensional vector space
\widehat{V}
with respect to the measure
\overline{\nu}
. Since
\widehat{V}_{0}
is a minimizer, we must have that
\widehat{V}
is a minimizer of
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{Proj}}(\widehat{V})
among
p
-dimensional subspaces. By Lemma
C.7
, it follows that

\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n-1}E_{j}\subset\widehat{V}\subset\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}E_{j},

where
E_{j}
denote the eigenspaces of the covariance operator of
\overline{\nu}
, given by

\int_{H}v\otimes v\,d\overline{\nu}(v)=\int_{H}(v-\mathbb{E}[v])\otimes(v-\mathbb{E}[v])\,d\nu(v)=\Gamma,

and corresponding to distinct eigenvalues
\overline{\lambda}_{1}>\overline{\lambda}_{2}>\dots
of
{\Gamma}
, as claimed. This concludes the proof of this theorem.
∎

C.4.
Proof of Lemma
3.13

Proof.

Let
V=\mathrm{span}(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{p})
. We first note that since we have a direct sum
L^{2}(U)=V^{\perp}\oplus V
, we can decompose any
u\in L^{2}(U)
uniquely as

u=u^{\perp}+\sum_{k=1}^{p}\alpha_{k}\tau_{k},

for coefficients
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{p}\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
\tau^{\ast}_{1},\dots,\tau^{\ast}_{p}\in\mathrm{span}(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{p})
denote the dual basis of
\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{p}
. Taking the inner product of the last identity with
\tau_{\ell}^{\ast}
, it follows that

\langle\tau_{\ell}^{\ast},u\rangle=\langle\tau_{\ell}^{\ast},u^{\perp}\rangle+\sum_{k=1}^{p}\alpha_{k}\langle\tau_{\ell}^{\ast},\tau_{k}\rangle=\alpha_{\ell},

where we have used that
u^{\perp}\perp V\ni\tau_{\ell}^{\ast}
, and
\langle\tau_{\ell}^{\ast},\tau_{k}\rangle=\delta_{k\ell}
. Applying this identity, we now note that

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}(u)

\displaystyle=\tau_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{p}[\mathcal{P}(u)]_{k}\tau_{k}=\tau_{0}^{\perp}+\sum_{k=1}^{p}\big([\mathcal{P}(u)]_{k}+\langle\tau_{k}^{\ast},\tau_{0}\rangle\big)\tau_{k},

and

\displaystyle u=u^{\perp}+\sum_{k=1}^{p}\langle\tau_{k}^{\ast},u\rangle\tau_{k}.

Hence

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}(u)-u

\displaystyle=\tau_{0}^{\perp}-u^{\perp}+\sum_{k=1}^{p}\big([\mathcal{P}(u)]_{k}+\langle\tau_{k}^{\ast},\tau_{0}-u\rangle\big)\tau_{k}.

The norm of the last term is clearly minimized if the sum over
k=1,\dots,p
vanishes. This is the case, provided that

[\mathcal{P}(u)]_{k}=\langle\tau_{k}^{\ast},u-\tau_{0}\rangle,\quad(k=1,\dots,p).

The claim follows.
∎

C.5.
Proof of Proposition
3.15

Proof.

Denote
\widehat{V}_{0}:=\mathrm{Im}(\mathcal{R})
the affine image of
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{\bm{\tau}}
. Taking into account Theorem
3.12
, we obtain

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}})^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{Y}\|\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}-\mathrm{Id}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{y}}\,d\nu

\displaystyle=\inf_{\widehat{v}\in\widehat{V}_{0}}\|\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[v]-\widehat{v}\|_{L^{2}_{y}}^{2}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}\|\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}\phi_{k}-\phi_{k}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{y}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[v]-\tau_{0}\|_{L^{2}_{y}}^{2}+\sum_{k\leq p}\lambda_{k}\|\tau_{k}-\phi_{k}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{y}}+\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{k}\|\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}\phi_{k}-\phi_{k}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{y}}

\displaystyle=\|\widehat{\tau}_{0}-\tau_{0}\|_{L^{2}_{y}}^{2}+\sum_{k=1}^{p}\lambda_{k}\|\tau_{k}-\widehat{\tau}_{k}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{y}}+\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{k}\|\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}\phi_{k}-\phi_{k}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{y}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\widehat{\tau}_{0}-\tau_{0}\|_{L^{2}_{y}}^{2}+\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}\right)\sup_{k=1,\dots,p}\|\tau_{k}-\widehat{\tau}_{k}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{y}}+\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{k}

\displaystyle\leq\left[1+{\mathrm{Tr}}(\Gamma_{\nu})\right]\sup_{k=0,1,\dots,p}\|\tau_{k}-\widehat{\tau}_{k}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{y}}+\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{k}.

Taking square roots of both sides, the claimed estimate (
3.26
) now follows from the trivial inequality
\sqrt{a+b}\leq\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}
for
a,b\geq 0
.
∎

C.6.
Proof of Lemma
3.16

Proof.

By the triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}

\displaystyle\leq\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}+\left(\int_{L^{2}}\|\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}(u)-\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}\circ\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}(u)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\,d\nu(u)\right)^{1/2}.

Note that by the assumed orthonormality of the
\widetilde{\tau}_{k}
, the projection can be written as follows (cp. (
3.21
))

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}\circ\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}(u)=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\langle\widetilde{\tau}_{k},u\rangle\widetilde{\tau}_{k}.

Furthermore, in terms of the dual basis
\tau_{1}^{\ast},\dots,\tau_{p}^{\ast}\in Y
, we have

\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}(u)=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\langle\tau_{k}^{\ast},u\rangle{\tau}_{k}.

In terms of this expansion we can again use the triangle inequality to obtain

(C.13)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \left(\int_{L^{2}}\right.&\|\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}(u)-\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}\circ\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}(u)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\left.\,d\nu(u)\right)^{1/2}\\
&\leq\sum_{k=1}^{p}\left(\int_{L^{2}}\|\langle\tau_{k}^{\ast},u\rangle\tau_{k}-\langle\widetilde{\tau}_{k},u\rangle\widetilde{\tau}_{k}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\,d\nu(u)\right)^{1/2}\end{aligned}

Since

(C.14)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|\langle\tau_{k}^{\ast},u\rangle\tau_{k}-\langle\widetilde{\tau}_{k},u\rangle\widetilde{\tau}_{k}\|_{L^{2}}&\leq\|\langle\tau_{k}^{\ast},u\rangle(\tau_{k}-\widetilde{\tau}_{k})\|_{L^{2}}+\|\langle\tau_{k}^{\ast}-\widetilde{\tau}_{k},u\rangle\widetilde{\tau}_{k}\|_{L^{2}}\\
&\leq\|\tau_{k}^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}}\|u\|_{L^{2}}\|\tau_{k}-\widetilde{\tau}_{k}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\tau_{k}^{\ast}-\widetilde{\tau}_{k}\|_{L^{2}}\|u\|_{L^{2}}\|\widetilde{\tau}_{k}\|_{L^{2}},\end{aligned}

we next wish to establish that under the assumptions of this lemma, we can bound

\|\tau_{k}^{\ast}-\widetilde{\tau}_{k}\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\sqrt{p}\max_{j=1,\dots,p}\|\tau_{j}-\widetilde{\tau}_{j}\|_{L^{2}},

for some absolute constant
C>0
, independent of
k
and
p
. To see this, we note that for any
k,j=1,\dots,p
, we have
\langle\tau_{k}^{\ast},\tau_{j}\rangle=\delta_{kj}
, and hence

\langle(\tau_{k}^{\ast}-\widetilde{\tau}_{k}),\widetilde{\tau}_{j}\rangle=\langle\tau_{k}^{\ast},\widetilde{\tau}_{j}\rangle-\delta_{kj}=\langle\tau_{k}^{\ast},\widetilde{\tau}_{j}\rangle-\langle\tau_{k}^{\ast},\tau_{j}\rangle=\langle\tau_{k}^{\ast},(\widetilde{\tau}_{j}-\tau_{j})\rangle.

If
u\in Y
is arbitrary, then we obtain from the above identity

\displaystyle|\langle(\tau_{k}^{\ast}-\widetilde{\tau}_{k}),u\rangle|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{j=1}^{p}\langle\widetilde{\tau}_{j},u\rangle\langle(\tau_{k}^{\ast}-\widetilde{\tau}_{k}),\widetilde{\tau}_{j}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{j=1}^{p}\langle\widetilde{\tau}_{j},u\rangle\langle\tau_{k}^{\ast},\widetilde{\tau}_{j}-\tau_{j}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{j=1}^{p}|\langle\widetilde{\tau}_{j},u\rangle|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{p}|\langle\tau_{k}^{\ast},\widetilde{\tau}_{j}-\tau_{j}\rangle|^{2}\right)^{1/2}

(C.15)

\displaystyle\leq\|u\|_{L^{2}}\,\|\tau_{k}^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}}\,\sqrt{p}\max_{j=1,\dots,p}\|\tau_{j}-\widetilde{\tau}_{j}\|_{L^{2}}.

By assumption, we have that

\sqrt{p}\max_{j=1,\dots,p}\|\tau_{j}-\widetilde{\tau}_{j}\|_{L^{2}}\leq\epsilon\leq\frac{1}{2}.

It follows that for any
k=1,\dots,p
, we have

\|\tau_{k}^{\ast}-\widetilde{\tau}_{k}\|_{L^{2}}=\sup_{\|u\|\leq 1}|\langle\tau_{k}^{\ast}-\widetilde{\tau}_{k},u\rangle|\leq\epsilon\,\|\tau_{k}^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}}\leq\epsilon\,\|\tau_{k}^{\ast}-\widetilde{\tau}_{k}\|_{L^{2}}+\epsilon\,\|\widetilde{\tau}_{k}\|_{L^{2}},

and hence

\|\tau_{k}^{\ast}-\widetilde{\tau}_{k}\|_{L^{2}}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}\|\widetilde{\tau}_{k}\|_{L^{2}}=\frac{\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}\leq 2\epsilon.

Note also that this implies

(C.16)

\displaystyle\|\tau_{k}^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|\tau_{k}^{\ast}-\widetilde{\tau}_{k}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\widetilde{\tau}_{k}\|_{L^{2}}\leq 2\epsilon+1\leq 2.

It now follows from (
C.14
), that

\displaystyle\|\langle\tau_{k}^{\ast},u\rangle\tau_{k}-\langle\widetilde{\tau}_{k},u\rangle\widetilde{\tau}_{k}\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|u\|_{L^{2}}\left((\sqrt{p}+1)\max_{j=1,\dots,p}\|\tau_{j}-\widetilde{\tau}_{j}\|_{L^{2}}.\right)

\displaystyle\leq 4\|u\|_{L^{2}}\sqrt{p}\,\max_{j=1,\dots,p}\|\tau_{j}-\widetilde{\tau}_{j}\|_{L^{2}}.

Substitution in (
C.13
), finally yields

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}\leq\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}+4\left(\int_{L^{2}}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\,d\nu(u)\right)^{1/2}\,p^{3/2}\,\max_{j=1,\dots,p}\|\tau_{j}-\widetilde{\tau}_{j}\|_{L^{2}}.

Letting

C:=4\left(\int_{L^{2}}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\,d\nu(u)\right)^{1/2},

we conclude that

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}\leq\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}+Cp^{3/2}\,\max_{j=1,\dots,p}\|\tau_{j}-\widetilde{\tau}_{j}\|_{L^{2}}\leq\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}+C\epsilon,

as claimed.

To prove the Lipschitz bound on
\mathcal{P}:L^{2}(U)\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
, let
u,u^{\prime}\in L^{2}(U)
be given, and denote
w=u-u^{\prime}
. Then

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}(u)-\mathcal{P}(u^{\prime})\|_{\ell^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{k=1}^{p}|\langle\tau_{k}^{\ast},w\rangle|^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{k=1}^{p}|\langle\tau_{k}^{\ast}-\widetilde{\tau}_{k},w\rangle|^{2}\right)^{1/2}+\left(\sum_{k=1}^{p}|\langle\widetilde{\tau}_{k},w\rangle|^{2}\right)^{1/2}

By (
C.15
) and (
C.16
), we can bound each term in the first sum by

|\langle\tau_{k}^{\ast}-\widetilde{\tau}_{k},w\rangle|\leq 2\sqrt{p}\max_{j=1,\dots,p}\|\tau_{j}-\widetilde{\tau}_{j}\|_{L^{2}}\,\|u\|_{L^{2}}.

Hence,

\left(\sum_{k=1}^{p}|\langle\tau_{k}^{\ast}-\widetilde{\tau}_{k},w\rangle|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\leq 2p\max_{j=1,\dots,p}\|\tau_{j}-\widetilde{\tau}_{j}\|_{L^{2}}\|u\|_{L^{2}}.

As the
\widetilde{\tau}_{k}
are orthonormal by assumption, the second sum can be estimated simply by

\left(\sum_{k=1}^{p}|\langle\widetilde{\tau}_{k},w\rangle|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\leq\|w\|_{L^{2}}.

Recalling that
w=u-u^{\prime}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}(u)-\mathcal{P}(u^{\prime})\|_{\ell^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+2p\max_{j=1,\dots,p}\|\tau_{j}-\widetilde{\tau}_{j}\|_{L^{2}}\right)\,\|u-u^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}}.

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+2\epsilon\right)\,\|u-u^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}}\leq 2\|u-u^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}}.

As
u,u^{\prime}\in L^{2}(U)
were arbitrary, the claimed estimate for
\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{P})
follows. Similarly, we can estimate for the reconstruction:

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}(\alpha)-\mathcal{R}(\alpha^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{p}(\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{k}^{\prime})\tau_{k}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{p}(\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{k}^{\prime})\widetilde{\tau}_{k}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{p}(\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{k}^{\prime})(\tau_{k}-\widetilde{\tau}_{k})\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}\|_{\ell^{2}}+\sum_{k=1}^{p}|\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{k}^{\prime}|\|\tau_{k}-\widetilde{\tau_{k}}\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+p^{1/2}\max_{k=1,\dots,p}\|\tau_{k}-\widetilde{\tau_{k}}\|_{L^{2}}\right)\|\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}\|_{\ell^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\epsilon\right)\|\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}\|_{\ell^{2}}\leq 2\|\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}\|_{\ell^{2}}.

Thus,
\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{R})\leq 2
.
∎

C.7.
Proof of Lemma
3.17

Proof.

We note that each element in the (real) trigonometric basis
\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{1},\dots,\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{p}
can be expressed in the form

\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{j}({x})=\cos(\kappa\cdot{x}),\quad\text{or}\quad\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{j}({x})=\sin(\kappa\cdot{x}),

for
\kappa=\kappa(j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
with
|{k}|_{\infty}\leq N
, where
N
is chosen as the smallest natural number such that
p\leq(2N+1)^{n}
. It follows from

|\kappa\cdot{x}|\leq n|\kappa|_{\infty}|{x}|_{\infty}\leq 2\pi nN,

that if
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon},\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
are neural networks, such that

(C.17)

\displaystyle\sup_{\xi\in[0,2\pi nN]}|\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}(\xi)-\cos(\xi)|,\;|\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}(\xi)-\sin(\xi)|\leq\epsilon,

then the map

{x}\mapsto((\kappa(j)\cdot{x}))_{j=1,\dots,p}\mapsto(\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}(\kappa(j)\cdot{x}),\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}(\kappa(j)\cdot{x}))_{j=1,\dots,p}

can be represented by a neural network
\mathcal{T}
with a size bounded by

\begin{gathered}\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{T})=\mathcal{O}(p\,\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon})+p\,\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon})),\\
0pt(\mathcal{N})=\mathcal{O}\left(\max\left(0pt(\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}),0pt(\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon})\right)\right).\end{gathered}

By
(
Elbrächter et al. 2021
, Theorem III.9)
, there exist
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
,
\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}
, satisfying
(
C.17
), with

\begin{gathered}\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}),\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon})=\mathcal{O}(\log(\epsilon^{-1})^{2}+\log(N)),\\
0pt(\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}),0pt(\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon})=\mathcal{O}(\log(\epsilon^{-1})^{2}+\log(N)).\end{gathered}

Finally, we note that
\log(N)\sim 1/n\log(p)=\mathcal{O}(\log(p))
. We thus conclude that for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a neural network
\mathcal{T}_{\epsilon}=(\mathcal{T}_{\epsilon,1},\dots,\mathcal{T}_{\epsilon,p})
, such that

\|\mathcal{T}_{\epsilon,j}-\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,2\pi]^{n})}\leq\epsilon,

for all
j=1,\dots,p
, and

\begin{gathered}\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{T}_{\epsilon})=\mathcal{O}(p\,(\log(p)+\log(\epsilon^{-1})^{2}),\\
0pt(\mathcal{T}_{\epsilon})=\mathcal{O}(\log(p)+\log(\epsilon^{-1})^{2}).\end{gathered}

To satisfy the estimate (
3.31
), we set
\bm{\tau}=\mathcal{T}_{\epsilon/p^{3/2}}
, for which we have

\begin{gathered}\mathrm{size}(\bm{\tau})=\mathcal{O}(p\,\log(\epsilon^{-1}p)^{2}),\\
0pt(\bm{\tau})=\mathcal{O}(\log(\epsilon^{-1}p)^{2}).\end{gathered}

and

\max_{j=1,\dots,p}\|\tau_{j}-\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,2\pi]^{n})}\leq\epsilon.

∎

C.8.
Proof of Proposition
3.19

Proof.

Proposition
3.19
follows form the following two claims:

Claim
.

If
\mu
is a non-degenerate Gaussian, then there exists a Lipschitz continuous mapping
P:X\to[0,1]
, such that
P_{\#}\mu=dx
is the uniform measure on
[0,1]
.

Claim
.

If
Y
is an infinite-dimensional Hilbert space with orthonormal basis
\{e_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, and
\gamma_{k}\geq 0
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, a sequence, such that

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k^{2}\gamma_{k}<\infty,

then there exists a Lipschitz continuous mapping
G:[0,1]\to Y
, such that the covariance operator of
G_{\#}dx
is given by

\Gamma_{G_{\#}dx}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\gamma_{k}(e_{k}\otimes e_{k}).

The sought-after map
\mathcal{G}
can then be defined as
\mathcal{G}=G\circ P:X\to Y
, where
P:X\to[0,1]
and
G:[0,1]\to Y
are defined as in the above claims.

To prove the
first claim
, we simply note that since
\mu
is a non-degenerate Gaussian measure, there exists a one-dimensional projection
L:X\to\mathbb{R}
, such that
L_{\#}\mu=\mathcal{N}(m,\sigma^{2})
is a non-degenerate Gaussian with mean
m\in\mathbb{R}
, and variance
\sigma^{2}>0
. Upon performing a translation by
\mu
and scaling by
1/\sigma
, we obtain an affine mapping
\widetilde{L}:X\to\mathbb{R}
, such that
\widetilde{L}_{\#}\mu=\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. We finally note that the error function

\mathrm{erf}(x):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{x}e^{-u^{2}/2}\,du,

maps the standard Gaussian distribution to the uniform distribution on
[0,1]
. And hence, we have
(\mathrm{erf}\circ\widetilde{L})_{\#}\mu=dx
on
[0,1]
.

To prove the
second claim
, we show that the function

G:[0,1]\to Y,\quad G(x):=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sqrt{2\gamma_{k}}\cos(2\pi kx)e_{k},

possesses the desired properties: We first note that

\|\partial_{x}G(x)\|_{Y}^{2}=(2\pi)^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2\gamma_{k}k^{2}|\sin(2\pi kx)|^{2}\leq(2\pi)^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2\gamma_{k}k^{2}<+\infty,

is uniformly bounded in
x
by assumption on
\gamma_{k}
. In particular, it follows that
x\mapsto G(x)
is Lipschitz continuous. Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\int_{Y}u\otimes u\,d(G_{\#}dx)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,k^{\prime}=1}^{\infty}2\sqrt{\gamma_{k}\gamma_{k^{\prime}}}\int_{0}^{1}\cos(2\pi kx)\cos(2\pi k^{\prime}x)(e_{k}\otimes e_{k^{\prime}})\,dx

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\gamma_{k}(e_{k}\otimes e_{k}).

∎

C.9.
Proof of Proposition
3.20

Proof.

Let
v_{1},\dots,v_{p}\in X
denote pairwise orthogonal eigenvectors corresponding to the first
p
eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{p}
of the covariance operator
\Gamma_{\mu}
.
It follows from Theorem
3.12
, that the
p
-dimensional affine subspace
V_{0}
, given by
V_{0}=v_{0}+V
, where
v_{0}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[u]
and
V=\mathrm{span}(v_{1},\dots,v_{p})
, satisfies

\int_{X}\inf_{v\in V_{0}}\|v-u\|^{2}\,d\mu(u)=\sum_{j>p}\lambda_{j}.

Let
W_{0}:=\mathcal{G}(V_{0})
. Since
\mathcal{G}:X\to Y
is linear,
W_{0}
is an affine subspace of dimension at most
p
. If
\dim(W_{0})<p
, we extend
W_{0}
to an affine subspace
W\subset Y
, such that
W_{0}\subset W
and
\dim(W)=p
, else set
W:=W_{0}
. Then, irrespective of the choice of the extension, we have

\displaystyle\int_{Y}\inf_{w\in W}\|w-u\|_{Y}^{2}\,d(\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu(u))

\displaystyle\leq\int_{Y}\inf_{w\in W_{0}}\|w-u\|_{Y}^{2}\,d(\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu(u))

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\inf_{w\in W_{0}}\|w-\mathcal{G}(u)\|_{Y}^{2}\,d\mu(u)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\inf_{v\in V_{0}}\|\mathcal{G}(v)-\mathcal{G}(u)\|_{Y}^{2}\,d\mu(u)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{X}\inf_{v\in V_{0}}\|\mathcal{G}\|^{2}\|v-u\|_{Y}^{2}\,d\mu(u)

\displaystyle=\|\mathcal{G}\|^{2}\,\sum_{j>p}\lambda_{j},

as claimed. Finally, we note that if
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{P})
are chosen such that
\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}:Y\to Y
is the orthogonal projection onto
W
, then

(\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}})^{2}=\int_{Y}\|\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}(u)-u\|^{2}\,d(\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu(u))=\int_{Y}\inf_{w\in W}\|w-u\|^{2}\,d(\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu(u)).

∎

C.10.
Proof of Theorem
3.8

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{D}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to X
is assumed to be linear, there exist
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{m}\in X
, such that

\mathcal{D}(u_{1},\dots,u_{m})=\sum_{k=1}^{m}u_{k}\psi_{k}.

Let
U:=\mathrm{span}(\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{m})\subset X
. Since
U
is a subspace of
X
of dimension at most
m
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{X}\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}-\mathrm{Id}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,d\mu

\displaystyle\geq\int_{X}\inf_{\widehat{u}\in U}\|u-\widehat{u}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,d\mu(u)

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{{\begin{subarray}{c}\widehat{U}\subset X;\\
\dim(U)=m\end{subarray}}}\int_{X}\inf_{\widehat{u}\in\widehat{U}}\|u-\widehat{u}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,d\mu(u).

By Theorem
3.10
, the infimum on the last line is attained for the subspace
\widehat{U}
spanned by the orthonormal eigenfunctions
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{m}
of the uncentered covariance operator
\Gamma_{\mu}
of
\mu
. For this space, we have

\displaystyle\int_{X}\inf_{\widehat{u}\in\widehat{U}}\|u-\widehat{u}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,d\mu(u)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\|u\|^{2}\,d\mu(u)-\sum_{k=1}^{m}\int_{X}|\langle u,\phi_{k}\rangle|^{2}\,d\mu(u)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{Tr}}(\Gamma)-\sum_{k=1}^{m}\langle\phi_{k},\Gamma\phi_{k}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}-\sum_{k=1}^{m}\lambda_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k>m}\lambda_{k}.

Thus, we conclude that

\int_{X}\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}-\mathrm{Id}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,d\mu\geq\sum_{k>m}\lambda_{k}.

∎

C.11.
Proof of Lemma
3.22

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}
is the identity on
\mathrm{span}(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{m})
, we have

\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}-\mathrm{Id}=\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}\circ P_{m}^{\perp}-P_{m}^{\perp}.

Furthermore, since
\mathrm{Im}(\mathcal{D})\subset\mathrm{span}(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{m})
, we also have
\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}(P_{m}^{\perp}u)\perp P_{m}^{\perp}u
, for all
u\in L^{2}_{x}
. Hence

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}})^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}-\mathrm{Id}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,d\mu(u)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}(P_{m}^{\perp}u)-P_{m}^{\perp}u\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,d\mu(u)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\int_{X}\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}(P_{m}^{\perp}u)\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}\,d\mu(u)}_{\displaystyle=(\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{aliasing}})^{2}}+\underbrace{\int_{X}\|P_{m}^{\perp}u\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,d\mu(u)}_{\displaystyle=(\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\perp})^{2}}.

The aliasing error

(\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{aliasing}})^{2}=\int_{X}\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}(P^{\perp}_{m}u)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,d\mu(u),

can be re-written as follows
3
3

3

we assume
\int_{X}u\,d\mu(u)=0
, but this is not essential here
: Let
\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\dots
denote the eigenfunctions of the covariance operator of
\mu
,
\Gamma\phi_{k}=\lambda_{k}\phi_{k}
, where
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots
. First, we note that there exist random variables
Z_{\ell}
(not necessarily iid), with

\mathbb{E}[Z_{\ell}Z_{\ell^{\prime}}]=\delta_{\ell\ell^{\prime}},

such that the random variable

\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}\,Z_{k}\phi_{k}\sim\mu

is distributed according to
\mu
. Then, we have

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\perp})^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\sqrt{\lambda_{\ell}}Z_{\ell}P^{\perp}_{m}\phi_{\ell}\right\|^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\sum_{\ell>m}\sqrt{\lambda_{\ell}}Z_{\ell}\phi_{\ell}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}>m}\sqrt{\lambda_{\ell}\lambda_{\ell^{\prime}}}\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{\ell}Z_{\ell^{\prime}}\right]\langle\phi_{\ell},\phi_{\ell^{\prime}}\rangle=\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}>m}\sqrt{\lambda_{\ell}\lambda_{\ell^{\prime}}}\,\delta_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell>m}\lambda_{\ell}.

With
\Phi_{M}:=(\phi_{i}(X_{j}))\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times M}
a matrix such that
\det(\Phi_{M}\Phi_{M}^{T})\neq 0
, the aliasing error is given by

\displaystyle\int_{X}\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}(P^{\perp}_{m}u)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,d\mu(u)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{m}\phi_{k}\sum_{j=1}^{m}[\Phi^{\dagger}]_{kj}\sum_{\ell>m}\sqrt{\lambda_{\ell}}\,Z_{\ell}\phi_{\ell}(X_{j})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\|\phi_{k}\|^{2}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=1}^{m}[\Phi^{\dagger}]_{kj}[\Phi^{\dagger}]_{kj^{\prime}}\quad\times

\displaystyle\qquad\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}>m}\sqrt{\lambda_{\ell}\lambda_{\ell^{\prime}}}\,\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{\ell}Z_{\ell^{\prime}}\right]\phi_{\ell}(X_{j})\phi_{\ell^{\prime}}(X_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\|\phi_{k}\|^{2}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=1}^{m}[\Phi^{\dagger}]_{kj}[\Phi^{\dagger}]_{kj^{\prime}}\sum_{\ell>m}\lambda_{\ell}\phi_{\ell}(X_{j})\phi_{\ell}(X_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell>m}\lambda_{\ell}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\Bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{m}[\Phi^{\dagger}]_{kj}\phi_{\ell}(X_{j})\Bigg)^{2}

Denote
\phi_{\ell}(\bm{X}):=(\phi_{\ell}(X_{1}),\dots,\phi_{\ell}(X_{m}))
. Then we can write the last line equivalently in the form

(\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathrm{aliasing}})^{2}=\int_{X}\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}(P^{\perp}_{m}u)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,d\mu(u)=\sum_{\ell>m}\lambda_{\ell}\|\Phi^{\dagger}\phi_{\ell}(\bm{X})\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}},

as claimed.
∎

C.12.
Proof of Lemma
3.23

Proof.

The minimal singular value of
A_{M}=\frac{|D|}{M}\Phi_{M}\Phi_{M}^{T}
is given by

\displaystyle\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}\left(A_{M}\right)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\|v\|_{\ell^{2}}=1}\langle v,A_{M}v\rangle

\displaystyle=\inf_{\|v\|_{\ell^{2}}=1}\left[\langle v,\bm{1}v\rangle-\langle v,(\bm{1}-A_{M})v\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=1-\sup_{\|v\|_{\ell^{2}}=1}\langle v,(\bm{1}-A_{M})v\rangle

\displaystyle=1-\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}\left(\bm{1}-A_{M}\right).

The maximal singular value (=spectral norm) of
\bm{1}-A_{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
can be estimated from above by

\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}\left(\bm{1}-A_{M}\right)\leq\|\bm{1}-A_{M}\|_{\mathrm{F}},

where we denote, for any matrix
A=(a_{ij})
, by
\|A\|_{\mathrm{F}}
the Frobenius norm,

\|A\|_{\mathrm{F}}=\left(\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}|a_{ij}|^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

Note that for all
i,j\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, we have that the
(i,j)
entry of
\bm{1}-A_{M}
is given by

\delta_{ij}-\frac{|D|}{M}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\phi_{i}(X_{k})\phi_{j}(X_{k}).

With
Y_{k}=-|D|\phi_{i}(X_{k})\phi_{j}(X_{k})
, this can be written in the form

\frac{1}{M}\sum_{k=1}^{m}Y_{k}-\mathbb{E}[Y_{k}],

where the
Y_{k}
are iid random variables bounded by
|D|\omega_{m}^{2}
. It follows from Hoeffding’s inequality that for any
\delta>0
, we have

\mathrm{Prob}\left[\left|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{k=1}^{m}Y_{k}-\mathbb{E}[Y_{k}]\right|\geq\delta\right]\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{2M^{2}\delta^{2}}{|D|^{2}\omega_{m}^{4}}\right).

Equivalently, for any
i,j\in\{1,\dots,m\}
we have:

\mathrm{Prob}\left[|\left[\bm{1}-A_{M}\right]_{ij}|\geq\delta\right]\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{2M^{2}\delta^{2}}{|D|^{2}\omega_{m}^{4}}\right).

It now follows that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Prob}\left[\|\bm{1}-A_{M}\|_{F}\geq\delta\right]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Prob}\left[\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}|(\bm{1}-A_{M})_{ij}|^{2}\geq\delta^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{Prob}\left[\max_{i,j=1,\dots,m}|(\bm{1}-A_{M})_{ij}|\geq\frac{\delta}{m}\right]

\displaystyle\leq m^{2}\max_{i,j=1,\dots,m}\mathrm{Prob}\left[|(\bm{1}-A_{M})_{ij}|\geq\frac{\delta}{m}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2m^{2}\exp\left(-2\left(\frac{M\delta}{|D|\omega_{m}^{2}m}\right)^{2}\right)

Choosing
\delta=1/\sqrt{2}
, we have

\mathrm{Prob}\left[\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}\left(\bm{1}-A_{M}\right)\geq 1/\sqrt{2}\right]\leq 2m^{2}\exp\left(-\left(\frac{M}{|D|\omega_{m}^{2}m}\right)^{2}\right),

and thus, from
\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}(A_{M})=1-\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{1}-A_{M})
, also

\mathrm{Prob}\left[\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}\left(A_{M}\right)<1-1/\sqrt{2}\right]\leq 2m^{2}\exp\left(-\left(\frac{M}{|D|\omega_{m}^{2}m}\right)^{2}\right).

∎

C.13.
Proof of Theorem
3.9

Proof.

Let

C=\frac{\sqrt{2}\,\max(|D|,1)}{\sqrt{2}-1}\sup_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}(1+\|\phi_{\ell}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}).

By assumption, we have
C<\infty
, and we also note that
C\geq 1
. Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{P})
be the probability space
\Omega=\prod_{\ell=1}^{\infty}D
, with probability measure
\mathcal{P}=\prod_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{Unif}(D)
, such that the iid random variables
X_{1},X_{2},\dots
are given by projection onto the corresponding factor

X_{\ell}:\Omega\to D,\quad X_{\ell}(\omega)=\omega_{\ell},

where
\omega=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\dots,)\in\Omega
.

By Lemma
3.24
, if we choose
M(m)=\lceil C\kappa m\log(m)\rceil
, with
\kappa=4
, then we have

(C.18)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Prob}\left[{\left\{\omega\,\middle|\,\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}(X_{1}(\omega),\dots,X_{M}(\omega))\leq C\textstyle\sum_{\ell>m}\lambda_{\ell}\right\}}\right]\geq 1-2m^{-2}.

where
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}=\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{M})
is the random encoding error based on the sensors
X_{1},\dots,X_{M}
. We note that asymptotically as
m\to\infty
, we have

M\sim C\kappa m\log(m)\gtrsim C\kappa m,

and hence
\log(M)\gtrsim\log(C\kappa)+\log(m)\geq\log(m)
, where we used that
C>1
and
\kappa=4
, so that
\log(C\kappa)\geq 0
in the last estimate. In particular, this implies that

M/\log(M)\lesssim\left[C\kappa m\log(m)\right]/\log(m)=C\kappa m.

It follows that, by possibly enlarging the constant
C>1
, we have

\sum_{\ell>M/C\log(M)}\lambda_{\ell}\geq\sum_{\ell>m}\lambda_{\ell},

and using also (
C.18
), we conclude that for sufficiently large
C
, we have

\mathrm{Prob}\left[{\left\{\omega\,\middle|\,\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{M})\leq C\textstyle\sum_{\ell>M/C\log(M)}\lambda_{\ell}\right\}}\right]\geq 1-C\frac{\log(M)^{2}}{M^{2}},

for all
M\in\mathbb{N}
. In particular, the probability that

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{M})>C\sum_{\ell>M/C\log(M)}\lambda_{\ell},

for infinitely many
M
, can be bounded by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Prob}

\displaystyle\Big[\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{M})>C\textstyle\sum_{\ell>M/C\log(M)}\lambda_{\ell}\text{ infinitely often}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{M_{0}\to\infty}\mathrm{Prob}\left[\bigcup_{M>M_{0}}{\left\{\omega\,\middle|\,\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}(X_{1}(\omega),\dots,X_{M}(\omega))>C\sum_{\ell>M/C\log(M)}\lambda_{\ell}\right\}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{M_{0}\to\infty}\sum_{M>M_{0}}\mathrm{Prob}\left[{\left\{\omega\,\middle|\,\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}(X_{1}(\omega),\dots,X_{M}(\omega))>C\sum_{\ell>M/C\log(M)}\lambda_{\ell}\right\}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C\limsup_{M_{0}\to\infty}\sum_{M>M_{0}}\frac{\log(M)^{2}}{M^{2}}

\displaystyle=0.

Thus, for almost all
\omega\in\Omega
, we have

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}(X_{1}(\omega),\dots,X_{M}(\omega))\leq C\sum_{\ell>M/C\log(M)}\lambda_{\ell},

for all sufficiently large
M>0
.
∎

C.14.
Proof of Lemma
3.25

Proof.

The starting point is the claim that if the covariance operator
\Gamma
be given by (
3.46
), the eigenfunctions and eigenvalues
(\phi_{k},\lambda_{k})
of
\Gamma
are given by

(C.19)

\displaystyle\phi_{k}(x)=e^{-ikx},\quad\lambda_{k}=\sqrt{2\pi}\ell\,e^{-(\ell k)^{2}/2},\quad(k\in\mathbb{Z}).

To see this, we note that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}k_{p}(x,x^{\prime})\phi_{k}(x^{\prime})\,dx^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sum_{h\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{-(x-x^{\prime}-h)^{2}/(2\ell^{2})}e^{-ikx^{\prime}}\,dx^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-(x-x^{\prime})^{2}/(2\ell^{2})}e^{-ikx^{\prime}}\,dx^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2\pi}\ell\,e^{-(\ell k)^{2}}\,e^{-ikx},

where we used that the Fourier transform on the real line
\mathbb{R}

\mathcal{F}[u](k)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}u(x^{\prime})e^{-ikx^{\prime}}\,dx^{\prime},

satisfies

\mathcal{F}[u({\,\cdot\,}-x)][k]=\mathcal{F}[u](k)\,e^{-ikx},

and we recall that for
\alpha>0
, the Fourier transform of a Gaussian is

\mathcal{F}[\exp(-\alpha x^{2})](k)=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{\alpha}}\exp(-k^{2}/4\alpha).

For simplicity, assume
m=2K+1
for
K\in\mathbb{N}
. Recall that the decoder
\mathcal{D}
for encoder
\mathcal{E}
is the discrete Fourier transform (
3.47
), it is straightforward to check that
\mathcal{E}\circ\mathcal{D}=\mathrm{Id}
, so
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D})
is an admissible encoder/decoder pair, and by the definition (
3.1
) of
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}
:

(\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}})^{2}\leq\int_{X}\left\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}-\mathrm{Id}\right\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,d\mu(u).

Let
P_{K}:L^{2}_{x}\to L^{2}_{x}
denote the orthogonal projection onto
\mathrm{span}(e^{ikx};\,|k|\leq K)
, and denote by
P_{K}^{\perp}:L^{2}_{x}\to L^{2}_{x}
the orthogonal projection onto the orthogonal complement, so that
\mathrm{Id}=P_{K}+P_{K}^{\perp}
.
We note that
\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}
is
linear
and we have
(\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E})(e^{ikx})=e^{ikx}
for all
|k|\leq K
(where
m=2K+1
). From this, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}-\mathrm{Id}

\displaystyle=\left[\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}\circ P_{K}-P_{K}\right]+\left[\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}\circ P_{K}^{\perp}-P_{K}^{\perp}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}\circ P_{K}^{\perp}-P_{K}^{\perp}

\displaystyle=P_{K}\circ\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}\circ P_{K}^{\perp}-P_{K}^{\perp},

where we used that
\mathcal{D}=P_{K}\circ\mathcal{D}
. The two terms on the last line are obviously perpendicular to each other. Hence

(C.20)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}-\mathrm{Id}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}=\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}\circ P_{K}^{\perp}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}+\|P_{K}^{\perp}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}.

We note that the non-vanishing of the first term in
C.20
is due to
aliasing
, i.e. the fact that for any point on the grid
x_{j}
,
j=1,\dots,m
, we have

e^{ikx_{j}}=e^{i(k+qm)x_{j}},\quad\text{for all }q\in\mathbb{Z}.

Therefore, the higher-order modes
e^{ikx}
for
|k|>K
map under
\mathcal{E}
to

\mathcal{E}(e^{ikx})=\mathcal{E}(e^{ik_{0}x}),

where
k_{0}\in\{-K,\dots,K\}
is the unique value such that there exists
q\in\mathbb{Z}
with
k_{0}=k+qm
.

As the functions
x\mapsto e^{ikx}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, are the eigenfunctions of the covariance operator of
\mu
. Let
\lambda_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, denote the corresponding eigenvalues. By the Karhunen-Loeve expansion for the Gaussian measure
\mu
, we can now write

\displaystyle\int_{X}\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}-\mathrm{Id}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,d\mu

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}(X)-X\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}\left(P_{K}^{\perp}X\right)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\|P_{K}^{\perp}(X)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\right],

where

X=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}X_{k}e^{ikx},

and the
X_{k}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
are iid Gaussian random variables with unit variance. Due to aliasing, we have

\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}\left(P_{K}^{\perp}X\right)=\sum_{k_{0}=-K}^{K}\left(\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\sqrt{\lambda_{k_{0}+qm}}X_{k_{0}+qm}\right)e^{ik_{0}x}.

And

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}\left(P_{K}^{\perp}X\right)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{k_{0}=-K}^{K}\left(\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\sqrt{\lambda_{k_{0}+qm}}X_{k_{0}+qm}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{0}=-K}^{K}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{q,q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\sqrt{\lambda_{k_{0}+qm}}\sqrt{\lambda_{k_{0}+q^{\prime}m}}X_{k_{0}+qm}X_{k_{0}+q^{\prime}m}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{0}=-K}^{K}\sum_{q,q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\sqrt{\lambda_{k_{0}+qm}}\sqrt{\lambda_{k_{0}+q^{\prime}m}}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{k_{0}+qm}X_{k_{0}+q^{\prime}m}\right].

As the
X_{k}
are iid with zero mean and unit variance, we have

\mathbb{E}[X_{k}X_{k^{\prime}}]=\delta_{k,k^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}1,&k=k^{\prime}\\
0,&k\neq k^{\prime}\end{cases}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}\left(P_{K}^{\perp}X\right)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{0}=-K}^{K}\sum_{q,q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\sqrt{\lambda_{k_{0}+qm}}\sqrt{\lambda_{k_{0}+q^{\prime}m}}\delta_{q,q^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{0}=-K}^{K}\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\lambda_{k_{0}+qm}

\displaystyle=\sum_{|k|>K}\lambda_{k}.

On the other hand, it is easy to see that

\mathbb{E}\left[\|P_{K}^{\perp}X\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\right]=\sum_{|k|>K}\lambda_{k}.

We thus conclude that

\displaystyle\int_{X}\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}-\mathrm{Id}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\,d\mu

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}\left(P_{K}^{\perp}X\right)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\|P_{K}^{\perp}(X)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=2\sum_{|k|>K}\lambda_{k}.

Recalling that by (
C.19
), we have

\lambda_{k}=\sqrt{2\pi}\ell\,e^{-(\ell k)^{2}/2},

we finally obtain

\displaystyle 2\sum_{|k|>K}\lambda_{k}

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{2\pi}\sum_{|k|>K}\ell\,e^{-(\ell k)^{2}/2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\pi\sqrt{2}\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{K}^{\infty}e^{-(\ell x)^{2}/2}\,\ell\,dx

\displaystyle=4\pi\left[\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{K}^{\infty}e^{-(\ell x/\sqrt{2})^{2}}\,d\left(\ell x/\sqrt{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=4\pi\,\mathrm{erfc}\left(\frac{\ell K}{\sqrt{2}}\right).

∎

C.15.
Proof of Lemma
3.26

Proof.

By Lemma
3.25
, the eigenfunctions of the covariance operator are
\phi_{k}=\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{k}
, the standard Fourier basis. By Theorem
3.9
, there exists a constant
C\geq 1
, depending on
|D|
and
\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\|\phi_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 2(2\pi)^{-d}
, such that with probability
1
in the random sensors
X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},\dots\in D
, we have for almost all
M\in\mathbb{N}
:

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}\leq C\sqrt{\sum_{k>M/C\log(M)}\lambda_{k}}.

By Lemma
3.25
(cp. (
3.48
), (
3.49
)), we have

\sqrt{\sum_{k>M/C\log(M)}\lambda_{k}}\lesssim\exp\left(-\widetilde{\gamma}\frac{M^{2}\ell^{2}}{C^{2}\log(M)^{2}}\right),\quad\forall\widetilde{\gamma}<\frac{1}{16}.

The implied constant here only depends on the value of
\widetilde{\gamma}
and on
|D|
. Thus, choosing e.g.
\widetilde{\gamma}=1/20
and noting that
|D|=2\pi
is a fixed constant, we conclude that

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}\leq C\exp\left(-\gamma\frac{M^{2}\ell^{2}}{\log(M)^{2}}\right),

for
\gamma=1/(20C^{2})
, where the constant
C>0
is independent of
\ell
and
M
.
∎

C.16.
Proof of Proposition
3.27

Proof.

Fix
u=u({\,\cdot\,};Y)
for some
Y\in[-1,1]^{\mathcal{J}}
. Let
\widehat{Y}=\widehat{Y}(\mathcal{E}(u))
be given by (
3.60
). Let
\widetilde{Y}
be given by (
3.58
), such that

\widehat{Y}_{j}=\mathrm{shrink}(\widetilde{Y}_{j}),\quad\forall\,j\in\mathcal{J}=\mathbb{Z}^{d}.

We note that since
Y_{j}\in[-1,1]
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|Y_{j}-\widehat{Y}_{j}\right|=\left|Y_{j}-\mathrm{shrink}(\widetilde{Y}_{j})\right|\leq\left|Y_{j}-\widetilde{Y}_{j}\right|,\quad\forall\,j\in\mathcal{J}=\mathbb{Z}^{d}.

But, by definition of
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
, we have that

u\mapsto u({\,\cdot\,};\widetilde{Y}(\mathcal{E}(u)))=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\widetilde{Y}_{j}(\mathcal{E}(u))\alpha_{j}\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{j}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\widehat{u}_{j}\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{j},

is the pseudo-spectral Fourier projection of
u
onto
\{\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{K}_{N}}
. In particular, it follows from standard estimates for the pseudo-spectral projection that for
u\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, with
s>n/2
, we have

\Big\|u({\,\cdot\,};Y)-u({\,\cdot\,};\widetilde{Y}(\mathcal{E}(u))\Big\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\|u\|_{H^{s}}\,N^{-s},

for some
C=C(s)>0
,
and hence also

\displaystyle\left\|u({\,\cdot\,};Y)-u({\,\cdot\,};\widehat{Y})\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\left|Y_{j}-\widehat{Y}_{j}\right|^{2}\alpha_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\left|Y_{j}-\widetilde{Y}_{j}\right|^{2}\alpha_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle=\Big\|u({\,\cdot\,};Y)-u({\,\cdot\,};\widetilde{Y}\Big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\|u\|_{H^{s}}\,N^{-s}.

∎

C.17.
Proof of Theorem
3.28

Proof.

We note that since
m=(2N+1)^{d}
, we have
N\gtrsim m^{1/d}
. Thus, it suffices to show that
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}\leq C\exp(-cN)
for some constants
c,C
independent of
N
. We now note that if
Y=(Y_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
are distributed according to the probability measure
\rho\in\mathcal{P}([-1,1]^{\mathcal{J}})
, and if
\widehat{Y}=\widehat{Y}(\mathcal{E}(u)
and
\widetilde{Y}=\widetilde{Y}(\mathcal{E}(u))
are given by (
3.60
), (
3.58
), respectively, then

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}})^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{L^{2}(D)}\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}(u)-u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,d\mu(u)

\displaystyle=\int_{L^{2}(D)}\|u({\,\cdot\,};\widehat{Y})-u({\,\cdot\,};Y)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,d\rho(Y)

As in the proof of Proposition
3.27
in appendix
C.16
, we see that – due to the exponential decay
\lesssim\exp(-|k|\ell)
of the Fourier coefficients of
u({\,\cdot\,};Y)
– there exist
C,c>0
, independent of
N
and
Y\in[-1,1]^{\mathcal{J}}
, such that the last term can be estimated by

\displaystyle\leq\int_{L^{2}(D)}\left[C\exp(-c\ell N)\right]^{2}\,d\rho(Y)

\displaystyle=\left[C\exp(-c\ell N)\right]^{2}.

This shows that
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}\leq C\exp(-c\ell N)
. And we recall that
N\lesssim m^{1/d}
, by definition of
m=(2N+1)^{d}
.

Finally, if

\mathcal{F}(\bm{y})=\mathcal{G}(u({\,\cdot\,};\bm{y})),

then for any
\bm{u}=(u_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{N}}
, we have by the definition of
\mathcal{D}(\bm{u})=u({\,\cdot\,};\widehat{Y}(\bm{u}))
(cp. (
3.59
))

\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}(\bm{u})=\mathcal{G}\left(u({\,\cdot\,},\widehat{Y}(\bm{u}))\right)=\mathcal{F}(\widehat{Y}(\bm{u})).

∎

C.18.
Proof of Corollary
3.34

Proof.

Let
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
be a neural network satisfying the estimates of Proposition
3.33
, with
N:=m
. Then

\mathcal{N}:=\mathcal{P}\circ\widehat{\mathcal{N}}=\sum_{\bm{\nu}\in\Lambda_{N}}\underbrace{(\mathcal{P}c_{\bm{\nu}})}_{\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{\bm{\nu}}(y_{\kappa(1)},\dots,y_{\kappa(N)})

is a linear combination of the neural network output of
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
, with coefficients in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
. In particular, by adding a linear output layer of size
\mathcal{O}(pN)
to the network
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
, we can represent
\mathcal{N}
as a neural network with

\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{N})\leq\mathrm{size}(\widehat{\mathcal{N}})+pN,\quad 0pt(\mathcal{N})\leq 0pt(\widehat{\mathcal{N}})+1.

The claimed bounds on the size of
\mathcal{N}
thus readily follow from the corresponding bounds for
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
. Furthermore, we have for any
\bm{y}\in[-1,1]^{\mathcal{J}}
:

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{F}(\bm{y})-\mathcal{N}(y_{\kappa(1)},\dots,y_{\kappa(N)}\right\|_{\ell^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{F}(\bm{y})-\mathcal{P}\circ\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(y_{\kappa(1)},\dots,y_{\kappa(N)}\right\|_{\ell^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{P}\|\left\|\mathcal{F}(\bm{y})-\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(y_{\kappa(1)},\dots,y_{\kappa(N)}\right\|_{V}.

The claimed error estimate thus follows from the error estimate in Proposition
3.33
.
∎

C.19.
Proof of Theorem
3.35

Proof.

The proof of this theorem requires the following simple Lemma on Neural Network Calculus,

Lemma C.10
.

Let
\mathcal{N}_{1}:\mathbb{R}^{n_{0}}\to\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}
,
\mathcal{N}_{2}:\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}\to\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}}
be two neural networks. Then the composition
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}_{2}\circ\mathcal{N}_{1}:\mathbb{R}^{n_{0}}\to\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}}
can be represented by a neural network
\mathcal{N}
with

\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{N})=\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{N}_{1})+\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{N}_{2}),\quad 0pt(\mathcal{N})=0pt(\mathcal{N}_{1})+0pt(\mathcal{N}_{2}).

C.19.1.
Proof of the Theorem
3.35

From the network size bounds of Corollary
3.34
,
\mathcal{N}
is a neural network of size

\begin{gathered}\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{N})\leq C(1+mp\log(m)\log\log(m))),\\
0pt(\mathcal{N})\leq C(1+\log(m)\log\log(m))).\end{gathered}

with a constant
C>0
, independent of
m
,
p
. By Lemma
3.29
, the map
\bm{u}\mapsto\widehat{Y}_{\kappa}(\bm{u})
can be represented by a neural network of size

\mathrm{size}(\widehat{Y}_{\kappa})\leq C(1+m\log(m)),\quad 0pt(\widehat{Y}_{\kappa})\leq C(1+\log(m)),

for a constant
C>0
, independent of
m
. Since the definition of
\widehat{Y}_{\kappa}
does not involve the projection
\mathcal{P}
at all, the constant is also independent of
p
. By the composition Lemma
C.10
it follows that
\bm{u}\mapsto\mathcal{A}(\bm{u})=(\mathcal{N}\circ\widehat{Y}_{\kappa})(\bm{u})
can be represented by a neural network with

\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{A})=\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{N})+\mathrm{size}(\widehat{Y}_{\kappa}),\,0pt(\mathcal{A})=0pt(\mathcal{N})+0pt(\widehat{Y}_{\kappa}).

We also note that the following estimate for the approximation error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{A}}
:

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{A}})^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{L^{2}(D)}\|\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}(\bm{u})-\mathcal{A}(\bm{u})\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}}\,d(\mathcal{E}_{\#}\mu)(\bm{u})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\bm{u}\in\mathrm{supp}(\mu)}\|\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}(\bm{u})-\mathcal{A}(\bm{u})\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\bm{u}\in\mathrm{supp}(\mu)}\|\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{F}(\widehat{Y}(\bm{u}))-\mathcal{N}(\widehat{Y}(\bm{u}))\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}}

By Corollary
3.34
we can further estimate the last term by

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\bm{y}\in[-1,1]^{\mathcal{J}}}\|\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{F}(\bm{y})-\mathcal{N}(y_{\kappa(1)},\dots,y_{\kappa(m)})\|_{\ell^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\mathcal{P}\|\,m^{-2s},\quad\text{where }s:=\frac{1}{q}-1>0,

provided that
\alpha_{k}\in\ell^{q}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})
with
q\in(0,1)
. But by the exponential decay assumption (
3.53
), we have
\alpha_{k}\in\ell^{q}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})
for any
q\in(0,1)
. The claim follows.
∎

Appendix D
DeepONet approximation of linear operators

In this section, we will illustrate the ability of a DeepONet to approximate bounded linear operators efficienty. A simple numerical example of the DeepONet approximation of a linear functional
\mathcal{G}:C([0,1]^{2})\to\mathbb{R}
is presented in Figure
2
, below.

(a)

(b)

(c)

(d)

Figure 2
.
Illustration of a DeepONet ((
1.1
)-(
1.3
), with
p=1
and
\sigma(x)=x
) approximating the operator
\mathcal{G}:C([0,1]^{2})\to\mathbb{R}
, defined as
\mathcal{G}(u):=\int_{\Delta}u(x)\,dx
, with integration domain
\Delta
(shown in red in (A)), with underlying measure being the law of a Gaussian random field with covariance kernel
k(x,y)=\exp(-|x-y|^{2}/2\ell^{2})
, with
\ell=0.1
.
m
sensor points
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
are drawn at random from the uniform distribution on
[0,1]^{2}
and the DeepONet is trained by minimizing the mean square loss function, with respect to
N_{u}
samples, drawn from the underlying measure. (A) Illustration of a typical sample drawn from the Gaussian random field. (B) Convergence of the test mean squared error (computed with respect to
10^{\prime}240
test samples) as
m\to\infty
, for different numbers of training samples
N_{u}
. We observe a clear exponential decay of error wrt
m
as well as “resonances” at
m=N_{u}
(double descent). (C) Convergence of the test MSE as
N_{u}\to\infty
, for different numbers of random sensor points
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
. (D) semilog plot of the MSE loss as
N_{u}\to\infty
where one observes that the test error decays as
\exp(-\sqrt{N_{u}})
when
N_{u}\leq m
. Note that similar behavior of the error is seen for more complicated operators in
Lu et al. 2019
.

Of particular interest is the observed exponential decay in the DeepONet approximation error as the number of sensors
m\to\infty
, which has also been observed for other (linear and even non-linear) problems in
Lu et al. 2019
. Can the theoretical framework developed in the present work explain such behaviour? To answer this question, a general error estimate for the DeepONet approximation of linear operators is derived in Theorem
D.2
. The estimate is then applied to a prototypical elliptic PDE (cf. example
D.4
). In the following, we consider the following setup:

Setup D.1
.

We consider data for the DeepONet approximation problem
\mu
,
\mathcal{G}
(cp. Definition
2.1
), where

•

\mathcal{G}:L^{2}(D)\to L^{2}(U)
is a bounded linear mapping,

•

\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(L^{2}(D))
is a probability measure with mean
0
, and with uniformly bounded eigenfunctions of the covariance operator
\Gamma_{\mu}
,

•

for
m\in\mathbb{N}
, the sensors
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(D)
are drawn iid random,

•

for
p\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
\widehat{\tau}_{k}
,
k=0,\dots,p
, the optimal choice for an affine reconstruction
\mathcal{R}_{\widehat{\bm{\tau}}}=\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{opt}}
for the push-forward measure
\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu
, as in Theorem
3.14
; i.e., in the present case,
\widehat{\tau}_{0}\equiv 0
, and
\widehat{\tau}_{k}
,
k=1,\dots,p
, are the first
p
eigenfunctions of the covariance operator
\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}
.

Under the above assumptions, we then have

Theorem D.2
.

Consider the setup
D.1
. Let
m,p\in\mathbb{N}
denote the number of sensors and the output dimension of the branch/trunk nets
(\bm{\beta},\bm{\tau})
, respectively. Let
\bm{\tau}
be a trunk-net approximation of
\widehat{\bm{\tau}}
, such that the associated reconstruction
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{\bm{\tau}}
and projection
\mathcal{P}
satisfy
\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{R}),\,\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P})\leq 2
. For
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the (random) encoder
\mathcal{E}:\,u\mapsto(u(x_{1}),\dots,u(x_{m}))
. Then, with probability
1
in the choice of the random sensor points, there exists a constant
C>0
, depending only on
\mu
and the measure of the domain
|D|
, such that for any
m,p\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a shallow ReLU approximator net
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
with

\mathrm{size}({\mathcal{A}})\leq 2(2+m)p,\quad 0pt(\mathcal{A})\leq 1,

and such that the DeepONet
