\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{R}_{\bm{\tau}}\circ\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{E}
, with branch net
\bm{\beta}=\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{E}
and trunk net
\bm{\tau}
satisfies the following asymptotic DeepONet approximation error estimate

(D.1)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}\leq C\sqrt{1+\|\mathcal{G}\|^{2}}\left\{\max_{k=0,\dots,p}\|\tau_{k}-\widehat{\tau}_{k}\|_{L^{2}(U)}+\sqrt{\sum_{\ell>p}\lambda_{\ell}}+\sqrt{\sum_{\ell>\frac{m}{C\log(m)}}\lambda_{\ell}}\right\},

for almost all
m
, as
m\to\infty
.

Proof.

By the DeepONet error decomposition of Theorem
3.3
and the assumed Lipschitz bounds
\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{R}),\,\mathrm{Lip}(\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P})\leq 2
, we have

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}\leq 2\|\mathcal{G}\|\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}+2\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{A}}+\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}.

We first observe that for any choice of the (affine) encoder/decoder and reconstruction/projection pairs
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D})
,
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{P})
, and for a linear mapping
\mathcal{G}
there exists an
exact, affine approximator

\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
, such that
\mathcal{A}(\bm{u})=\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}(\bm{u})
for all
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{A}
can be represented by a shallow ReLU neural net of the claimed size, on account of the fact that
Ax+b=\sigma(Ax+b)-\sigma(-(Ax+b))
has an exact representation for the ReLU activation function
\sigma(x)=\max(x,0)
. Thus, the approximation error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{A}}
can be made to vanish in this case,
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{A}}=0
.

Under the assumptions of this theorem, the random encoding error has been estimated in Theorem
3.9
, where it is shown that there exists a constant
C\geq 1
, depending only on the uniform upper bound of the eigenfunctions of
\Gamma_{\mu}
, and on
|D|
, such that (for almost all
m\in\mathbb{N}
):

(D.2)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}\leq C\sqrt{\sum_{\ell>m/C\log(m)}\lambda_{\ell}},

as
m\to\infty
, with probability
1
in the iid random sensors
x_{1},x_{2},\dots\sim\mathrm{Unif}(D)
. Here,
C=C(|D|,\mu)
is a constant.

Finally, we estimate the reconstruction error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}
: By Proposition
3.15
, we have

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}\leq\sqrt{1+{\mathrm{Tr}}(\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu})}\max_{k=0,\dots,p}\|\widehat{\tau}_{k}-\tau_{k}\|_{L^{2}(U)}+\sqrt{\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{k}^{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}},

where
\lambda^{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}_{1}\geq\lambda^{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}_{2}\geq\dots
denote the eigenvalues of the covariance operator
\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}
of the push-forward measure
\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu
. By Proposition
3.20
, we can estimate

\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{k}^{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}\leq\|\mathcal{G}\|^{2}\sum_{k>p}\lambda_{k},

in terms of the eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots
, of the covariance operator
\Gamma_{\mu}
of
\mu
, and in particular
{\mathrm{Tr}}(\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu})\leq\|\mathcal{G}\|^{2}{\mathrm{Tr}}(\Gamma_{\mu})\leq C\|\mathcal{G}\|^{2}
, where
C=C(\mu)
depends only on
\mu
. It thus, follows that there exists a constant
C=C(\mu)
, such that

(D.3)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}\leq C\sqrt{1+\|\mathcal{G}\|^{2}}\left\{\max_{k=0,\dots,p}\|\widehat{\tau}_{k}-\tau_{k}\|_{L^{2}(U)}+\sqrt{\sum_{\ell>p}\lambda_{\ell}}\right\}.

Combining (
D.2
) and (
D.3
) with the error decomposition, and taking into account that
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{A}}=0
, yields the desired result.
∎

Remark D.3
.

From the proof of Theorem
D.2
, it is clear that to achieve the error bound (
D.1
), we can replace the shallow ReLU approximator net
\mathcal{A}
, by a single-layer affine approximator
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
,
\mathcal{A}(\bm{u}):=A\cdot\bm{u}+b
, where
A\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times m}
,
b\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. This clearly corresponds to the use of the linear activation function
\sigma(x)=x
in the branch net. In contrast, the activation function in the trunk net must be kept non-linear to be able to approximate the optimal trunk net
\widehat{\bm{\tau}}
.

Example D.4
.

To illustrate Theorem
D.2
, we consider the following example of a linear operator
\mathcal{G}:L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,
f\mapsto v
, mapping the source term
f
to the solution of the PDE
\Delta v=f-\fint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}f\,dx
, with periodic boundary conditions and imposing
\fint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}v\,dx=0
. It is well-known that this operator is bounded; in fact, we have
\|\mathcal{G}\|\leq 1
. We fix the initial measure
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
as a Gaussian random field, with Karhunen-Loeve expansion

f=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\alpha_{k}X_{k}\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{k},

where
|\alpha_{k}|\leq\exp(-\ell|k|)
have exponential decay with typical length scale
\ell>0
,
\{\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is the standard Fourier basis on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, and the
X_{k}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
are iid Gaussian random variables. In this case, the eigenfunctions of the associated covariance operator
\Gamma_{\mu}
of
\mu
are given by the
\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{k}
, with corresponding eigenvalues
\alpha_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. In particular, it follows that there exists constants
C,c>0
, depending only on
d
and
\ell
, such that the last two terms in (
D.1
) can be bounded from above by

\leq C\exp\left(-c\,p^{1/d}\right)+C\exp\left(-\frac{c\,m^{1/d}}{\log(m)^{1/d}}\right).

In particular, Theorem
D.2
implies that for any fixed
\sigma>0
, and with
p\sim\log(\epsilon^{-1})^{d}
,
m\sim\log(\epsilon^{-1})^{d(1+\sigma)}
, we can achieve an overall DeepONet approximation error

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}\lesssim\epsilon+\max_{j=0,\dots,p}\|\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{j}-\tau_{j}\|_{L^{2}(U)},

where we have taken into account that the Fourier basis
\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{j}
is an eigenbasis also for the push-forward measure
\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu
. Furthermore, it follows from Lemma
3.17
, that for any fixed
\sigma>0
, there exists a trunk net
\bm{\tau}
with asymptotic size (as
\epsilon\to 0
)

(D.4)

\displaystyle\mathrm{size}(\bm{\tau})\lesssim\log(\epsilon^{-1})^{d+2+\sigma},\quad 0pt(\bm{\tau})\lesssim\log(\epsilon^{-1})^{2+\sigma},

and such that
\max_{k=0,\dots,p}\|\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{j}-\tau_{j}\|_{L^{2}(U)}<\epsilon
. Thus, for the present example, an overall DeepONet error
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}\lesssim\epsilon
can be achieved with a DeepONet
(\bm{\beta},\bm{\tau})
with trunk net
\bm{\tau}
satisfying the size bounds (
D.4
), and a branch net
\bm{\beta}
of size

(D.5)

\displaystyle\mathrm{size}(\bm{\beta})\lesssim\log(\epsilon^{-1})^{2d+\sigma},\quad 0pt(\bm{\beta})\lesssim 1,

for any fixed
\sigma>0
. The implied constants here depend on the length scale
\ell>0
, the dimension
d
and the additional parameter
\sigma>0
, which was introduced to avoid the appearance of multiple logarithms.

Appendix E
Proofs of Results in Section
4

E.1.
Proof of Lemma
4.1

Proof.

Let
v,v^{\prime}
solve (
4.2
) with forcing
u,u^{\prime}
, respectively. Then we can write

(E.1)

\displaystyle\frac{d(v-v^{\prime})}{dt}=G(v,v^{\prime})(v-v^{\prime})+(u-u^{\prime}),

where

G(v,v^{\prime})=\int_{0}^{1}g^{(1)}(sv+(1-s)v^{\prime})\,ds,

so that
|G(v,v^{\prime})|\leq\|g^{(1)}\|_{L^{\infty}}
for all
v,v^{\prime}
. It follows readily from (
E.1
) that

\frac{d}{dt}|v-v^{\prime}|^{2}\leq C|v-v^{\prime}|^{2}+|u-u^{\prime}|^{2},

for some
C>0
depending only on
\|g^{(1)}\|_{L^{\infty}}
. Gronwall’s inequality then implies that

|v-v^{\prime}|^{2}(t)\leq\int_{0}^{t}|u-u^{\prime}|^{2}\,ds\,e^{Ct}\leq\|u-u^{\prime}\|^{2}_{L^{2}([0,T])}\,e^{CT}.

The claim follows by integration over
t\in[0,T]
.
∎

E.2.
Proof of Lemma
4.3

Proof.

We first show that there exists a constant
C>0
, depending only on the final time
T
, such that

\|v\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\|u\|_{L^{2}}.

To this end, we simply note that integrating (
4.2
) from
0
to
T
and taking into account that
v(0)=0
, we have

\displaystyle|v(t)|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}|g(v(s))|\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}|u(s)|\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\|g^{(1)}\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{0}^{t}|v(s)|\,ds+\sqrt{T}\|u\|_{L^{2}([0,T])}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\int_{0}^{t}|v(s)|\,ds+\sqrt{T}\|u\|_{L^{2}([0,T])}.

Gronwall’s inequality implies that

|v(t)|\leq\sqrt{T}\|u\|_{L^{2}([0,T])}e^{C_{1}T},\quad\forall\,t\in[0,T].

Thus,
\|v\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])}\leq C\|u\|_{L^{2}([0,T]}
, where
C=\sqrt{T}\exp(C_{1}T)
depends only on
T
. For general
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we take
k
derivatives of (
4.2
) to find:

(E.2)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}v^{(k)}=g^{(k)}(v)\,v^{(k)}+P_{k}\left(\{g^{(\ell)}\}_{\ell=1}^{k-1},\{v^{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{k-1}\right)+u^{(k)}.

Here,
P_{k}=P_{k}\left(\{g^{(\ell)}\}_{\ell=1}^{k-1},\{v^{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{k-1}\right)
is a polynomial of the following form

P_{k}\left(\{g^{(\ell)}\}_{\ell=1}^{k-1},\{v^{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{k-1}\right)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k-1}g^{(\ell)}(v)\left(\sum_{\bm{\gamma}}C_{\ell,\bm{\gamma}}\prod_{j=1}^{N_{\bm{\gamma}}}v^{(\gamma_{j})}\right).

The sum in the parentheses is over the (finite) set of
\bm{\gamma}=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N_{\bm{\gamma}}})
,
\gamma_{j}\in\mathbb{N}
, satisfying

\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\bm{\gamma}}}\gamma_{j}=k,\quad 1\leq\gamma_{j}<k,\quad\forall\,j=1,\dots,N_{\bm{\gamma}}.

The coefficients
C_{\ell,\bm{\gamma}}
are combinatorial coefficients that depend only on
k
, and which can in principle be determined for any given
k
. We will prove the claimed estimate on
\|v^{(k)}\|_{L^{\infty}}
by induction on
k=1,2,\dots
. We will first estimate the size of the polynomial
P_{k}
: To this end, note that for
k=1
, the sum defining
P_{k}
is necessarily empty and thus, we have
P_{k}\equiv 0
. For
k>1
, we assume that the claimed inequality for
\|v^{(k)}\|_{L^{\infty}}
has already been proven to hold for derivatives
v^{(\gamma_{j})}
of order
\gamma_{j}\leq k-1
. In this case, we can estimate

|P_{k}|\leq\sum_{\ell=1}^{k-1}\|g^{(\ell)}\|_{L^{\infty}}\left(\sum_{\bm{\gamma}}|C_{\ell,\bm{\gamma}}|\prod_{j=1}^{N_{\bm{\gamma}}}\|v^{(\gamma_{j})}\|_{L^{\infty}}\right),

and

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{N_{\bm{\gamma}}}\|v^{(\gamma_{j})}\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{j=1}^{N_{\bm{\gamma}}}A_{\gamma_{j}}\left(1+\|u\|_{H^{\gamma_{j}}}\right)^{\gamma_{j}}

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{j=1}^{N_{\bm{\gamma}}}A_{\gamma_{j}}\left(1+\|u\|_{H^{k}}\right)^{\gamma_{j}}

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{j=1}^{N_{\bm{\gamma}}}A_{\gamma_{j}}\right)\left(1+\|u\|_{H^{k}}\right)^{k}.

Thus, taking into account that the derivatives
g^{(\ell)}(v)
of order
\ell\leq k-1
are assumed to be uniformly bounded,
|g^{(\ell)}(v)|\leq C_{k}
, we can now estimate

|P_{k}|\leq C(1+\|u\|_{H^{k}}^{k}),\quad(k>1),

where
C=C(k,g)>0
is a constant depending only on
k
and
g
. As pointed out above, this inequality for
P_{k}
holds trivially also for
k=1
, since
P_{k}\equiv 0
, in this case. Inserting the above estimate in (
E.2
), and integrating over
[0,t]
, we obtain

\displaystyle|v^{(k)}(t)|

\displaystyle\leq\|g^{(k)}\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{0}^{t}|v^{(k)}(s)|\,ds+C(1+\|u\|_{H^{k}})^{k}+\int_{0}^{T}|u^{(k)}(s)|\,ds

\displaystyle\leq C_{k}\int_{0}^{t}|v^{(k)}(s)|\,ds+C(1+\|u\|_{H^{k}})^{k}+\sqrt{T}\|u\|_{H^{k}}.

Increasing the constant
C
if necessary, we can absorb the last term in the second term and conclude that there exists a constant
C=C(k,g,T)>0
, such that

|v^{(k)}(t)|\leq C_{k}\int_{0}^{t}|v^{(k)}(s)|\,ds+C(1+\|u\|_{H^{k}})^{k}.

Gronwall’s inequality now yields

|v^{(k)}(t)|\leq Ce^{C_{k}T}(1+\|u\|_{H^{k}})^{k},

for all
t\in[0,T]
. Hence, for
A_{k}=Ce^{C_{k}T}
, we have

\|v^{(k)}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])}\leq A_{k}(1+\|u\|_{H^{k}})^{k},

where
A_{k}
is independent of
u
.
∎

E.3.
Proof of Lemma
4.4

Proof.

The Legendre polynomials form an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}([0,T])
. For
p\in\mathbb{N}
, let
P_{p}:L^{2}([0,T])\to L^{2}([0,T])
denote the orthogonal projection onto the span of the first
p
Legendre polynomials,
\mathrm{span}(\widetilde{\tau}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\tau}_{p})
. By
(
Canuto & Quarteroni 1982
, Theorem 2.3)
, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a constant
C=C(T,k)>0
, such that

\|u-P_{p}u\|_{L^{2}([0,T])}\leq Cp^{-k}\|u\|_{H^{k}([0,T])}.

Thus, if
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{\widetilde{\bm{\tau}}}
is the reconstruction with trunk net
\widetilde{\bm{\tau}}=(0,\widetilde{\tau}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\tau}_{p})
, and if
\mathcal{P}
denotes the corresponding optimal projection (
3.20
), then
P_{p}=\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}
, and

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{X}\|\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}(v)-v\|_{L^{2}([0,T])}^{2}\,d(\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu)(v)\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{p^{k}}\left(\int_{X}\|v\|_{H^{k}([0,T])}^{2}\,d(\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu)(v)\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\frac{C}{p^{k}}\left(\int_{X}\|\mathcal{G}(u)\|_{H^{k}([0,T])}^{2}\,d\mu(u)\right)^{1/2}

By Lemma
4.3
, we can furthermore estimate
\|v\|_{H^{k}}\leq C(1+\|u\|_{H^{k}})^{k}
, for some constant
C>0
, depending on
T
and
k
. Hence, we find

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{R}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{p^{k}}\left(\int_{X}\left(1+\|u\|_{H^{k}([0,T])}\right)^{2k}\,d\mu(u)\right)^{1/2},

where
C=C(k,T)>0
is a constant independent of
p
.
∎

E.4.
Proof of Lemma
4.6

Proof.

We have by (
4.12
)

|v_{i}(t)|\leq\alpha+\int_{0}^{t}|v_{i}(s)|\beta(s)\,ds,

where
\alpha=\|\mathrm{Im}(u)\|_{L^{\infty}}T
, and
\beta(s)=\gamma(1+\exp(|v_{i}(s)|))
. By Gronwall’s inequality, it follows that

\displaystyle|v_{i}(t)|

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\beta(s)\,ds\right)

\displaystyle=\|\mathrm{Im}(u)\|_{L^{\infty}}T\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\gamma\left(1+e^{|v_{i}(s)|}\right)\,ds\right).

Let

\delta:=\frac{1}{2T\exp(\gamma(1+e)T)}.

We claim that if
\|\mathrm{Im}(u)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\delta
, then
|v_{i}(t)|<1
for all
t\in[0,T]
. Suppose this was not the case. If there exists
t\in[0,T]
such that
|v_{i}(t)|\geq 1
, then the set

B:={\left\{t\in[0,T]\,\middle|\,|v_{i}(t)|\geq 1\right\}}

is nonempty. Let
t_{0}
be given by

t_{0}=\inf B.

By the continuity of
t\mapsto v_{i}(t)
,
B
is a closed set. In particular, this implies that
t_{0}\in B
. Since
v_{i}(0)=0
, we must have
t_{0}>0
, and
|v_{i}(t)|<1
for all
t\in[0,t_{0})
.
But then,

\displaystyle|v_{i}(t_{0})|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathrm{Im}(u)\|_{L^{\infty}}T\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\gamma\left(1+e^{|v_{i}(s)|}\right)\,ds\right)

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathrm{Im}(u)\|_{L^{\infty}}\,T\exp(\gamma(1+e)T)

\displaystyle\leq\delta\,T\exp(\gamma(1+e)T)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}<1,

leads to a contradiction to the assumption that
|v_{i}(t_{0})|\geq 1
. Thus, we conclude that the set
B
must in fact be empty, i.e. that

|v_{i}(s)|<1,\quad\text{for all }s\in[0,T].

∎

E.5.
Proof of Lemma
4.7

Proof.

We argue by contradiction. Suppose the claim is not true. Then there exists
u:[0,T]\to\mathbb{C}
,
u\in L^{\infty}([0,T])
, and
0<T_{1}<T
such that the solution
4
4

4

We note that short-time existence follows from the fact that the right-hand side is locally Lipschitz continuous in
v
, so that
T_{1}>0
.
of (
4.11
) is defined on
[0,T_{1})
, but
\lim_{t\nearrow T_{1}}|v(t)|=\infty
. Since the right-hand side of (
4.11
) is uniformly Lipschitz continuous in
v_{r}
, this can only be the case, if
\lim_{t\nearrow T_{1}}|v_{i}(t)|=\infty
. In particular, there exists
T_{0}<T_{1}
, such that
|v_{i}(T_{0})|>1
. But then, this would imply the existence of a solution of (
4.11
), which is defined on
[0,T_{0}]
, for which
\sup_{s\in[0,T]}|\mathrm{Im}(u(s))|\leq\delta
, and such that we have
\sup_{s\in[0,T_{0}]}|v_{i}(s)|\geq|v_{i}(T_{0})|>1
. This is clearly in contradiction with Lemma
4.6
. By contradiction, it thus follows that we must have
T_{0}=T
, and hence the solution of (
4.11
) exists on
[0,T]
for any
u\in L^{\infty}([0,T])
, with
\|\mathrm{Im}(u)\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])}\leq\delta
. Furthermore, it follows from Lemma
4.6
that
\sup_{s\in[0,T]}|v_{i}(s)|\leq 1
in this case.
∎

E.6.
Proof of Lemma
4.8

Proof.

Fix
z_{k}\in E_{\rho_{k}}
for
k\neq j
. For
z_{j}\in E_{\rho_{j}}
, and by slight abuse of notation, let us denote the forcing by
u(t,z_{j})=u(t,\bm{z})
, and the corresponding solution of the pendulum equations (
4.11
) by
v(t,z_{j})=\mathcal{F}(\bm{z})(t)
, where
\bm{z}=(z_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
. Then the claim is that
z_{j}\mapsto v(t,z_{j})
is complex-differentiable in
z_{j}\in E_{\rho_{j}}
. We note that
v(t,z_{j})
is the unique fixed point of

v(t,z_{j})=\int_{0}^{t}G(s,z_{j},v(s,z_{j}))\,ds,

where
G(t,z_{j},v):=g(v)+u(t,z_{j})
. To show boundedness, we note that by (
4.13
), the assumed
(\bm{b},\delta)
-admissibility of
\bm{\rho}
and Lemma
4.7
, the solution
t\mapsto v(t,z_{j})
exists for any
z_{j}\in E_{\rho_{j}}
, and that
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\mathrm{Im}(v(t,z_{j}))|\leq 1
. But then, also the real part
v_{r}(t):=\mathrm{Re}(v(t,z_{j}))
is bounded, because from (
4.11
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{dv_{r}(t)}{dt}\right|

\displaystyle\leq|g_{r}(v_{r}(t),v_{i}(t))|+|\mathrm{Re}(U(t))|

\displaystyle\leq|v_{r}(t)|+k\cosh(|v_{i}(t)|)+|\mathrm{Re}(U(t))|

\displaystyle\leq|v_{r}(t)|+k\cosh(1)+\|\mathrm{Re}(U)\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])},

which implies by Gronwall’s inequality that

|v_{r}(t)|\leq\left(k\cosh(1)+\|\mathrm{Re}(U)\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])}\right)Te^{T},

for all
t\in[0,T]
. We furthermore note that

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{Re}(U)\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}|\mathrm{Re}(z_{k})|\alpha_{k}\|\psi_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}|\mathrm{Re}(z_{k})|b_{k}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\rho_{k}b_{k}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}b_{k}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(\rho_{k}-1)b_{k}

\displaystyle\leq\|\bm{b}\|_{\ell^{1}(\mathbb{N})}+\delta<\infty,

is uniformly bounded for all
\bm{z}\in E_{\bm{\rho}}
for any admissible
\bm{\rho}
. This shows that

\displaystyle\sup_{\bm{z}\in E_{\bm{\rho}}}\|v\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}([0,T])}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\bm{z}\in E_{\bm{\rho}}}\sqrt{T}\|v\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\bm{z}\in E_{\bm{\rho}}}\sqrt{T}\|v_{r}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])}+\sup_{\bm{z}\in E_{\bm{\rho}}}\sqrt{T}\|v_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])}

\displaystyle\leq T\sqrt{T}e^{T}\left(\gamma\cosh(1)+\|\bm{b}\|_{\ell^{1}(\mathbb{N})}+\delta\right)+\sqrt{T}

\displaystyle=:C,

is uniformly bounded for all
(\bm{b},\epsilon)
-admissible
\bm{\rho}
.

Finally, we prove the holomorphy of
z_{j}\mapsto v(t,z_{j})
: By definition,
z_{j}\mapsto u(t,z_{j})
is an affine function. Hence
z_{j}\mapsto G(t,z_{j},v)
is also an affine function of
z_{j}
, and in particular differentiable in
z_{j}
. Since
v(t,z_{j})
exists and is bounded for all
z_{j}\in E_{\rho_{j}}
, it then follows from the general theory of parametric ODEs that
\partial_{z_{j}}v(t,z_{j})
exists and that

\partial_{z_{j}}v(t,z_{j})=\int_{0}^{t}\left\{\partial_{z_{j}}G(s,z_{j},v(s,z_{j}))+\partial_{v}G(s,z_{j},v(s,z_{j}))\partial_{z_{j}}v(s,z_{j})\right\}\,ds.

This implies that
z_{j}\mapsto v(t,z_{j})
is a holomorphic mapping.
∎

E.7.
Proof of Lemma
4.14

Proof.

The difference
w=u-u^{\prime}
is a solution of the equation

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\left(a\nabla w\right)

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot\left(a\nabla u\right)-\nabla\cdot\left(a\nabla u^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=f-\nabla\cdot\left(a\nabla u^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot\left(a^{\prime}\nabla u^{\prime}\right)-\nabla\cdot\left(a\nabla u^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot\left((a^{\prime}-a)\nabla u^{\prime}\right).

By elliptic theory, we thus have

\displaystyle\|u-u^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(D)}

\displaystyle=\|w\|_{L^{2}(D)}\leq\|\nabla\cdot\left((a^{\prime}-a)\nabla u^{\prime}\right)\|_{H^{-1}(D)}

\displaystyle\leq\|(a^{\prime}-a)\nabla u^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(D)}\leq\|a^{\prime}-a\|_{L^{\infty}(D)}\|\nabla u^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(D)}

\displaystyle\leq\|a^{\prime}-a\|_{L^{\infty}(D)}\|u^{\prime}\|_{H^{1}_{0}(D)}\leq\|a^{\prime}-a\|_{L^{\infty}(D)}C\|f\|_{L^{2}(D)}.

∎

E.8.
Proof of Lemma
4.16

Proof.

It is well-known that if
u
is a solution of (
4.18
), with smooth coefficient
a(x)
and right-hand side
f\in H^{k}
, then
u\in H^{k+1}
. The main point of this lemma is the explicit dependence on the norm of
a
, which will be required to estimate the reconstruction error. Let
\bm{k}=(k_{1},\dots,k_{m})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{m}
denote any multi-index. Then by differentiation of (
4.18
), we find

(E.3)

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\left(a(x)\nabla\partial_{x}^{\bm{k}}u\right)=-\nabla\cdot\left(\sum_{\bm{\ell}<\bm{k}}{\bm{k}\choose\bm{\ell}}\partial_{x}^{\bm{k-\ell}}a(x)\nabla\partial_{x}^{\bm{\ell}}u\right)+\partial_{x}^{\bm{k}}f.

where
\ell
runs over all indices
\bm{\ell}=(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{m})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, such that
\ell_{j}\leq k_{j}
for all
j=1,\dots,m
, with a strict inequality for at least one
j
. We also write

{\bm{k}\choose\bm{\ell}}:=\prod_{j=1}^{m}{k_{j}\choose\ell_{j}}.

Integrating (
E.3
) against
\partial_{x}^{\bm{k}}u
, with
k:=|\bm{k}|=k_{1}+\dots+k_{m}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\lambda\|\nabla\partial_{x}^{\bm{k}}u\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{T}^{n}}a(x)|\nabla\partial_{x}^{\bm{k}}(x)|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle\leq C_{k}\sum_{\bm{\ell}<\bm{k}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{n}}\left|\partial_{x}^{\bm{k-\ell}}a(x)\right|\left|\nabla\partial_{x}^{\bm{\ell}}u(x)\right|\left|\nabla\partial_{x}^{\bm{k}}u(x)\right|\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\mathbb{T}^{n}}|\partial_{x}^{\bm{k}}f||\partial_{x}^{\bm{k}}u|\,dx

\displaystyle\leq C_{k}\|a\|_{C^{k}}\|u\|_{H^{k}}\|\nabla\partial_{x}^{\bm{k}}u\|_{L^{2}}+\|\partial_{x}^{\bm{k}}f\|_{L^{2}}\|u\|_{H^{k}}.

Using the inequality
ab\leq 2^{-1}\epsilon a^{2}+(2\epsilon)^{-1}b^{2}
for
a,b,\epsilon>0
, we find

\displaystyle\lambda\|\nabla\partial_{x}^{\bm{k}}u\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{k}^{2}\|a\|_{C^{k}}^{2}}{2\lambda}\|u\|_{H^{k}}^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{2}\|\nabla\partial_{x}^{\bm{k}}u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{2}\|\partial_{x}^{\bm{k}}f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2\lambda}\|u\|_{H^{k}}^{2},

and hence

\|\nabla\partial_{x}^{\bm{k}}u\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}\leq(C_{k}^{2}\|a\|_{C^{k}}^{2}+1)\frac{\|u\|_{H^{k}}^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}+\|\partial_{x}^{\bm{k}}f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Summing the last estimate over all
|\bm{k}|=k
, and increasing the constant
C_{k}
, if necessary, we obtain

\|u\|^{2}_{H^{k+1}}\leq C_{k}(\|a\|_{C^{k}}^{2}+1)\|u\|_{H^{k}}^{2}+\|f\|_{H^{k}}^{2},

where the new constant
C_{k}=C_{k}(k,\lambda)
now depends on both
k
and
\lambda
. Repeating the same argument for any
\ell\leq k
, we also find

\displaystyle\|u\|^{2}_{H^{\ell+1}}\leq C_{\ell}(\|a\|_{C^{\ell}}^{2}+1)\|u\|_{H^{\ell}}^{2}+\|f\|_{H^{\ell}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}(\|a\|_{C^{k}}^{2}+1)\|u\|_{H^{\ell}}^{2}+\|f\|_{H^{k}}^{2},

with
C^{\prime}:=\max_{\ell\leq k}C_{\ell}
a fixed constant depending only on
k
and
\lambda
. Writing the last inequality in the form

\|u\|^{2}_{H^{\ell+1}}\leq A\|u\|^{2}_{H^{\ell}}+\|f\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

it follows by induction on
\ell=0,\dots,k
, that

\|u\|^{2}_{H^{k+1}}\leq A^{k}\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\sum_{\ell=0}^{k-1}A^{\ell}.

For
k=0
, we have the well-known bound
\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\leq C^{\prime\prime}\|f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
with
C^{\prime\prime}=C^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)
. We may wlog assume
C^{\prime\prime}\geq 1
. In particular, we then conclude that

\|u\|^{2}_{H^{k+1}}\leq C^{\prime\prime}\|f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}A^{\ell},

where
A:=C^{\prime}(\|a\|_{C^{k}}+1)
. Finally, for fixed
k
, we note that since
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}(k,\lambda)
,
C^{\prime\prime}=C^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)
, there exists a constant
C
depending only on
k
and
\lambda
, but independent of
\|a\|_{C^{k}}
, such that

C^{\prime\prime}\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}A^{\ell}=C^{\prime\prime}\,C^{\prime}\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}(1+\|a\|_{C^{k}}^{2})^{\ell}\leq C(1+\|a\|^{2k}_{C^{k}}).

For such
C=C(k,\lambda)>0
, we conclude that

\|u\|^{2}_{H^{k+1}}\leq C\|f\|_{H^{k}}^{2}(1+\|a\|_{C^{k}}^{2k}),

as claimed.
∎

E.9.
Proof of Theorem
4.22

To prove Theorem
4.22
, we recall some notions and results from the theory of second order parabolic equations (cp.
Lieberman 1996
). First, let
U=[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
denote the domain of the solution of the parabolic equation (
4.3
). We denote by (cp.
(
Lieberman 1996
, p. 46)
)

\mathcal{C}^{k,\alpha}(U):={\left\{v\in C(U)\,\middle|\,|v|_{(k,\alpha)}<\infty\right\}},\quad k\in\mathbb{N},\;\alpha\in(0,1),

the parabolic Hölder space on
U
, where

|v|_{(k,\alpha)}:=\sum_{\beta+2j\leq k}\sup_{U}|\partial_{x}^{\beta}\partial^{j}_{t}v|+[v]_{(k,\alpha)},

and

[v]_{(k,\alpha)}:=\sum_{\beta+2j=k}[\partial^{\beta}_{x}\partial^{j}_{t}v]_{\alpha},

and where the parabolic Hölder semi-norm
[w]_{\alpha}
of a function
w
is defined by

[w]_{\alpha}:=\sup_{(t,x)\in U}\sup_{(t^{\prime},x^{\prime})\neq(t,x)}\frac{|w(t,x)-w(t^{\prime},x^{\prime})|}{\left(|x-x^{\prime}|+|t-t^{\prime}|^{1/2}\right)^{\alpha}}.

We then have the following Schauder estimate:

Lemma E.1

(Schauder estimate)
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1)
. For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a constant
C>0
, such that if
v
is a solution to

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}v-\Delta v&=f,\\
v(t=0)&=0,\end{aligned}\right.

with
f\in\mathcal{C}^{(k,\alpha)}(U)
, then

|v|_{(k+2,\alpha)}\leq C|f|_{(k,\alpha)}+\|u\|_{C^{k+2,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}.

Proof.

For
k=0
, this follows e.g. from
(
Lieberman 1996
, Theorem 4.28)
. For
k>0
, we note that the coefficients in equation
\partial_{t}v-\Delta v=f
are constant, and hence we can apply the base-case to partial derivatives of the equation (more precisely, finite-difference approximations thereof, and take the limit).
∎

We also note the following strong
L^{p}
estimate for parabolic equations:

Lemma E.2
.

Let
p\in[2,\infty)
. There exists a constant
C>0
, such that if
v\in L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
is a weak solution of
\partial_{t}v-\Delta v=f
, for
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, and with initial data
v(t=0)=u
, then

\|v\|_{W^{1,p}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leq C\left(\|f\|_{L^{p}}+\|u\|_{W^{2,p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\right).

Proof.

Theorem 7.32 of
Lieberman 1996
provides a sharper estimate, from which the claim readily follows.
∎

Corollary E.3
.

Let
d\in\{2,3\}
. There exists a constant
C>0
, and
\alpha\in(0,1)
, such that if
f\in L^{\infty}
, and
u\in C^{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
, and if
v
solves
\partial_{t}v-\Delta v=f
, with initial data
v(t=0)=u
, then

\|v\|_{(0,\alpha)}\leq C\left(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|u\|_{C^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\right).

Proof.

This follows directly from Lemma
E.2
and the fact that by Sobolev embedding, we have
W^{1,p}{\hookrightarrow}C^{\alpha}
, for
\alpha\leq 1-(d+1)/p
for
p>d+1
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.22
.

The claim follows from a bootstrap argument: From the a priori estimate of Theorem
4.21
for the Allen-Cahn equation, we know that for initial data
\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 1
, we have
\|v(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 1
for all
t\in[0,T]
. In particular, it follows that
|f|=|f(v)|\leq C
is bounded in
L^{\infty}
. Thus,
v
solves

(E.4)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}v-\Delta v&=f,\\
v(t=0)&=u,\end{aligned}\right.

with source term
f\in L^{\infty}
. By the strong
L^{p}
estimate (cp. Corollary
E.3
), and the assumed smoothness of
u\in C^{4,\alpha}
, it follows that
v\in\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
. In turn this implies the following chain of improved regularity based on the Schauder estimate of Lemma
E.1
, and using also the fact that
u\in C^{k,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
for all
k\leq 4
:

\displaystyle v\in\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}(U)

\displaystyle\implies f(v)\in\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}(U)\implies v\in\mathcal{C}^{2,\alpha}(U)

\displaystyle\implies f(v)\in\mathcal{C}^{2,\alpha}(U)\implies v\in\mathcal{C}^{4,\alpha}(U).

Thus, we conclude that if
u\in C^{4,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, then we must have
v\in\mathcal{C}^{4,\alpha}(U)
. Furthermore, it follows from the estimates of Corollary
E.3
, Lemma
E.1
, that there in fact exists a constant
\sigma=\sigma(\|u\|_{C^{4,\alpha}})>0
, depending on the
C^{4,\alpha}
-norm of the initial data
u
, such that

|v|_{(4,\alpha)}\leq\sigma(\|u\|_{C^{4,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}).

Clearly, we have
\|v\|_{C^{(4,2)}(U)}\leq|v|_{(4,\alpha)}
for any
\alpha>0
. The claimed estimate thus follows.
∎

E.10.
Proof of Corollary
4.23

Proof.

Since we have
\|v(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})},\,\|v^{\prime}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}
for all
t\in[0,T]
, the difference
w=v-v^{\prime}
, solves the following equation

\partial_{t}w=\Delta w+F(v,v^{\prime})w,

where
F(a,b):=1-a^{2}-ab-b^{2}
. It follows from the uniform boundedness of
v,v^{\prime}
that

|F(v,v^{\prime})|\leq 4
is uniformly bounded. Multiplying the equation by
w
and integrating over
x
, we obtain

\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}w^{2}\,dx\leq 8\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}w^{2}\,dx.

And hence, by Gronwall’s inequality, we must have
\|w(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\|w(0)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}e^{8t}
for all
t\in[0,T]
. We conclude that

\|v(T)-v^{\prime}(T)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leq e^{4T}\|u-u^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}.

∎

E.11.
Proof of Theorem
4.27

Proof.

Our main observation is that the local truncation error for the scheme (
4.40
), given by

T^{n}_{j}:=(I-\Delta tD_{\Delta x})v(t_{n},x_{j})-(v(t_{n},x_{j})+\Delta t\left(v(t_{n-1},x_{j})-v(t_{n-1},x_{j})^{3}\right),

has a Taylor expansion

\displaystyle T^{n}_{j}

\displaystyle:=\Delta t\left(\frac{\partial v(t_{n-1},x_{j})}{\partial t}-\Delta v(t_{n-1},x_{j})-(v(t_{n-1},x_{j})-v(t_{n-1},x_{j})^{3})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\Delta t\,R(\Delta t,\Delta x),

where similar to
Tang & Yang 2016
, the remainder term
R(\Delta t,\Delta x)
can be estimated by

\displaystyle|R(\Delta t,\Delta x)|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\Delta t\,\left\|\frac{\partial v}{\partial t}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}+\Delta t\,\left\|\frac{\partial^{2}v}{\partial t^{2}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}+\Delta x^{2}\,\sum_{k=1}^{d}\left\|\frac{\partial^{4}v}{\partial x_{k}^{4}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\Delta t+\Delta x^{2}\right)\|v\|_{C^{(2,4)}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})},

where
\|v\|_{C^{(2,4)}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}
is defined by (
4.33
).
We note that in contrast to our estimate, the remainder term was bounded in
Tang & Yang 2016
by the larger norm
\|v\|_{C^{2}([0,T];C^{4}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))}
. In view of the available a priori estimates for parabolic equations, the parabolic norm
\|v\|_{C^{(2,4)}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}
appears better adapted to the problem, and provides a less restrictive convergence result for the scheme. The remainder of the proof is the same as in
(
Tang & Yang 2016
, Theorem 4.1)
.
∎

E.12.
Proof of Lemma
4.28

Proof.

By a result of Yarotsky
(
Yarotsky 2017
, Prop. 2, 3)
, there exists a constant
C^{\prime}>0
, such that for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a ReLU network
\widetilde{\times}:[-2,2]\to\mathbb{R}
, such that
\mathrm{size}(\widetilde{\times})\leq C^{\prime}(|\log(\epsilon)|+1)
, and

\sup_{\xi,\eta\in[-2,2]}|\widetilde{\times}(\xi,\eta)-\xi\eta|<\epsilon/2.

In fact, the mapping constructed in
Yarotsky 2017
is based on the identity
\xi\eta=\frac{1}{2}\left((\xi+\eta)^{2}-\xi^{2}-\eta^{2}\right)
, and finding a suitable neural network approximation
f_{m}(x)\approx x^{2}
, of the form
(
Yarotsky 2017
, paragraph above eq. (3))

f_{m}(x)=x-\sum_{s=1}^{m}2^{-2s}g_{s}(x)
, with
m=\mathcal{O}(|\log(\epsilon)|)
and with
g_{s}
a
s
-fold iteration of the following sawtooch function
g(x)
:

g(x)=\begin{cases}2x,&x<\frac{1}{2},\\
2(1-x),&x\geq\frac{1}{2},\end{cases}\quad g_{s}(x)=\underbrace{(g\circ\dots\circ g)}_{s\text{ times}}(x).

It is then immediate that
\mathrm{Lip}(g_{s})\leq\mathrm{Lip}(g)^{s}\leq 2^{s}
, and hence
\mathrm{Lip}(f_{m})\leq 1+\sum_{s=1}^{m}2^{-2s}\mathrm{Lip}(g_{s})\leq 1+\sum_{s=1}^{m}2^{-s}\leq 2
. It is then readily seen that there exists a constant
M>0
, independent of
\epsilon
, such that

\mathrm{Lip}(\widetilde{\times}:[-2,2]^{2}\to)\leq M.

in addition to the approximation property

\sup_{\xi,\eta\in[-1,1]^{2}}|\widetilde{\times}(\xi,\eta)-\xi\eta|<\epsilon/2.

In particular, with the mapping constructed above, we then have
\sup_{\eta\in[-1,1]}|\widetilde{\times}(\eta,\eta)-\eta^{2}|<\epsilon
, and from the assumption that
\epsilon<1
, it follows that
\widetilde{\times}(\eta,\eta)-1\in[-2,2]
for all
\eta\in[-1,1]
. Observing that
\xi^{3}-\xi=\xi(\xi^{2}-1)
, we find for any
\eta\in[-1,1]

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\times}\left(\eta,(\widetilde{\times}(\eta,\eta)-1)\right)-(\eta^{3}-\eta)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\widetilde{\times}\left(\eta,(\widetilde{\times}(\eta,\eta)-1)\right)-\eta(\widetilde{\times}(\eta,\eta)-1)\right|

\displaystyle\quad+\left|\eta(\widetilde{\times}(\eta,\eta)-1)-\eta(\eta^{2}-1)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\xi\in[-2,2]}\left|\widetilde{\times}\left(\eta,\xi\right)-\eta\xi\right|

\displaystyle\quad+|\eta|\left|\widetilde{\times}(\eta,\eta)-\eta^{2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\sup_{\xi,\eta\in[-1,1]}\left|\widetilde{\times}\left(\eta,\xi\right)-\eta\xi\right|

\displaystyle<\epsilon.

To finish the proof, we note that if
(\xi,\eta)\mapsto\widetilde{\times}(\xi,\eta)
is represented by a ReLU neural network of size
\leq C^{\prime}(|\log(\epsilon)|+1)
, then the function

g_{\epsilon}(\eta):=\max\left(-1,\min\left(1,\widetilde{\times}\left(\eta,(\widetilde{\times}(\eta,\eta)-1)\right)\right)\right)

can be represented by a ReLU neural network of size
\leq C(|\log(\epsilon)|+1)
, where
C
is a constant multiple of
C^{\prime}
, and we have
g_{\epsilon}(\eta)\in[-1,1]
for all
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
. Furthermore, since
\eta^{3}-\eta\in[-1,1]
for all
\eta\in[-1,1]
, it also follows from the above estimate that

\sup_{\eta\in[-1,1]}|g_{\epsilon}(\eta)-(\eta^{3}-\eta)|\leq\epsilon.

∎

Remark E.4
.

If we consider neural networks
g
with
any smooth
(e.g.
\sigma\in C^{3}(\mathbb{R})
) non-linear activation function
\sigma:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, then the previous approximation result can be considerably improved
(
Pinkus 1999
, Prop. 3.4)
: Indeed, by assumption on
\sigma
there exists a point
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, such that
\sigma^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})\neq 0
. Then for
h>0
,
\eta\in[-1,1]
, we have by Taylor expansion

\frac{\sigma(x_{0}+\eta h)-2\sigma(x_{0})+\sigma(x_{0}-\eta h)}{\sigma^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})h^{2}}=\eta^{2}+\mathcal{O}(h),

as
h\to 0
, uniformly in
\eta\in[-1,1]
. In particular, it follows that for smooth
\sigma
there exists a neural network architecture
of fixed size
, such that and for any
h>0
, there is neural network
g:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
,
\eta\mapsto g(\eta)
, such that
|g(\eta)-\eta^{2}|\leq Ch
for all
\eta\in[-1,1]
. As a consequence, using the representation
Yarotsky 2017

xy=\frac{1}{2}((x+y)^{2}-x^{2}-y^{2}),

it follows that there exists a constant
C>0
, such that for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a neural network
\times_{\epsilon}:[-1,1]^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
of size
\mathrm{size}(\times_{\epsilon})\leq C
, such that

\sup_{x,y\in[-1,1]}|\times_{\epsilon}(x,y)-xy|<\epsilon.

But then, arguing as in the proof of Lemma
4.28
, we find that for any
\epsilon>0
, the function
g_{\epsilon}(x)=\times_{\epsilon}(\times_{\epsilon}(x,x),x)-x
can be represented by a neural network, with a neural network size,
\mathrm{size}(g_{\epsilon})\leq C^{\prime}
, which is uniformly bounded in
\epsilon>0
, and such that
\sup_{\eta\in[-1,1]}|g_{\epsilon}(\eta)-(\eta^{3}-\eta)|<\epsilon
.

E.13.
Proof of Lemma
4.29

Proof.

By Lemma
4.28
, the non-linearity in (
4.41
) can be represented by a neural network with size bounded by

\begin{gathered}\mathrm{size}(g_{\epsilon})\leq C(1+|\log(\epsilon)|),\quad 0pt(g_{\epsilon})\leq C(1+|\log(\epsilon)|).\end{gathered}

It follows that the mapping

\left(\widetilde{U}^{k}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{U}^{k}_{m}\right)\mapsto\left(g_{\epsilon}\left(\widetilde{U}^{k}_{1}\right),\dots,g_{\epsilon}\left(\widetilde{U}^{k}_{m}\right)\right)=:G_{\epsilon}(\widetilde{U}^{k}),

can be represented by a neural network
G_{\epsilon}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
, with

\begin{gathered}\mathrm{size}(G_{\epsilon})=\mathcal{O}(m|\log(\epsilon)|),\quad 0pt(G_{\epsilon})=\mathcal{O}(|\log(\epsilon)|).\end{gathered}

Since the identity mapping
\widetilde{U}^{k}\mapsto\widetilde{U}^{k}
can be represented by a ReLU network with
\mathrm{size}=\mathcal{O}(m)
,
0pt=\mathcal{O}(1)
, there exists a neural network with
\mathrm{size}=\mathcal{O}(m|\log(\epsilon)|)
,
0pt=\mathcal{O}(|\log\epsilon|)
, which represents

\widetilde{U}^{k}\mapsto\widetilde{U}^{k}+\Delta tG_{\epsilon}(\widetilde{U}^{k}).

Finally, we note that

\widetilde{U}^{k}+\Delta tG_{\epsilon}(\widetilde{U}^{k})\mapsto R_{\Delta x,\Delta t}(\widetilde{U}^{k}+\Delta tG_{\epsilon}(\widetilde{U}^{k})),

is simply a matrix-vector multiplication of a
fixed
matrix
R_{\Delta x,\Delta t}=(I-\Delta t\,D_{\Delta x})^{-1}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
(independent of
\widetilde{U}^{k}
), with a vector in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. This operation can be represented by a ReLU neural network layer with
\mathrm{size}=\mathcal{O}(m^{2})
,
0pt=\mathcal{O}(1)
. We conclude that the composition

\widetilde{U}^{k}\mapsto\widetilde{U}^{k}+\Delta t\,G_{\epsilon}(\widetilde{U}^{k})\mapsto R_{\Delta x,\Delta t}\left(\widetilde{U}^{k}+\Delta t\,G_{\epsilon}(\widetilde{U}^{k})\right)=\widetilde{U}^{k+1},

can be represented by a neural network
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
with

\displaystyle\mathrm{size}(\widehat{\mathcal{N}})=\mathcal{O}(m^{2}+m|\log\epsilon|),\quad 0pt(\widehat{\mathcal{N}})=\mathcal{O}(|\log\epsilon|).

Since
\widetilde{U}^{0}\mapsto\widetilde{U}^{1}\mapsto\dots\mapsto\widetilde{U}^{n}
involves the composition of
n
such steps, we conclude that
\widetilde{U}^{0}\mapsto\widetilde{U}^{n}
can be represented by a neural network
\mathcal{N}=\widehat{\mathcal{N}}\circ\widehat{\mathcal{N}}\circ\dots\circ\widehat{\mathcal{N}}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
(
n
iterations) with

\displaystyle\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{N})=\mathcal{O}(n(m^{2}+m|\log\epsilon|)),\quad 0pt(\mathcal{N})=\mathcal{O}(n|\log(\epsilon)|).

∎

E.14.
Proof of Lemma
4.30

Proof.

We note that

\displaystyle U^{k+1}-\widetilde{U}^{k+1}

\displaystyle=R_{\Delta x,\Delta t}\left(U^{k}-\widetilde{U}^{k}+\Delta t\left[g_{\epsilon}(U^{k})-g_{\epsilon}(\widetilde{U}^{k})\right]\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\Delta tR_{\Delta x,\Delta t}\left(\left[g_{\epsilon}(U^{k})-f({U}^{k})\right]\right).

We note that
\|U^{k}\|_{\ell^{\infty}}\leq 1
for all
k>0
, by Theorem
4.26
. Furthermore, it has been shown in
Tang & Yang 2016
that
\|R_{\Delta x,\Delta t}\|_{\ell^{\infty}\to\ell^{\infty}}\leq 1
. It follows that

\left\|R_{\Delta x,\Delta t}\left(\left[g_{\epsilon}(U^{k})-f({U}^{k})\right]\right)\right\|_{\ell^{\infty}}\leq\sup_{\eta\in[-1,1]}|g_{\epsilon}(\eta)-f(\eta)|\leq\epsilon.

Denote now
E^{k}=\|U^{k}-\widetilde{U}^{k}\|_{\ell^{\infty}}
, for
k=0,\dots,n
. Then

\displaystyle E^{k+1}

\displaystyle\leq\|R_{\Delta t,\Delta x}\|_{\ell^{\infty}\to\ell^{\infty}}\left(E^{k}+\Delta t\,\mathrm{Lip}(g_{\epsilon})E^{k}\right)+\Delta t\,\epsilon

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\Delta t\,\mathrm{Lip}(g_{\epsilon})\right)E^{k}+\Delta t\,\epsilon.

Summing over
k=1,\dots,\ell
, and taking into account that
E^{0}=0
, we find for any
\ell\in\{1,\dots,n\}
that

\displaystyle E^{\ell}

\displaystyle=E^{0}+\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-1}[E^{k+1}-E^{k}]

\displaystyle\leq T\epsilon+\Delta t\,\sum_{k=1}^{\ell-1}\mathrm{Lip}(g_{\epsilon})E^{k}.

By the Gronwall inequality, it follows that

E^{n}\leq\epsilon\,T\exp(\mathrm{Lip}(g_{\epsilon})T).

The result follows from
\mathrm{Lip}(g_{\epsilon})\leq M
.
∎

E.15.
Proof of Proposition
4.31

Proof.

By definition, the approximator neural network
\mathcal{A}
should provide an approximation
\mathcal{A}\approx\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}
. Our goal is to construct
\mathcal{A}
based on the neural network approximation (
4.41
) of the convergent numerical scheme (
4.40
). To this end, we first note that by Lemmas
4.29
,
4.30
, there exists a constant
C=C(\sup_{u\in\mathrm{supp}(\mu)}\|u\|_{C^{(2,4)}},T)>0
, independent of
m
,
n
and
\epsilon
, and a neural network
\mathcal{N}
with

\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{N})\leq C(1+n(m^{2}+m|\log(\epsilon)|)),\quad 0pt(\mathcal{N})\leq C(1+n|\log(\epsilon)|),

such that for any
u\in\mathrm{supp}(\mu)
, we have

\max_{j=1,\dots,m}|\mathcal{N}_{j}(\bm{u})-v(x_{j},T)|\leq C(\epsilon+\Delta x^{2}+T/n),

where
\bm{u}=(u(x_{1}),\dots,u(x_{m}))=\mathcal{E}(u)
.
We note that for the present choice of the
x_{j}
as nodes on an equidistant grid, we have
\Delta x\sim m^{-2/d}
. Thus, we choose
n=\lceil Tm^{2/d}\rceil
,
\epsilon=m^{-2/d}
to conclude that there exists a neural network
\mathcal{N}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
, such that

(E.5)

\displaystyle\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{N})\leq C(1+m^{2+2/d}),\quad 0pt(\mathcal{N})\leq C(1+m^{2/d}\log(m)),

and

(E.6)

\displaystyle\max_{j=1,\dots,m}|\mathcal{N}_{j}(\bm{u})-v(x_{j},T)|\leq Cm^{-2/d}.

Given the grid points
x_{j}
, we note that we can define a linear mapping

\mathcal{L}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to C(D),\quad\bm{v}=(v_{1},\dots,v_{m})\mapsto\mathcal{L}(\bm{v})(x),

by employing local linear interpolation of the values
v_{j}
on the mesh
x_{j}
, i.e. such that
\mathcal{L}(\bm{v})(x_{j})=v_{j}
. Given the projection operator
\mathcal{P}
, we define a linear mapping
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
by
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{v}):=\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{L}(\bm{v})
. With these definitions, we now set
\mathcal{A}:=\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\circ\widehat{\mathcal{N}}=\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{L}\circ\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
. We note that since
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}=\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{L}
is a linear mapping
\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
, and since
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
is a neural network with size bounded by (
E.5
), we can represent
\mathcal{A}
as a neural network with

\displaystyle\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{O}(m^{2+2/d}+mp),

\displaystyle 0pt(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{O}(m^{2/d}\log(m)+1)=\mathcal{O}(m^{2/d}\log(m)).

Furthermore, for this choice of
\mathcal{A}
, we have

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{A}})^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\|\mathcal{A}(\bm{u})-\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}(\bm{u})\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}}\,d(\mathcal{E}_{\#}\mu)(\bm{u})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{supp}(\mu)}\|\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{E}(u)-\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}(u)\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}}\,d\mu(u)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{supp}(\mu)}\|\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{L}\circ\widehat{\mathcal{N}}\circ\mathcal{E}(u)-\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}(u)\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}}\,d\mu(u)

Furthermore, we can estimate the integrand for any
u\in\mathrm{supp}(\mu)
:

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{L}\circ\widehat{\mathcal{N}}\circ\mathcal{E}(u)

\displaystyle-\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}(u)\|_{\ell^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{P}\|_{L^{2}(U)\to\ell^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{p})}\|\mathcal{L}\circ\widehat{\mathcal{N}}\circ\mathcal{E}(u)-\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}(u)\|_{L^{2}(U)}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{L}\circ\widehat{\mathcal{N}}\circ\mathcal{E}(u)-\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}(u)\|_{L^{2}(U)}.

If we denote by
x\mapsto v(x,T)=\mathcal{G}(u)
the solution at
t=T
of the Allen-Cahn equation (
4.3
) with initial data
u
, and
\bm{u}=(u(x_{1}),\dots,u(x_{m}))=\mathcal{E}(u)
, then we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{L}\circ\widehat{\mathcal{N}}\circ\mathcal{E}(u)

\displaystyle-\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}(u)\|_{L^{2}(U)}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{L}\circ\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(\bm{u})-\mathcal{G}(u)\|_{L^{2}(U)}+\|\mathcal{G}(u)-\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}(u)\|_{L^{2}(U)}

(E.7)

\displaystyle\leq C\|\mathcal{L}\circ\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(\bm{u})-\mathcal{G}(u)\|_{L^{\infty}(U)}+C\|\mathcal{G}(u)-\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}(u)\|_{L^{\infty}(U)}.

To estimate the first term, we note that since
\mathcal{L}\circ\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(\bm{u})
and
v({\,\cdot\,},T)=\mathcal{G}(u)
are Lipschitz continuous functions with Lipschitz constant that can be bounded by
\|u\|_{C^{4,\alpha}}
for a fixed
\alpha\in(0,1)
, as in Theorem
4.22
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{L}\circ\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(\bm{u})-\mathcal{G}(u)\|_{L^{\infty}(U)}

\displaystyle\leq C(\|u\|_{C^{4,\alpha}})\Delta x

\displaystyle\qquad+\max_{j=1,\dots,m}|\mathcal{L}\circ\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(\bm{u})(x_{j})-\mathcal{G}(u)(x_{j})|

\displaystyle=C(\|u\|_{C^{4,\alpha}})\Delta x+\max_{j=1,\dots,m}|\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{j}(\bm{u})-v(x_{j},T)|

\displaystyle\leq Cm^{-1/d}.

In the last step, we used the fact that
\Delta x\lesssim m^{-1/d}
and (
E.6
).

To estimate the other second term in (
E.7
), we note that the numerical scheme (
4.40
) applied to
u
and
\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}(u)
starts from the same discrete initial data
\mathcal{E}(u)=(u(x_{1}),\dots,u(x_{m}))
, since
\mathcal{E}\circ\mathcal{D}=\mathrm{Id}
, by assumption. It then follows from the error estimate of Theorem
4.27
, and the fact that the Lipschitz constant of
\mathcal{G}(u)
and
\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}(u)
are bounded in terms of
\|u\|_{C^{4,\alpha}}
, that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{G}(u)-\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}(u)\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq Cm^{-1/d}+\max_{j=1,\dots,m}|\mathcal{G}(u)(x_{j})-\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}(u)(x_{j})|

\displaystyle\leq Cm^{-1/d}+C\Delta x^{2}\leq Cm^{-1/d}.

The constant
C=C(u)>0
depends on
u
only through
\|u\|_{C^{4,\alpha}}
. In particular, there exists a constant
C^{\prime}>0
, such that
C(\|u\|_{C^{4,\alpha}}\leq C^{\prime}
for all
u\in\mathrm{supp}(\mu)
. Combining the above estimates, we can now estimate

\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{A}}\leq C^{\prime}m^{-1/d}.

To conclude, we have shown that there exists an approximator network
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
, for
p=m
, such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{O}(m^{2+2/d}+mp),\quad 0pt(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{O}(m^{2/d}\log(m)),

and
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathcal{A}}=\mathcal{O}(m^{-1/d})
.
∎

E.16.
Proof of Lemma
4.37

Proof.

Let
\sigma(x)=\max(x,0)
be the ReLU activation function. We assume wlog that
\epsilon<b-a
(otherwise decrease
\epsilon
). We note that

\chi^{\epsilon}_{[a,b]}(x):=\frac{\sigma(x-a)-\sigma(x-a-\epsilon/2)+\sigma(x-b)-\sigma(x-b-\epsilon/2)}{\epsilon/2},

is continuous, satisfies

\chi^{\epsilon}_{[a,b]}(x)=\begin{cases}0,&x\notin[a,b],\\
1,&x\in[a+\epsilon/2,b-\epsilon/2],\\
\end{cases}

and is linear on
[a,a+\epsilon/2]
and
[b-\epsilon/2,b]
. In particular, it follows that

\|\chi^{\epsilon}_{[a,b]}-1_{[a,b]}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\leq|[a,a+\epsilon/2]|+|[b-\epsilon/2,b]|=\epsilon.

Furthermore,
\chi^{\epsilon}_{[a,b]}
is represented by the same neural network architecture for any choice of
a,b,\epsilon
.
∎

E.17.
Proof of Theorem
4.38

Proof.

By Theorem
4.35
, there exists a constant
C>0
, and a neural network
\mathcal{N}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
, with

(E.8)

\displaystyle\mathrm{size}(\mathcal{N})\leq Cm^{-5/2},\quad 0pt(\mathcal{N})\leq Cm,

such that for any
u\in\mathrm{BV}_{M}
, we have

\left\|\mathcal{G}(u)-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{N}_{j}(\overline{\mathcal{E}}(u))\,1_{C_{j}}({\,\cdot\,})\right\|_{L^{1}(D)}\leq\frac{C}{m^{\alpha}}.

We now define
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
by
\mathcal{A}(\bm{u}):=(\mathcal{N}_{1}(\bm{u}),\dots,\mathcal{N}_{p}(\bm{u}))
, where we formally set
\mathcal{N}_{j}\equiv 0
, if
j>m
. For each
j=1,\dots,m
, let
\tau_{j}(y):=\chi^{\epsilon}_{C_{j}}
, where
\chi^{\epsilon}_{C_{j}}
is a neural network approximation of
1_{C_{j}}
as in Lemma
4.37
, i.e. such that

(E.9)

\displaystyle\mathrm{size}(\tau_{j})\leq C,\quad 0pt(\tau_{j})=1,

such that

\|\tau_{j}-1_{C_{j}}\|_{L^{1}}\leq\epsilon,\text{for $j=1,\dots,m$.}

For
j>m
, we define
\tau_{j}\equiv 0
, corresponding to an approximation of
C_{j}:=\emptyset
for
j>m
. Note that we clearly obtain from (
E.9
) for the trunk net
\bm{\tau}=(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{p})
:

(E.10)

\displaystyle\mathrm{size}(\bm{\tau})\leq Cp,\quad 0pt(\bm{\tau})=1.

Similarly, we define for
j=1,\dots,p
the branch net
\bm{\beta}=(\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{p})
by:

\beta_{j}(u):=\begin{cases}\mathcal{N}_{j}(\overline{\mathcal{E}}(u)),&(j\leq m)\\
0,&(j>m).\end{cases}

By (
E.8
), we note that

(E.11)

\displaystyle\mathrm{size}(\bm{\beta})\leq Cm^{-5/2},\quad 0pt(\bm{\beta})\leq Cm,

Then, clearly we have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{G}(u)-\sum_{j=1}^{p}\beta_{j}(u)\tau_{j}\right\|_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\mathcal{G}(u)-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{N}_{j}(\overline{\mathcal{E}}(u))1_{C_{j}}\right\|_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{j=1}^{p}|\mathcal{N}_{j}(\overline{\mathcal{E}}(u))|\left\|1_{C_{j}}-\tau_{j}\right\|_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{j=p+1}^{m}|\mathcal{N}_{j}(\overline{\mathcal{E}}(u))|\left\|1_{C_{j}}\right\|_{L^{1}}

The first term can be estimated by
Cm^{-\alpha}
. Each term in the sum for
j=1,\dots,p
can be estimated by

\|1_{C_{j}}-\tau_{j}\|_{L^{1}}=\left.\begin{cases}\|1_{C_{j}}-\tau_{j}\|_{L^{1}},&(j=1,\dots,m),\\
0,&(j>m)\end{cases}\right\}\leq\epsilon,

by choice of the
\tau_{j}
. We also note that
|\mathcal{N}_{j}(\overline{\mathcal{E}}(u))|\leq M
for all
u\in\mathrm{BV}_{M}
(cp. Theorem
4.35
). Hence, we have

\sum_{j=1}^{p}|\mathcal{N}_{j}(\overline{\mathcal{E}}(u))|\left\|1_{C_{j}}-\tau_{j}\right\|_{L^{1}}\leq Mp\epsilon.

Finally, the last sum over
j=p+1,\dots,m
, if non-empty, can be estimated by

\displaystyle\sum_{j=p+1}^{m}|\mathcal{N}_{j}(\overline{\mathcal{E}}(u))|\left\|1_{C_{j}}\right\|_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=p+1}^{m}\frac{2\pi M}{m}

\displaystyle=\max(m-p,0)\frac{2\pi M}{m}

\displaystyle=2\pi M\,\max\left(1-\frac{p}{m},0\right).

In particular, we conclude that there exists a constant
C>0
, independent of
p
and
m
, such that for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a DeepONet
(\bm{\beta},\bm{\tau})
with size bounded by (
E.11
) and (
E.10
), such that

\left\|\mathcal{G}(u)-\sum_{j=1}^{p}\beta_{j}(u)\tau_{j}\right\|_{L^{1}}\leq Cm^{-\alpha}+Cp\epsilon+C\max\left(1-\frac{p}{m},0\right),\quad\forall u\in\mathrm{BV}_{M}.

Since
\epsilon>0
is arbitrary, we may set
\epsilon=m^{-\alpha}p^{-1}
, and absorb the second term in the first. Integrating against
\mu
with
\mu(\mathrm{BV}_{M})=1
, we find that

\int_{L^{1}(D)}\left\|\mathcal{G}(u)-\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{A}\circ\overline{\mathcal{E}}(u)\right\|_{L^{1}}\,d\mu(u)\leq Cm^{-\alpha}+C\max\left(1-\frac{p}{m},0\right),

where we recall that, by definition, we have

\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{A}\circ\overline{\mathcal{E}}(u)\equiv\sum_{j=1}^{p}\beta_{j}(u)\tau_{j}.

The claimed estimate on
\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{L^{1}}
thus follows with trunk and branch net complexity bounds (
E.10
) and (
E.11
).
∎

E.18.
Proof of Lemma
4.41

Proof.

We first note that the covariance operator
\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}
can be represented in the form

(\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu}u)(x)=\int_{0}^{2\pi}k(x,x^{\prime})u(x^{\prime})\,dx^{\prime},

where

\displaystyle k(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}u(x)u(x^{\prime})\,d(\mathcal{G}_{\#}\mu)(u)

\displaystyle=\int_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\mathcal{G}(u)(x)\mathcal{G}(u)(x^{\prime})\,d\mu(u).

Next, we note that for any functional
\mathcal{F}\in L^{1}(\mu)
, we have

\int_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\mathcal{F}(u)\,d\mu(u)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathcal{F}(u({\,\cdot\,}-\widehat{x}))\,d\widehat{x},

and the solution with initial data
u_{0}(x-\widehat{x})
at
t=\pi/2
is given by
\mathcal{G}(u({\,\cdot\,}-\widehat{x}))=v_{t}(x-\widehat{x})
. It follows that

\displaystyle\int_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\mathcal{G}(u)(x)\mathcal{G}(u)(x^{\prime})\,d\mu(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathcal{G}(u({\,\cdot\,}-\widehat{x}))(x)\mathcal{G}(u({\,\cdot\,}-\widehat{x}))(x^{\prime})\,d\widehat{x}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}v_{t}(x-\widehat{x})v_{t}(x^{\prime}-\widehat{x})\,d\widehat{x}.

By a change of variables, we thus find

\displaystyle k(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}v_{t}(x-\widehat{x})v_{t}(x^{\prime}-\widehat{x})\,d\widehat{x}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}v_{t}(x-x^{\prime}+\xi)v_{t}(\xi)\,d\xi

\displaystyle=g(x-x^{\prime}),

where

g(x):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}v_{t}(x+\xi)v_{t}(\xi)\,d\xi,

is written as a convolution. In particular,
k(x,x^{\prime})=g(x-x^{\prime})
is a
stationary
kernel. From the stationarity of
k(x,x^{\prime})
, it follows that the eigenfunctions of
k(x,x^{\prime})
are given by the Fourier basis
\{\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
, with corresponding eigenvalues

\lambda_{k}=(2\pi)\widehat{g}(k),

where
\widehat{g}(k)
denotes the
k
-th Fourier coefficient of
g
. Finally, we note that

\lambda_{k}=(2\pi)\widehat{g}(k)=|\widehat{v}_{t}(k)|^{2}=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}k^{2}}+o\left(\frac{1}{k^{2}}\right).

∎

Appendix F
Proof of Theorem
5.3

We note that, since
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
is a minimizer of
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}
, and
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{N}
is a minimizer of
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{N}
, we have the following well-known bound:

\displaystyle\left|\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{N}\right)-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{N}}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{N}\right)-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{N}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{N}\right)-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{N}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{N}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{N}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{N}}\right)-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{N}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\sup_{\theta}\left|\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{N}\left(\mathcal{N}_{\theta}\right)-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\left(\mathcal{N}_{\theta}\right)\right|,

where the supremum is taken over all admissible
\theta\in[-B,B]^{d_{\theta}}
. Starting from this bound, the proof of Theorem
5.3
relies on the following lemmas, which follow very closely the argument in
(
Welti 2020
, Chapter 5.3)
(see also
Cucker & Smale 2002
,
Berner et al. 2020
).

Lemma F.1
.

Under assumptions
5.1
and
5.2
, we have

\left|S^{N}_{\theta}-S^{N}_{\theta^{\prime}}\right|\leq\frac{4}{N}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\Psi(Z_{j})\Phi(Z_{j})\right)\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{\ell^{\infty}}.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle|S^{N}_{\theta}-S^{N}_{\theta^{\prime}}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left||\mathcal{G}(Z_{j})-\mathcal{N}_{\theta}(Z_{j})|^{2}-|\mathcal{G}(Z_{j})-\mathcal{N}_{\theta^{\prime}}(Z_{j})|^{2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left(2|\mathcal{G}(Z_{j})|+|\mathcal{N}_{\theta}(Z_{j})|+|\mathcal{N}_{\theta^{\prime}}(Z_{j})|\right)|\mathcal{N}_{\theta}(Z_{j})-\mathcal{N}_{\theta^{\prime}}(Z_{j})|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}4|\Psi(Z_{j})||\Phi(Z_{j})|\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{\ell^{\infty}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{N}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}|\Psi(Z_{j})||\Phi(Z_{j})|\right)\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{\ell^{\infty}},

as claimed.
∎

Lemma F.2
.

If
\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{K}
are such that for all
\theta\in[-B,B]^{d}
, there exists
j
with
\|\theta-\theta_{j}\|_{\ell^{\infty}}\leq\epsilon
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\theta\in[-B,B]^{d}}\left|S^{N}_{\theta}-\mathbb{E}[S^{N}_{\theta}]\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}\leq 8\epsilon\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\Psi\Phi\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}+\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{j=1,\dots,K}\left|S^{N}_{\theta_{j}}-\mathbb{E}[S^{N}_{\theta_{j}}]\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}.

Proof.

Fix
\epsilon>0
. Define a mapping
j:[-B,B]^{d}\to\mathbb{N}
, by
j(\theta)=\min{\left\{j\in\{1,\dots,K\}\,\middle|\,|\theta-\theta_{j}|\leq\epsilon\right\}}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\theta\in[-B,B]^{d}}|S^{N}_{\theta}-\mathbb{E}[S^{N}_{\theta}]|^{p}\right]^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\Bigg(\sup_{\theta\in[-B,B]^{d}}\left|S^{N}_{\theta}-S^{N}_{\theta_{j(\theta)}}\right|+\left|S^{N}_{j(\theta)}-\mathbb{E}[S^{N}_{j(\theta)}]\right|\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\left.+\left|\mathbb{E}[S^{N}_{\theta_{j(\theta)}}]-\mathbb{E}[S^{N}_{\theta}]\right|\Bigg)^{p}\right]^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\Bigg(\max_{j=1,\dots,K}\left|S^{N}_{j(\theta)}-\mathbb{E}[S^{N}_{j(\theta)}]\right|\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\left.+\frac{8\epsilon}{N}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}|\Psi(Z_{j})||\Phi(Z_{j})|\right)\Bigg)^{p}\right]^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{j=1,\dots,K}\left|S^{N}_{\theta_{j}}-\mathbb{E}[S^{N}_{\theta_{j}}]\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\frac{8\epsilon}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\left[|\Psi(Z_{j})\Phi(Z_{j})|^{p}\right]^{1/p}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{j=1,\dots,K}\left|S^{N}_{\theta_{j}}-\mathbb{E}[S^{N}_{\theta_{j}}]\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}

\displaystyle\quad\qquad+8\epsilon\mathbb{E}\left[|\Psi(Z_{1})\Phi(Z_{1})|^{p}\right]^{1/p},

as claimed.
∎

Lemma F.3
.

Let
K\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{K}\in[-B,B]^{d_{\theta}}
be given. Then, for any
p\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{j=1,\dots,K}\left|S^{N}_{\theta_{j}}-\mathbb{E}[S^{N}_{\theta_{j}}]\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}\leq K^{1/p}\max_{j=1,\dots,K}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|S^{N}_{\theta_{j}}-\mathbb{E}[S^{N}_{\theta_{j}}]\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}.

Proof.

This follows readily from the fact that for any measurable
X_{1},\dots,X_{K}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{j=1,\dots,K}|X_{j}|^{p}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{K}|X_{j}|^{p}\right]=\sum_{j=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}[|X_{j}|^{p}]\leq K\max_{j=1,\dots,K}\mathbb{E}[|X_{j}|^{p}].

∎

Lemma F.4
.

Let
2\leq p<\infty
. For any
\theta\in[-B,B]^{d_{\theta}}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|S^{N}_{\theta}-\mathbb{E}[S^{N}_{\theta}]\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}\leq\frac{16\sqrt{p-1}\mathbb{E}\left[|\Psi|^{2p}\right]^{1/p}}{\sqrt{N}}.

Proof.

It follows from
(
Welti 2020
, Corollary 5.18)
, with
S^{N}_{\theta}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}X_{j}
,
X_{j}:=|\mathcal{G}(Z_{j})-\mathcal{N}_{\theta}(Z_{j})|^{2}
, that

\mathbb{E}\left[|S^{N}_{\theta}-\mathbb{E}[S^{N}_{\theta}]|^{p}\right]^{1/p}\leq\frac{2\sqrt{p-1}}{\sqrt{N}}\left[\max_{j=1,\dots,N}\mathbb{E}[|X_{j}-\mathbb{E}[X_{j}]|^{p}]^{1/p}.\right]

But by the boundedness assumption
5.1
, we have

X_{j}\leq 2|\mathcal{G}(Z_{j})|^{2}+2|\mathcal{N}_{\theta}(Z_{j})|^{2}\leq 4|\Psi(Z_{j})|^{2}.

Hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|X_{j}-\mathbb{E}[X_{j}]|^{p}]^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq 8\mathbb{E}[|\Psi(Z_{j})|^{2p}]^{1/p}=8\mathbb{E}[|\Psi|^{2p}]^{1/p},

where the last equality follows from the fact that the
Z_{j}
are iid. Hence

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|S^{N}_{\theta}-\mathbb{E}\left[S^{N}_{\theta}\right]\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}\leq\frac{16\sqrt{p-1}\mathbb{E}\left[|\Psi|^{2p}\right]^{1/p}}{\sqrt{N}}.

∎

Lemma F.5
.

Let
\epsilon>0
be given. Let
p\geq 2
. Then we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\theta\in[-B,B]^{d}}\left|S^{N}_{\theta}-\mathbb{E}[S^{N}_{\theta}]\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}\leq 16\|\Psi\|_{L^{2p}}\left(\epsilon\|\Phi\|_{L^{2p}}+\frac{\sqrt{p}\,\|\Psi\|_{L^{2p}}}{\sqrt{N}}K(\epsilon)^{1/p}\right),

where
K(\epsilon)
denotes the
\epsilon
-covering number of
[-B,B]^{d_{\theta}}
.

Proof.

Denote
K:=K(\epsilon)
the covering number of
[-B,B]^{d_{\theta}}
. Then, by the definition of a covering number, there exist
\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{K}
, such that for any
\theta\in[-B,B]^{d_{\theta}}
, there exists
j\in\{1,\dots,K\}
, such that
|\theta-\theta_{j}|\leq\epsilon
. By Lemma
F.2
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\theta\in[-B,B]^{d_{\theta}}}\left|S^{N}_{\theta}-\mathbb{E}\left[S^{N}_{\theta}\right]\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}\leq 8\epsilon\mathbb{E}[\left|\Psi\Phi\right|^{p}]^{1/p}+\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{j=1,\dots,K}\left|S^{N}_{\theta_{j}}-\mathbb{E}\left[S^{N}_{\theta_{j}}\right]\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}.

We estimate the first term by

8\epsilon\mathbb{E}[\left|\Psi\Phi\right|^{p}]^{1/p}\leq 8\epsilon\mathbb{E}[\left|\Psi\right|^{2p}]^{1/2p}\mathbb{E}[\left|\Phi\right|^{2p}]^{1/2p}=8\epsilon\|\Psi\|_{L^{2p}}\|\Phi\|_{L^{2p}}.

By Lemma
F.3
and Lemma
F.4
, we can estimate the last term

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{j=1,\dots,K}\left|S^{N}_{\theta_{j}}-\mathbb{E}\left[S^{N}_{\theta_{j}}\right]\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq K^{1/p}\max_{j=1,\dots,K}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|S^{N}_{\theta_{j}}-\mathbb{E}\left[S^{N}_{\theta_{j}}\right]\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{16K^{1/p}\sqrt{p}\,\mathbb{E}\left[|\Psi|^{2p}\right]^{1/p}}{\sqrt{N}}

\displaystyle=\frac{16K^{1/p}\sqrt{p}\,\|\Psi\|^{2}_{L^{2p}}}{\sqrt{N}}.

Substitution of these upper bounds now yields

\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{j=1,\dots,K}\left|S^{N}_{\theta_{j}}-\mathbb{E}\left[S^{N}_{\theta_{j}}\right]\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}\leq 8\epsilon\|\Psi\|_{L^{2p}}\|\Phi\|_{L^{2p}}+\frac{16K^{1/p}\sqrt{p}\,\|\Psi\|^{2}_{L^{2p}}}{\sqrt{N}}.

The claimed bound follows.
∎

We also remark the following well-known fact:

Lemma F.6
.

The covering number of
[-B,B]^{d}
satisfies

\displaystyle K(\epsilon)\leq\left(\frac{CB}{\epsilon}\right)^{d},

for some constant
C>0
, independent of
\epsilon
,
B
and
d
.

Proof.

For a proof, see e.g.
(
Welti 2020
, Lemma 5.11)
.
∎

Often, one can prove a bound of the form

\mathcal{G}(u)\leq\Psi(u,y)\leq C\left(1+\|u\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\right)^{\kappa},

and for Gaussian measures
\mu
, we note that there exists
\alpha>0
, such that

\int_{L^{2}_{x}}e^{\alpha\|u\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}}\,d\mu(u)<\infty.

We next want to derive some estimates on
\|\Psi\|_{L^{p}}
, as a function of
p
.

Lemma F.7
.

Let
p\geq 1
,
\alpha>0
. The mapping

\displaystyle[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R},\quad x\mapsto p\log(1+x)-\alpha x^{2},

satisfies the upper bound

p\log(1+x)-\alpha x^{2}\leq p\log\left(1+\frac{p}{2\alpha}\right).

Lemma F.8
.

If
A=\int_{L^{2}_{x}}\exp(\alpha\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}})\,d\mu(u)<\infty
, then

\left(\int_{L^{2}_{x}}\left(1+\|u\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\right)^{\kappa p}\,d\mu(u)\right)^{1/p}\leq A\left(1+\frac{\kappa p}{2\alpha}\right)^{\kappa}.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle(1+\|u\|_{L^{2}})^{\kappa p}

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\kappa p\log(1+\|u\|_{L^{2}})\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\kappa p\log(1+\|u\|_{L^{2}})-\alpha\|u\|^{2}\right)e^{\alpha\|u\|^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(\sup_{x\in[0,\infty)}\kappa p\log(1+x)-\alpha x^{2}\right)e^{\alpha\|u\|^{2}}.

From Lemma
F.7
, it follows that

(1+\|u\|)^{\kappa p}\leq\left(1+\frac{\kappa p}{2\alpha}\right)^{\kappa p}e^{\alpha\|u\|^{2}}.

Thus, we conclude that

\displaystyle\left(\int_{L^{2}}(1+\|u\|_{L^{2}})^{\kappa p}\right)^{1/p}\leq A^{1/p}\left(1+\frac{\kappa p}{2\alpha}\right)^{\kappa}\leq A\left(1+\frac{\kappa p}{2\alpha}\right)^{\kappa},

for all
p\geq 1
, where in the last step, we have used that

A=\int_{L^{2}}\exp(\alpha\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}})\,d\mu(u)\geq 1,

for any
\alpha>0
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.3
.

Note that

\left|\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{N})-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(\widehat{\mathcal{N}})\right|\leq 2\sup_{\theta\in[-B,B]^{d_{\theta}}}\left|\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{N}(\mathcal{N}_{\theta})-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{N}_{\theta})\right|.

We now claim that

(F.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\theta\in[-B,B]^{d_{\theta}}}\left|\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{N}(\mathcal{N}_{\theta})-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{N}_{\theta})\right|\right]\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{N}}\left(1+d_{\theta}\log(CB\sqrt{N})\right)^{2\kappa+1/2},

for
C=C(\alpha,\kappa,\Psi,\Phi)
, from which the claimed bound on the generalization error follows. To prove the claimed inequality (
F.1
), we note that

\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{N}(\mathcal{N}_{\theta})-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{N}_{\theta})=S^{N}_{\theta}-\mathbb{E}[S^{N}_{\theta}].

By Lemma
F.5
and
F.6
, we have for any
p\geq 2
and
\epsilon>0
:

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\theta\in[-B,B]^{d_{\theta}}}\left|S^{N}_{\theta}-\mathbb{E}\left[S^{N}_{\theta}\right]\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}\leq 16\|\Psi\|_{L^{2p}}\left(\epsilon\|\Phi\|_{L^{2p}}+\left(\frac{CB}{\epsilon}\right)^{d_{\theta}/p}\frac{\sqrt{p}\,\|\Psi\|_{L^{2p}}}{\sqrt{N}}\right).

By assumption on
\Psi,\Phi
, there exist constants
C>0
,
\kappa>0
, such that

(F.2)

\displaystyle|\Psi(u,y)|,\,|\Phi(u,y)|\leq C(1+\|u\|_{L^{2}})^{\kappa}.

By Lemma
F.8
, we can thus estimate

\|\Psi\|_{L^{2p}},\,\|\Phi\|_{L^{2p}}\leq C\left(1+\gamma\kappa p\right)^{\kappa},

for constants
C,\gamma>0
, depending only the measure
\mu
and the constant
C
appearing in the upper bound (
F.2
). In particular, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\theta\in[-B,B]^{d_{\theta}}}\left|S^{N}_{\theta}-\mathbb{E}\left[S^{N}_{\theta}\right]\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}\leq 16C^{2}\left(1+\gamma\kappa p\right)^{2\kappa}\left(\epsilon+\left(\frac{CB}{\epsilon}\right)^{d_{\theta}/p}\frac{\sqrt{p}}{\sqrt{N}}\right),

for some constants
C,\gamma>0
, independent of
\kappa
,
\mu
,
B
,
d_{\theta}
,
N
,
\epsilon>0
and
p\geq 2
.

We now choose
\epsilon=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}
, so that

\epsilon+\left(\frac{CB}{\epsilon}\right)^{d_{\theta}/p}\frac{\sqrt{p}}{\sqrt{N}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\left(1+\left(CB\sqrt{N}\right)^{d_{\theta}/p}\sqrt{p}\right).

Next, let
p=d_{\theta}\log(CB\sqrt{N})
. We may wlog assume that
p\geq 2
(otherwise, increase the constant
C
). Then,

\left(CB\sqrt{N}\right)^{d_{\theta}/p}\sqrt{p}=\exp\left(\frac{\log(CB\sqrt{N})d_{\theta}}{p}\right)\sqrt{p}=e\,\sqrt{d_{\theta}\log(CB\sqrt{N})},

and thus we conclude that

\epsilon+\left(\frac{CB}{\epsilon}\right)^{d_{\theta}/p}\frac{\sqrt{p}}{\sqrt{N}}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\left(1+e\,\sqrt{d_{\theta}\log(CB\sqrt{N})}.\right).

On the other hand, we have

\left(1+\gamma\kappa p\right)^{2\kappa}=\left(1+\gamma\kappa d_{\theta}\log(CB\sqrt{N})\right)^{2\kappa}.

Increasing the constant
C>0
, if necessary, we can further estimate

\left(1+\gamma\kappa d_{\theta}\log(CB\sqrt{N})\right)^{2\kappa}\left(1+e\,\sqrt{d_{\theta}\log(CB\sqrt{N})}.\right)\leq C\left(1+d_{\theta}\log(CB\sqrt{N})\right)^{2\kappa+1/2},

where
C>0
depends on
\kappa
,
\gamma
,
\mu
and the constant appearing in (
F.2
), but is independent of
d_{\theta}
,
B
and
N
. We can express this dependence in the form
C=C(\mu,\Psi,\Phi)>0
, as the constants
\kappa
and
\gamma
depend on the Gaussian tail of
\mu
and the upper bound on
\Psi
,
\Phi
.

To conclude, we have shown that there exists a constant
C=C(\mu,\Psi,\Phi)>0
, such that for any
d_{\theta}
,
B
and
N
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\theta\in[-B,B]^{d_{\theta}}}\left|\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{N}(\mathcal{N}_{\theta})-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{N}_{\theta})\right|\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\theta\in[-B,B]^{d_{\theta}}}\left|S^{N}_{\theta}-\mathbb{E}\left[S^{N}_{\theta}\right]\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\theta\in[-B,B]^{d_{\theta}}}\left|S^{N}_{\theta}-\mathbb{E}\left[S^{N}_{\theta}\right]\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{N}}\left(1+d_{\theta}\log(CB\sqrt{N})\right)^{2\kappa+1/2}.

This is the claimed inequality (
F.2
).
∎
